# Knowledge Map

Persistent memory across `/learn` labs. One block per learned concept; terms
listed here are treated as known vocabulary in future lessons, and takeaways
here get a one-line refresher instead of a re-teach. `[name?]` = suggested,
not yet learned.

> **Where things are (2026-09-08).** Lab folders are numbered `NN-slug` in the
> order given by `LEARNING-PATH.md`; the `lab:` field on each entry below is
> the folder name. New labs slot in by decimal insertion (`03.5-slug`). Every
> README follows the voice contract in `STYLE.md`; entries marked *rewritten
> for delivery* had their README re-told in that voice with no content
> removed (`tools/preserve-check.py` was run on each).
>
> **Historical note.** Labs created before 2026-08-29 carry runnable Java
> (converted from Python on 2026-08-28; originals under `_python-original/`,
> and `_java-original/` where a lab was later rebuilt as no-code). Most run
> with `java Foo.java`; the labs needing a real database use H2 (and
> `database-landscape` DuckDB) from `lib/`. Labs since 2026-08-29 contain no
> runnable code. Measured numbers in the entries below are the Java figures.

## url-shortener-system-design — learned 2026-09-07 — lab: 65-url-shortener-system-design/

- **No-code lab** (doc + one focused visualization + exercises/solutions). A capstone HLD lab, built to lean on prerequisites already in this map rather than re-teach them — reads apply `caching` wholesale, sharding applies `consistent-hashing` wholesale, and the CP/AP split applies `cap-theorem`/`pacelc` wholesale. Visualization published: https://claude.ai/code/artifact/1f7101e0-1db6-42e1-a737-b35d2a2302a5
- Takeaway: the storage half of "shorten a URL" is trivial (key → value); the entire interesting design surface is **minting globally unique short codes under concurrent, distributed writers without a coordination bottleneck**. A single DB auto-increment counter gives short, dense, unique codes for free — until more than one writer needs one, at which point "give me the next integer" becomes exactly the global-coordination problem `consistent-hashing` eliminated for key *placement*, now showing up for key *generation* instead.
- The two wrong fixes, both computed exactly: **random-guess-and-retry** doesn't remove the coordination dependency, it makes it probabilistic — every insert, colliding or not, still needs a synchronous uniqueness check against shared state, so it's a strictly worse trade than the counter it replaced. And it has a real wall: at 100M links/day, a 6-character base62 keyspace (62⁶ ≈ 56.8B) is *fully exhausted in 568 days* — a hard collision certainty, not a tunable rate — while 7 chars (62⁷ ≈ 3.52T) sits at a mere 5.2% per-guess collision probability after the same 5-year, 182.5B-link corpus.
- The fix that ships: **block allocation** (Key Generation Service / ticket-server pattern) — a small coordination service atomically advances the one true counter by a whole block (e.g. 1,000) per request, and each writer mints from its private block locally with zero network calls until it's exhausted. This changes coordination *frequency*, not its existence — block size trades 1:1 against coordination-call volume (1,000× fewer KGS calls at block=1,000) against ids wasted on a crash (≤ block size − 1, irrelevant against a trillion-scale keyspace). Contrasted explicitly with **Snowflake** (timestamp+worker-id+sequence, zero coordination service at all, but a longer encoded id — the trade a length-obsessed product like this one specifically won't take).
- The self-routing idea, reused directly from `consistent-hashing`'s own thesis ("the machine count should appear nowhere in the placement rule"): reserve low bits of the minted id to name a shard, so decoding "which DB shard owns this code" is pure arithmetic on the code itself, no lookup table, no metadata service to keep in sync — precisely how Snowflake's worker-id field also works.
- The correctness split, stated precisely: the **one actual invariant** is "two codes never point to two different URLs" — narrower than "the whole system is consistent." Write path (id issuance) is CP-leaning; read path (redirect) is deliberately, unapologetically AP — a stale cached redirect for a few seconds after an alias edit is a chosen feature, not a bug, because a redirect is a promise made in the past that must survive the write path having a bad day (the read/write availability *asymmetry* is the spine of the whole lesson, stated in §5 before any mechanism is introduced).
- Production-shaped adds treated as genuine tradeoffs, not defaults: **301 vs. 302** — 301 lets browsers cache the redirect client-side (saves your infra load forever) but costs you all future click visibility and the ability to ever repoint the link; 302 costs a hit every time but keeps full control, which is why analytics-selling products deliberately eat that cost. Click-counting and malicious-URL screening are kept off the redirect's critical path (async event / pre-write check) for the same reason caching exists at all.
- Key terms now known: short code, base62 encoding, hot key/hot link (URL-shortener flavor), Key Generation Service (KGS)/ticket server, block allocation, Snowflake-style id, self-routing/self-describing id, 301 vs. 302, vanity/custom alias.
- Builds on: [[caching]] (cache-aside redirect path + single-flight stampede protection, applied not re-derived), [[consistent-hashing]] (sharding by code; its explicit "hot key is not solved by hashing" finding, reused unchanged), [[cap-theorem]]/[[pacelc]] (write-CP / read-AP split), [[distributed-rate-limiting]] (create-API abuse protection), [[database-landscape]] (async click-analytics store, OLTP-vs-OLAP shape), [[communication-protocols]] (at-least-once click-event delivery) · Related: [[load-balancing]] (assumed present for the redirect fleet, not re-derived), [[quorums-and-replication]] (if the mapping store is also replicated), [[http-caching]] (the 301/302 discussion is this lab's subject applied to a redirect response) · Next: [[distributed-id-generation?]] (Snowflake/ULID deep dive), [[database-sharding?]] (resharding/rebalancing mechanics), [[cdn?]] (the edge-deployment idea from §15, opened up)
## mongodb — learned 2026-09-05 — lab: 68-mongodb/

- **No-code lab** (doc + 7-scenario replica-set simulator + exercises/solutions). The first *product* capstone on the document-database side, and deliberately the assembly of sixteen earlier labs rather than a new mechanism: almost every part of MongoDB is something already learned, wearing MongoDB's name. Every default, limit and quoted sentence was checked against the **MongoDB 8.3** manual (8.3.0 released 2026-05-04, the current-stable line; 8.1/8.2 rapid releases are already in the legacy docs) or a named paper; the eleven facts that came from memory instead are listed by name in the README's Sources section. The simulator's engine was verified headlessly on JavaScriptCore (`osascript -l JavaScript`, DOM stubbed) — **48 engine assertions + 66 stub-DOM UI checks**, with three invariants asserted on every tick of every scenario: one primary per term, strictly increasing oplog per member, and **no entry at or below any member's commit point ever appears in a rollback file**.
- **The two sentences the lab is built on.** (1) **The document is the unit of everything** — atomicity (one document changes all-or-nothing with no transaction, always), storage locality (one contiguous WiredTiger value), replication (one oplog entry) and distribution (a shard key *inside* the document places the whole document). Embed what you read and write together and all four come free; cross the boundary and each is paid for (transactions, `$lookup`, scatter-gather), and grow a document without bound and you hit the 16 MiB wall. (2) **Durability is a line in a replicated log — the majority commit point — and every consistency knob is a position relative to that line**: `w:1` acknowledges above it, `w:"majority"` at or below it, read concern `local` may return above it, `majority` returns only at or below the member's *known* line, `linearizable` additionally confirms by a majority round trip that the line it knows is the current one, and a causal session is a position relative to your own last operation. Hold that and the read/write-concern reference collapses into one picture.
- **The failure the whole lab is built around (§4, scenario 2, traced tick by tick):** `ord-2` acknowledged with `w:1` at tick 2; `m1` cut off before any secondary pulls; `m2`/`m3` elect at tick 7 (10 s), `m1` steps down at tick 8, heals, syncs, finds the common point `ts 100:1 t1`, and **rolls back** the acknowledged write into `<dbpath>/rollback/<collectionUUID>/removed.<timestamp>.bson`. Nobody crashed, nothing was misconfigured, the customer has the confirmation e-mail. The ack was *true and useless*: it answered "has the primary applied it" when the question that cannot later become false is "is it below the line". Catch-up (`catchUpTimeoutMillis` −1) preserves such writes only when the old primary is *reachable* during the election, which a clean step-down allows and a partition or crash does not — hence the docs' "often the result of a network partition".
- **MongoDB's replication is Raft with one structural change, and it is worth being able to say why**: it is **pull-based** (NSDI 2021, Zhou & Mu — "a replica pulls new data from another replica, which can be initiated by any replica and can happen between any two replicas"). A secondary picks a sync source (primary, or a secondary that is ahead — chaining on by default), tails its oplog, and reports its position back; the primary computes the commit point from reports. That is the visible one-tick gap in the simulator between "m2 pulls" and "commit point advances" (majority ack at tick 2, not 1). Raft's Figure 8 rule survives intact: the commit point only advances over current-term entries, so **a new primary writes a no-op immediately** (watch it in every election). Numbers: heartbeat 2 s, `electionTimeoutMillis` 10 000, candidate must have the highest optime it can see, ≤ 7 voters of ≤ 50 members, no writes during an election, median failover "should not typically exceed 12 seconds", flow control throttles the primary toward `flowControlTargetLagSeconds`.
- **`w:"majority"` precisely**: acknowledged when a *calculated majority* — min(voting majority, number of voting data-bearing members) — has the entry in its **journal** (`writeConcernMajorityJournalDefault: true` implies `j:true`). Implicit default since 5.0 "for *most* deployments" — the exception is the **P-S-A trap** (scenario 6): with an arbiter and data-bearing voters ≤ voting majority, the implicit default silently reverts to `w:1`, because `w:"majority"` would need *both* data members and one failure would block every write; so a double failure loses acknowledged writes exactly as in §4. `wtimeout` "does **not** undo" the write; without it an unachievable concern "will block indefinitely". **An error means nothing**: scenario 3 shows one write that errors and is rolled back and one that errors and survives; the client cannot tell which.
- **Retryable writes are the `idempotency` lab's key built into the protocol**: every single-document write carries `(lsid, txnNumber)`, the driver retries **once** (4.2+ drivers default on), and a primary that already holds that pair returns the stored result instead of applying again — scenario 3's second half, where the ack was lost after replication and the retry lands on the new primary. Not retryable: `updateMany`/`deleteMany`, writes inside transactions. 6.1 added the `NoWritesPerformed` label so "nothing happened" is distinguishable from "unknown".
- **The read side, run against the stale primary (scenario 4 = `split-brain`'s window, from the reader's chair)**: client on `m1`'s side, `m2` elected and holding a newer write. `local` → old value (and would be dirty had `m1` taken writes); `majority` → old value, **durable but stale** — it is served from `m1`'s own frozen commit-point snapshot with no round trip, which is why it is cheap and why it can be stale; `linearizable` → **times out** rather than answer, because it must confirm a majority first (`maxTimeMS` mandatory; primary only; single-document filter only). The 2015 Jepsen post found exactly the first two rows on 2.6; `"majority"` (3.2) and `"linearizable"` (3.4) are the response. **Read preference chooses *who* answers, read concern chooses *what* they may answer with** — orthogonal, and the most-confused pair in the product.
- **Causal sessions = `causal-consistency` + `hybrid-logical-clocks` shipped**: cluster time is an HLC (SIGMOD 2019, Tyulenev et al., uses those words), gossiped on every message and HMAC-signed; a read carrying `afterClusterTime` makes the member *wait* until it has applied that far. Scenario 5: majority ack at tick 2 counted `m1`+`m2`, plain secondary read on lagging `m3` → NOT FOUND (legal), causal read waits until tick 4. The guarantee needs **`majority` reads AND `majority` writes** — the four-row table (rc/wc → RYW / monotonic reads / monotonic writes / WFR: maj/maj all yes; maj/w1 loses monotonic writes; local/anything loses RYW) exists because the 2018 Jepsen report on 3.6.4 showed sub-majority sessions "do not" give causal safety.
- **Inside a document: WiredTiger** = `btree-and-lsm` + `mvcc-and-vacuum` with the optimistic posture of `cockroachdb`: document-level concurrency, intent locks only above document level, write conflicts *transparently retried* for plain writes; every operation reads a point-in-time snapshot but **a cursor is not one snapshot** (the docs: it "could return the same document more than once"); journal buffered and synced every **100 ms** (`commitIntervalMs`) or on `j:true`, checkpoint every **60 s**, journaling mandatory since 6.1, cache = max(50 % of (RAM − 1 GB), 256 MB), snappy blocks / prefix-compressed indexes / zstd for time series. Heap-plus-index layout (record id, `_id` index separate) — not clustered by default.
- **Transactions (4.0 replica sets, 4.2 sharded) are snapshot-isolated on an optimistic engine, priced to be rare** — the manual says twice that "multi-document transactions are not necessary for many practical use cases". Write skew commits cleanly (`isolation-levels` applies unchanged). Failure directions, verbatim: an outside write that touched the document first → the transaction **aborts** with `WriteConflict`; an outside write after the transaction's lock → the outside write **waits**. 5 ms lock wait then abort; **60 s** `transactionLifetimeLimitSeconds`; `TransientTransactionError` = rerun everything, `UnknownTransactionCommitResult` = retry only the commit; oplog: as many ≤ 16 MB entries as needed (4.2); cache pressure aborts with a write conflict or `TransactionTooLargeForCache`. Concerns are set *on the transaction* and do not inherit: with a `w:1` commit, `snapshot` read concern "provides **no** guarantee" — the exact hole the May 2020 Jepsen report on 4.2.6 walked through (plus retry bug SERVER-48307 → 4.2.8), which is why 5.0 flipped the default. Sharded commit always includes `{w:"majority", j:true}` parts and refuses any shard with an arbiter.
- **Sharding = `partitioning-and-rebalancing` with MongoDB's names**: shard key (ranged `1` or `"hashed"`), ranges/chunks of **128 MB** default, `mongos` (no persistent state, refreshes on stale-version errors = MOVED/ASK), config server replica set (metadata goes **read-only** if it loses its primary; data reads/writes continue; no migrations), balancer on the CSRS primary migrating when shards differ by **≥ 3 × range size (384 MB)**, ≤ ⌊n/2⌋ concurrent migrations, the seven-step `moveRange` handover (copy → delta → critical section → metadata → asynchronous orphan deletion — the reason `"available"` read concern exists: `"local"` filters orphans by ownership, `"available"` does not). Chunks split only at migration now (auto-split removed in 6.x). Shard-key properties with the Thali failure for each: cardinality (`city` caps effective shards at 30), frequency (three hot restaurants → jumbo), monotonicity (`_id`/`placedAt` → every insert on the `MaxKey` range → hashed sharding). **Targeted vs scatter-gather is the whole latency story**: queries without the key hit every shard and "do not scale linearly when more shards are added"; `updateOne`/`deleteOne` *must* carry the shard key or `_id`. Changing your mind: shard-key values mutable since 4.2 (not `_id`), `refineCollectionShardKey`, `reshardCollection` (5.0), **8.0**: `moveCollection`/`unshardCollection` for unsharded collections (5-min minimum, ~2 s write block) and **config shards**; **8.3**: DDL only via `mongos`, `removeShard` replaced by draining commands.
- **Indexes and the planner**: `_id` unique index always; ≤ 64 indexes, ≤ 32 fields per compound; **ESR** (equality → sort → range; `$ne`/`$nin`/`$regex` are ranges, `$in` is equality up to 200 elements) — the kitchen-screen index `{restaurantId, status, placedAt}` vs the blocking `SORT` you get otherwise; index builds simultaneous on all data members since 4.4 with a commit quorum, exclusive lock only at start and end, 200 MB default, resumable once. **Plan selection is empirical**: candidates race for a trial and "the winning plan is the query plan that produces the most results during the trial period while performing the least amount of work", cached per query shape (Inactive → Active, LRU, flushed on DDL, replanned when work drifts); **8.3 makes a cost-based ranker the backup default for eligible queries** ("only invokes the CBR for a small amount of queries"). Aggregation: ≤ 1 000 stages, 100 MB per stage then spill, `allowDiskUseByDefault: true` since 6.0.
- **Change streams are the CDC you already have**: a resumable cursor over the oplog that emits **only majority-committed** events, `resumeAfter` / `startAfter` (the latter survives invalidation), resumable only within the oplog window, pre/post-images since 6.0. For a single-document write the document *is* the event — `transactional-outbox` and the business row are the same row; the outbox is still needed when the event is not the document.
- **The Jepsen history read as the changelog of the defaults** (2013 lost writes at every concern → pv1; 2015 stale/dirty primary reads → `majority`/`linearizable` read concerns; 2018 sub-majority causal sessions → the four-row table; 2020 transaction defaults + retry bug → 5.0 `w:"majority"` default). Every safety knob in the lab has a row. Wrong models the lab kills: "the ack means saved" · "MongoDB loses data" (2013 folklore; the free-play challenge is to produce a rollback with majority writes only — you cannot, and the engine asserts it) · "`w:majority` means my read sees it" · "read concern = read preference" · "`majority` reads are the fresh ones" · "an arbiter is a free third vote" · "transactions make it PostgreSQL" · "sharding scales every query" · "schemaless = no schema" · "the oplog is a log like Kafka's".
- **Spring Boot specifics** (§13.3): `w=majority&readConcernLevel=majority&retryWrites=true` in the URI so a topology change cannot change durability; `MongoTransactionManager` with explicit `TransactionOptions` (snapshot/majority) because transactions do not inherit; no HTTP calls inside a transaction; `@DBRef` is the join you designed away.
- Key terms now known: document/BSON/ObjectId (12 bytes, roughly time-ordered), collection, replica set, primary/secondary/arbiter, `priority`/`hidden`/`secondaryDelaySecs`, oplog (`local.oplog.rs`, capped, idempotent, `ts`+`t`), oplog window / initial sync, optime, term, **majority commit point** / `lastCommittedOpTime`, calculated majority, write concern (`w`, `j`, `wtimeout`), `writeConcernMajorityJournalDefault`, implicit default write concern + the P-S-A formula, rollback / recover-to-timestamp / `rollbackTimeLimitSecs` (24 h) / `removed.*.bson`, `electionTimeoutMillis`, sync source / chaining, catch-up / `catchUpTimeoutMillis`, mirrored reads, flow control, retryable write / `lsid` / `txnNumber` / `NoWritesPerformed`, read concern (`local`/`available`/`majority`/`linearizable`/`snapshot`), read preference (5 modes, tag sets, `maxStalenessSeconds`), causally consistent session / `afterClusterTime` / cluster time (HLC, signed), WiredTiger (document-level concurrency, intent locks, write conflict, snapshot, journal `commitIntervalMs` 100 ms, checkpoint 60 s, cache formula), transaction (`transactionLifetimeLimitSeconds` 60, 5 ms lock wait, `TransientTransactionError`, `UnknownTransactionCommitResult`, `TransactionTooLargeForCache`), shard / shard key / ranged vs hashed, range/chunk (128 MB) / jumbo, `mongos`, config server (CSRS) / config shard, balancer / `moveRange` / migration threshold / `_secondaryThrottle` / orphaned documents, targeted vs scatter-gather, `refineCollectionShardKey` / `reshardCollection` / `moveCollection` / `unshardCollection`, `analyzeShardKey`, ESR rule, blocking sort, covered query, multikey/partial/TTL/hashed/wildcard/text/2dsphere indexes, commit quorum, multi-planner / plan cache / cost-based ranker (8.3), aggregation pipeline / `allowDiskUseByDefault`, change stream / resume token / pre- and post-images, `$jsonSchema` validation, bucket/computed/subset/outlier/schema-versioning patterns, time-series collections
- Lab contents: `README.md` (16 stages, 4 depth layers, **5 prediction checkpoints**, three-clerks-and-a-ledger vs engineering mental models with a **5-item "where the analogy lies"**, the machine built in **eight versions** each forced by the previous one's failure, 12-failure catalogue, 10 wrong models, ~60-term glossary, Sources section that separates fetched documentation from the eleven from-memory attributions). `visualization.html` = **The Order Book**, <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/f318abe8-dd80-437b-a77c-c55b788d9b9e> — three members with live oplogs coloured by commit status, the client panel with every request's concern and fate, **7 stepped scenarios** (two write concerns · the ack that lied · majority + wtimeout + retry dedup · the stale primary under three read concerns · lagging secondary with and without a causal session · P-S-A double failure · primary crash and the 12 s outage), **4 predict-then-run cards**, step/back/play by deterministic replay, and free play (write with either concern, read with any concern/preference/session, cut `m1`, heal, kill/restart `m2`, tick). `EXERCISES.md` (8 rungs, 36 exercises) + `SOLUTIONS.md` fully worked. Publishing decision: **published** as an artifact (URL above) as well as kept on disk.
- Builds on: [raft] (pull-based, plus Figure 8 as the no-op), [quorums-and-replication] (the two "majorities"), [split-brain] (scenario 4 is its window), [linearizability] (the read write-back as a read mode), [causal-consistency] + [hybrid-logical-clocks] (cluster time, sessions), [btree-and-lsm] + [mvcc-and-vacuum] (WiredTiger), [isolation-levels] (snapshot isolation, write skew), [partitioning-and-rebalancing] + [consistent-hashing] (sharding), [jepsen] (four reports as the defaults' history), [cockroachdb] + [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] (proceed-and-detect), [idempotency] (retryable writes), [database-landscape], [cap-theorem]/[pacelc] (the CP posture when a majority is unreachable)
- Related: [change-data-capture] + [transactional-outbox] (change streams), [cqrs] (read-side collections for scatter-gather queries), [distributed-transactions] (the sharded commit), [redis] (the other acknowledged-from-memory system), [kafka] (what the oplog is not) · Commonly confused: write concern vs read concern vs read preference · consensus majority vs quorum overlap · rollback vs abort · range vs shard · cluster time vs oplog `ts`
- Next: [mongodb-schema-design-patterns?], [mongodb-aggregation?], [wiredtiger-internals?], [dynamodb?], [cassandra?], [elasticsearch?], [database-indexing?] (asked for by 5 labs already; this one adds ESR and the empirical planner as evidence)

## paxos — relearned 2026-09-01 (first learned 2026-08-27) — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-08 — lab: 39-paxos/

- **Rebuilt as a no-code lab** (doc ~1020 lines + the existing message-level simulator + 8-part exercises/solutions); the 2026-08-27 Java is archived under `_java-original/`, the original Python under `_python-original/`. This was the workspace's **first-ever lab** and it had drifted furthest behind: it still shipped 5 `.java` files, and it predated ~30 downstream labs (`raft`, `flp-impossibility`, `zab`, `split-brain`, `quorums-and-replication`, `jepsen`, `google-spanner`) that it now connects forward to. Every number below was produced by driving `visualization.html`'s own protocol engine headlessly (`osascript -l JavaScript`, DOM stubbed) — **40 behavioural checks green**, all five guided experiments, plus five one-rule mutations and a 500-schedule soak.
- **Takeaway: Paxos is a protocol for asking permission to speak, where the price of permission is having to repeat what you were told.** A proposer is a courier, not an author: it may use its own value *only* if a majority reports that nothing has ever been accepted. Because any two majorities share a member, the past can never hide. Safety is unconditional; liveness is not guaranteed at all, and that missing half is the entire reason leaders exist.
- **The headline measurement, and the one table to remember.** Identical 500-run soak (60 steps, random deliver/drop/duplicate/crash; 14,751 deliveries, 3,300 dropped, 2,835 duplicates, 1,782 crash-restarts, highest proposal number 33): **duelling proposers decided 36/500; one non-overlapping leader decided 220/500; safety violations 0 in both.** ~6× the decision rate from changing nothing but who is allowed to speak. That is the empirical case for Multi-Paxos/Raft/ZAB — a leader is a **throughput** mechanism, never a correctness one, so two leaders at once is a latency incident, not corruption.
- **Five one-rule mutations, each measured, each breaking something different** — this is the spine of the rebuilt lab: (1) **quorum = N/2** → `{A,B}` and `{D,E}` are disjoint deciders → `chosen=["Zoo","Museum"]`; half-plus-one is the smallest number that *forces* intersection. (2) **adoption deleted** → witness C reports the Zoo and is ignored → two decisions; *overlap only buys safety if you act on what it tells you*. (3) **adopt lowest `accepted_n` instead of highest** → a value never chosen (`Zoo@1` on one acceptor) resurrected on top of one that was (`Museum@2` on a majority) → `chosen=["Museum","Zoo"]`. (4) **phase 1 skipped** ("acceptors already refuse low numbers, so just send Accept with a big n" — the most commonly proposed Paxos optimization) → blind `Accept(5,Zoo)` to `{A,B,C}` then blind `Accept(9,Museum)` to `{C,D,E}` → two decisions, because `9 >= 5`. (5) **accept condition `>` instead of `>=`** → `chosen=[]`, 5 NACKs, one proposer, no faults — every proposer refused by *its own* phase-1 promise.
- **The danger sentence about testing, worth more than the protocol detail.** Re-run the same 500-schedule soak with **durability off** (acceptors forget on restart — the classic real-world bug) and the result is **identical: 0 violations**. Random chaos never reaches the state where durability matters, because breaking safety that way needs *two* decisions in one run and decisions are rare under loss. The bug takes six deliberate steps to reproduce (experiment 5: `chosen=["Zoo","Museum"]`) and is invisible otherwise. **A green soak is evidence about your soak's coverage, not about your durability** — the [jepsen] lesson, arrived at from inside a protocol.
- **The two rules that are the whole algorithm**, both pure functions: **Rule A** — with a majority of promises, propose the reported value with the **highest `accepted_n`**, or your own if none reported anything (*not* the most common — one report of `(4,Zoo)` beats two of `(2,Museum)`, and that trap is mutation 3). **Rule B** — chosen means a majority accepted the **same `(n,v)` pair**, not merely the same value. Everything else in Paxos is plumbing.
- **Multi-Paxos, now a full stage rather than a paragraph.** One Paxos instance per log slot; the refinement is that `Prepare(n)` claims `n` for slot *k and every slot after it*, so a proposer completing phase 1 once holds a standing promise and goes straight to `Accept` — **one round trip per command instead of two**, with phase 1 re-run only on leader change. A leader is therefore *not* a role the protocol grants: it is simply the proposer currently holding phase-1 promises from a majority. Multi-Paxos permits **holes** (slot 11 decided while 10 is not) and out-of-order acceptance, which is what enables pipelining; the price is that a new leader must run phase 1 across the log tail and propose a **no-op** into every hole it finds.
- **Key terms now known** (additions to the 2026-08-27 set): Rule A / Rule B as named objects, witness, slot, hole/gap, no-op, replicated state machine, partial synchrony, atomic commitment (as the thing Paxos is *not*), Byzantine failure (as the assumption boundary).
- **Nine wrong models the lab now actively kills**, the load-bearing ones being: *chosen means someone announced it* (it is a global property that can be true while nobody knows — hence learners, and hence a client timeout meaning **unknown**, never **no**); *the highest proposal number wins* (numbers order **attempts**, never values — `n=9,000,000` still has to carry `(3,"Zoo")`); *phase 1 is permission* (it is **discovery**; remember it as permission and you will approve mutation 4); *Paxos elects a leader* (it decides the **value** `"the leader is node 3"`, which is then interpreted as leadership); and *more acceptors = more throughput* (the opposite — scale is many groups, per [consistent-hashing]).
- **Builds on:** [quorums-and-replication] (the overlap property this entire lab rests on; note its *consensus quorum decides / read-write quorum merely intersects* distinction — Paxos is emphatically the first), [two-phase-commit] (**the contrast that defines it**: 2PC is atomic commitment, needs everyone, every participant has a veto, and **blocks forever** if the coordinator dies; Paxos is consensus, needs half plus one, nobody has a veto, and any proposer can finish anyone's work), [cap-theorem] (Paxos *is* the CP corner — the majority side works, the minority refuses) · **Related:** [raft] (**the direct sequel and the answer to this lab's closing line** — Raft's term is Paxos's proposal number spent on shifts; Raft **forbids** the holes Multi-Paxos permits, and that one restriction is most of why it is easier), [flp-impossibility] (why the liveness gap is permanent, not a gap someone will close), [zab], [split-brain] (*make the loser harmless* rather than prevent two leaders — the same conclusion the leader measurement reaches), [jepsen] (why the green soak above is not reassurance), [google-spanner] + [cockroachdb] (one group per shard, in production), [idempotency] (what actually makes the client retry safe — Paxos guarantees *a* value is chosen, never *whose*) · **Next:** [replicated-state-machine?], [byzantine-fault-tolerance?] (quorum arithmetic moves `N/2+1` → `2N/3+1`), [cluster-reconfiguration?] (changing membership under a running protocol is where overlap gets genuinely hard). Primary sources now readable: *Paxos Made Simple* (2001, 14 pages), *Paxos Made Live* (2007, the Chubby engineering account).

## cqrs — relearned 2026-09-01 (first learned 2026-08-27) — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-09 — lab: 51-cqrs/

- **Rebuilt as a no-code lab** (doc ~1550 lines + 5-panel visualization + 8-rung exercises/solutions); the 2026-08-27 Java version is archived under `_java-original/`. Built because **two later labs filed complaints against it**: `change-data-capture` asked for "consistency window given a measurement" and `eventual-consistency` for "their consistency window finally given edges". This entry is those edges. Engine validated before use against M/M/1 (399.1 vs 400.0), M/M/4 (141.5 vs the 140 this map's `queueing-theory` entry already recorded), the poll-floor formula, and the capacity formula; the page's JS was exercised headlessly against a DOM stub across 37 control combinations and 13 figure spot-checks — and that exercise **found a real hang** (batch × hazard ≫ 1 replays unboundedly), now a work budget that surfaces the poison batch instead.
- **Takeaway: the read model is allowed to be stale only because it makes no decisions.** That one sentence is the licence for everything else — denormalised, duplicated, disposable, behind — and the moment a command consults a projection the window stops being a UX cost and becomes a correctness bug. Measured: **one unit of stock, 40 orders/s, an utterly ordinary 500 ms poll → 11.95 orders accepted on average, 29 at p99, 41 at worst.** The count is exactly λ × window (predicted 11.87). Tightening the window 2 s → streaming takes it 46.66 → **0.33 and never to zero**, which is the whole lesson: *you cannot fix a decision made on stale data by making the data less stale; you can only move the decision.* One conditional `UPDATE … WHERE units >= 1` on the write model is 0 at any window. Grep command handlers for reads of projection tables — every hit is a bug or a documented decision, and most are neither.
- **The window, finally given edges.** `mean ≈ (poll/2)(1+ρ) + apply + ckpt` — checked at ρ=0.96: formula 491, measured 491. So at the low utilisation a healthy projector runs at, **the consistency window is a config value someone copied from another service**: poll 100/500/1000/2000/5000 ms → mean 65.1 / **297.2** / 586.8 / 1160.9 / 2513.0 ms. Visibility is **quantised** (applies + checkpoint share one transaction, so a whole batch appears at one instant) — the distribution is a sawtooth, not a bell, which is why p50 ≈ mean and p95 jumps.
- **The cliff, and the operational conclusion that follows from it.** ρ = 0.10 → 0.98 moves the mean window **307 → 509 ms (1.7× across almost the entire operating range)**; ρ = 0.98 → 1.02 multiplies it by **17× (509 → 8,661 ms)**; past that it is not a number (14.3 / 29.0 / 57.9 / **118.2 s** as the run doubled 5→40 min). Flat because a polling projector is a **bulk-service queue** — below capacity the window is set by the sleep, not the queue. Therefore: **projector lag is not a leading indicator of projector saturation; you must graph the projector's utilisation separately.** Same shape as `load-shedding`'s "50.1/s during a total outage and 50.0/s during optimal operation".
- **Streaming fixes the floor, not the ceiling: 297.2 → 8.1 ms mean (37×) with capacity identical at 487.8/s.** And a correction to my own prior intuition, worth keeping: **batching does not trade latency for throughput** — batch 1 → 2000 changed capacity 3.5× and the window not at all (8.12 ms throughout), because the batch is *opportunistic*. Batching costs latency only when you **wait** to fill it — and a poll interval **is** a linger timer. That is the entire v2-vs-v3 gap, one mechanism seen twice.
- **RYOW with numbers:** at poll 500 ms, **89.4% of users see their own write missing** if the page queries 100 ms later (59.5% at 250 ms, 9.5% at 500 ms); streaming is 0.0% at every think time — the highest-leverage fix available and a config change, not an architecture one. Honest limit: at ρ=0.92 streaming's own window is 89.6 ms mean / 197 p99 and 87% fail at 50 ms.
- **Two read models are never in sync with each other — ever.** Two projectors with **identical** config, load and code, whose timers merely fire 250 ms apart, disagree on **100% of page loads** (mean gap 10.1 events). Dashboard-vs-5s-revenue-board: 100%, gap 90.6, max 238. So: never compute a value by combining two read models; anything a page must show consistently belongs in **one** read model behind **one** projector — a real reason to denormalise harder than feels comfortable.
- **Projection correctness is one requirement with two independent purchases.** Crash matrix (200k events, 210 crashes): idempotent+atomic 0.00%, idempotent-only 0.00%, atomic-only 0.00%, **neither +5.39%** — either alone suffices, and the failure is silent (no error, no alert, drifts forever). **Batch size is the blast radius**: with no protection, error by batch = 0.09 / 0.49 / 5.39 / 32.92 / **242.81%** (batch 1/10/100/500/2000 — reported revenue 3.4× the truth at 2000). And a *second*, separate batch limit that bites **even when fully correct**: replayed events are themselves exposed to the hazard, so **B·h** governs wasted replay — 0.05 / 0.33 / 0.76 / 2.43 / **27.13×** at B·h = 0.1/0.5/1/2/5, confirmed at two independent hazard rates (0.13/0.29/0.70/1.96 at the same B·h). At B·h=12 the projector applied **1.5% of the feed** and had no forward progress: the **poison batch**, self-inflicted, and worst exactly when h is already elevated.
- **The most useful production fact in the lab: Kafka takes one of the two protections away.** The atomic checkpoint requires the checkpoint to be a row in the read store. A Kafka offset lives in Kafka, no transaction spans both, so **every Kafka-fed projection is structurally the non-atomic column and idempotency is the only protection it has** — audit every `+=`. The standard escape: store the offset in the read store yourself in the same transaction and `seek()` on assignment (the exactly-once-sink pattern).
- **Ordering: partitioning by key converts a correctness failure into a latency failure.** 8 round-robin workers, one 1-second stall → **3 of 20,000 orders cancelled-but-showing-alive, permanently** (not stale — converged wrong, and a rebuild disagrees); keyed by order id, same stall → **0**, with the stall becoming 990 ms of delay. Honest note: stalling *one* worker of W looks safer as W grows (8→3→1) purely because the blast radius is 1/W; let every worker stall once and the total is **flat from 8 workers up** (18/23/29/29/29).
- **Rebuild = H/(μ − λ), so your rebuild speed is your projector's SPARE capacity.** 50 M events: **31.0 h at ρ=0.08, 158.2 h (6.6 days) at ρ=0.82** — a number most teams would call healthy — 780 h at 0.96, and **never at ρ=1**, which means a read-side schema change you can never make. Compute it quarterly, not during the incident. A rebuild SLO is not operational hygiene: it constrains your **partitioning key and retention at design time** (4-hour rebuild of 50 M needs 3,472 ev/s of *spare* capacity — 7× the whole projector — so it forces parallel keyed replay).
- **The case for the split is two arguments, and the one people lead with is the weaker one.** (1) **Isolation, measured**: checkout 300/s @5 ms sharing 8 workers with 800 ms reports — p99 **23 ms at 0 reports/s → 406 ms at 4 → 1,590 ms at 6 → 13,192 ms at 8**, with checkout's own traffic and code never changing; head-of-line blocking, not CPU (406 ≈ half one report's service time). **Giving checkout 25% FEWER workers (6, alone) beat 8 shared with 6 reports/s by 69× (23 ms vs 1,590).** This is `load-balancing`'s pooling result read backwards: pool identical work, **separate dissimilar work**. (2) **The arithmetic**: read-time cost is R·J, projection-time cost is N·J′, so the deciding quantity is **R/N and nothing else** — not data volume, not "we're at scale". At R/N = 0.1 projections cost **10× more** than the queries they replace. And the distinction replicas obscure: **a read replica multiplies the machines paying R·J; a projection deletes R·J.**
- **v1 (CQRS-lite) deserves more respect than it gets and should be said out loud in design reviews**: synchronous projection inside the write transaction, **window exactly 0**, RYOW free, no projector/checkpoint/rebuild. Costs measured: 0/1/2/3/5/8 inline read models → checkout p99 **23/32/42/51/70/108 ms**. Only three things break it, and only one is performance: you cannot put Elasticsearch in your transaction; the search index being down now fails checkout (availability coupled backwards); and the write path inherits every read model's worst day.
- Corrections/distinctions this lab now pins: **CQS ≠ CQRS** (Meyer 1988, a rule about *methods*, vs Young ~2010, a rule about *models*) — splitting handler classes and keeping one schema buys **nothing** from the contention table; **CQRS ≠ event sourcing** (this lab's write model stores current state and emits a feed; delete the feed and it is still complete) and most production CQRS is **not** event sourced; **read model ≠ cache** (populated eagerly not on demand; repaired by *replay* not *invalidation*; and a miss is the answer, not a slow correct answer); CQRS does **not** scale writes.
- Key terms now known: command vs query, CQS vs CQRS, write/read model, event feed, projection/projector, checkpoint (= consumer offset = LSN), consistency window as a *distribution*, μ = 1000·B/(B·apply+ckpt), ρ for a projector, bulk-service queue, visibility quantisation, poll interval as a linger timer, opportunistic batching, idempotent apply vs atomic checkpoint, **B·h / poison batch**, effectively-once, blast radius, read-your-own-writes via consistency token (a session guarantee, not a global one), cross-model skew, rebuild = H/(μ−λ), blue/green projection, parallel keyed replay, reconciliation residual, CQRS-lite
- Lab contents: `README.md` (16 sections, 4 depth layers, 6 prediction checkpoints, 8 failure scenarios, 8 wrong models, ~28-term glossary, sources), `visualization.html` = **The Two Desks**, 5 panels (the window + the cliff · RYOW and the oversell, with a predict-then-run · the shared-vs-split pool · the crash matrix, batch blast radius and ordering-under-stall · the rebuild curve), `EXERCISES.md`/`SOLUTIONS.md` (8 rungs; rung 5 hand-traces six events through a batching projector and a crash — verified digit-for-digit against the engine; rung 6 has 8 planted flaws; 8.4 asks which decisions *can* safely be made from a read model — answer: advisory ones, **monotone** predicates where a stale *yes* is safe though a stale *no* is not, and ones with a written-down bounded cost; 8.5 removes the impossibility and finds the window, the oversell, the skew and the rebuild all survive it).
- Builds on: [transactional-outbox] (assumed throughout — without it a read model is not stale but wrong), [idempotency] (**its rule applied to one specific consumer; this lab's only correctness requirement**), [queueing-theory] (ρ = λ/μ, and the cliff — here in bulk-service form, which is *why* it gives no warning)
- Answers, retroactively: [eventual-consistency] (**its "CQRS and caching never give the window edges" complaint cashed in** — a formula, a distribution, and the point where it stops being finite; and EC's liveness framing is why this lab measures the window instead of testing the promise), [change-data-capture] (same complaint from the other side; "rebuildable projections given a mechanism" = §11.8's H/(μ−λ)), [load-balancing] (**its pooling result read backwards** — separate dissimilar work — with checkout's 69× as the number), [kafka] (offsets as checkpoints, and the structural loss of the atomic checkpoint)
- Related: [event-sourcing] (the neighbour it is confused with; §14.1 draws the line), [saga], [caching] (§14.5's five-row table separates them properly), [two-phase-commit]/[distributed-transactions] (the price v1 refuses to pay — 8.5's counterfactual prices it explicitly), [causal-consistency] (the consistency token is a session guarantee), [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] (the conditional write, again)
- Next: [materialized-views?] (the same projection idea run by the database — still the top open suggestion from this lab, as it was in 2026-08-27), [observability-and-slos?] (**this lab alarms on four distinct things and defines none of them**; now named by several labs), [ddd-aggregates?] (what decides where a write model's boundary goes)

## kafka-exactly-once — learned 2026-09-01 — lab: 55-kafka-exactly-once/

- **No-code lab** (doc + 9-scenario protocol simulator + exercises/solutions). Deliberately the *machine underneath* [kafka] §10.11's sixty-line sketch — that section gave the boundary ("exactly-once inside Kafka") without the mechanism, and you cannot debug a boundary. Every version number, default and quoted sentence was checked against the **Kafka 4.3** docs (May 2026) and the KIPs, never recalled; the simulator's engine was verified headlessly (`osascript -l JavaScript`, DOM stubbed) — **1,987 checks**, all nine scenario outcomes, LSO monotonicity, offset consecutiveness, reader invariants, plus every UI interaction driven forwards and backwards.
- **The one sentence the lab is built on: Kafka has no rollback. It has retraction, published after the fact, which the reader is responsible for honouring.** Records are appended to their real partitions at their real offsets *before any decision exists*; the decision is then published as a **control record** appended into each participating partition; readers who care refuse to read past the first undecided record. Once you hold that, the LSO, hanging transactions, offset gaps, aborted-data bandwidth and "why is my consumer still seeing aborted records" stop being separate facts.
- **What is actually made atomic**: a set of *appends to Kafka logs* — the outputs **plus the consumer offset commit**, swept into the same set because in Kafka a committed offset *is a record in a topic*. That single move converts the three-action problem (read → write → record progress) into one action. A broker that keeps per-consumer ack state in a database could not do this at all.
- **The nine-step protocol, traced with real offsets**: `FindCoordinator` → `InitProducerId` (pid+epoch, fences the previous holder) → `beginTransaction()` **sends nothing** → `AddPartitionsToTxn` (**this is where `transaction.timeout.ms` starts**, not `beginTransaction()` — verified in the 4.3 producer docs) → produce (records visible to `read_uncommitted` *now*) → `AddOffsetsToTxn` to the txn coordinator + `TxnOffsetCommit` to the **group** coordinator → `EndTxn` → **`PrepareCommit` = the commit point** → `WriteTxnMarkers` → `CompleteCommit`. States: Empty · Ongoing · PrepareCommit/PrepareAbort · CompleteCommit/CompleteAbort · Dead.
- **Why this 2PC does not block, and it is the most transferable idea in the lab.** Three stacked reasons: (1) **participants never vote**, so no broker ever enters an uncertain state — the data is already appended, no locks are held, and a partition leader *cannot refuse* a marker; (2) the coordinator's memory **is a replicated Kafka log** (`__transaction_state`, 50 partitions, RF 3, min ISR 2), so failover is a leader election plus a replay — [two-phase-commit] said the fix for a blocking coordinator was to replicate its state, and Kafka gets that free; (3) the waiting that remains is **bounded and voluntary** — only `read_committed` readers wait, capped by `transaction.timeout.ms`. **General rule now stated: when you meet a distributed commit problem, ask first whether the uncertainty can be pushed to the reading side — a reader that waits is merely stale, a writer that waits is holding locks.**
- **The LSO is a property of the PARTITION, not of a producer** — *"one less than the offset of the first open transaction"* (4.3 consumer docs). So one hung transaction blocks *everything behind it*, including healthy non-transactional records from unrelated applications. **A transaction's blast radius is the partition**, and transaction duration on a shared topic is an unpublished latency SLA you have imposed on five other teams.
- **Two fencing mechanisms, two different zombies** — constantly conflated: **producer epoch** (transaction coordinator, keyed on `transactional.id`) catches a stale *producer instance*; **group generation** (group coordinator, via `ConsumerGroupMetadata`'s `member.id`/`generation.id` passed to `sendOffsetsToTransaction`) catches a stale *partition owner*. The second is KIP-447 / Kafka 2.5, and it is the whole reason EOS became practical: **EOS v1 needed one `transactional.id`, hence one producer, per input partition** (40 partitions = 40 producers, re-initialised on every rebalance). v1/beta removed in 4.0 — `processing.guarantee` valid values in 4.3 are exactly `[at_least_once, exactly_once_v2]`.
- **The KIP-890 bug, derivable from two facts and worth being able to reconstruct**: records are appended before any decision, and in protocol v1 a commit/abort does **not** bump the epoch — so a produce request that stalls in the network, whose transaction then aborts, arrives carrying `(pid 4711, epoch 0)` into the *next* transaction of the same producer and is committed by its marker. Variant two is worse: if that partition is not re-added, no marker is ever coming → **hanging transaction**, LSO stuck forever, **and no timeout fires because the coordinator has no open transaction**. Fix phase 1 = **verification** (`transaction.partition.verification.enable`, default **true**), fix phase 2 = **bump the epoch on every transaction** so `(pid, epoch)` identifies a *transaction* not a *producer* — `transaction.version=2`, automatically enabled on the server since 4.0. Also: `AddPartitionsToTxn` moved server-side, and the old 20 ms client backoff for `CONCURRENT_TRANSACTIONS` became server-side retries, so **produce latency percentiles step up on upgrade while end-to-end latency is flat** (documented, not folklore).
- **The three silent failures.** (1) **`isolation.level` is still `read_uncommitted` by default in 4.3** — a team enables EOS on the producer, ships it, and every downstream consumer keeps reading aborted records as real data; and this is *worse* than not using EOS, because EOS deliberately generates aborted records on every rebalance. **First audit question, always: show me the consumer's `isolation.level`.** (2) **A random `transactional.id`** (UUID / pod name / timestamp) fences nothing — two instances commit overlapping work and both succeed, with the tell being an id count that grows every deploy and never shrinks (`transactional.id.expiration.ms` = 7 days). (3) **A hanging transaction** — high watermark rising, LSO flat, `read_committed` lag climbing linearly while `read_uncommitted` consumers are perfectly healthy, log cleanup stopped. Alert on **(HW − LSO) growing monotonically**; nothing else sees it. Recovery is manual: `kafka-transactions.sh --find-hanging` / `--abort` (KIP-664) — **abort only, never commit**.
- **What it does NOT guarantee, precisely**: your processing function still runs **at-least-once** (every abort is a reprocessing) — only the losing attempts' *Kafka writes* stay invisible; nothing outside Kafka; no isolation in the [isolation-levels] sense (no locks, no conflict detection — which is exactly *why* §10.4's protocol can be non-blocking); and no dedup of two `send()`s your own code made. Against [acid] you get **A** and **D** (with Kafka's page-cache-not-fsync caveat), and neither **C** nor **I**.
- **Costs, all real**: markers are appended into *every* partition touched, on every commit — and Streams silently changes `commit.interval.ms` **30000 → 100** under `exactly_once_v2` (documented default), i.e. 300× more transactions, and that interval becomes the downstream latency floor; aborted records are **written, replicated, stored, and fetched by every consumer group** before the *client* discards them (the broker can't filter — that would kill `sendfile`); **an abort costs more than a commit** (same data plus a marker) and reclaims nothing; every live `transactional.id` holds coordinator state for 7 days.
- **New since the folklore**: KIP-618 Connect source EOS (3.3); KIP-939 **2PC with an external coordinator** — `transaction.two.phase.commit.enable` + `prepareTransaction()` + `initTransactions(keepPreparedTxn)`, **shipped 4.1** (dated by diffing the 4.0 and 4.1 broker-config pages; the upgrade notes don't name it). Read its own config description: *"transactions that this client starts never expire"* — **the timeout that bounds every reader's wait is switched off**, i.e. [two-phase-commit]'s blocking problem deliberately re-admitted, for frameworks with their own durable coordinator (Flink, FLIP-319) and not for application code. For a Spring Boot service the answer is still the outbox.
- **Spring specifics** (the learner's stack): `transaction-id-prefix` creates a `KafkaTransactionManager`; the listener container runs the EOS loop and enforces `enable.auto.commit=false`; Spring Kafka 3.x supports **only** `EOSMode.V2`; `ChainedKafkaTransactionManager` is **deprecated since 2.7** and never delivered what its name suggests — nesting gives an **ordering, not atomicity**, so DB-commits-then-Kafka-fails still reprocesses and double-writes.
- The hop→tool map the lab exists to complete: **Kafka→Kafka** = transactions · **DB→Kafka** = [transactional-outbox]/[change-data-capture] · **Kafka→DB** = [idempotent-consumer] · **Kafka→external API** = [idempotency] · **multi-service business txn** = [saga].
- Wrong models the lab kills: "exactly-once means my code runs once" · "abort is a rollback" (it is neither a rollback nor a [saga] compensation — a third thing) · "turning it on in the producer is enough" · "any unique string works as a transactional.id" · "Kafka transactions are ACID" · "it's just slower, nothing else changes" · "a hanging transaction will time out eventually" · "it's off by default because it's experimental".
- Key terms now known: three-action problem, `transactional.id` (names a *job*, not a process), producer id/epoch, transaction coordinator vs group coordinator, `__transaction_state`, `AddPartitionsToTxn`/`AddOffsetsToTxn`/`TxnOffsetCommit`/`EndTxn`/`WriteTxnMarkers`, `PrepareCommit` as the commit point, control record/transaction marker, last stable offset, `AbortedTransactions` list + `.txnindex`, `isolation.level`, `ProducerFenced`/`InvalidProducerEpoch`, `ConsumerGroupMetadata` + generation fencing, EOS v1 (eos-alpha) vs EOS v2 (KIP-447), transaction version 2 (KIP-890), transaction verification, hanging transaction, `kafka-transactions.sh`, `transaction.timeout.ms`/`transaction.max.timeout.ms`/`transactional.id.expiration.ms`, `processing.guarantee`, KIP-939 2PC participation, effectively-once
- Lab contents: README (4 depth layers, **5 prediction checkpoints**, clerk-and-countersign mental model with **4 explicit analogy breaks**, the coordinator state machine, a **six-version mechanism ladder** each forced by the previous version's failure, 8-failure catalogue, 8 wrong models, 40-term glossary, and a Sources section separating documentation from inference). `visualization.html` ("The Settlement Run"): app / coordinator / three partition logs / two readers, **9 stepped scenarios** (commit · abort · producer crash · coordinator crash · zombie fenced · zombie unfenced · KIP-890 leak v1 · the same delay under v2 · hanging transaction), an `isolation.level` toggle that changes the live payout verdict, step/back/play, and 4 predict-then-run cards.
- Builds on: [kafka] (**this is its §10.11 opened up** — log, offsets, partitions, ISR, high watermark, groups and the idempotent producer are all assumed, none retaught), [two-phase-commit] (the protocol's shape, and §10.4 is entirely about why this one does not block), [idempotency] (the answer for every hop that leaves Kafka), [idempotent-consumer] (**exactly complementary scope** — it covers every redelivery source except the one a transaction removes; this covers the one hop it declines), [transactional-outbox] (the same dual-write problem inbound; together they complete the hop map), [split-brain]/[distributed-locking] (the fencing token, appearing twice here for two different zombies), [saga] (compensation vs abort), [acid]/[isolation-levels] (the vocabulary for saying what a Kafka transaction is *not*), [queueing-theory] (marker amplification and the commit-interval/throughput trade)
- Related: [change-data-capture], [event-sourcing], [cqrs] · Commonly confused: idempotent producer vs transaction · Kafka `read_committed` vs a database's READ COMMITTED · leader epoch vs producer epoch · compaction vs abort · rebalance vs transaction abort
- Next: [kafka-streams?], [flink?], [debezium?], [stream-processing-semantics?], [xa-transactions?]

## idempotent-consumer — learned 2026-09-01 — lab: 54-idempotent-consumer/

- **No-code lab** (doc + 4-design simulator + exercises/solutions). Deliberately a *sibling* of `idempotency`, not a repeat: that lab solved the request handler with a caller; this one removes the caller and multiplies the redelivery sources. Every outcome asserted in the README was reproduced headlessly from the simulator's own engine across a 4-designs × 5-faults matrix (`osascript -l JavaScript`, DOM stubbed) — checked, not asserted.
- Takeaway: **"Make the consumer idempotent" is not one obligation but three, and they fail independently.** **R1 Identity** — the message needs a name that survives republication; **R2 Atomicity** — the record of processing must commit in the same act as the effect; **R3 Memory** — that record must still exist when the duplicate arrives, and *the arrival time of the duplicate is not bounded by anything in your code*. R2 is the only one the `idempotency` lab already solved. R1 and R3 are the consumer's own problems and each has a popular answer that fails with no error message.
- **The seven redelivery sources, and the two properties of the list that generate the whole lab**: relay republish · producer network retry · crash before offset commit · rebalance · retry topic · DLQ replay · deliberate reprocess. Their *time gap* spans six orders of magnitude (ms → months), and their *identity* differs by source — sources 3 and 4 redeliver the same record at the same offset, sources 1, 5, 6 redeliver the same fact at a **new** offset. Any scheme handling only same-offset redelivery handles two of seven.
- **Result 1 — offsets are DELIVERY identity, not MESSAGE identity.** Dedup on `(topic, partition, offset)` under a PK, in the effect's transaction, correctly kills the crash case AND the rebalance zombie — which is why so many production consumers stop there — and is then defeated by the relay republishing the same outbox row (measured in the sim: design B, republish → the fact applied twice, 130 pts vs 80). Same hole for retry topics, MirrorMaker'd clusters, any re-publish. Fix: the producer mints the id **at fact-creation time** (the outbox row's own id, inside the state-change transaction), so it is unchanged across every republication.
- **Result 2 — the consumer's dual write (effect in Postgres, offset in Kafka) is BENIGN, and this is worth being able to argue.** Four crash points: (✗,✗) redelivered+processed ✓ · (✓,✗) redelivered, inbox suppresses ✓ · (✗,✓) **loss** · (✓,✓) ✓. Row 3 is unreachable if the offset is committed *after* the DB transaction, so the inbox converts "lost offset commit" from a correctness problem into a performance one. Corollary that inverts common advice: **offsets-in-your-database is strictly WEAKER than an inbox**, not stronger — it is offset-dedup wearing a hat, blind to republication, plus it makes you own a `ConsumerRebalanceListener`, plus a deliberate replay defeats it. You need it only if you have decided not to keep an inbox.
- **Result 3 — the incident that actually happens: retention.** 4,000/s = 345M inbox rows/day, so someone adds `DELETE … older than 7 days`. Nine days after an incident an engineer replays 40,000 DLQ records to *repair* it and every one is applied twice; the replay tool reports 40,000 successes. **The inbox's retention must exceed the maximum redelivery age — and redelivery age is set by human operational behaviour, not by any config in the repo.** So for the DLQ/reprocess sources a retention-based inbox is a *bet*, not a guarantee, and its losing side is silent. The three honest responses: write the limit in the runbook and have the replay tool *refuse* beyond it; give replays their own dedup scope; or stop needing retention.
- **Result 4 — the watermark dissolves R3 rather than sizing it.** Store the highest applied per-key sequence *on the entity row* and guard with `UPDATE … SET points=…, last_seq=:s WHERE id=:k AND last_seq < :s`. Storage O(entities) not O(messages) (millions vs 126 billion/yr); no pruning, so no window, so no F5; **reorder-safe for free**; and it is the conditional write in yet another costume. Costs, all real: needs per-key ordered delivery (so parallelising a partition across threads, or non-blocking retries, breaks it — and its failure mode is **silent loss, not duplication**), needs a monotone producer sequence (CDC gives it free via `source.lsn`; a hand-written outbox needs a counter), and **a deliberate reprocess becomes a silent no-op** — reset to offset 0, everything is below the watermark, full-speed run, clean logs, zero rows changed.
- **Suppression is not safety.** v3's retention trap and v4's replay no-op are the same coin: whatever makes duplicates disappear also makes *intentional* re-application disappear. Hence the lab's operational rule — **a recomputation is not a replay**: derive the answer from the source of truth and *assign* it (assignments are naturally idempotent and re-runnable), then write the watermark forward so the live consumer resumes cleanly. Never "just replay the topic".
- **The v5 decision order every handler must answer**: (1) natural idempotency — is the effect an *assignment* (`SET status=…`, upsert-by-PK) rather than an accumulation? then almost none of this is needed; (2) watermark, when events for a key are ordered and carry a monotone sequence — the default for projections/read models/CDC sinks; (3) inbox table `(consumer, message_id)`, when events are independent facts, with retention ≥ max redelivery age; (4) claim-first + `RUNNING` + reaper, when the effect leaves the database (= `idempotency`'s v4, unchanged).
- Failure catalogue specific to living behind a broker: **the zombie** (handler exceeds `max.poll.interval.ms`, coordinator reassigns, two live writers — partition assignment is exclusive from the *coordinator's* view, never from the view of code already running; only a conditional write saves you, and the fenced pod's `CommitFailedException` arrives *after* the damage) · **retry-topic reordering** (`@RetryableTopic` buys availability by spending per-key ordering; addition commutes, assignment does not) · **F7's claim-before-effect trap** (inbox insert in its own transaction turns a loud poison message into a *silent skip* — for local effects the insert must roll back with the effect) · **non-transactional sinks** (make the sink hold the dedup: ES `_id = eventId`, or `version_type=external` with the sequence = a watermark implemented by the sink) · **dedup ≠ concurrency control** (two *different* events on one row is a lost update, not a duplicate) · **shared inbox across consumers** (key on `message_id` alone and the fastest consumer silently starves the other two) · **F10** (partition-and-DROP, never `DELETE`, or the retention job becomes the incident).
- Three orthogonal defences that stack and are constantly confused: **idempotency** handles duplicates · **versioning** handles order · **locking** handles concurrency. `UPDATE … SET status='SHIPPED'` is idempotent *and* not reorder-safe.
- Wrong models the lab kills: "we enabled the idempotent producer / EOS so consumers are covered" (EOS is exactly-once **inside Kafka**; correct *only* for Kafka-to-Kafka stream processors, covers nothing that leaves the cluster) · "dedup on the offset" · "an inbox gives exactly-once" (it gives at-most-once *application* given four assumptions, and lapses silently when one goes) · "check if seen, then process" · "7 days of retention is plenty" (plenty against machines, useless against humans) · "idempotent means replay is safe" · "our handler is an UPDATE so we're done" · "the consumer's dual write is the outbox bug again".
- **The dashboard, and the one metric nobody has**: consumer lag (everyone has it) · duplicate-suppression rate (a permanent **zero** is worse than a spike — the mechanism has never been exercised) · **event age at apply time** = `now() − event_timestamp` at commit, the *only* thing that catches an out-of-window replay before it doubles the data · inbox size + oldest-row age vs a **written-down** maximum replay age. Plus the one runbook line that prevents the worst incident here: *records older than the inbox retention must be regenerated from source, never replayed.*
- Vendor facts: SQS Standard duplicates arrive via **visibility-timeout expiry** (the zombie again, far more frequent than a Kafka rebalance); SQS FIFO `MessageDeduplicationId` is a **5-minute** window = producer-retry dedup and nothing else; RabbitMQ's `redelivered` flag is a hint that can be `false` on a genuine redelivery after failover — never branch on it; `enable.auto.commit=true` is not at-least-once (its meaning depends on code far from the config).
- Key terms now known: redelivery source, redelivery age, delivery identity vs message identity, inbox table (the outbox's mirror), dedup scope (`consumer` in the key), watermark/high-water-mark dedup, accumulative vs assignment effect, zombie consumer, event age at apply time, `@RetryableTopic` vs `DefaultErrorHandler` (availability vs order), claim-before-effect trap, recomputation vs replay
- Builds on: [idempotency] (**its protocol with the caller removed** — claim-under-UNIQUE and the check-then-act gap reused verbatim; its v4 claim-first is this lab's v5 tool #4), [kafka] (offsets, groups, rebalance, `max.poll.interval.ms`, retry topics, DLQ, idempotent producer, EOS — all assumed, none retaught), [transactional-outbox] (**the inbox is its mirror image**, and its relay is redelivery source #1; its "consumers must be idempotent" now has a consumer-side mechanism), [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] (the watermark predicate is the conditional write again), [change-data-capture] (`source.lsn` is a ready-made watermark; CDC sinks are the easiest idempotent consumers there are), [distributed-locking] (the zombie IS its expired lease — don't trust ownership, guard the write), [mvcc-and-vacuum] (why `DELETE`-based retention on a hot table is worse than partition-and-drop), [clock-synchronization] (why a timestamp must never be the watermark sequence) · Related: [saga], [cqrs], [event-sourcing] (all three assumed this mechanism), [whatsapp-system-design]/[uber-system-design]/[google-docs-system-design] (their "at-least-once + idempotent receiver" line, implemented) · Next: [backpressure?] (the consumer-side pressure this lab assumed away; now deferred by three labs), [observability-slos?] ("event age at apply time" needs somewhere to live), [schema-evolution?] (the inbox survives a redeploy — does the payload?), [stream-processing-semantics?] (windowing and joins, where dedup and ordering stop being separable)
- Next: [kafka-exactly-once] (**done 2026-09-01** — the one hop this lab declines: what EOS genuinely covers for a Kafka-to-Kafka stage, and exactly why the coverage stops at the cluster edge)

## load-balancing — learned 2026-08-31 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-09 — lab: 59-load-balancing/

- **No-code lab** (doc + 4-panel visualization + exercises/solutions). The most-requested open topic in the map — **nine** labs named it, and `load-shedding` (the day before) closed with it as the #1 next step. Simulator validated against the cases queueing theory knows exactly (M/M/1 ρ=0.8: 398.6 vs 400; M/D/1 ρ=0.8: 235.0 vs 240; **M/M/4 pooled ρ=0.8: 139.8 vs the 140 this map's `queueing-theory` entry already recorded**), and the engine embedded in `visualization.html` reproduces every figure in the README digit-for-digit — checked, not asserted.
- Takeaway: **A load balancer performs no work and adds no capacity; it only decides which queue you wait in.** All of its value is one negative — not letting a request wait behind a long queue while another is empty — and the entire budget it competes for is the gap between N separate queues and one shared queue. Measured on 8 backends × 4 workers (80 ms, 50/s each, 400/s fleet) at ρ=0.9: one shared line **91.5 ms mean / 384 ms p99**, eight separate lines with random placement **244.0 / 994.9**. Same hardware, same traffic, **2.7×**, purely from where the line forms. That is `queueing-theory`'s pooling result turned into a subject: "eight servers behind a load balancer" is not one line, it is eight, and the balancer's whole job is faking the shared one.
- **The algorithm only matters when you are already in trouble.** p99 by ρ, random ÷ ideal: **1.0× at 0.3 and 0.5**, 1.2× at 0.7, 1.5× at 0.8, 2.6× at 0.9, **4.3× at 0.95**. At half load all four rules are within 4% of each other and of the theoretical optimum. Which is both a reason not to agonise on a comfortable fleet and a reason to have the right one set before the day you need it — because that is the day you cannot safely change it.
- **Every dispatch rule is a bet, and its performance is exactly how right the bet is.** ρ=0.9 mean/p99/worst-queue: random 244.0/994.9/80 · round robin 156.8/605.7/42 · power-of-two 108.6/413.9/13 · least-connections 93.9/390.8/9 · ideal 91.5/384.1. **Round robin bets all requests cost the same**: at c²ₛ=0 it ties the ideal *exactly* (99.7 vs 99.7 — it does not approximate the shared queue, it IS the shared queue) and beats both load-aware rules; at c²ₛ=16 it is 3,863 vs random's 3,847, i.e. marginally worse than not thinking. Two independent sources of imbalance — randomness in *placement* and in *request size* — and round robin defeats the first completely and the second not at all.
- **The result worth carrying out of the lab: using less information, less decisively, produced a system that survived bad information.** Least-connections is 98% of the ideal *with a live view*, and that view does not exist. Staleness τ at ρ=0.9 (mean): 0→93.9, 50→196.2, **100→330.7 (worse than random's 244)**, 250→708.4 (**2.9× worse than random**), 1000→2335.7. Mechanism is a positive feedback loop: `argmin` sends *every* arrival to whatever was emptiest at the snapshot, so the dispatcher's own actions falsify its information — then the stream swings to the next victim. Average utilisation never moves, so **no dashboard shows it**. Power-of-two at the same τ: 108.6/118.8/130.4/159.9/295.5 — **6.1× better at τ=500**, because a fresh random pair per request means independent dispatchers cannot converge on a victim. **Crossover ≈ 30 ms**, less than one metrics scrape. Same story with dispatcher count (each seeing only its own outstanding): lc 93.9 → 292.4 at 32 LBs, po2 108.6 → 159.5; **po2 wins from 16 LBs up**. And d=2 captures **90%** of what d=8 captures (mean 244→108.6 of a 150 ms gap; d=3 buys 8 more ms) — random's worst queue *grows* with fleet size (58→86 from n=4→128) while po2's *shrinks* (18.5→6.8), the `log n/log log n` vs `log log n/log d` result made visible.
- **The two failures that point in opposite directions, and force the lab's design principle.** *Failure magnet* (backend errors in 1 ms instead of working, fair share 12.5%): rr **12.5%** · po2 **23.1%** · least-conn **66.1%** · least-response-time **100.0%**. Every load-aware signal measures how fast a backend *disposes* of requests, and failing is the fastest disposal there is — so the better the signal, the more completely the fleet commits to the corpse. *Gray failure* (backend 5× slower): rr/random **12.5%** and the queue grows without bound on a fleet at 80% (p99 172s/330s/652s at 5/10/20 simulated minutes — it doubles with the window, which is how you prove "unbounded" rather than assert it), po2 3.1%, lc 3.0%, leastresp 1.0%. **So: balancing and health must be two separate mechanisms.** One rule doing both gives you F1 or F2 depending on whether today's failure happens to be fast or slow. A dependency-free `/health` is the mechanism by which the magnet survives the thing meant to prevent it.
- Production failures with numbers, all invisible on an ordinary dashboard: **L4 balances connections, not requests** — 8 long-lived HTTP/2 connections hashed over 8 backends put **42.2%** (worst seed 62.5% = 200 req/s onto a 50 req/s box) on the busiest; you need ~1,024 connections before hash placement is safe, and 256 still leaves 57 req/s vs a 50 req/s capacity. This is every "why is one gRPC pod hot" story, and kube-proxy is v1-random at L4. **Sticky sessions convert balancing into sharding**: Zipf tenants over 8 backends gave 27.4% (64 tenants) / 24.1% (256) / 20.5% (4,096) — while 4,096 *equal* tenants give 12.6% and a healthy 554 ms. **The key count is nearly irrelevant; the skew is everything.** **`(N−1)/N` is the headroom ceiling**: 8 nodes at ρ=0.875 losing one → ρ=1.0, p99 402→2,082 ms and climbing; at 0.9 → 16.6 s; at 0.8 it is survivable (384→427). Three pods means **66%**, below most autoscaling targets — so many small deployments cannot survive their own rolling update. **A cold restart under round robin**: 5× slow for two minutes at its full 12.5% share → **p99 76 seconds**, vs 1.0 s (po2) and 0.58 s (lc); this is what slow start exists for.
- Ordering that matters more than the algorithm (the algorithm is worth 1.7×; these are worth two orders of magnitude): health checks that exercise a real dependency with asymmetric thresholds and `max_ejection_percent` → L7 or client-side balancing for HTTP/2 or gRPC → fleet headroom below `(N−1)/N` → slow start → *then* the rule. Diagnostic worth memorising: **shedding while the fleet has spare capacity is a balancing problem, not a capacity problem** — so graph shed rate *per backend*. The one metric nobody has: **max ÷ mean in-flight across backends** (1.0 perfect, 3.4 in the 8-connection case) — it degrades before latency does.
- Corrections to assumptions this map already contained: **Envoy's `LEAST_REQUEST` is power-of-two-choices, not a full scan** (configurable `choice_count`), and NGINX's `random two least_conn` / HAProxy's `random` are the same technique — so "least connections" in a product usually is not `argmin`. And **power-of-two is not a cheap approximation of least-connections**; it is *better* in most real deployments, and the randomness is the mechanism, not the compromise.
- Key terms now known: backend/upstream/host, dispatcher, dispatch rule, view, staleness τ, exact/stale/local view, round robin, least connections (least outstanding requests), power of two choices (d choices), random dispatch, shared queue/pooling, herd/stampede, failure magnet, gray failure, health check vs outlier ejection, slow start, connection draining, session affinity/sticky sessions, bounded-load consistent hashing (`hash-balance-factor`), L4 vs L7, ECMP/5-tuple hashing, subsetting, deterministic aperture, retry budget, hedging, `(N−1)/N`, max÷mean in-flight, `max_ejection_percent`, `pick_first` vs `round_robin`, Maglev, ring hash
- Builds on: [queueing-theory] (this lab is its M/M/k pooling line turned into a subject; the ρ=1 cliff is F4 reached by subtraction), [load-shedding] (its 4-worker/80 ms/50-per-second service is *one backend* here; balancing picks the queue, shedding decides whether you join one, and balancing cannot help above ρ=1), [consistent-hashing] (hot keys and bounded loads, now with a load balancer's numbers; its "a load balancer's tool, never a database's" line is explained here), [distributed-rate-limiting] (the staleness budget is the same dial and here it has a measured crossover, ≈30 ms), [microservices] (timeouts/breakers/bulkheads — this is the one that protects the *callee*) · Related: [caching] (F6's hot pod is a cold-cache story too), [io-multiplexing] (what one backend does while its queue builds), [gossip-protocols] (how the backend set propagates) · Next: [tail-latency-and-hedging?] (the direct sequel to F8 — Dean & Barroso), [backpressure?] (deferred by this lab AND load-shedding), [service-discovery?] (this lab assumed the backend set was known), [observability-slos?] (six labs now assume percentiles and error budgets; none teaches them), [metastable-failures?]

## clock-synchronization — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 30-clock-synchronization/

- **No-code lab** (doc + 4-panel visualization + exercises/solutions). The prerequisite learned *last*: [google-spanner], [hybrid-logical-clocks] and [distributed-transactions] all **spend ε** without ever explaining where it comes from, and `google-spanner/README.md:1543` names this lesson explicitly as the next one. Vendor figures checked against primary sources (RFC 5905, chrony.conf(5), AWS EC2 time docs, Meta engineering, BIPM), not from memory.
- Takeaway: **You cannot measure another machine's clock offset — you can only bound it.** From a round trip you get one equation (out + back = δ) and two unknowns, so the delay split is *unobservable*: three worlds with true offsets **85.5 / 100.5 / 70.5 ms** produce **byte-for-byte identical** T1–T4. Same argument shape as [flp-impossibility] — identical evidence, different worlds — except FLP removes the clock and this removes only the network's *symmetry*, and that alone is enough. Therefore the honest output is an interval, and **ε = δ/2** is where the letter you have been spending for three labs actually comes from. The interval is exactly **[T2 − δ, T2]**, and the point-estimate error is exactly **(out − back)/2** — verified over 11,737 parameter combinations in the lab's own core.
- **The section that changes how you read a host (§10):** NTP has computed ε correctly since 1985 and **every application throws it away**, because `clock_gettime()` has nowhere to put it. RFC 5905's **root distance = rootdelay/2 + rootdisp** is defined as *"the maximum error due to all causes of the local clock relative to the primary server"* — that IS ε. AWS's published ClockBound formula `SYSTEM TIME + .5*ROOT DELAY + ROOT DISPERSION + PHC` is **the same formula** with one hardware term added. Two hosts, same `chronyc tracking` format, both "in sync", both green: **86 µs vs 18.2 ms — a factor of 212**, and no application code can tell them apart. The uncertainty is not lost to physics; **it is lost to a type signature**.
- **The eight refinements, each forced by the last** (§9): four timestamps → keep the **minimum-delay** sample of 8 (delay noise is *one-sided*, so min beats mean: ±36.5 → ±19.5 ms on the lab's data; verified min gives the tightest bound in 400/400 random trials) → **intersect several sources** (Marzullo: rejects the falseticker *and* narrows ε — worked example [47,49] at ε=1 ms beats the best single source's 2.5 ms) → **slew not step** (buys *zero* accuracy, buys monotonicity) → **correct the frequency** (1.28 ms → 6.4 µs per 64 s poll, **200×**, for a few bytes of drift file) → **hardware timestamps at the NIC** → **PTP transparent clocks** → **GPS/PHC on the machine**. Only rows 7 and 8 attack the theorem — they supply the missing second equation (residence time; satellite geometry). AWS published: **<100 µs over NTP, <40 µs via PHC** — the 2012 Spanner moat, drained.
- **Derived rather than asserted:** Spanner's 1–7 ms sawtooth = 30 s poll × 200 µs/s + 1 ms comms; RFC 5905's **PHI = 15 ppm** makes poll interval an *accuracy* setting, not a bandwidth one (**0.24 / 0.96 / 15.36 ms** at 16 / 64 / 1024 s). Honesty note that lands: ε is built from the drift you're **prepared to be held to** (200 ppm), never the ~0.1 ppm residual actually achieved — **the gap between the band and the line is the price of the guarantee**.
- **chrony's real defaults, which surprise people:** slewing is the default and `makestep` is **not configured** at all; `maxslewrate` **83333.333 ppm** (one twelfth → a 4.32 s offset takes ≥ **51.84 s** to slew away, at up to 8.3% rate error); `corrtimeratio` 3; and **`maxdistance` = 3 seconds** — out of the box chronyd will follow a source admitting it might be **three seconds** wrong.
