Nothing in a stock config enforces a tight ε or warns when it degrades.
- **Three independent reinventions of the same idea**, which is how you know it's the problem's real shape: Google **TrueTime** `[earliest, latest]` (2012), AWS **ClockBound** confidence interval (2023), Meta **fbclock** *"Window of Uncertainty"* (2022–25). All three **removed the scalar from the API** so callers cannot ignore ε. The GPS hardware came second.
- **The code rule with the outage attached:** wall clock for *when*, monotonic for *how long*. Cloudflare, 1 Jan 2017 — Go's non-monotonic `time.Now()`, a leap second, a negative duration into `rand.Int63n()` which panics; ~0.2% of DNS queries affected; **Go 1.9 added monotonic readings to `time.Time`** as a result. Also: `CLOCK_MONOTONIC` is still *slewed* (`_RAW` isn't) and **stops during suspend** (`CLOCK_BOOTTIME` doesn't) — so a 4½-minute VM pause means every timeout survives it. `nanoTime()` across two processes is **undefined**, not merely imprecise.
- **Leap seconds:** `23:59:60` is legal and unrepresentable in POSIX time, so implementations repeat a second — a **planetary simultaneous backward step**. 2012 Linux hrtimer livelock (Reddit, Mozilla, LinkedIn, Qantas); smearing is §9.4 slewing applied on purpose (Google/AWS **24 h**, Meta **17 h**, chosen because hundreds of Stratum-2 servers must smear in step). **The trap: never mix smeared and unsmeared sources** — AWS says this explicitly, and AWS's own PHC does *not* smear, so PHC + smeared NTP is precisely the warned-against config; the intersection algorithm cannot break a 2-way tie. **27th CGPM (Nov 2022) resolved to end leap seconds by or before 2035** — which changes less than you'd hope: smear days make ε ~1000× wider with every health check green.
- **The rule to carry:** if correctness depends on comparing two clocks on two machines, you have a bug whose severity is ε — a number you probably haven't measured. **Physical time for timeouts and heuristics; logical mechanisms (fencing tokens, epochs, quorums) for safety.** You can only order events separated by **more than the sum of the two bounds** — a sub-ε difference is not a weak signal, it is noise. Shortening a lease reduces the window, not the possibility: a paused process doesn't know it was paused.
- Key terms now known: oscillator/crystal, ppm, offset vs drift vs skew vs jitter, accuracy vs precision, Cristian's algorithm, T1–T4, δ, θ, **delay asymmetry (the unobservable)**, clock filter (min-delay of 8), falseticker, Marzullo/intersection algorithm, step vs slew, `makestep`/`maxslewrate`/`maxdistance`/`corrtimeratio`, drift file, `adjtimex`/`ntp_adjtime`, hardware timestamping, PTP/IEEE 1588, transparent clock, boundary clock, PPS, PHC, stratum, root delay, root dispersion, **root distance (= ε)**, PHI, ClockBound, Window of Uncertainty, `CLOCK_REALTIME`/`MONOTONIC`/`MONOTONIC_RAW`/`BOOTTIME`, leap second, smearing, holdover
- Lab contents: `README.md` (18 sections, ~1730 lines, sources list at the end), `visualization.html` ("The Honest Clock", 4 panels — **1 · the measurement**: one slider moves delay between the legs while T1–T4 stay frozen and the truth sweeps [66, 105]; plus the 8-sample min-vs-mean-vs-median filter; **2 · the drift**: 10 min of a disciplined clock across none/step/slew/slew+freq with the ε band and a stopwatch that goes negative across a backward step, and a preset that reproduces Spanner's sawtooth; **3 · the ledger**: builds ε term by term across five deployments from public pool to PHC, with live `chronyc tracking` output and "what can be built on this ε"; **4 · what breaks**: 6 predict-then-run scenarios), `EXERCISES.md` (8 levels), `SOLUTIONS.md`. All arithmetic machine-checked: **51 numeric claims** plus the core's own **40+ assertions** (bound-contains-truth over 11,737 cases; min-filter tightest in 400/400 trials; AWS's <100 µs and <40 µs reproduced from the formula).
- Builds on: [flp-impossibility] (**the identical-evidence argument, reused on the network instead of the scheduler**), [networking-for-developers] (RTT, routing, queueing)
- Explains, retroactively: [google-spanner] (**this is its `clock-synchronisation?` suggestion cashed in** — TrueTime's ε derived rather than quoted; the GPS hardware reframed as a *latency* purchase), [hybrid-logical-clocks] (what *produces* the skew its inversion counts are plotted against; the `l − pt` gauge as a downstream-of-a-liar detector), [distributed-transactions] (why client clocks give 10 stale snapshots at skew 8 and 0 at skew ≤2 — the calm-network trap, now with a mechanism), [distributed-locking] (§12.6 is the concrete three-clock scenario that makes fencing tokens non-optional), [split-brain], [cockroachdb] (max-offset 500 ms, node suicide), [jepsen]
- Related: [causal-consistency] (the other family — the two answer different questions; §1 draws the boundary), [distributed-rate-limiting] (exercise 6.3 breaks its bucket key with 10 ms of skew), [tls]/[ssh] (clock as a security boundary, and §12.5's chicken-and-egg: you need TLS to fetch time securely, and a valid clock to validate TLS)
- Next: [observability-and-tracing?] (where the 4.3-second phantom latency actually lands), [gps-and-satellite-navigation?], [network-time-security?] (NTS, RFC 8915)

## load-shedding — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 58-load-shedding/

- **No-code lab** (doc + 5-tab visualization + exercises/solutions). The `Next` named by 5 labs, and the missing half of [queueing-theory]: that lab *proved* the cliff at ρ=1 and ended on "shed hard or don't bother" without ever showing a shedder. Production claims sourced to primary documents (Google SRE ch.21, Facebook "Fail at Scale" ACM Queue 2015, Envoy adaptive-concurrency docs, Netflix concurrency-limits) — **two of which corrected my draft** (see below).
- Takeaway: Under overload requests are lost either way — `capacity ÷ offered` is a hard ceiling and no design beats it — so **the only thing you control is whether loss is directed and instant or undirected and expensive**. Shedding does not cause the loss, it *converts* it. Headline pair, same hardware, same offered load: **0.1% served without admission control, 33.3% with a queue cap of 20** (~500×), and at only **1.2× capacity (60/s vs 50/s) the unprotected service serves 0.7%, not 83%**. The doorman is **free when idle** (100.0% at ρ=0.70 with cap 20) — so a shedder that is only switched on during incidents is a shedder that has never been tested.
- **The metric, not the mechanism, is the real lesson.** Throughput read **50.1/s during a total outage and 50.0/s during optimal operation**, and median latency of served requests was *better* during the outage (532ms vs 214ms) because **every latency metric is conditioned on survival**. Goodput (or success over *offered*, which needs rejections counted) is the only number that separated them. Add **queue delay** if you add one thing.
- **The v1→v5 chain, each break measured.** v1 count the queue: caps of 400/200/**100 all give 0.1% — identical to no cap** ("we have a bounded queue" is false unless correctly bounded); optimum is a **plateau from 30 down to 2**, so tune *small*, not *well*; usable window ~8–30 chosen from the healthy-load cost (cap 3 throws away 4.7% of a normal day). The deadline formula `c·(D/S−1)`=46 and Little's Law `μ·D`=50 are **ceilings, not settings** — they size the queue so the *average* request just makes it, so half don't: measured 18.7% each vs 33.3% optimal. **Halve or third them.** → **What breaks v1 is the central result:** a dependency taking 80→400ms drops capacity 50/s→10/s with *no traffic change, no alert, no config change*, and the optimal cap of 20 now yields **0.1% against a 16.7% ceiling**. Any threshold in units of *work* secretly encodes how long work takes. → v2 measure *time*: queue-delay target 13.1% and **adaptive LIFO+CoDel 15.4% (92% of achievable) — beating the static limit hand-tuned by someone who knew the answer in advance. 154× the static cap.** → v3 adaptive concurrency (AIMD found limit 17.7 at S=80, 3.2 within 30s of the step, 11.5 on recovery — nobody told it anything). → v4 criticality: at 3× overload checkout goes **33%→97%** while overall stays 33.0% (priority spends the same capacity, it does not create any). → v5 the other two levers.
- **The most surprising measurement in the lab: LIFO alone, with no admission control, no rejection and no config, took 0.1% → 33.4% (the theoretical ceiling)**, median 532→66ms, wasted work 100%→0% — because the head of a backed-up FIFO queue is by definition the request most likely to be already dead. **But the queue stayed at 60,047**: order fixes goodput, only a cap/drop rule fixes memory. Hence FB's pairing. Also: drop-on-dequeue **against the deadline is worth 3.8%, against `deadline − E[S]` 22.5%, with 250ms slack 32.1%** — the subtraction matters more than the drop. And LIFO **violates liveness** (bottom of stack never served), so it's an overload *mode*: cap-FIFO and LIFO give identical 33% goodput but the other 67% either get a 1ms "no" they can act on, or wait a full second for nothing. **Failing fast is a feature you ship to callers.**
- **Failures. F1 the reject is not free:** ceiling = `workers ÷ cost per reject` = 4÷2ms = **2,000/s**, measured collapse to 0.0 goodput at ~1,800–2,400/s offered with 90–100% of CPU spent refusing; 0.4ms moves it to ~10,000/s (5×, verified). A shedder is a service and can be overloaded. **F2 retries:** 150/s became **394 admission decisions/s** with goodput unchanged — and **backoff/jitter made *no* difference (394/393/395)**, because they fix *synchronisation*, not volume; only *not retrying the 503* worked (back to 150/s). The amplification is a **feedback fixed point** `A=150(1+q+q²), q=1−50/A` → 396 (sim 394), not the naive 317. **F3 metastable:** one 3,000-request burst into a healthy 35/s system — absorbed in 4 min without retries, **52,206-deep and growing 13 min later with them**; with a cap the queue never exceeds 1. Recovery after a 2-min spike: **+5s with any shedder, never without.** F5 wrong layer, F6 shedding `/healthz` (the mitigation cascades), F7 blind-to-identity = the 80% tenant keeps 80%, F8 the untested shedder.
- **Corrections I had to make to my own draft** (both found by checking, not by memory): (1) **"never shed on CPU" is wrong** — Google SRE ch.21 explicitly uses *CPU rate against reserved CPU* as its primary signal; it is right for CPU-bound RPC servers with controlled pools and wrong for the IO-blocked case (100% worker occupancy at ~0% CPU), which is most services. (2) **ρ=1.0 has no stable answer at all** — I had published "21.2%" as a figure; 20 seeds range **1.1%–70.6%** and it *falls with run length* (28.6% at 10min, 11.7% at 1hr) because the queue is a null-recurrent random walk. Fixed in doc + viz. "We run at exactly 100% utilisation" is a coin toss that gets worse with time.
- **Building the adaptive controllers was itself a finding.** Three implementations, two wrong in instructive ways: a *min*-RTT baseline pins at its floor with variable service times (limit stuck at 4); short-vs-long-window gradient **runs away under sustained overload** (limit → 346, goodput → 0) because the long window normalises the bad state; adding a multiplicative decrease on a real drop fixes it — which is exactly what Netflix's library does and not an embellishment. Envoy solves the baseline problem by **periodically pinning concurrency to `min_concurrency` to force an uncontended minRTT measurement**, and its docs are candid that this causes extra 503s. Honest conclusion the marketing doesn't volunteer: **ship queue-delay (v2b) before adaptive concurrency** — most of the benefit, a fraction of the risk.
- Verified primary-source facts worth keeping: FB CoDel **target 5ms / interval 100ms** (Wangle, HHVM) — and **copying those onto 80ms work costs 10% of healthy traffic**, so scale the target to your service time; Envoy `limit = gradient × limit + sqrt(limit)`, `gradient = (minRTT+B)/sampleRTT`, defaults p90 sample / 100ms window / 60s minRTT recalc / 10% jitter / 50 requests; Google criticality `CRITICAL_PLUS · CRITICAL · SHEDDABLE_PLUS · SHEDDABLE`, and **client-side adaptive throttling `max(0,(requests − K·accepts)/(requests+1))`, K=2** — the only fix that moves rejection cost *off* the overloaded server.
- Priority must ride on an **adaptive** limit: fractions of a *fixed* limit of 12 threw away 42% of low-priority traffic at a healthy ρ=0.70 (79.0% overall); on an adaptive limit the same fractions cost almost nothing (95.3%) with identical protection at 3× (checkout 96% vs 97%).
- Lab shape: `README.md` (16 stages, 4 depth layers, 6 prediction checkpoints, 8 failure scenarios, 8 wrong models, ~35-term glossary), `EXERCISES.md`/`SOLUTIONS.md` (8 rungs; rung 5 hand-traces the rejection ceiling and the retry fixed point, rung 6 has 7 planted flaws, 8.4 asks what perfect deadline propagation would make unnecessary — answer: not admission control, because deadlines solve wasted work and not the resource bound). `visualization.html` = **The Overload Desk**, <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/175a445a-1314-4caa-b2f8-71cd11134949> — 5 tabs (the collapse + live load curve · seven shedders side by side with a dependency-slowdown toggle · queue order/drop/CoDel with no admission control · 4 failure injections as matched pairs · priority across the load range). Engine validated against Little's Law, M/M/1, M/D/1, D/D/1, Erlang-C M/M/4 and Kingman before use (8-seed means within 0.7%); its JS was exercised headlessly against a DOM stub across every control combination, and 21 of 22 page figures cross-check against the doc (the 22nd was the ρ=1 instability above).
- Key terms now known: goodput vs throughput, offered load, admission control, load shedding vs rate limiting vs throttling vs backpressure vs autoscaling, shed rate, wasted work, signal/set point/actuator, queue depth, queue delay (sojourn time), standing queue, in-flight/concurrency limit, CoDel, adaptive LIFO, drop-on-dequeue, deadline slack, AIMD, gradient controller, minRTT, sampleRTT, criticality, client-side adaptive throttling, retry budget, metastable failure, deadline propagation (`grpc-timeout`), rejection ceiling, survivorship bias in latency metrics
- Builds on: [queueing-theory] (**this is its missing half** — ρ=1, Little's Law, `maxQueue ≈ deadline/S`, goodput vs throughput, "shed hard or don't bother"; and it corrects that lab's implied stability at ρ=1), [microservices] (timeout/retry/bulkhead/breaker — and the finding that **four of those five protect the caller; only shedding protects the callee**), [distributed-rate-limiting] (its one-line glossary conflation now separated; `Retry-After` as a synchroniser reappears as F2), [idempotency] (mandatory once a shed 503 or a timeout is retried), [caching] (the cache-fill request you must not shed), [work-pressure-and-deadlines] ("yes, and here is what comes out" is admission control in a person) · Related: [cap-theorem]/[pacelc] (fail-open vs fail-closed), [io-multiplexing] (the ready connections *are* the queue), [java-virtual-threads] (cheap threads relocate the queue and make admission control *more* necessary, not less) · Next: [load-balancing?] (**now asked for by 9 labs — the top gap**), [backpressure?], [tail-latency-and-hedging?], [observability-slos?], [metastable-failures?] (F3 as a subject; HotOS 2021 paper)

## kafka — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 53-kafka/

- **No-code lab** (doc + 4-tab visualization + exercises/solutions). The most-requested gap in the map — named as `Next` by 21 labs. Config defaults verified against the **Kafka 4.3** docs (May 2026), not from memory.
- Takeaway: Kafka is not a queue with better throughput; it is **a different answer to what consuming means**. A classic broker's core service is knowing, per message per consumer, what has been handled — and *that one commitment* produces every limit it has (one audience per message, no replay, redelivery-therefore-reordering, random-access bookkeeping as the bottleneck). Kafka refuses it: **append the fact once, keep it for a declared time, and make each reader remember its own position.** Requirements 2/5/6 (reading doesn't consume · each reader has its own position · per-reader state must be tiny) *force* the design: a reader's whole state must be **one integer**, so the records must be ordered and addressable by position — a log with offsets. Everything else in Kafka is engineering downstream of that.
- **The one sentence that decides every design argument: ordering exists inside a partition and nowhere else** — therefore per key, therefore only while the partition count is unchanged. When someone says "we need ordering", the only useful reply is *"ordering of what, relative to what?"*
- **The modulo Kafka walks into deliberately.** `partition = murmur2(key) % n` is exactly the design [consistent-hashing] spent a lab destroying — and the ring's fix does not apply, because a partition is not a bucket you can rehash: it is an ordered immutable log with consumers holding positions inside it, so moving record 41 of p2 into p3 would break every offset, bookmark and replica. So Kafka freezes the count instead: **existing records never move, and from the moment you add a partition a key can live in two of them, permanently, with no error and no repair.** Operational consequences: over-provision at creation; adding partitions to a keyed topic buys silent disorder; never on a compacted topic.
- **The durability trap, and it is three settings or none.** `acks=all` means *all currently **in-sync** replicas* — and the ISR is a **dynamic quorum** that shrinks under failure (unlike [quorums-and-replication]'s fixed majority). Traced: two followers slow → ISR={leader} → `acks=all` succeeds on one copy → leader's disk dies → the producer was told it was durable. `min.insync.replicas` (**default 1**, the one production-wrong default) is the floor under a quorum that would otherwise shrink to nothing. **Failures tolerated for writes = RF − min.insync.replicas**, so RF=3+min.insync=3 tolerates *zero*. The trio is RF=3 · acks=all · min.insync=2.
- **LEO / high watermark / ISR, and why the HW exists.** HW = lowest LEO in the ISR = the commit point; consumers may not read past it. Without it a consumer could act on a record that a failover then replaces with *different content at the same offset* — every consumer would need its own two-phase commit against replication state. The HW is the cheap alternative: **publish nothing until it cannot be unpublished** (one round trip of staleness).
- **The bug that created the leader epoch** (KIP-101, 0.11, 2017): followers used to truncate to their *own* HW, which propagates one fetch round late — traced to two logs permanently disagreeing about the content of one offset (divergence, worse than loss). Fix: every leadership change bumps a monotonic epoch, records are stamped with it, and a returning follower **asks** `OffsetsForLeaderEpoch` ("where did epoch 5 end?") instead of inferring from a stale local number. General rule now stated: *after a leadership change, how much of your state is still valid must come from the new authority, tagged with a generation number.* Seventh name for [split-brain]'s one object.
- **Delivery semantics, precisely.** Idempotent producer = `(PID, epoch, sequence)` per partition; the broker drops retried batches and rejects out-of-order ones, which buys **no duplicates AND ordering with 5 batches in flight** (so `max.in.flight=1` is obsolete advice that costs throughput for nothing). Default since 3.0, and it dedups *a network conversation, not your business process* — a producer restart gets a new PID. Consumer side is a forced choice with no third option: commit **before** processing = at-most-once (silent gaps), **after** = at-least-once (duplicates). The whole sentence: **at-least-once delivery + an idempotent consumer = effectively-once processing.** `enable.auto.commit=true` (the default) is a *third, sneakier* position whose behaviour depends on the shape of code far from the config — inline processing makes it coarse commit-after; handing batches to a pool makes it silent at-most-once.
- **Exactly-once is exactly-once *inside Kafka*.** Transactions (coordinator + `__transaction_state` + control records + LSO + `read_committed`) make read→process→write→commit-offset atomic across partitions. They cover no side effect that leaves the cluster; the payment API still needs [idempotency]'s key. Two costs: a hung transactional producer stalls every `read_committed` consumer until `transaction.timeout.ms` (60 s), and `isolation.level` is **still `read_uncommitted` by default in 4.3**, so enabling transactions on the producer changes nothing until the consumer is changed too.
- **Consumer groups: the unit of assignment is the partition, not the record.** One partition → exactly one member, which is why there is no redelivery (so no reordering), why per-key order survives parallel consumption, and why **partition count is a hard ceiling on group parallelism** (12 partitions, 20 consumers → 8 idle forever). Two independent liveness clocks that get confused constantly: `session.timeout.ms` (45 s — heartbeat thread; process dead/frozen/partitioned) vs `max.poll.interval.ms` (5 min — your thread; processing too slow). Rebalance generations: eager (stop-the-world, every membership change) → cooperative incremental (KIP-429, 2.4) → **broker-driven KIP-848, GA on the broker in 4.0 but the client default `group.protocol` is still `classic` in 4.3**. Static membership (`group.instance.id`) makes a restart inside the session timeout cost **zero** rebalances. Measured in the viz: 8 consumers, eager, rolling restart = 16 rebalances ≈ 384 partition-seconds stopped.
- **A Kafka rebalance moves no data** — it reassigns *who reads what*, milliseconds to seconds. The operation that moves bytes is **partition reassignment** (`kafka-reassign-partitions`/Cruise Control), which is [partitioning-and-rebalancing]'s rebalance, throttled and slow. Same word, two universes; confusing them is the top misconception carried in from that lab.
- **Retention is a clock, not a refcount — and it is your recovery-time budget.** Deletion is by whole **segments** (never per record; GDPR erasure is a design problem, not an API call). The silent killer: a consumer down longer than retention restarts, gets `OFFSET_OUT_OF_RANGE`, and `auto.offset.reset=latest` (**the default**) jumps it to the end — **lag falls vertically to zero and the dashboard turns green** while days of records were never processed. Alert on *lag ÷ retention*, and use `auto.offset.reset=none` on anything that bills.
- **Compaction ≠ deduplication.** It keeps *at least* the latest value per key, eventually, on **closed** segments past `min.cleanable.dirty.ratio` (0.5) — so consumers see duplicates and must be idempotent regardless. Deletion is a **tombstone** (null value) kept for `delete.retention.ms` (24 h) because *absence cannot be shipped*. A compacted topic is table-shaped ([cqrs]/[change-data-capture]'s log–table duality) and is how `__consumer_offsets`, Connect state and Streams stores work; materialising one has two standard bugs — serving before reaching the log end offset, and ignoring nulls (phantom keys forever).
- **The honest durability caveat**: Kafka acknowledges when replicas hold the record **in page cache**, not after `fsync` (`flush.messages` effectively never). Durability comes from *replication*, not syncing — a normal process crash is survived, a simultaneous power loss across replicas is not. Contrast [acid], where durable meant exactly fsync.
- **Why it is fast, with no magic:** sequential-only writes ([btree-and-lsm]'s result applied to a network service), the OS page cache instead of a JVM cache (so [jvm-garbage-collectors]: bytes never on the heap are bytes never scanned), `sendfile` zero-copy — **lost the moment TLS is enabled** — and batch-level compression. Kafka 4.0 changed `linger.ms` 0 → **5**.
- Three failures that present as **silence**: ISR shrunk to one (throughput fine, latency *improves*, only `UnderMinIsrPartitionCount` sees it) · unclean leader election (committed records replaced at the same offsets) · retention outrunning a consumer. The four metrics: `UnderMinIsrPartitionCount` · per-partition lag (its *shape* names the cause) · `UnderReplicatedPartitions`+`IsrShrinksPerSec` · `RequestHandlerAvgIdlePercent` (below ~30% you are on [queueing-theory]'s cliff).
- **ZooKeeper is gone** — KRaft (a Raft metadata quorum, i.e. [raft] running inside Kafka) production-ready in 3.3, ZooKeeper **removed entirely in 4.0** (March 2025). Mentally delete every ZooKeeper paragraph from any pre-2024 Kafka guide. Also new since the folklore: tiered storage (KIP-405, shipped 3.6), follower fetching by `client.rack` (KIP-392, a real cross-AZ money saving), and **share groups (KIP-932)** — per-record acking and multiple consumers per partition, early access 4.0 → preview 4.1 → production-ready 4.2, i.e. Kafka growing a second, queue-shaped consumption model.
- When Kafka is the wrong answer: per-message retry/priority/delay task queues (RabbitMQ/SQS shaped) · request-response · random access by key (that is a table; materialise the compacted topic into one) · payloads over ~1 MB · strict global ordering (= one partition = one consumer = one machine).
- Wrong models the lab kills: "Kafka is a queue" · "Kafka guarantees ordering" · "acks=all means all replicas" · "exactly-once means my HTTP call happens once" · "more partitions = more throughput" · "more consumers = faster" · "rebalancing moves data" · "compaction removes duplicates" · "writes to disk so it's slow / it's in-memory" · "raise the replication factor and we stop losing data" (RF protects against disk failure and nothing else on the list).
- Key terms now known: log (as a product, not a printf), record/topic/partition/offset, key-as-ordering-unit, sticky partitioning (KIP-480), producer/consumer as *libraries*, broker, segment + active segment, replica/leader/follower, follower-fetch (a follower is a consumer that writes what it reads), LEO, high watermark, committed record vs committed offset (one word, two meanings), ISR as a dynamic quorum, `replica.lag.time.max.ms`, acks 0/1/all, `min.insync.replicas`, NOT_ENOUGH_REPLICAS, truncation, leader epoch + OffsetsForLeaderEpoch (KIP-101), log divergence, unclean leader election, idempotent producer, PID/producer epoch/sequence number, `__consumer_offsets`, at-most-once/at-least-once/effectively-once, `auto.offset.reset` + OFFSET_OUT_OF_RANGE, consumer group, group coordinator, `session.timeout.ms` vs `max.poll.interval.ms`, eager vs cooperative-sticky vs KIP-848, static membership (KIP-345), consumer lag (and the shape of its graph), rebalance storm, poison record + DLQ-as-your-problem, retention as recovery budget, log compaction, tombstone + `delete.retention.ms`, log–table duality, transaction coordinator, `transactional.id`, ProducerFenced, control record, LSO, `read_committed`, EOS, KRaft, controller, `ActiveControllerCount`, tiered storage (KIP-405), follower fetching (KIP-392), quotas, share groups (KIP-932), purgatory, `UnderMinIsrPartitionCount`, `sendfile`/zero-copy, `linger.ms`
- Builds on: [btree-and-lsm] (sequential I/O and the append-only segment as a legitimate engine — Kafka is that result exposed *between* systems instead of hidden inside one), [partitioning-and-rebalancing] (partition as the unit of ownership; hot keys; and the two meanings of "rebalance" now separated), [consistent-hashing] (the modulo Kafka keeps on purpose, and exactly why the ring cannot help), [quorums-and-replication] (leader/follower and quorum arithmetic; the ISR is its dynamic cousin), [split-brain] (epoch/fencing token — seventh name; and slow-vs-dead as ISR flapping), [idempotency] (the retry that creates duplicates, and the only real answer to delivery semantics), [transactional-outbox] (the dual-write problem Kafka is usually the *destination* of), [two-phase-commit] (the transaction coordinator's shape), [queueing-theory] (lag, catch-up rate, and why a 90%-busy broker behaves nothing like a 60% one), [jvm-garbage-collectors] (why brokers keep small heaps, and the pause that ejects a healthy replica), [acid] (WAL, and the fsync distinction Kafka deliberately declines), [cap-theorem] (unclean election and min.insync as the choice, per topic, in two config lines), [raft] (KRaft) · Related: [event-sourcing], [cqrs], [change-data-capture] (Debezium's sink), [saga], [microservices], [zab] (what KRaft replaced), [jepsen] (unclean leader election is a finding you can now read) · Next: [kafka-streams?], [schema-evolution?], [load-shedding?] (quotas and backpressure), [observability?], [pulsar?]
- Next: [kafka-exactly-once] (**done 2026-09-01** — §10.11's transactions paragraph opened into a full lab: the nine-step protocol with real offsets, both fencing mechanisms, KIP-890, and hanging transactions), [kafka-streams?], [share-groups?]

## jepsen — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 44-jepsen/

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). The direct sequel [linearizability-vs-serializability] asked for by name (`Next: [jepsen?]`). Where every prior consistency lab taught what a guarantee *means*, this one is the machine that decides whether a running system actually delivers it.
- Takeaway: Jepsen is not a test suite and not a certification — it is a **falsification instrument**. It generates a concurrent workload against a real cluster under injected faults, records a **history** (every operation as an *interval*: invocation + completion, never a single point), and afterwards asks one question with a yes/no answer: **does any ordering exist that a correct implementation could have produced?** No expected values, no assertions. `valid? false` is a self-contained proof checkable by hand; `valid? true` means only *"we did not catch it this time."* The asymmetry is the whole design.
- **The idea that breaks every home-grown version, and the lab's central lesson: the operation that neither succeeded nor failed.** Jepsen has *three* outcomes — `:ok` (definitely happened, has a logical effect), `:fail` (definitely did not, has **no** effect), `:info` (**indeterminate**: may or may not have, **now or at any later time**, so its interval extends to the *end of the history*). The two ways to get this wrong fail in opposite directions and only one is loud: **over-claiming** (recording an indeterminate op as definite) → the checker is too strict → *false alarms*, the test cries wolf every run and gets deleted; **under-claiming** (recording a *definite* failure as `:info` because it was easier — the `catch (Exception e) → info` "safe default") → the checker loses a constraint → **missed bugs, silently**. The safe-looking mistake is the dangerous one. Corollary rule: **a process that receives an `:info` is retired forever**, because the op may still be in flight and would otherwise overlap itself.
- **Measured in the lab's own checker** (`k` mutually-overlapping writes + one unsatisfiable read, states explored): unpruned **65 / 326 / 1,957 / 13,700 / 109,601** at k=4…8 (tracking `e·k!`) vs pruned **53 / 166 / 487 / 1,352 / 3,593** → ratio **1.2× → 2.0× → 4.0× → 10.1× → 30.5×**, *doubling with every extra concurrent op*. Pruning changes the growth rate, not a constant — that is what made linearizability checking practical (WGL = Wing & Gong's backtracking + Lowe's "skip a state already seen"). Below k=4 pruning saves nothing. Consequences that shape every real test: **short runs, low concurrency, few keys** — and per-key splitting, which is legal *only* because linearizability is local (the composition theorem from [linearizability-vs-serializability], cashed in as an optimisation).
- **The counter-intuitive design rule, measured in the viz:** more concurrency makes a test **weaker**, not stronger. Ordering constraints come *only* from operations that do **not** overlap (A precedes B iff `A.comp < B.inv`). At 500 concurrent processes on one key nearly every pair overlaps, so nearly nothing is constrained and the checker can explain almost anything away. Verified: S2's stale read becomes perfectly legal the moment p3's read is moved to overlap p2's.
- **The other measured shock — erase invocation timestamps and almost everything becomes invisible.** Keeping only completion times: S2 (stale read), S4 (value changes twice), S5 (lost acknowledged write) *all* flip to valid; only a garbage read (a value nobody wrote) survives. **The invocation timestamp carries the entire real-time axis of linearizability.** Related architectural detail: all timestamps come from the single **control node**, which is why the `:clock` nemesis can scramble clocks on the database nodes without corrupting the transcript's real-time ordering.
- **Two checkers because there are two axes** (the grid from [linearizability-vs-serializability]): **Knossos** searches for an *order* over single-object operations (NP-complete, WGL); **Elle** (Kingsbury & Alvaro, VLDB 2020) checks *transactional isolation* by **cycle detection** over Adya dependency graphs — polynomial, because you never have to *find* a serial order to prove none exists. Elle's two moves: (1) **change the datatype so writes stop destroying evidence** — objects are lists, writes append a unique value, so one read of `[1,4,9,2]` recovers the *complete* write order for that key (the most reusable idea in the lab: don't ask "how do I get better logging", ask "can I choose a datatype whose reads reveal their own history?"); (2) infer `ww`/`wr`/`rw` edges and look for a cycle. The **`rw` anti-dependency** is the subtle one — an ordering forced by what a transaction did **not** see. Anomaly named by counting `rw` edges in the cycle: all-`ww` → **G0** · none, with `wr` → **G1c** · exactly one → **G-single** (read skew) · two or more → **G2-item** (write skew). Plus non-cycle findings caught directly: **G1a** aborted read, **G1b** intermediate read, duplicate writes, garbage reads, internal inconsistency.
- **The datatype toggle is the whole argument for Elle in one control** (viz tab 2): E2's G0 write cycle is *fully visible* with list-append and **completely invisible** with registers — both `ww` edges vanish because a register read returns only the latest value, and Elle reports a clean run against an identically broken database. But E4 (G-single) and E5 (G2-item) **survive** register mode, because their evidence is *the absence of a write* (reading the initial version), which a register preserves. Register inference is **weaker, not absent** — an honest distinction the lab makes explicitly.
- **Real published findings used as the reality check** (all jepsen.io): **PostgreSQL 12.3** — `SERIALIZABLE` allowed **G2-item during normal operation, no faults at all**; every instance involved a read-write conflict on a *freshly inserted row*; a decade-old conflict-detection flaw (wrong XID attributed to both tuple versions); patched. The lesson is not "Postgres is bad" but **nobody had checked, because until Elle there was no practical way to**. **MongoDB 4.2.6** — at read concern `snapshot` + write concern `majority`: read skew, G1c, duplicate writes, retrocausal reads, ~10% of transactions anomalous *without faults*; causes were transactions silently ignoring db/collection-level concerns (defaulting to `local`/`w:1`) plus a retry bug. Lesson: **a system can implement a guarantee correctly and then fail to apply it.** **NATS 2.12.1** (Dec 2025) — in 2.10.22 *process crashes alone* could destroy an entire JetStream stream. Lesson: the humblest fault, `:kill`, is still one of the most productive; durability claims are claims about `fsync` and are routinely wrong.
- **The nemesis catalogue is not chaos — each fault attacks a named mechanism**: `partition-halves` / `-random-halves` (quorum arithmetic) · `partition-majorities-ring` (**every node sees *a* majority but no two see the *same* one** — breaks "I see a majority therefore I'm the only leader") · `bridge` (non-transitive connectivity) · `:kill` (durability/fsync) · **`:pause`/`hammer-time` (SIGSTOP/SIGCONT — a long GC pause; cheapest fault, finds the most bugs, and is the [split-brain]/[distributed-locking] frozen-leader case)** · `:clock`/`clock-scrambler` (leases, TTLs, LWW) · `:packet` (delay/loss/corruption/duplication) · `:file-corruption` (`bitflip`, `truncate-file`) · `:membership`. **If you inject only one fault, inject `:pause`.** Faults compose via `nemesis.combined`; real bugs often need two at once.
- Anatomy of a test (six parts, six decisions): `db` (install from scratch — testing the product, not your deployment) · **`client` (the error-classification decision — where tests are won and lost)** · `nemesis` (which failures you believe you survive; it is *also* a client, so faults are timestamped into the same history, which is what lets a report say "stale reads began 200 ms after the partition") · `generator` (the concurrency/checkability trade-off) · `checker` (which claim you are testing; composable) · `os`/`remote` (SSH).
- **The ladder for actually using this**: (1) read the analyses for the two databases you run — an afternoon, best return; (2) **Maelstrom** — Jepsen-based workbench, write a node in *any* language speaking JSON over stdin/stdout, it simulates the network and runs the real checkers; the **Gossip Glomers** challenge set (Fly.io + Kingsbury) is the guided path, and the natural next *doing* project given [raft]/[crdts]/[causal-consistency]; (3) **Elle standalone against your own stack** — often the highest-value check, because the bug is usually *yours*: a PgBouncer transaction-mode pool can lose session-scoped `SET TRANSACTION ISOLATION LEVEL`, and a read-replica router can silently downgrade recency below what the DB offers; (4) a full Jepsen test. Complementary technique: **deterministic simulation testing** (FoundationDB, TigerBeetle, Antithesis) — perfect replay by seed but usually needs the code written for it; **Jepsen's superpower is that it needs no cooperation from the system under test**, which is why it can be pointed at someone else's database and produce a report they cannot dismiss.
- Wrong models the lab kills: "Jepsen certifies databases" (no suite, no badge — a *dated report on one version*; correct response to "Jepsen tested" is *which version, and what did it say?*) · "passing means correct" · "Jepsen tests network partitions" (pauses and crashes find more) · "a timeout is a bug" (a timeout under partition is *correct* — [cap-theorem]) · **"the checker compares against expected values"** (there is no expected value; it searches for *any* explanation) · "`:info` is the safe default when unsure" · "Elle is a linearizability checker for transactions" (different axis) · "weird interleaving is suspicious" (overlapping ops are unordered).
- Commonly confused: **Jepsen vs chaos engineering** (*is the data correct?* in a lab, judged by a checker — vs *is the service up?* in production, judged by metrics: opposite questions, similar appearance) · **vs fuzzing** (adverse timing/failure vs malformed input) · **vs property-based testing** (Jepsen *is* one, with a consistency model as the property and environment failure in the generator) · **vs TLA+** (checks the *design* exhaustively vs the *implementation* by sampling — a system can be TLA+-verified and still fail Jepsen, and several have) · **Knossos vs Elle**.
- Key terms now known: history, operation-as-interval, `:invoke`/`:ok`/`:fail`/`:info`, indeterminate operation, process retirement, over-/under-claiming, linearization point, counterexample, checker, Knossos, WGL algorithm (Wing & Gong + Lowe pruning), nemesis, generator, control node, `hammer-time`, `partition-majorities-ring`, `bridge`, Elle, list-append workload, `ww`/`wr`/`rw` edges, anti-dependency, G0/G1a/G1b/G1c/G-single/G2-item, duplicate write, garbage read, Maelstrom, Gossip Glomers, deterministic simulation testing, falsification
- Builds on: [linearizability] (**the model Knossos checks** — that lab's hand-built checker is the thing Knossos does properly; its 79M-state blowup is now explained as NP-completeness and answered by WGL pruning), [linearizability-vs-serializability] (**the direct parent** — its two axes are exactly why there are two checkers; its locality/composition asymmetry becomes per-key splitting being legal for Knossos and illegal for Elle; its F1–F5 confusions are what Elle finds in the wild), [isolation-levels] (Elle's anomaly names *are* that ladder, restated as graph shapes; "learn the anomalies, not the level names" is now mechanised), [split-brain] + [distributed-locking] (what `:pause` and the partition topologies are hunting; fencing stops *effects*, and Jepsen can only ever see effects), [cap-theorem] (why a timeout under partition is correct behaviour, not a bug), [idempotency] (the same indeterminacy, seen from the client's side), [acid] (what `:kill` tests), [redis] (its 500 vanished acked writes is scenario S5), [raft] (the `:membership` fault; reconfiguration is the hardest part), [flp-impossibility] (why liveness has no finite counterexample and is outside Jepsen's reach) · Related: [hybrid-logical-clocks], [google-spanner], [cockroachdb] (its documented not-strict gap is the kind of claim a report settles) · Next: [deterministic-simulation-testing?], [tla-plus?], [kafka?] (unclean leader election — a finding you can now read), [zookeeper?], [failure-detectors?], [mvcc-and-vacuum?] (the machinery under the PostgreSQL G2-item bug)

## mvcc-and-vacuum — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 09-mvcc-and-vacuum/

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). Cashes in the `[mvcc-and-vacuum?]` suggestion left by **three** earlier labs — [isolation-levels], [acid] and [btree-and-lsm] — and deliberately re-teaches none of them.
- Takeaway: MVCC is not a performance trick, it is the answer to *"a read-only report must not stop the business from taking payments"*. Under 2PL a 40-minute report holds the book and every writer queues; the fix is not to stop the writer but to **stop her destroying the past** — so `UPDATE` becomes insert-a-new-tuple + stamp the old one's `xmax`, and `DELETE` becomes a stamp and nothing else. **The row is never modified and never erased.** That buys readers-don't-block-writers and O(1) rollback, and the bill is that *someone must collect the garbage later* — and may only collect what **no live snapshot could still want**.
- **The one number that explains everything: the horizon** = `min(snapshot xmin)` over every snapshot alive **anywhere in the database**. A global minimum, held by a client the database does not control. A dead tuple whose `xmax` is not *before* the horizon must be kept however obviously dead it looks. ⇒ *"vacuum ran successfully and removed nothing"* is the normal, correct behaviour of a healthy vacuum with a pinned horizon, and **the first query on a bloat page is `pg_stat_activity`, never `last_autovacuum`.**
- **The four surprises the lab is built to kill** (all four are one 03:10 incident: 8.1M rows ≈ 2.4 GB of data in a 41 GB table): `DELETE` frees nothing and *costs* disk (WAL + dirtied pages); `VACUUM` never returns bytes to the OS, it makes slots reusable **inside** the file, so its healthy outcome is *growth stops*, not *size falls* (truncation needs empty pages **contiguous and at the end**); a 6-hour `idle in transaction` session **holding no locks** blocks all cleanup database-wide; and the real fix is a schema decision — **partition so the retention job is a `DROP`, not a `DELETE`**.
- **Mechanism, precisely:** tuple = one physical row version, carrying `xmin`/`xmax` (+`cmin/cmax`, `ctid`, infomask). Number comparison is **not** enough — transactions do not commit in start order — so visibility needs the **commit log** (`pg_xact`, 2 bits/XID) plus a **snapshot** `(xmin, xmax, xip[])`, which is *a set of transactions to believe, never a timestamp*. **The rule:** *visible iff created by a transaction that had committed before my snapshot, and not deleted by one that had.* No locks, no coordination — visibility is a pure local function, which is why it scales. `xmax` doubles as the **write-conflict detector** (first-committer-wins) and can also record a mere row *lock*.
- **Rollback is O(1) and free** — two bits, nothing walked back — the exact inverse of [acid]'s ARIES undo pass, because a no-overwrite heap has nothing to copy back. *The work did not vanish; it moved into a background process on a schedule you do not control.*
- **VACUUM's five phases, each with its own production symptom:** scan+prune (in-page, also done opportunistically by plain `SELECT`s — one of the two reasons read-only queries write, the other being **hint bits**) → **vacuum every index** (a full scan per index; the dominant cost, and why dropping an unused index makes every future vacuum permanently cheaper; the dead-TID list lives in `maintenance_work_mem`, whose overflow caused repeated index passes until PG 17's TidStore) → reclaim line pointers to UNUSED → update FSM + **visibility map** → **freeze**. Vacuum is deliberately throttled (`vacuum_cost_limit` 200 / `vacuum_cost_delay`, autovacuum's dropped 20 ms → **2 ms** in PG 12) — "autovacuum can't keep up" is usually a speed limit, not a stall.
- **HOT is the highest-leverage schema fact here.** Two conditions — *no indexed column changed* **and** *the new version fits on the same page* — buy an update that touches **no index at all** (old line pointer becomes a REDIRECT; the chain is prunable in-page with no index scan). ⇒ **an index on a frequently-updated column does not merely cost its own upkeep, it destroys HOT for every update of that table**, multiplying heap bloat, index bloat and vacuum cost — from a migration diff that looks completely innocent. The other lever is `fillfactor` (default 100 reserves nothing; 70–90 spends disk to buy HOT).
- **The visibility map's second job reaches into query plans:** an `Index Only Scan` is *a hope, not a promise* — read **`Heap Fetches:`** in `EXPLAIN (ANALYZE)`. ⇒ **whether vacuum ran recently is an input to query performance, not just to disk**, and a plain `VACUUM` after a bulk load speeds up reads while removing zero dead tuples (it is setting all-visible bits).
- **Freezing / wraparound:** XIDs are **32-bit** and compared **modulo 2³²**, so only the nearest ~2 billion count as the past; an unfrozen tuple that ages past that **starts looking like the future and silently vanishes — no error, no log line, not in a logical dump either.** Hence freezing (a flag bit since 9.4, preserving the original xmin), and hence *vacuum must eventually visit every page of every table, including read-only ones*. The four ages: `vacuum_freeze_min_age` 50M · `vacuum_freeze_table_age` 150M (aggressive scan) · **`autovacuum_freeze_max_age` 200M (forced anti-wraparound vacuum that does NOT yield to lock requests — the classic "migration hung for 6 hours" outage)** · `vacuum_failsafe_age` 1.6B (PG 14+, drops index cleanup and throttling). Endgame: warnings at ~40M remaining, refusal to assign XIDs at ~1M. Second wrapping counter almost nobody monitors: **multixacts** (`autovacuum_multixact_freeze_max_age` 400M), generated by concurrent row locks and, routinely, **foreign-key checks on a hot parent row**. *64-bit XIDs had not landed in core as of PG 18 — stated as a claim to verify, not a fact.*
- **Autovacuum's trigger is the tuning knob that matters:** `dead > threshold(50) + scale_factor(0.2) × reltuples` — i.e. *"wait until 20% of the table is dead"*, which on a 100M-row table means **20 million dead tuples before anything starts**. ⇒ per-table `autovacuum_vacuum_scale_factor = 0.01` on big hot tables is the single most common correct change. PG 13+ adds an **insert-only** trigger (`..._insert_threshold` 1000), without which append-only tables were never vacuumed, never got visibility-map bits, and sat until a wraparound emergency.
- **Ten failure shapes, all one event in different costumes** (the horizon stopped moving, or cleanup lost the race): `idle in transaction` · a real long query/`pg_dump` · **a long query on a replica with `hot_standby_feedback=on` (cause on another machine; the honest choice is primary bloat vs `canceling statement due to conflict with recovery`)** · an abandoned **replication slot** (pins WAL *and* the horizon) · an orphaned **prepared transaction** (survives restarts, visible only in `pg_prepared_xacts`) · autovacuum outrun · autovacuum starved by DDL · the anti-wraparound vacuum blocking a migration · mass `DELETE` with no space returned · wraparound emergency. Plus the invisible eleventh: an index added to a hot-updated column.
- **The comparison that makes it transferable — same problem, four places to put the garbage.** *PostgreSQL:* versions in the heap; rollback free, current reads pay for the dead in the way, cleanup = **VACUUM**, retention **unbounded**. *InnoDB:* in-place + **undo log** via `DB_TRX_ID`/`DB_ROLL_PTR`; table stays compact but *old* reads reconstruct by walking undo and get slower the longer you run; cleanup = **purge threads**; the bill is **`History list length`** (the noun [acid] left dangling, now cashed); **48-bit trx ids ⇒ no wraparound, no freezing, none of that family of failures**; secondary indexes carry a `PAGE_MAX_TRX_ID` — *InnoDB's visibility map at page granularity*. *Oracle:* undo segments + `UNDO_RETENTION`, and **`ORA-01555 snapshot too old` is a design statement, not an error**. *SQL Server RCSI:* version store in `tempdb` (so the symptom is "tempdb filled up"). *CockroachDB:* versions in the key encoding, **`gc.ttlseconds` 4h**, **protected timestamps** = an explicit, named, revocable handle on the horizon — arguably what PostgreSQL lacks.
- **The dichotomy underneath all of them, and the line to keep:** retention is bounded by the **oldest snapshot**, and there are only two answers — **bound the space and sacrifice the reader** (Oracle, CockroachDB) or **guarantee the reader and let the space grow** (PostgreSQL, InnoDB, SQL Server). There is no third. PostgreSQL's opt-in to the first camp, `old_snapshot_threshold`, was **removed in PG 17**, so on modern PostgreSQL *you cannot opt out of unbounded retention; your only lever is client discipline.* ⇒ **"Find the horizon. Find who holds it."** — the same shape governs undo growth, `tempdb`, MVCC GC queues, S3 lifecycle backlogs, Kafka retention and git pack size. **A long-running transaction is not a slow query; it is a resource leak with global scope, and the only kind an idle, read-only, unprivileged client can cause.**
- **VACUUM vs GC, the comparison worth keeping** ([jvm-garbage-collectors]): a collector defines death by **reachability from roots**; vacuum defines it by **visibility relative to the oldest snapshot** — a horizon, not a graph walk. Same pathology from the same cause (a long-lived reference keeps garbage alive), same background-process-competing-with-the-app cost. **The oldest snapshot is vacuum's GC root.** Also distinct from LSM compaction ([btree-and-lsm]): compaction *rewrites and merges files* and does reclaim to the filesystem; vacuum frees slots **in place** and does not.
- Key terms now known: tuple (= one physical version, PostgreSQL's usage, ≠ the SQL standard's), version chain, tuple `xmin`/`xmax` vs snapshot `xmin`/`xmax`/`xip[]`, XID, commit log/`pg_xact`/clog, `cmin`/`cmax`, infomask, hint bits, heap, page, **line pointer** (NORMAL/DEAD/UNUSED/REDIRECT), `ctid`, dead tuple, horizon/`OldestXmin`/`backend_xmin`, pruning, free space map, **visibility map** (all-visible / all-frozen), index-only scan + `Heap Fetches`, cost-based delay, `maintenance_work_mem`/TidStore, `VACUUM FULL`, `pg_repack`/`pg_squeeze`, truncation, **HOT** / heap-only tuple, `fillfactor`, bottom-up index deletion, autovacuum launcher/workers/triggers, anti-wraparound vacuum, freezing, transaction ID wraparound, `relfrozenxid`/`datfrozenxid`, `vacuum_failsafe_age`, multixact, `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout`, replication slot, prepared transaction/`pg_prepared_xacts`, `hot_standby_feedback`, `max_standby_streaming_delay`, undo log/rollback segment, `DB_TRX_ID`/`DB_ROLL_PTR`, read view, purge thread, History list length, ORA-01555, `old_snapshot_threshold` (removed), version store, protected timestamp, `pgstattuple`/`pg_visibility`/`pageinspect`/`pg_stat_progress_vacuum`
- Lab contents: `README.md` (27 sections, 4 depth layers, 4 prediction checkpoints, registry-book + engineering mental models with a **4-item "where the analogy breaks"** — including "a snapshot is not a timestamp" — a v1→break→refine derivation of both the visibility rule and the vacuum rule, 10 failure shapes, an operator's toolkit of 4 queries, 10 wrong models, 60-term glossary). `EXERCISES.md` 8 rungs / 26 exercises with counterfactuals; `SOLUTIONS.md` fully worked. `visualization.html` — **"Nothing Is Ever Erased"** (<https://claude.ai/code/artifact/c0f183fb-0b6c-446d-ba34-68491ddae988>): the heap drawn as pages of line pointers coloured by *classification* (live / too-new / frozen / dead-but-protected / dead-and-removable / DEAD / REDIRECT / unused), transactions as bars on an XID axis with **the horizon drawn as a red line whose position decides every colour on the page**, an index panel that makes HOT visible, a "what each transaction sees" strip, click-to-inspect on every slot (stamps, commit status, removable-or-not *with the reason*, and a visible-to list), vacuum stepped **phase by phase**, and 8 predict-then-run scenarios. Four dials, each live only in the scenario it teaches (the rest are dimmed, so no control is decorative): long-reader-holds-snapshot, index-on-updated-column, fillfactor, freezing.
- **Verified headlessly before shipping** (`./validate-visualization.sh`; no node/deno/bun on this machine, so JavaScriptCore via `osascript -l JavaScript` + a DOM shim): **85 engine assertions** (modular XID arithmetic incl. the exact wrap cliff, the visibility rule in isolation, every scenario's end state value-by-value, HOT under all 4 dial combinations, determinism, and non-mutation of earlier states) + **117 render/control assertions** (all 8 scenarios × 6 dial combinations stepped forward **and** backward, every control fired, every panel checked non-empty, dial enable/disable matched against each scenario's declared dials). **202 checks, all passing.** Bugs the tests caught: `$` as a function name collides with JavaScriptCore's ObjC-bridge global (renamed `byId`) — the same class of bug the cockroachdb lab hit; a HOT update could be lost if `placeHot` returned null; and the fillfactor dial was decorative until HOT was made to require *reserved* space rather than *any* space.
- Deliberate simplifications, stated on the page: 6 slots/page, 6-bit XIDs (so the wrap is watchable), vacuum phases as discrete steps, and fillfactor 100 forbidding HOT outright (real PostgreSQL will use incidental free space; the packed-table steady state is the same).
- NOT covered: TOAST, index internals beyond "every version needs an entry", logical decoding, `pg_repack`'s implementation, SSI (already owned), WAL/recovery (already owned), query planning beyond `Heap Fetches`.
- Builds on: [isolation-levels] (**the machinery under the snapshot half of its matrix**; explains mechanically why PG's `READ UNCOMMITTED` cannot produce a dirty read, and why snapshot isolation ≠ serializable), [acid] (**the striking inverse** — no-overwrite ⇒ no undo pass ⇒ free rollback; its InnoDB "undo tablespace also serves MVCC reads" note fully cashed), [btree-and-lsm] (pages, line pointers, the amplification scorecard, *compaction-is-logically-invisible* = vacuum's invariant), [jvm-garbage-collectors] (**the oldest snapshot is vacuum's GC root**), [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] (first-committer-wins is `xmax`, physically) · Related: [cockroachdb]/[google-spanner] (`gc.ttlseconds`, protected timestamps — the same horizon under other names), [change-data-capture] (replication slots as horizon holders), [pacelc] (the `hot_standby_feedback` choice is an "else"-branch bill), [two-phase-commit] (`pg_prepared_xacts`) · Next: [database-indexing?] (**highest value — the layer this lab kept as a black box**), [wal-replication-and-pitr?], [postgres-query-planning?], [toast-and-large-values?], [zheap-and-undo-in-postgres?]

## jvm-performance-tuning — learned 2026-08-30 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-09 — lab: 23-jvm-performance-tuning/

- Takeaway: Tuning is not flag-picking, it is **bottleneck-finding** — a request's elapsed time is a budget that splits exactly into seven buckets (waiting for a thread · running your code · code not yet compiled · waiting on the database · waiting for a lock · frozen for the collector · ready-to-run with no core free), and at any moment **one** of them dominates while every change to the other six buys precisely nothing. The two things you naturally measure both fail: a **total** tells you how bad and never why, and an **average** describes nobody — on the lab's own default run the *average* request was dominated by the database (25.0 ms) while the *slowest 5%* were dominated by CPU (93.7 ms), a different diagnosis from the same run. Flags redistribute work; only design removes it, by doing less, doing it at a better time, or not doing it at all.
- **The killer demonstration** (why "seems like it might help" is not a diagnosis): a 16-core box only 11% busy, bad p99. Quadruple the heap 512 MB → 4 GB and **p99 goes 1,327 ms → 1,327 ms**, identical to the millisecond, because the collector ran twice all run and cost 0.0 ms of the average request. The actual cost was **87.3 ms per request queued for one lock**; drop that section from 45% to 6% of the work and **p99 → 211 ms** with no flags and no hardware. Doubling the thread pool instead: 1,327 → 1,266 ms (nothing), and the lock bucket goes *up*.
- **More is not more, measured.** CPU-bound, 8 cores, full queue, cross-talk 3%: pool 8 = **300.0 req/s**, pool 128 = **211.3 req/s** — 29.6% LESS work on identical hardware, p99 1,623 → 8,798 ms, per-thread work inflated 1.75×. Damage starts early: 16 threads on 8 cores already costs 33% more p99 for zero throughput. Turn cross-talk off entirely and **7/8 of the damage remains** (222.3/s) — two independent mechanisms, coherence between *running* threads and scheduling churn from *runnable* ones. The mirror case (25 ms DB call, λ=280): 16 threads → 277.3/s, 64 → 277.7/s, the extra 48 bought nothing. Both cliffs are real; aim for the flat bit.
- **The ceiling arithmetic.** **Amdahl** `1/(s+(1−s)/N)`: s=5% → ceiling 20×, and you get 9.1× at 16, 15.4× at 64, 18.6× at 256 (the last 192 threads bought 3.2×). **USL** `N/(1+s(N−1)+κN(N−1))` adds coherence growing as N², so the curve **turns down**: same s with κ=0.1% peaks at **9.0× at 31 threads** and is back to **3.2× at 256** — worse than 16. Peak is computable in advance: `N* = sqrt((1−s)/κ)`. Amdahl also applies to *optimisation*: attack a part that is fraction p and your ceiling is `1/(1−p)`, so a method that is 2% of the request caps you at 1.02×. **Compute that number before the week starts.**
- **GC as a tail problem, not an average problem.** Serial collector, 1.2 GB retained, 1 MB garbage/req: the AVERAGE request's buckets say queue=118 ms, gc=22.6 ms; the SLOWEST 5% say **gc=309.5 ms**. Frequency = garbage ÷ room; cost ≈ what is still alive. Four fixes on that service (p99 573 ms baseline): 4× heap → 174 ms but longest freeze still 519 ms; G1 → 200 ms; **allocate 8× less → 174 ms AND freeze 519 → 8.1 ms** (the only fix that moved both dimensions); 4× threads → **752 ms, worse**; 4× cores → 562 ms, nothing. Order of attack: allocation rate → retention → heap size → collector. Setting a pause goal is not on the list (`MaxGCPauseMillis` low makes G1 shrink the young gen → more collections → usually worse p99).
- **Collector choice is a trade, never an upgrade.** Same service, 4 collectors: Serial p99 573 / freeze 519 ms / 0% tax · Parallel 207 / 153 / 0% · G1 200 / 67 / 20% · **ZGC 220 / 6.4 / 40%**. ZGC cut the freeze **81×** and its p99 came out *worse than G1's*, because a concurrent collector moves the work off the pause and onto the CPU bill, and the bill scales with allocation rate.
- **The four measurement lies, all reproduced in the lab's own page.** (1) **The average**: mean 47.5 ms vs p99 248 ms on one run, 5.2×. (2) **The stopwatch started too late** (coordinated omission's in-app form): same overloaded run, in-handler timer p99 **120 ms**, arrival-to-answer p99 **880 ms**, 7.3×, all of it the 730.9 ms spent queued before any handler touched it. (3) **Safepoint bias**: one request path, three profilers — on-CPU sampler says `computePricing()` 60.0%; safepoint-limited sampler says `applyLimits()` **65.2%** and drops computePricing to 4.8% (92% of samples displaced out of a counted loop with no safepoint poll); wall-clock says `loadCustomer()` **56.9%**. None is buggy; they answer different questions. (4) **A CPU profiler cannot see waiting at all** — 6 ms work + 180 ms DB call is **4.5% on-CPU**, so a 60%-of-CPU method is 2.7% of the request and halving it wins 1.4%.
- **Two failures worth recognising in seconds.** **Container CPU limit** (16 cores visible, quota 2): p99 188 → **5,214 ms** (28×, not the 8× the ratio suggests, because capacity fell below arrivals and the queue then grows without bound) — and the biggest bucket is **queue**, not CPU-starvation, so throttling shows up as a queueing problem while the host graph shows an idle machine; alert on throttling counters directly. **Time-to-safepoint**: ZGC with sub-ms pauses still froze for **181.4 ms** and p99 hit 2,189 ms because a pause costs the collector's work PLUS the arrival time of the slowest thread; invisible in `-Xlog:gc*`, needs `-Xlog:safepoint*` (a different log). With a 150 ms TTSP, ZGC's advantage over Serial collapses from **60.7× to 3.25×** — fix TTSP before changing collector.
- **Warmup, measured**: cold JVM at 80 req/s — p50 **99 ms at 1.5 s** (interpreted, 96 calls) → 54 ms at 6 s (C1, 454 calls) → **41 ms at 45 s** (C2, 3,424 calls). Compilation is triggered by *call count*, not elapsed time, so a low-traffic instance stays slow far longer. The penalty is diluted by waiting: **2.4× with a 25 ms DB call, 4.6× without one** — the more compute-bound the service, the more warmup matters.
- **Little's Law as pool sizing, verified**: λ=200/s × W=43 ms → L=8.6 threads busy. Pool 8 (below the floor) pins throughput at **174.7/s** — a too-small pool does not make you slow, it makes you a *smaller service*; pool 9 reaches 196.7/s but p99 226 ms; pool 12/16/24 all identical at p99 173 ms. `L=λW` is the **floor**; the setting is `λS ÷ ρ_target` (≈13 here). Prefer a **concurrency limit over a rate limit** — it re-derives itself when service time changes.
- Key terms now known: bottleneck, time budget / decomposition, throughput vs latency vs footprint (the triangle), percentile, tail latency, on-CPU vs off-CPU, monitoring vs profiling vs tracing vs benchmarking, sampling profiler, flame graph (look for *wide*, not tall), **safepoint bias**, time-to-safepoint, interpreter, JIT, tiered compilation (C1/C2), warmup, deoptimisation, allocation rate vs live set/retention, serial fraction s, Amdahl's law, Universal Scalability Law, coherence κ, retrograde scaling, coordinated omission, microbenchmark, dead-code elimination, JMH, goodput, CPU quota / cgroup limit, CPU throttling, NMT, RSS, JFR, `MaxRAMPercentage`, `AlwaysPreTouch`, CDS / AOT cache, pinning, backpressure, admission control.
- **The nine flags that earn their place** (everything else is noise): `-XX:MaxRAMPercentage` (never `-Xmx` from the container limit — exit 137 with no stack trace), `-Xlog:gc*` always on, `-Xlog:safepoint*` when investigating, `-XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError`, JFR always-on, `-XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError`, `-XX:ActiveProcessorCount` only when the cgroup cannot be fixed, `-XX:+AlwaysPreTouch` for latency-critical, and one collector choice made with evidence. **Dashboard**: percentiles at the edge (never the mean as the latency signal), throughput AND goodput, **`time_in_queue`** (the single highest-value metric to add), spare capacity and queue-depth *derivative* rather than utilisation.
- Lab shape: **no runnable code** (2026-08-29 rule) — `README.md` (16 stages, four depth layers, 5 prediction checkpoints, sandwich-shop vs seven-bucket mental models with a four-point "where the analogy breaks", the method built and broken four times, 13 failure modes, mechanically checked so no term precedes its definition), `EXERCISES.md` (8 rungs) + `SOLUTIONS.md` (every numeric answer verified against the engine), and `visualization.html` = **The Bottleneck Machine**, the only executable artifact: a deterministic discrete-time JVM-service simulator with 7-bucket wall-clock accounting that sums exactly to elapsed time, average-vs-slowest-5% toggle, step-backward by exact replay, 8 predict-then-run scenarios, plus an Amdahl/USL tab and a "how measurement lies" tab with a working safepoint-bias demo. Published: <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/a12d63c9-7eaf-4596-9171-a92a41b28044>. Validated headlessly on JavaScriptCore (no node on this machine — `osascript -l JavaScript`): **25 engine assertions + 115 stub-DOM render checks, all passing**; every README number came from that same engine.
- **Deliberately NOT modelled** (each has caused real incidents): class loading and metaspace, on-stack replacement, deoptimisation storms, NUMA, page faults/swap/THP, cache hierarchy and false sharing, connection pools and cascading failure, retry storms, TLS/serialization/logging I/O.
- Builds on: [jvm-garbage-collectors] (**named this as its Next**; safepoint, pause, allocation rate, live set, ergonomics reused as known vocabulary, and its "the work is conserved" line generalised to the whole subject), [queueing-theory] (**named `universal-scalability-law?` as its Next — this is it**; Little's Law for pool sizing, the `1/(1−ρ)` cliff, goodput, coordinated omission, `maxQueue ≈ deadline/S − c`, the retry-storm numbers), [java-multithreading] (the lock becomes Amdahl's `s`; convoy, pool arithmetic, safety-vs-liveness), [java-virtual-threads] (what they change here: the pool-sizing arithmetic and the *waiting for a thread* bucket as a capacity problem — and what they do NOT change: CPU, locks, or the need for explicit backpressure), [io-multiplexing] (waiting is the real job), [caching] / [work-pressure-and-deadlines] (the fastest work is work you did not do), [idempotency] (required before hedging or retrying) · Related: [java-memory-model] (**the third "barrier/safepoint" word collision** — GC write barrier vs memory barrier vs safepoint-as-profiler-sampling-point) · Next: [load-shedding-and-admission-control?], [jit-and-escape-analysis?], [off-heap-memory?], [tail-at-scale?], [observability-and-tracing?]

## jvm-garbage-collectors — learned 2026-08-30 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-09 — lab: 22-jvm-garbage-collectors/

- Takeaway: A collector never finds the dead — it walks outward from the roots (thread stacks, static fields), marks everything it reaches, and declares everything unreached dead **by definition**. That inversion is why cycles cost nothing and why the cost of a collection is proportional to the **live** data, not the garbage. Every collector ever shipped is a different answer to one remaining question: *how do I do that walk without stopping the program for too long?*
- **The chain that makes it click** (each design is the scar of the previous one's failure): mark–sweep → fragments, so mark–compact → still walks the whole heap every time, so **generational** (most objects die young; a young collection copies survivors out and frees Eden wholesale, so the dead are never touched) → but a young-only walk misses old→young pointers, so the **write barrier + card table** → the old generation still fills, so **concurrent marking** → but the mutator moves references mid-walk and a live object gets lost, so **SATB** (mark the graph as it was when marking began; the price is floating garbage) → evacuation is still stop-the-world and unbounded, so **G1 regions + a pause budget** (evacuate the most garbage-rich regions; compaction is a side effect of copying) → copying is still a pause, so **ZGC/Shenandoah: concurrent relocation** with forwarding pointers and a self-healing **load barrier** on every reference read. Each step buys latency with throughput or footprint. The work is conserved; you are choosing *when* and *in what size pieces* to pay.
- Key terms now known: mutator, GC roots, reachable (a conservative over-approximation of "in use" — the gap is where leaks live), safepoint, time-to-safepoint, stop-the-world, mark/sweep/compact/evacuate, fragmentation (largest contiguous free block, not total free), weak generational hypothesis, Eden/survivor/old, age, promotion, minor vs full GC, TLAB, write barrier, card table, remembered set, tri-colour marking, the tri-colour invariant (marking may not end with a black object pointing at a white one), lost object problem, SATB, incremental update, floating garbage, concurrent mode failure, allocation stall, region, collection set, garbage-first, mixed collection, humongous object, forwarding pointer, colored pointer, load barrier, self-healing, throughput/pause/footprint, ergonomics, Metaspace, escape analysis, dominator tree.
- **The confusion this lab exists to kill:** a **GC write barrier** is bookkeeping code the JIT injects at every reference store so the collector learns about a pointer. A **memory barrier** from [java-memory-model] is a CPU instruction constraining reordering. Same word, no shared mechanism.
- **[MODEL] measured in the lab's own simulator** (`visualization.html`, 20 headless assertions passing): across 72 stress runs over 6 collectors × 3 heap sizes × 4 workloads, **no reachable object was ever lost with the barriers on**; with the write barrier switched off, 43 live objects were freed in a single 160-tick run. Scenario 1 ends in OutOfMemoryError with 38% of the heap free and a largest gap of 2 cells. Pause numbers on the page are model milliseconds from a stated formula — the ratios teach, the absolutes mean nothing.
- **[JVM] production shape:** G1 is the default since JDK 9 and the right answer until measured otherwise; Parallel for batch; Serial for small containers; ZGC (generational by default since JDK 23, non-generational removed in JDK 24) for large heaps with strict tail latency, at ~5–15% throughput and more footprint; CMS removed in JDK 14. Set `-XX:MaxRAMPercentage`, never `-Xmx` from the container limit (exit 137 with no stack trace is the OOM-killer signature). Turn on `-Xlog:gc*` everywhere before you need it. Lowering `MaxGCPauseMillis` usually makes latency worse; more heap is the first fix.
- Builds on: [java-memory-model] (barriers — the confusing pair), [java-multithreading] (mutator threads racing the collector), [caching] (the generational hypothesis is a locality claim; a churning cache is the GC's worst customer), [btree-and-lsm] (**evacuation is LSM compaction**; GC copy cost is write amplification; GC headroom is space amplification) · Related: [java-virtual-threads] (a virtual thread's stack is a heap object, so a million of them is a million live objects) · Next: [jvm-performance-tuning] (**done 2026-08-30**), [jit-and-escape-analysis?], [off-heap-memory?], [tail-latency-and-hedging?]


## terraform — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 63-terraform/

- Takeaway: **Terraform is not a tool for creating infrastructure — it is a tool for maintaining a *mapping* between names you chose and objects a cloud created.** Nothing in the cloud knows that mapping (names are not unique, immutable or queryable, and the cloud never saw your file), so Terraform writes it down, and every difficult thing about the tool is a consequence of that written record being shared, going stale, and being the only thing in the world that knows what you own. That forces the central mechanism: a **three-way** comparison of **desired** (config) vs **recorded** (state file) vs **actual** (refreshed from the provider) — two-way is impossible because config-vs-cloud can neither tell you what to *delete* (nothing in the cloud says "this used to be in your config", so "delete what isn't in my config" would destroy other teams' infrastructure) nor tell you *which* object is yours.
- **The centrepiece, and the best cross-lab connection in the curriculum so far: `count` IS `hash(key) % n`.** With `count`, a resource's address is its *position in a list*, so identity is a function of a global property; remove one element from a five-element list and the addresses renumber, so **4 are destroyed and 3 recreated** where 1 deletion was asked for (verified in the lab's simulator: 3 add / 4 destroy). `for_each` keys by a stable string: **0 add / 1 destroy**. Merely *reordering* two entries under `count` — same five values, no additions or removals — is 2 destroyed and 2 created (also verified). That is [consistent-hashing]'s opening bug (modulo moved 99.0% of keys where 1.0% had to) and `for_each` buys the same **monotonicity** guarantee the ring did. Rule: `count` only when instances are genuinely interchangeable and the count is the only thing that changes; `for_each` whenever they have identities — which is nearly always. Fix a wrong choice with a **`moved` block** (a *record edit*, reviewable, auto-applied by everyone) not `terraform state mv`.
- **The capstone rule that explains Terraform's most confusing errors:** the *set of addresses* must be fully known at plan time, because addresses are the keys of the record. Hence `for_each value depends on resource attributes that cannot be determined until apply` is a **safety property**, not a limitation — if addresses were only discoverable during an apply, a partially-failed apply would leave Terraform unable to say what it owns, collapsing the forward-recovery model. Its sibling is `(known after apply)`: a plan is a *prediction*, so a plan full of new resources is systematically less informative than one that only changes existing ones, and `Plan: 12 to add` tells a reviewer far less than `Plan: 0 to add, 1 to change`.
- **The one guarantee, with all four qualifications doing work:** *if the record is accurate and the apply completes without error, reality matches your configuration for every resource Terraform manages.* Not guaranteed: atomicity (no rollback — a failed apply leaves a partial world, and the record is written incrementally so re-running is safe); that the plan is what happens (F7 — a plan is computed from a snapshot); ordering beyond the DAG; idempotence of provider operations (that is the provider's job); protection from your own `destroy`; secrecy of state contents (**every attribute including generated passwords is stored in plain text; `sensitive` only hides console output**); and that two runs of the same config produce identical infrastructure (**IaC gives reproducible *descriptions*, not reproducible *infrastructure***).
- **The deep trade, easy to miss:** before Terraform the cloud was the only place truth lived and you could not review it; after Terraform there are *three* places — config, record, cloud — and keeping them in agreement is a permanent job. Every one of the lab's ten failures is a disagreement among those three. You exchanged "I cannot see the truth" for "I must maintain it in three places", which is a good trade made with open eyes.
- Failures (10, ordered by how often you meet them): F1 **drift silently reverting a 2am emergency fix** (the config is the definition of correct; the revert arrives mixed into someone else's unrelated change), F2 concurrent apply = the **lost update** from [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] — nothing errors, and the dangerous version is when the record loses an object's *existence*, because a refresh cannot rediscover an id it never had, F3 lost state file (`Plan: 47 to add` **alongside** running production — the worst available action is applying it; infrastructure and config are both intact, only the mapping is gone; prevention is bucket versioning, ~10 minutes), F4 partial apply, F5 unmanaged resources → `import` blocks, F6 **`-/+ forces replacement` on a production database** from a one-line change, caused by a *provider* decision not yours, F7 stale plan (use `plan -out` + apply the artefact, never re-plan at apply time), F8 provider/cloud eventual consistency ("run it again" that actually works), F9 secrets in state, F10 one giant root module (correct, and it destroys throughput, plan speed and reviewability — an 8-minute 900-resource plan does not get read, which disables the human control F1 and F6 depend on).
- Two naming collisions worth remembering: **state lock** (mutual exclusion during apply; DynamoDB table historically, S3-native lockfile in recent versions) vs **`.terraform.lock.hcl`** (pinned provider versions, like package-lock.json) — same word, unrelated. And **root module = the unit of state, lock, plan AND blast radius**; nothing else in Terraform is. Split by rate of change: `network/` (monthly) · `data/` (rarely, `prevent_destroy` everywhere) · `services/` (hourly). Pass values between layers as explicit pipeline inputs, never `terraform_remote_state` reaching into another layer's file.
- **The AWS lab's §13.2 claim, now defended AND qualified:** IaC converts disaster recovery from an unbounded task into a bounded one — but the configuration creates an *empty* database (data is the long pole), the DR region needs its own state structured before the disaster, quotas are per-account-per-region ([amazon-web-services] F4) so an unapplied DR config is a hypothesis, and a config unapplied for a year may not apply at all. Same conclusion as the AWS lab reached about RTO by another road: an untested recovery is a wish.
- Key terms now known: infrastructure as code, resource, **resource address** (the primary key of everything), HCL, provider, desired/recorded/actual state, state file, plan, apply, `+ ~ - -/+`, forces replacement, dependency graph (DAG from references — ordering is derived from data flow, never declared), `depends_on`, backend, state lock, dependency lock file, serial/lineage, drift, refresh, `-refresh-only`, `-refresh=false`, module, root module, `count` vs `for_each`, `moved` block, `import` block, `lifecycle`, `prevent_destroy`, `create_before_destroy`, `ignore_changes`, `(known after apply)`, `-replace=`, `force-unlock`, workspace, data source, OpenTofu
- Lab shape: **no-code** — README (~1,900 lines, 16 stages, 7 prediction checkpoints), `visualization.html` (a reconciler console: four aligned columns Address | Config | State file | Cloud so the three-way comparison reads across a row, real `+ ~ - -/+` plan output, 8 predict-then-run scenarios, step-through apply with injectable failure, undo, click-to-inspect), EXERCISES + SOLUTIONS across all eight rungs. Validated under JavaScriptCore with a DOM-stub harness (no node on this machine): every scenario, every fault toggle and the count/for_each arithmetic asserted against the doc's numbers. The harness caught a regression where a cleanup to the re-keying helper silently broke the `for_each` scenario into 4-add/5-destroy — the exact bug the scenario exists to disprove.
