- Builds on: [amazon-web-services] (the things being managed, and its §13.2 promise), [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] (the state serial IS `WHERE version = ?`; the lost update is F2), [distributed-locking] (the state lock, and why `force-unlock` is dangerous — slow and dead are indistinguishable), [consistent-hashing] (`count` vs `for_each`, the centrepiece), [caching] (the record is a stale replica; refresh is revalidation), [saga] (forward recovery with the compensations deliberately removed — Terraform can do that because infrastructure ops are re-runnable to convergence and "charge the customer" is not), [eventual-consistency] (F8), [idempotency] (why "create" is not a safe thing to say twice) · Related: [two-phase-commit] (what splitting state too finely turns a single change into) · Next: [kubernetes?] (its controllers are this reconciler running continuously), [gitops?] (that continuous reconciler pointed at git — makes F1 self-healing), [ci-cd-pipelines?], [secrets-management?] (F9 is a hole this lab could only point at), [observability?], [kafka?]

## amazon-web-services — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 62-amazon-web-services/

- Takeaway: AWS is a shop where you rent other people's distributed systems, and it is three inventions stacked: **the pool + rental API** (capacity becomes a runtime decision instead of a purchase), **the failure map** (the physical boundaries that fail together are published and contractual, so "two copies" can be made to mean two chances), and **the badge** (every single API call is signed and authorized, deny by default). Engineers reliably learn the first, ignore the second, and are taught the third by an incident. The reframing that made the lab worth doing at this point in the curriculum: **nothing under the hood was new** — S3 is quorum replication, DynamoDB is hash partitioning with per-partition ceilings, Aurora is `W=4 R=3 N=6` across three AZs, Global Tables are LWW multi-master, SQS Standard is at-least-once. What was new was the *shop*: where the shelves physically are, what the receipt actually promises, and which parts of the job stay yours after you have paid.
- **The single highest-value idea, and the one to carry into any cloud: static stability.** The control plane (create/modify/delete) and the data plane (use what exists) fail **separately**, and the control plane fails more often and region-wide, while data planes are built to keep serving from last-known configuration. So the diagnostic question for any design is: *does recovery require a successful control-plane call?* A load balancer removing an unhealthy target, traffic shifting to already-running instances, and an RDS failover to an **already-existing** standby all pass. `RunInstances`, creating an RDS instance from a snapshot, editing a Route 53 record by hand, and scaling a DynamoDB table all fail — precisely when you need them, because you are competing with every other customer whose design also depended on launching. Worked in the lab with numbers: 12 instances at 4/AZ serving 1,000 rps, lose one AZ with the control plane frozen → stuck at 800 rps, ~20% of payments failing, **and the graph is flat** (a flat degradation graph is the signature of F3; a normal incident's graph moves). Same fault, 18 instances at 6/AZ → 0 shed, `RunInstances` still fails and it does not matter. Cost of the difference: **50% overprovisioning at N=3** (the rule is any N−1 zones must carry 100% of peak, so 33% at N=4 and 100% at N=2 — which is the real reason three zones is standard and two is a trap).
- Two more invariants worth memorising alongside it: **redundancy is a count of independent failure domains, never a count of machines** (two instances in one AZ are one instance with extra steps — the mistake AWS *would* have told you about, since the zone was a field on the launch screen); and **replication protects against infrastructure, only backups protect against you** (Multi-AZ replicates your `DROP TABLE` to the standby in milliseconds and Aurora's six copies all agree — every reliability mechanism working perfectly, against you).
- Storage boundary rule, the most useful one-liner in the whole platform: **instance store dies with the host; EBS dies with the AZ; S3/DynamoDB die with the region.** Three levels of "gone", picked per piece of data. Corollary that catches everyone: a **subnet is AZ-scoped**, so "three subnets" is only three chances if they are in three zones.
- Anatomy of one API call (§10.1, hand-traced): credentials → endpoint resolution → SigV4 signing → transport → **IAM authorization** → **quota/throttle** → the actual distributed system. **Five of the seven steps have nothing to do with the service**, which is the shape of nearly every AWS incident an application team meets: expired credentials, a clock 12 minutes fast (`SignatureDoesNotMatch`, presents as a per-*host* failure across every AWS service at once), a missing action (`AccessDeniedException` — 0 ms of database work done, 100% failure, loud and instantly findable, which is the *good* kind of IAM failure), or throttling (retried by the SDK with backoff, so it shows up as **p99 latency, not errors**).
- Things that are true and rarely taught: AZ *names* (`ap-south-1a`) are scrambled per account — the stable cross-account identifier is the **AZ ID** (`aps1-az1`); quotas are **per account per region**, so an increase in your primary region does nothing for the DR region you would fail over to, and a load test in a shared staging account can consume production's headroom; a handful of things are global with control planes concentrated in **`us-east-1`** (IAM, Route 53 control plane, CloudFront, Organizations), which is the honest structural reason those incidents make the news; **an SLA is a refund, not a guarantee**, and availability multiplies downward (four independent 99.95% components = 99.80%, ~17 hours/year); and the meter has no idle discount plus a *direction* — in is free, out costs, and **cross-AZ traffic inside your own region is billed both ways**, so the architecture that buys you zone independence has a line item.
- Failure catalogue (11, in the order a working engineer meets them): F1 instance death (detection window ≈60s; in-flight requests have unknown outcome → idempotency keys are mandatory), F2 AZ loss (+ connection pools holding dead sockets), **F3 control plane frozen**, F4 quota reached (nothing turns red), F5 hot partition (DynamoDB per-partition ceilings ≈3,000 RCU / 1,000 WCU; fix the key, not the capacity), F6 region event + the `us-east-1` twist, F7 IAM too-narrow (loud, good) vs too-wide (silent, bad), F8 the deletion that replicated perfectly, F9 the bill, F10 **slow not dead** (auto-scaling turns it into a positive feedback loop; a *deep* health check ejects the whole fleet and converts a degradation into an outage), F11 failover completed but clients did not notice (JVM DNS cache + pool holding established TCP).
- Lab shape: **no-code** — README (~2,000 lines, 16 stages, 6 prediction checkpoints), `visualization.html` (an ops console: 3 AZ columns with the control plane drawn deliberately *outside* them, 5 architecture versions v1→v5, 8 predict-then-run scenarios, step-forward/back over a snapshot history, click-to-inspect any node, and every "arbitrary" choice — which zone dies, whether the control plane answers, where the database sits, how much headroom — turned into a control), EXERCISES + SOLUTIONS across all eight rungs. Validated with a DOM-stub harness under JavaScriptCore (`osascript -l JavaScript`, no node on this machine): all 5 versions, all 8 scenarios and every fault toggle exercised; the harness caught four real bugs, the best being that the "no servers left" case printed a message about the database instead of the actual lesson (a healthy load balancer with an empty target group).
- Key terms now known: region, availability zone, AZ ID, partition (aws/aws-cn/aws-us-gov), control plane vs data plane, static stability, service quota, blast radius, managed service, shared responsibility model, IAM principal/policy/role, temporary credentials, explicit-deny-wins, SCP, permission boundary, elasticity vs scalability, VPC, subnet (AZ-scoped), security group, endpoint, ARN, SigV4, AMI, instance store vs EBS, ALB/NLB, target group, health check, Auto Scaling Group, RDS Multi-AZ, Aurora (W=4 R=3 N=6), DynamoDB, hot partition, Global Table, SQS Standard vs FIFO, Kinesis shard, Lambda, Fargate, Route 53, CloudFront, KMS, CloudWatch, RPO/RTO, egress charge, NAT Gateway, Savings Plan/Reserved/Spot, Organization, IaC, AWS Health Dashboard, Fault Injection Service, Well-Architected
- Builds on: [quorums-and-replication] (Aurora and S3 are it, with the *failure domain* rather than the disk as the unit tolerated), [consistent-hashing] + [partitioning-and-rebalancing] (DynamoDB's hot partition is F5, and sub-key splitting is the same remedy), [cap-theorem] + [pacelc] (RDS Multi-AZ is PC/EC and pays a cross-AZ round trip on every write; Global Tables are the AP corner with LWW), [eventual-consistency] + [crdts] (why LWW must not hold money), [idempotency] (mandatory on SQS Standard and on any retried write — the SDK retries for you), [microservices] (availability multiplies downward; slow-beats-dead is F10), [caching] (CloudFront, ElastiCache, replica lag and read-your-own-writes), [networking-for-developers] (DNS caching, connection pools, timeout budgets — F11 is that lab with a specific trigger), [redis] (ElastiCache is it, rented), [distributed-rate-limiting] (quotas are that lab pointed at you; fail-open vs fail-closed chosen per limit), [transactional-outbox] + [saga] (SQS/EventBridge and Step Functions) · Related: [google-spanner] (why synchronous cross-region is not on the menu for an interactive path: Mumbai↔Ireland ≈120ms one way) · Next: [terraform?] (IaC **is** the disaster-recovery plan — highest leverage), [kubernetes?], [observability?], [kafka?] (still the biggest unopened primitive, flagged since consistent-hashing and redis), [load-balancing?] (flagged by four labs, still unopened), [disaster-recovery?]

## queueing-theory — learned 2026-08-30 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-09 — lab: 57-queueing-theory/

- Takeaway: Waiting is not proportional to load. One machine, one line, no bugs, no distribution — and the time a request spends inside grows as **1/(1−ρ)**, so the last few percent of a box's capacity cost more delay than the first eighty put together (measured, S=80ms: 2.0× its own work at 50% busy, 5.1× at 80%, 10.3× at 90%, 21.4× at 95%). The one sentence that reframes every capacity conversation: **latency is set by spare capacity, not capacity** — `W = 1/(μ−λ)`, so 25% more traffic on a 72%-busy box cut slack from 3.5 to 1.25 req/s (2.8×) and multiplied total time by 2.9× and p99 by 3.3× (198→560ms median, 1,326→3,735ms p99). "We have 20% headroom" is not a sentence about safety.
- Two formulas generate the subject. **Little's Law `L = λW` is an identity, not a model** — no assumption about arrival pattern, service distribution, order, or server count; only steady state and a consistent boundary. Verified to 0.01 jobs at ρ=0.5/0.8/0.9/0.95. It is the one thing here worth quoting without qualification, and pool sizing is it read backwards. **Kingman `Wq ≈ (ρ/(1−ρ))·((c²ₐ+c²ₛ)/2)·S`** is three independent multipliers, matched within 5% across eight variability combinations. Killer exhibit: **identical λ, identical ρ=0.8, identical 80ms mean service — Wq from 0ms to 2,730ms purely from irregularity** (clockwork/clockwork 0 · random/clockwork 161 · random/random 326 · bursty/bursty 1,286 · random/spiky(c²=16) 2,730). Utilisation and mean service time cannot tell those systems apart; only c² can. Corollaries: halving S is a **6× win** on total time (400→67ms) because it moves two factors; jitter is free capacity; the c²ₛ tail (3% of jobs at 16×) costs more than the other 97%.
- Pooling is a genuine economy of scale: 4 servers at 80% busy as **four separate lines wait 320ms, as one shared line 61ms (5.2×; 11× at 50% busy)** — idle capacity in a separate line is not merely perishable, it is *unreachable*. At fixed ρ=0.8, Wq falls 316→147→61→24→8→2ms for 1/2/4/8/16/32 servers: **32 servers at 80% beat one server at 50%.** So one big cluster beats four small ones and sharding a queue by tenant is usually a latency regression dressed as isolation — override it only for a real noisy neighbour.
- The failure half, all measured. **F2, ρ≥1:** just 5% over → 27 waiting at 1min, 385 at 5, **1,619 at 30 and still linear**; throughput sits pinned at capacity looking perfectly healthy; recovery = backlog ÷ spare capacity, so a timid shed to 0.95 takes 18 min where 0.85 takes 6 — *shed hard or don't bother*. **F3 metastable retry storm:** a one-instant 400-job burst into a healthy 85% system, clients retrying twice on a 600ms timeout → queue 3,249 → 9,983 → **37,115 at 20 min and growing**, while the identical burst without retries is absorbed inside four minutes (403→6); retries tripled offered load to exactly ρ=1.00 and 99% of server time went to clients that had left. Removing the trigger does not restore service — which is *why* turning traffic off and on works. **F6 the unbounded queue:** 9,122 requests at ρ=1.2 with a 1s deadline — throughput identical (7,711) at every queue size while **goodput ran from 64 (unbounded, and also at caps of 200/100/50) to 7,033 at a cap of 5**; the rule `maxQueue ≈ deadline/S − c` = 11.5 lands at 6,352, within 10% of optimum. **F7 FIFO under overload:** oldest-first answers 64 in time, newest-first **6,660** (104×) — but p99 doubles to 36s, so LIFO is an overload *mode*, not a default. Also F1 invisible cliff, F4 coordinated omission (a closed-loop load generator's arrival rate is a property of the system under test, not the test), F5 head-of-line blocking, F8 batched arrivals (same load, 6× the wait).
- Production translation: you do not have a queue, you have five or six in series and configured about one. **Little's Law gives the floor, not the setting** — measured at λ=100/s, S=200ms: 20 servers is ρ=1.00 (W=3.2s and rising), 25 gives 209ms; size to `λS ÷ ρ_target`. A too-small pool does not make you slow, it makes you a smaller service (10 servers → throughput pinned at 50/s). Prefer a **concurrency limit over a rate limit** (it re-derives itself when S changes; a rate limit is correct at exactly one downstream latency). `ThreadPoolExecutor` + bare `LinkedBlockingQueue` and `spring.task.execution.pool.queue-capacity` (Integer.MAX_VALUE) are F6 shipped as defaults; Hikari's 30s `connectionTimeout` is F6 with a 30× multiplier. Dashboard rules: split W into Wq and S (add `time_in_queue` if you add nothing else), alert on **spare capacity and queue-depth derivative** rather than utilisation or latency, and measure **goodput** — throughput never moved across the whole F6 range.
- Lab shape: **no runnable code** (per the 2026-08-29 rule) — `README.md` (16 stages, four depth layers, 5 prediction checkpoints), `EXERCISES.md` (8 rungs), `SOLUTIONS.md`, and `visualization.html` = the only executable artifact: a discrete-event simulator with seeded PRNG, step-back by deterministic replay, failure injection, and 8 predict-then-run scenarios. Published: <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/a660c0b4-e331-4ee3-b641-b727397d994d>. Every number in the README came from that same engine run headlessly on JavaScriptCore (no node on this machine — `osascript -l JavaScript`), validated first against Little's Law, M/M/1, M/D/1, D/D/1, Erlang-C and Kingman.
- Key terms now known: arrival rate λ, service time S, service rate μ, utilisation ρ, stability (ρ<1), saturation, Wq/W/Lq/L, Little's Law, coefficient of variation c², memoryless/exponential, Kendall notation A/B/c, M/M/1, M/D/1, D/D/1, M/M/c, Erlang-C, Kingman's formula, offered load, throughput vs goodput, pooling, work-conserving, head-of-line blocking, backpressure, load shedding, admission control, metastable failure, retry storm, retry budget, coordinated omission, open- vs closed-loop load, bulkhead, concurrency limit, jitter, adaptive LIFO, deadline propagation, deadline-drop-on-dequeue
- **Corrects an earlier lab:** `work-pressure-and-deadlines` printed `ρ/(1−ρ)` beside "at 80% busy a request takes 5× its own work time". The 5× is right but belongs to `1/(1−ρ)` (total time ÷ work); `ρ/(1−ρ)` is the *queue* multiplier and equals 4 at ρ=0.8. Measured here: Wq/S = 4.07, W/S = 5.08. They differ by exactly one service time.
- Builds on: [work-pressure-and-deadlines] (named this as its Next; the ρ/(1−ρ) collapse and the WIP U-curve are this maths applied to people), [io-multiplexing] (waiting is the server's real job; the *ready* connections are the queue), [distributed-rate-limiting] (this is *why* the λ<μ boundary is a cliff and not a slope; also why a concurrency limit beats its rate limit), [caching] (thundering herd = F8), [idempotency] (mandatory once a late answer causes a client retry — the 8.1 payments design depends on it) · Related: [java-virtual-threads] (cheap threads relocate the queue from somewhere visible to somewhere invisible — they do not remove it), [cap-theorem] (fail-open/fail-closed is the same shape of forced trade) · Next: [backpressure?], [load-shedding-and-admission-control?], [tail-at-scale?], [universal-scalability-law?], [performance-modelling?]

## java-multithreading — learned 2026-08-30 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-09 — lab: 16-java-multithreading/

- Takeaway: Threads are easy until two of them touch the same memory, and then only two questions matter: **can both be inside this code at once** (atomicity) and **is there any guarantee one can see what the other wrote** (visibility). They are genuinely separate problems — `volatile` fixes the second and not the first, which is why `count++` under `volatile` still loses updates. A monitor fixes **both at once** (mutual exclusion + a happens-before edge on release/acquire), which is why `synchronized` is the default and everything else is a refinement of it or an escape from it.
- **The framing that made this lab land:** it was learned AFTER [java-virtual-threads], [structured-concurrency] and [scoped-values], so it is written as *the machine underneath the labs already done* — the pile of problems those three were invented to hide. Every bug here still happens on virtual threads (same memory model, same locks); cheap threads changed the cost of **waiting**, not the rules of **sharing**. The one failure virtual threads genuinely delete is pool-starvation deadlock, because there is no pool to starve.
- **The best single insight, vs [distributed-locking]:** notice what is *missing* from every lock in this lab — no TTL, no lease, no fencing token. Not sloppiness: inside one JVM the runtime genuinely knows when a thread has died and releases its locks. **A single JVM is the one place where a lock can be exactly a lock.** Everything that lab called a compromise was the price of leaving this building.
- Measured (deterministic simulation engines in `visualization.html`, replayed by `./validate-visualization.sh` — **42 engine checks + 12 render checks, all passing**; two checks are *exhaustive* over every interleaving, not sampled):
  - **Lost update**: 2 threads × `count++` from 0 — exhaustive enumeration gives reachable finals **exactly {1, 2}**, never 3. The bad outcome is half the state space, not an exotic corner.
  - **Visibility**: without `volatile`, memory holds `true` while the worker loops **13** more times reading `false` from its private copy — never terminates, 100% CPU, no error. With `volatile`: terminates.
  - **Lock**: exhaustive over *every* schedule of the `synchronized` version → always 2. Same interleaving with the lock removed → 1. Mutual exclusion doesn't slow threads, it **deletes interleavings from existence** (the blocked thread's program counter does not move).
  - **Deadlock**: opposite lock order → STUCK, both BLOCKED, each holding what the other wants. Consistent order → DONE. `synchronized` has no timeout and no detection.
  - **`if` vs `while`**: 2 consumers asleep + producer adds ONE item + `notifyAll` → with `if`, `items = −1` (two consumers took one item); with `while`, `items = 0` and consumer 2 correctly re-sleeps. Also verified: `notifyAll()` with an empty wait set logs **"woke nobody"** — a notification is not queued, counted or remembered.
  - **CAS**: final 2, exactly **1** refusal + **1** retry, **no thread ever BLOCKED**; exhaustively safe to depth 18. The cost moves from *waiting* to *redoing*.
  - **Pool sweep** (12 tasks × 5 ticks): makespans **60 / 30 / 20 / 15 / 15 / 10** for 1–6 workers = `D × ceil(N/W)`, NOT `ceil(N·D/W)` — **4 and 5 workers tie at 15** because whole tasks can't be split. Capacity stops buying anything before the graph looks full.
  - **Pool policies**: queue cap 0, 12 tasks, 2 workers → **10 of 12 rejected**; with CallerRunsPolicy → **0 rejected, 12 completed, 4 run by the submitting thread** (which then can't submit). A queue never creates throughput, it only decides who waits.
  - **Pool starvation deadlock**: tasks submitting sub-tasks to their own pool → DEADLOCK at tick 4 with 2 workers, every worker `blockedOn` a sub-task; at 8 workers the identical config completes — so a bigger pool **only moves the cliff to peak load**.
- The two rules worth carrying: **never hold a lock across I/O** (one slow holder converts its own delay into everyone's — the in-JVM twin of [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking]'s `lunch` result), and **never block, inside a pool task, on work submitted to that same pool**.
- Key terms now known: thread, shared mutable state, race condition, lost update, read-modify-write / check-then-act, atomicity, visibility, critical section, mutual exclusion, monitor / intrinsic lock, entry set vs wait set, reentrant, `wait`/`notify`/`notifyAll`, spurious wakeup, deadlock, lock ordering, livelock, starvation, convoy, fairness, barging, safety vs liveness, thread confinement, immutability, safe publication, compare-and-set, atomic classes / `LongAdder`, thread pool / executor, bounded vs unbounded queue, rejection policy, back-pressure, pool starvation deadlock, bulkhead, interruption, `ThreadLocal` leak, thread dump / `jstack`, BLOCKED vs WAITING vs RUNNABLE, false sharing, Amdahl's law
- Lab contents: **no runnable code** (per the 2026-08-29 no-code rule). `README.md` — 16 stages, 4 depth layers, 3 prediction checkpoints, stockroom-tally vs engineering mental models + a 3-point "where the analogy breaks", 12 failure modes each tagged safety-or-liveness, an on-call symptom table, 40-term glossary, mechanically checked so no term appears before it is defined. `visualization.html` ("**The Interleaving Machine**", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/10d168d6-8a4f-49fe-9b39-471e067a6bc7>) — pure state+reducer engine, each Java line decomposed into machine steps, **the learner picks which thread runs next**; per-thread private registers + private-copy row with live STALE markers, shared heap, monitor owner/entry set/wait set, an **interleaving ribbon** (one coloured tick per step, click to rewind), 6 predict-then-run scenarios, per-scenario toggles (volatile / remove lock / lock ordering / if-vs-while), plus a second tab: thread-pool simulator with worker bars, queue slots, nesting-deadlock and CallerRuns toggles, and a live makespan sweep. `EXERCISES.md` (8 rungs) + `SOLUTIONS.md`. `validate-visualization.sh` runs both engines headlessly (node, else JavaScriptCore via osascript) **plus a stub-DOM pass that re-renders every scenario** so render errors fail there, not in the browser. NOT built: instruction reordering (that's [java-memory-model]), livelock as a scenario (described only), `ReentrantLock`/`Condition`/`ReadWriteLock`, `ConcurrentHashMap` internals, ForkJoinPool work stealing, false sharing.
- Builds on: [java-memory-model] (**its direct sequel** — that lab answered "which values may a thread see", this one answers "which interleavings exist"; happens-before, `volatile`, reordering and the store buffer reused as known vocabulary, and its `Publication` / `DoubleCheckedLocking` experiments are this lab's failure #12), [java-virtual-threads] / [structured-concurrency] / [scoped-values] (**learned earlier, reframed here as the answers to this lab's problems** — pool sizing arithmetic, failure #7, and the `ThreadLocal` leak respectively), [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] (**the twin one level up** — its version column IS compare-and-set in SQL; its `lunch` and `crossover` results reused as the database-scale versions of the convoy and of CAS retry waste), [distributed-locking] (the same fight across machines — and why a JVM lock needs no lease or fencing token), [isolation-levels] (lost update, third scale), [raft] (randomised back-off for livelock = randomised election timeouts for split votes), [idempotency] (exercise 8.2's real answer: remove the consequence, not the race), [linearizability] (`compareAndSet` as the canonical atomic op) · Related: [io-multiplexing] (the other answer to "do not block a thread") · Next: [java-concurrent-collections?], [amdahls-law?], [queueing-theory?], [completable-future?], [jvm-thread-dumps?], [reactive-streams?]

## microservices — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 56-microservices/

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). Deliberately *not* a re-teach of [software-architecture-patterns]'s one-paragraph catalogue entry: this is the **mechanics of cutting a system apart**, with numbers, and it is the single wound that [saga], [transactional-outbox], [cqrs], [idempotency], [two-phase-commit] and [eventual-consistency] are each a **scar** of.
- Takeaway: Microservices buy exactly **one** thing — **independent deployability** (a team releases its own part without asking or breaking anyone) — and the only way to get it is to make the boundary a **data** boundary: exactly one service may touch a given table, everyone else asks. The test is *"can you rename a column at 4pm on a Friday without telling anybody?"*; if not, it is not separate however many repos it has. The bill is arithmetic, not opinion.
- **The three numbers to be able to say.** (1) **Availability multiplies**: five 99.9% services on one path = 99.501% = **43.7 h/yr vs 8.8 h** for a monolith — a 5× regression bought with a large amount of work, and redundancy cannot repay it (replicas fix machine failure only; not bad deploys, not dependency failure, not shared-bottleneck overload). The *only* lever that changes the exponent is **removing services from the required set** — critical path down to gateway+orders = 99.8% = 17.5 h, still 2× the monolith, and **that gap is the honest price**. (2) **A slow dependency is far worse than a dead one**: Little's Law `workers = rate × duration` — dead = 100/s × 0.001 s = 0.1 threads (contained, error rate spikes, wrong-but-visible); slow = 100/s × 5 s = **500 threads needed, 200 exist, pool empty in 2.0 s**, and then *every* endpoint dies including ones that never call it, while **every dashboard stays green because the sick service returns 200 OK**. (3) **Tail amplification**: `1−(1−p)^N` — p99 dependencies at fan-out 6 → 5.9% slow, at 40 → 33%, at 100 → **63%** (Dean & Barroso, *The Tail at Scale*, CACM 2013). A p99 is a property of a call, not of a system.
- **The v1-breaks-then-refine chain that generates every pattern you already knew**: sync everything → availability product + cascade → **timeout** (100×1 s = 100 threads: does NOT buy success, buys **containment**) → **retries** re-create the failure on both sides (100×3 s = 300 > 200 caller occupancy *and* payments receives 300/s — the fix aimed at the dying service) → **bulkhead** (cap 40, 160 always free: isolation) → **breaker** (open ⇒ ~0 threads and callee load collapses, which is the condition it needs to recover) → **async off the critical path** ([transactional-outbox] + [saga] + idempotent consumers). Why exactly those four siblings — the same quartet [distributed-rate-limiting] said it "needed to survive" — is now *derived* from Little's Law rather than listed. Two breaker facts usually got wrong: it protects the **caller**, not the callee; and state must be **per (dependency, endpoint)** or the mitigation manufactures the cascade.
- **Retry safety rule (from [idempotency], now placed):** connection-refused/503/429 = certainly not received = retry freely; **timeout and 500 = the third outcome, *unknown*** = idempotency key mandatory. **"A method call has two outcomes; a network call has three"** — splitting a monolith IS the act of introducing that third outcome where it did not exist.
- **Where boundaries go — five rules + one metric.** Cut around (1) things that change together, (2) data ownership (one writer, enforced by **grants**, not a wiki), (3) never through an invariant that must hold exactly (no distributed transaction exists ⇒ atomic things stay in one service — the [saga] escape hatch is "don't split"), (4) teams (Conway), (5) **never along technical layers**. The metric almost nobody collects: **median number of deployables changed per shipped feature** — must be 1. Its blind spot (visualization tab 5, third option): a capability cut sharing one database scores a perfect median while 10/10 features still need a coordinated release — *the matrix measures where code changes, the release log measures what must move together.*
- **What you permanently gave up, and its replacement**: the compiler that found every caller → **consumer-driven contract tests** + schema registry + **expand/contract migration (always three deploys: add, migrate, remove)**; the stack trace → **distributed tracing** (trace ID minted at the edge, forwarded on *every* outbound call — one service dropping it breaks everything below, hence a platform job); database transactions → sagas/outbox/idempotent consumers. Safety here = no cross-service data access, no double charge, no invalid saga end (testable); liveness = every outbox row published, every saga terminal, every projection caught up — **alarm on AGE, never on count** (a queue-depth alarm on an outbox goes silent exactly when the relay dies, and a poison row head-of-line-blocks while depth stays low and flat).
- **The retreats are the useful half** (all real, dated): Segment 2018 re-merged 100+ services to a monolith; Uber 2020 DOMA moved from ~2,200 services toward coarser domains; Prime Video 2023 cut one component's cost ~90% by re-merging (widely mis-reported as "Amazon abandons microservices" — it was one component, and the lesson is rule 1). **Re-merging two services that always deploy together is a healthy engineering act, not a defeat**; the honest default for a small org is a modular monolith.
- Key terms now known: independent deployability, distributed monolith, bounded context, database-per-service (earned), contract, cascading failure, thread pool exhaustion, Little's Law (used, not yet formalised), critical path, fan-out, tail latency amplification, retry storm, retry budget, fail fast, exponential backoff with jitter, service discovery, distributed tracing/span/traceparent, consumer-driven contract testing, expand/contract (parallel change), tolerant reading, chatty boundary, event-carried state transfer, load shedding, canary deploy, SLO, error budget, slow-call rate
- Lab contents: `README.md` (16 stages, 4 depth layers, 4 prediction checkpoints, market-street vs finite-pool mental models with 5 analogy breaks, 9 failure scenarios, 50-term glossary). `EXERCISES.md`/`SOLUTIONS.md` (8 rungs; rung 5 is a hand-worked Little's Law trace of a 6-second incident, rung 6 has 4 planted-flaw designs incl. the shared *domain* library and the single global breaker, rung 8 includes an availability-budget design where an external 99.9% card-network SLA makes the promised 99.95% **unachievable** and only two structural options reach it). `visualization.html` ("The Checkout Cascade", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/ec33c164-a395-4748-bdac-513db3cbf96e>) — 5 tabs: shared-vs-private store rename demo, availability calculator, a **transient thread-pool simulator** (cohort delay-line, step-back, 7 predict-then-run scenarios, narrated 10-stage story mode, pool size and traffic rate exposed as sliders), tail-amplification, and the feature×service boundary matrix. Simulator verified against the doc's numbers: healthy 2/200 · slow 100 @1 s, 200 @2 s · timeout 100/200 · 3 attempts → 300/s at payments · bulkhead 40 with history 100% · breaker → ~0 threads · pool 2000 → exactly 500 threads (exercise 3.2 live).
- Builds on: [software-architecture-patterns] (the catalogue entry this expands — and its F2 "premature microservices"), [saga] + [transactional-outbox] + [cqrs] + [event-sourcing] (the async critical path of 10g), [two-phase-commit] (the road not taken; why rule 3 exists), [idempotency] (the third outcome; the double-charge in exercise 6.3), [distributed-rate-limiting] (**its F2 slow-store-exhausts-threads is this lab's general law**; the four siblings; stranded quota reappears in the 3-region design), [communication-protocols] (what the calls are; N+1 across a network), [eventual-consistency] + [caching] (every cross-service read; display-not-decisions), [work-pressure-and-deadlines] (`ρ/(1−ρ)` under 40% utilisation), [java-virtual-threads] (cheap threads move the bottleneck and make bulkheads *more* important) · Related: [cap-theorem]/[pacelc] (per boundary, and the 3-region inventory question) · Next: [domain-driven-design?] (where rule 1–5 came from — the missing half), [distributed-tracing?], [queueing-theory?] (Little's Law + Kingman properly), [kubernetes?], [service-mesh?], [kafka?], [api-versioning?], [load-shedding?], [observability-slos?]

## flp-impossibility — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 33-flp-impossibility/

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). The promised sequel to [paxos]'s closing line and [split-brain]'s "no observation available to S2 would tell it so".
- Takeaway: In an **asynchronous** system (no bound on message delay, no clocks) where even **one** process may crash, **no deterministic protocol can guarantee Agreement + Validity + Termination together** — because a slow process and a dead process produce *literally identical evidence*, and no observation separates them. Consensus is not impossible; a **guarantee on finishing** is. Every real system keeps safety unconditionally and buys liveness with a timing assumption it is responsible for maintaining.
- **The sentence to be able to say:** *"CAP takes the messages away; FLP leaves every message intact and only takes away the clock — and that alone is enough."* Second: **the proof's run has ZERO crashes** — it needs only the *obligation to tolerate* one, which is the door the adversary walks through. Third: **determined ≠ terminating** (a univalent configuration can still stall forever).
- The three facts the proof is built from, each **computed live** in the visualization, not asserted: (1) **some starting point is bivalent** — witnessed by exhibiting one schedule per ending; (2) **events at different processes commute** — measured **133 of 133 swaps identical, 0 exceptions**, while same-process pairs differ 5 of 48; (3) **a bivalent configuration always has a bivalent next move** (the commuting diamond + the crash-indistinguishability argument). Verified: with Ada silent from step 0, the world where she wanted DARK and the world where she wanted LIGHT both end Bo/Cy on **LIGHT at step 10** — her preference had no effect because nobody heard it.
- **The headline measurement, and the thing that makes FLP ≠ CAP visceral.** Adversary scheduler vs rotating-coordinator Paxos, **5000 steps**: decided **0**, attempt number **682**, letters delivered **4545**, letters **lost 0**, longest any letter waited **7 steps**, longest any process idle **4 steps**, crashes **0**, agreement violations **0**. The run is fully **admissible** — the adversary only ever chooses the *order*. Fair ferry on the same start: settled in **15 steps**.
- **The adversary's v1→refine chain:** "just withhold the letter that would complete the majority" → *fairness forces eventual delivery, so it only postpones* → **"make the letter harmless before delivering it"**: never let two acceptors accept the same attempt, by bumping the target's promise with a higher-numbered PREPARE first. That IS [paxos]'s dueling-proposers livelock, revealed as a deliberate strategy rather than bad luck.
- **The four escapes, all measured against the same protocol, agreement never broken in any row:** **partial synchrony Δ** (breaks *asynchronous*) — Δ=2/4/10/25 settle at step 19/23/33/63, always on **attempt 1**; loose Δ costs latency, not correctness. **Leader oracle ◇-style** (breaks *no failure information*) — settles in **13 steps with NO timing assumption**, but is unimplementable in pure async (else FLP is refuted); in practice built from timeouts = Δ in a hat. **Ben-Or randomised *decisions*** (breaks *deterministic* properly) — probability-1 termination, no timing assumption; **described, not simulated**. **Randomised *timeouts*** — the surprise: with no Δ the adversary won **60 runs out of 60**. Raft's randomised election timeout cures **collisions**, NOT asynchrony; with Δ=4 present it added nothing measurable (23.0 avg steps, same as Δ alone).
- Also measured: a **uniformly random** (non-adversarial) scheduler left **54/400** no-crash runs and **18/200** one-crash runs undecided at a 500-step cap — ~13%. You do not need an adversary; "we've never seen it" just means you called it a slow period. And **0 agreement violations in 500 random schedules**, one third with a crashed process.
- **The eight production sightings** (§11), each tied to a guarantee: duelling proposers · **GC as the adversary** (reuses [split-brain]'s 1,011 µs → 191,661 µs, 190× p99 collapse) · confidently-wrong failure detector (reuses [gossip-protocols]' 100 nodes / 0 crashes / 100% beliefs DEAD) · **grey failure** (alive enough to heartbeat, too slow to commit — every dashboard green, latency infinite; the most common way this bites people) · symmetric timeouts (reuses [raft]'s *term 40 in 6000 ticks, leader NEVER*) · adding replicas not helping · the healed partition that doesn't recover · random ordering stalling anyway. **Across all eight, Agreement never broke. Only finishing did.**
- **The metric to keep if you keep one: time since the commit index last advanced.** It catches grey failure, leader loss, quorum loss and disk stalls in one number. Alarm on the **ratio** electionTimeout ÷ observed broadcastTime, not absolutes. Monitor **GC pause p99 and fsync p99 as correctness signals**, not performance ones.
- **A build note worth keeping.** The commuting lemma initially failed in the simulator (38 of 142 swaps differed). Cause: a process computed its next attempt number from a **global** variable. A real process cannot read a global. Once each computed it from *what it had itself seen*, exceptions went to **0**. The lemma is diagnostic: a violation means a process is reading something nobody sent it.
- Key terms now known: asynchronous vs synchronous system, crash failure, non-Byzantine, step, event, **null step** (a timeout is not outside the model — it is a step where `receive` returns nothing), configuration, schedule, run, **admissible run** (the adversary's fairness contract), deterministic, **valency / bivalent / univalent / 0-valent / 1-valent**, partial synchrony, **GST**, failure detector, **◇W** (weakest failure detector for consensus, Chandra–Hadzilacos–Toueg 1996), grey failure, pre-vote, check-quorum, Dijkstra Prize.
- Verified: the page's simulation engine is exercised by a **71-check headless harness** (JavaScriptCore via `osascript -l JavaScript`, stub DOM) covering both schedulers, every escape, the commutation audit, the crash argument, all controls and all three prediction widgets. It caught three real bugs: the global-variable commutation break, a duplicate DECIDE firing on a third acceptance, and `+''===0` silently crashing Ada when a select had no value.
- Lab contents: `README.md` (16 stages, 4 depth layers), `EXERCISES.md` (8 rungs), `SOLUTIONS.md`, `visualization.html` — "The Ferryman's Choice", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/59306f2a-8884-4ffa-b06c-81faf73ad1d5> — 4 tabs: *Slow, or dead?* (the one observation nobody can make) · *Play the ferryman* (**you are the adversary**; a live fairness audit proves you are not cheating) · *Why the ferryman always wins* (the three facts, computed) · *The four escapes* (nine combinations, one table).
- Builds on: [paxos] (**this is the answer to its closing line** — its livelock trace re-derived as a deliberate strategy; its P2c safety reused rather than re-proved), [split-brain] (**its t=70 sentence was FLP's engine room, found empirically**; its 190× GC measurement reused), [raft] (**corrects a widespread belief** — randomised timeouts attack *collisions*, not asynchrony; measured 60/60 adversary wins without Δ), [cap-theorem] (the crisp separator), [gossip-protocols] (the fallible failure detector, measured), [google-spanner] (TrueTime buys external consistency, **not** liveness — atomic clocks do not repeal the theorem) · Related: [quorums-and-replication], [distributed-locking] (fencing makes a stale process harmless, it does not stop the stall), [eventual-consistency]/[crdts] (the "solve a weaker problem" escape) · Next: [partial-synchrony?] (the DLS 1988 model your cluster actually assumes — the natural next lab), [failure-detectors?], [byzantine-fault-tolerance?], [atomic-broadcast?], [multipaxos?]

## java-version-features — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 21-java-version-features/

- **No-code lab** (README + **17 per-version .md files** + visualization + exercises). Covers **Java 9 → 25 (current LTS)**, one file per release: `java-09.md` … `java-25.md`, zero-padded so they sort.
- Takeaway: Java nearly froze to death on version 8 because releases were **feature-driven** — the bus left when the last passenger boarded, so Java 9 took 3.5 years and arrived carrying **91 JEPs at once** while Java 10 shipped **12** six months later. The fix was not a feature, it was three inventions that answer three requirements: **the six-month release train** (a date, not a readiness condition), **preview features** (ship a language feature and still be allowed to change it), and **LTS** (a stop conservative shops can sit on). Every odd detail of modern Java is a scar from one of those.
- **The sentence to be able to say:** *"Upgrade cost is superlinear in the size of one delta, so frequency is a safety feature."* The ratchet: an upgrade that is a *project* gets scheduled against other projects, loses, and the next one is bigger — which is why teams stuck on 8 didn't like 8, they just looked up too late.
- **The enforcement mechanism, measured on JDK 25.0.4 during the build.** Preview code isn't gated by a flag — the flag protects the *compiler*, and the **jar** is what travels into repos and containers. So the mark goes in the artifact: normal class file = `cafe babe 0000 0045`; preview = `cafe babe **ffff** 0045`. Minor **65535** + **exact** major match ⇒ `UnsupportedClassVersionError: Preview features are not enabled for R (class file version 69.65535)`; with `--enable-preview` it prints `42`. Consequence: **a preview-compiled jar runs on exactly one Java version and nothing else** — never publish one. **Trap:** the flag stamps the class file even when no preview feature was used, so a leftover `--enable-preview` pins your artifact for no benefit.
- **The three gates are not synonyms.** Preview = language/VM, `--enable-preview`, stamped in the class file. Incubator = API, `jdk.incubator.*` + `--add-modules`, **package renames on graduation** (`jdk.incubator.foreign` → `java.lang.foreign` broke every early adopter, by design). Experimental = VM only, `-XX:+UnlockExperimentalVMOptions`.
- **Ladder lengths are a measure of design risk, not team velocity.** Healthy shape = 2 previews then final (records, sealed, virtual threads, gatherers, Class-File API). Pattern matching for `switch` = 5 releases (had to settle sealed/records/generics/null interactions first). FFM = **8 years**, two separate incubators that merged in 17. Vector API = **10 incubations by 25, 11 by 26** — blocked on **Valhalla** value types, per its own JEP; a long stay signals a *dependency*, not a failure. **Scoped values finalised in 25 while structured concurrency, which entered a release EARLIER, is still previewing in 26** — same team, same project, different design risk.
- **The load-bearing event: string templates were WITHDRAWN.** Previewed in 21 and 22, removed from 23 with no replacement and no successor JEP. Preview only *means* something because withdrawal actually happens — and `STR."…"` code became uncompilable, exactly as the contract said.
- **`yield` exists because of a mistake caught in preview.** Switch expressions shipped in 12 with `break value;`, which ambiguously overloaded `break`; the 13 preview replaced it with `yield`; final in 14. Cost of a wrong language syntax under this system: one release, behind a flag.
- **The removal ladder (deprecate → terminally deprecate → remove) is itself a guarantee** — you are always told twice, and frequent releases are what make it affordable. Most upgrade pain is meeting step 3 as a surprise after ignoring 1 and 2.
- **Migration hazards, verified on 25.0.4 where marked.** Strong encapsulation is a **5-release ladder**: 9–15 permit+warn → **16 flips the default to deny** → **17 removes `--illegal-access` entirely**. Measured: `InaccessibleObjectException: module java.base does not "opens java.lang"`, cured by `--add-opens java.base/java.lang=ALL-UNNAMED`. `sun.misc.Unsafe` measured warning: *"A terminally deprecated method … will be removed in a future release"* — and it **still ran** (`x=7`). `--release 8` warns as obsolete; supported set on 25.0.4 is **8–25**, `--release 7` already errors. **Asymmetry to keep: running Java 8 bytecode on 25 is fine forever; compiling for old targets is what expires.**
- **The distinction that actually matters in an upgrade: loud vs silent.** Loud failures (F1–F5, F7, F9–F12) are found by compiling and booting — they cost an afternoon. **Silent ones reach customers**: JEP 400's UTF-8 default (18) and biased locking's removal (15). Therefore **compiling and booting on a new JDK proves almost nothing**; the control is your full behavioural suite — tests asserting on *output content* — running on the next JDK in a **non-blocking CI job, continuously**, which converts the upgrade from a project into a stream of small known fixes (the ratchet, run backwards).
- **Corrections this lab makes to earlier labs.** [java-virtual-threads]'s `synchronized`-pins-the-carrier advice was true on 21 and is **obsolete from 24 (JEP 491)** — that fix is what makes virtual threads adoptable on a large existing codebase you can't audit. [structured-concurrency] documents **JEP 505, a fifth preview**, already redesigned once (`ShutdownOnFailure` → `open(joiner)`), sixth preview in 26 — don't build a library on it. [scoped-values] is **final** as of 25 (JEP 506).
- **Free performance nobody attributes correctly.** CDS has been **on by default since JDK 12** (JEP 341): measured **956 of 1003 classes** loaded from the shared archive with no flags. AOT cache (JEP 483/514/515) measured **0.1050 s → 0.0862 s, 18% faster startup**, 12.6 MB cache, 8 runs each — small precisely *because* CDS was already helping; larger apps gain more (documented, not measured here).
- **Verdict:** target **25** (premier to 2030, extended 2033). Routes: 8 → 17 → 25 and 11 → 17 → 25 (do 11→17 as its own piece of work — nearly all the risk is there); 17 → 25 and 21 → 25 direct. 8→25 = **298 JEPs across 17 releases**; 21→25 = 66 across 4.
- Key terms now known: JEP · JSR · GA · rampdown · LTS (a **vendor promise**, not a property of the build) · release train · preview vs incubator vs experimental · withdrawn · class file major (subtract 44) / minor 65535 · deprecated vs terminally deprecated · removal ladder · split package · unnamed module · `opens` / `--add-opens` · strong encapsulation · deep reflection · `--release` vs `-source`/`-target` · `jdeps --jdk-internals` · CDS · AOT cache · `jlink` · `jpackage` · VarHandle · FFM · Projects Amber / Loom / Panama / Leyden / Valhalla.
- Verified: JEP lists and per-release counts scraped from the OpenJDK release pages (9–26); support dates from Oracle's published roadmap; all behavioural claims marked **[measured]** were executed on **OpenJDK 25.0.4**. The visualization's logic is covered by a **144-check headless harness** (JavaScriptCore via `osascript -l JavaScript`, stub DOM) exercising all 8 class-file combinations, every hazard range incl. empty and reversed, pipeline carry-forward, all narration branches and every control. A separate link checker validates **344 internal cross-links**.
- Lab contents: `README.md` (16 stages, 4 depth layers), **`java-09.md` … `java-25.md`** (17 files), `EXERCISES.md` (8 rungs), `SOLUTIONS.md`, `visualization.html` — "The Java Departure Board", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/7b7a1370-fb28-4a44-bcfe-32b4e499a35d> — 4 tabs: *The timetable* (delta counter for any jump) · *The pipeline* (step 9→26, watch ladders climb; click any feature) · *The class-file lock* (compile/run combinations, real bytes) · *Hazard scanner* (from/to, loud vs silent).
- Builds on: [java-virtual-threads] (**corrected**: JEP 491), [structured-concurrency] (**contextualised**: still preview), [scoped-values] (**now final**), [java-memory-model] (VarHandle access modes are its happens-before rules at finer grain) · Related: [io-multiplexing] · Next: [java-module-system?] (JPMS properly — F1/F3 are its surface), [project-valhalla?] (what the Vector API has waited 9 years for), [foreign-function-and-memory-api?], [jvm-garbage-collectors?] (G1/ZGC/Shenandoah — F8/F12 are its surface), [class-file-api?], [pattern-matching-in-java?]

## cockroachdb — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 67-cockroachdb/

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). The companion piece to [google-spanner]: **the same problem, the bill sent to the other end of the transaction**. It is the `[cockroachdb?]` suggestion from that lab, cashed in.
- Takeaway: Spanner bought a clock that admits its error and made **writers** pay it off by sleeping. CockroachDB buys nothing: it **declares** a bound (`--max-offset`, default **500 ms** — an assumption, not a measurement) and makes **readers** carry the doubt — any committed value in `(read_ts, read_ts + max_offset]` is *unresolvable*, so the read restarts higher rather than risk a stale answer. The same posture generates everything else: never coordinate to prevent a conflict, proceed and **detect** it, and throw work away. Hence **SQLSTATE 40001 is part of the API contract, not an error path** — a rolling restart alone produces them.
- **The carrying trace** (reproduced exactly by the simulator): Mumbai +180, Ohio −120, **both well inside the 500 ms budget, neither faulty**. Asha's payment commits on Mumbai at true 1000 → stamped **1180**, acked **1060**; she telephones Ben; Ben reads on Ohio at true 1080 → read_ts **960**, window **(960, 1460]**. 1180 lands *inside* → `ReadWithinUncertaintyIntervalError` → restart at **1180⁺** (ceiling stays 1460, so doubt shrinks 500 → 280 ms) → SETTLED, for one extra round trip. Drag max_offset to 0 and the identical trace returns **PENDING** — a wrong answer about money with **no error, no log line**.
- **The derived condition, swept exhaustively (1,344 cfg combinations, 0 mismatches): a stale read is possible iff `off_writer − off_reader > max_offset + Δ`**, Δ = real time between the writer taking its stamp and the reader taking its own. Three corollaries: only the *difference* of two clocks matters (all clocks an hour fast is harmless); **Δ helps you, so a faster network is a more exposed one** (same inversion as Spanner's `g < ε₁ − ε₂ − P`, third sighting); and lowering `--max-offset` buys latency with correctness margin. Crossover measured at Mumbai **+460 → restart / +480 → silent stale read** — one slider notch.
- **The v1→break→refine chains** (every odd detail is the scar of a watched failure): hash the key → *range scans die* → **ordered ranges** in one monolithic sorted map (512 MiB split / 128 MiB merge, `/tableID/indexID/pk → cols`, meta1/meta2) → *sequential PKs pile into the last range* → UUID / hash-sharded index. Read through Raft → *every read a consensus round* → **leaseholder** → *leader-leaseholder split hangs a range under partition* → **leader leases, default v25.2+** (leader IS leaseholder; killed the node-liveness SPOF). Percolator 2 rounds → *the outcome was already forced a round ago* → **parallel commits**: write the `STAGING` record listing in-flight keys *alongside* the intents; **committed is redefined as a checkable property of data** ("record STAGING + all listed writes achieved consensus"), so the ack comes after **one** round — **121 ms vs 181 ms**, exactly one WAN RTT saved at every latency (10/60/120/180 → diff 10/60/120/180) → *who cleans up?* → **transaction status recovery**, run by any passing reader; the abort branch must **raise the timestamp cache above the missing key first, then write ABORTED** (decision creates the condition that makes it true). Read at your own now → *stale read* → **uncertainty interval** → *could restart forever* → **fixed ceiling** (doubt only shrinks) + **observed timestamps** (value > the clock reading you saw on first contact = provably future) → **at most one restart per node**; measured 1 restart vs 2 with the optimisation off, and it *changes which snapshot you get* (PENDING@580 vs REFUNDED@1285) — both serializable.
- **Serializable without locking a reader:** reads leave a mark (**timestamp cache**, in-memory, per-leaseholder, **never replicated**) → a write below it is **pushed** (not blocked) → a pushed txn must **read-refresh** its spans over the gap → dirty ⇒ `RETRY_SERIALIZABLE` 40001. Write skew closed: doctors trace ends 1 doctor on call; switch refresh off and both go off call (= snapshot isolation, = what `READ COMMITTED` does, GA v24.1, not default). **The refused transaction is the one that commits SECOND in real time, not the one with the lower timestamp** (verified by swapping the clocks: T_A survives at 880 in one run and at 1190⁺ in the other) ⇒ under contention the *slower* txn starves ⇒ `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` converts a late refusal into an early wait. The timestamp cache does two jobs (serializability + the parallel-commit barrier) and its non-replication is why a dead leaseholder costs you `ABORT_REASON_NEW_LEASE_PREVENTS_TXN`.
- **The honest boundary, and it is the point of the lab.** CockroachDB guarantees **serializability + "no stale reads"** and explicitly **not strict serializability** ("we're fairly close to it"). The gap is the **causal reverse**, traced with every clock inside budget: audit begins Frankfurt t=600 (ceiling 1100, fixed for life); Mumbai marks invoice PAID at **1180**, acked true **1060**; *because of that* Ohio raises a credit limit at **960**, begun true **1080**; the audit sees the effect (restart onto 960⁺) and skips the cause (1180 > 1100) → **effect without cause, serializable throughout**. Needs three txns, two sharing no data — had Ohio *read* the invoice its own window would have caught it. Raising max_offset does **not** fix it (at 900 it persists via observed timestamps; at ≤250 the audit simply sees neither write). Second boundary: **everything is conditional on the declared bound** — exceed it and correctness ends silently (scenario 7, self-check off). The **80%-of-budget self-termination** is what makes the assumption self-enforcing, and the 20% margin exists so removal happens *before* the promise breaks. ⇒ **alert on clock offset as a correctness signal**, third sighting after [hybrid-logical-clocks] (+60 ms node dragged a ±8 bound 16 → 67) and [google-spanner] (external consistency died *with commit wait on*).
- **The four-row who-pays table is not a property of a database — it is a per-table dial.** `GLOBAL` tables invert this lab entirely: closed timestamps are set a few hundred ms **in the future**, writers are forced above the present and must **commit-wait**, so readers never block and never leave the region. That is Spanner's protocol, in CockroachDB, on commodity clocks (`kv.closed_timestamp.lead_for_global_reads_override` > 800 ms above 150 ms RTT, 1500 ms = call support). Default tables charge the reader; GLOBAL charges the writer; you choose per table by read/write ratio.
- Production: five layers (SQL → transaction → distribution → replication → storage/Pebble). Defaults worth memorising — `range_min_bytes` 128 MiB, `range_max_bytes` **512 MiB**, `gc.ttlseconds` **14400 (4 h)**, `num_replicas` 3 (5 for system ranges), tolerated failures (RF−1)/2. Multi-region: `REGIONAL BY TABLE` / **`REGIONAL BY ROW`** (the main tool against cross-region txns) / `GLOBAL`; survival goals ZONE vs REGION; **non-voting replicas** (v21.1) = read capacity without enlarging the write quorum. Follower reads via **closed timestamps** (`kv.closed_timestamp.target_duration` **3s**, `side_transport_interval` **200ms**, `follower_read_timestamp()`, exact staleness recommended ≥ **4.2 s**, bounded staleness `with_max_staleness`) = Spanner's t_safe/MinNextTS under other names. On-call watches: clock offset (correctness alarm), retries **by reason**, hot ranges, `crdb_internal.cluster_contention_events`, unavailable ranges, circuit breakers (`kv.replica_circuit_breaker.slow_replication_threshold` **1m0s**), admission control. `--max-offset` **250 ms** recommended with the multi-region abstractions. Buffered writes on by default for explicit SERIALIZABLE txns.
- Key terms now known: range, replica, leaseholder, leader lease / epoch-based lease / expiration-based lease, leader-leaseholder split, write intent, transaction record (PENDING/STAGING/COMMITTED/ABORTED/absent), anchor key, parallel commits, implicitly committed, transaction status recovery, uncertainty interval, `ReadWithinUncertaintyIntervalError`, uncertainty restart, observed timestamp, timestamp cache, push, read refresh, `RETRY_SERIALIZABLE`/40001, `RETRY_WRITE_TOO_OLD`, `ABORT_REASON_NEW_LEASE_PREVENTS_TXN`, `RETRY_ASYNC_WRITE_FAILURE`, `RETRY_COMMIT_DEADLINE_EXCEEDED`, latch manager, lock table, `TxnWaitQueue`, meta1/meta2, range descriptor, hash-sharded index, closed timestamp, follower read, bounded staleness, non-blocking transaction, survival goal, `gc.ttlseconds`, causal reverse, strict serializability (re-earned)
- Lab contents: `README.md` (16 stages, 4 depth layers), `EXERCISES.md` (24 exercises, 8 rungs), `SOLUTIONS.md`, `visualization.html` — "The Reader Pays", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/2119fede-4cbd-44ca-9e82-b4583323640b> — **two lanes on one shared millisecond scale**: real time on top (which no node can see), database timestamps below, and a **dashed connector whose slant IS that node's clock error, drawn to scale with the figure on it**; the reader's window of doubt is an amber band and every version dot is coloured past/ambiguous/future relative to it, so an uncertainty restart is literally the read line jumping while the band's right edge stays nailed down. Per-node clock-offset sliders, max_offset, WAN RTT, four switches (parallel commits / observed timestamps / read refresh / node self-check), step/step-back/play/run-to-end, click-to-inspect every transaction (status, in-flight list, observed clocks, restarts), 8 predict-then-run scenarios. Engine verified headlessly before publication (`osascript -l JavaScript`, no node on this machine): **71 checks** — all eight traces value by value, the stale-read condition swept over 1,344 configurations, write skew closed at every clock setting, the anomaly predicate over 120, classification totality over ~4,600 points, determinism — plus **25 DOM checks** driving every scenario × 13 configs forward *and backward*, every dial to both extremes and every switch to both positions. That DOM pass caught two real shipped bugs (a shadowed `var el = el(...)`, and leftover `$(id)` references that would have killed every control). NOT built: real Raft, persistence, the SQL layer, the lock table, load-based splitting.
- Builds on: [google-spanner] (**the inversion — same ε, opposite payer; its `[cockroachdb?]` next-step, taken**), [hybrid-logical-clocks] (**its reader-pays row implemented; its conditional-bound warning re-demonstrated a third time**), [distributed-transactions] (**Percolator generalised** — staged locks → write intents, the appointed primary → a transaction record of its own; its "timestamp the world" branch taken without an oracle), [two-phase-commit] (**blocking deleted a third way**: the commit becomes a checkable property of replicated data), [raft]/[paxos] (one group per range, black box), [isolation-levels] (write skew closed by refreshes instead of locks), [linearizability-vs-serializability] (the exact gap CockroachDB leaves open, named), [split-brain] (the lease as a fencing token; leader leases as the structural fix), [partitioning-and-rebalancing] (range — not hash — partitioning, and the hot-spot bill it comes with), [idempotency] (the retry loop; the ambiguous commit), [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] (the starvation shape under contention), [btree-and-lsm], [consistent-hashing] (the road not taken), [cap-theorem]/[pacelc] (CP, PC/EC), [saga] (what a migration deletes) · Related: [causal-consistency], [quorums-and-replication], [change-data-capture] (protected timestamps) · Next: [calvin-deterministic-databases?] (**highest value — the third answer: fix the order first and delete distributed commit**), [jepsen?] (how "serializable" gets falsified), [clock-synchronisation?] (what `max_offset` and ε are actually made of), [query-planning-and-distsql?] (the one layer this lab did not open)

## anti-entropy — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 38-anti-entropy/

- Takeaway: Replication with W<N *creates* divergence as a normal by-product of working correctly — a write that reached 2 of 3 replicas is a success, and **nothing in the write or read path is responsible for the third**. Anti-entropy is the background comparison that fixes it, and the whole design is three layers with complementary blind spots: **hinted handoff** (a note parked on the coordinator; covers seconds to hours), **read repair** (fixes what a read notices; covers rows people read), and **scheduled Merkle repair** (compare hash trees top-down, prune matching subtrees, stream only mismatched leaf ranges; covers everything else). Measured over a simulated week on a 64-row catalogue with 8 hot rows: nothing = 16/64 divergent (3 hot, 13 cold); read repair asking only the R=2 replicas = **16/64, i.e. no improvement at all** because the stale replica is never in the read set; read repair digesting all 3 = 13/64 (0 hot, 13 cold — it fixes exactly the rows people read and nothing else); hinted handoff only = 10/64 (24 hints delivered, **35 expired past the 3h window, 10 lost when the coordinator restarted**); both layers = 8/64; **plus one Merkle pass = 0/64, streaming 8 rows where a full sync moves 192**, using 118 hashes.
- The Merkle numbers, one differing row of 16: full sync 32 rows / 6,400 bytes; Merkle at 16 leaves **18 hashes, 1 row, 976 bytes**. Search cost for a single difference is exactly `2 + 4·depth` hashes (6/10/14/18 at depths 1–4), so it is logarithmic in the data. Two honest counter-results: **when all 16 rows differ Merkle costs 8,384 bytes against full sync's 6,400** (nothing prunes, so you pay for the tree *and* stream everything — which is why a long-dead node is wiped and bootstrapped, never repaired); and **repairing two perfectly in-sync replicas still reads every row off disk** (2 hashes, 64 bytes on the wire, 32 rows scanned) — Merkle makes the *network* cost proportional to the differences, never the *disk* cost. That scan is Cassandra's validation compaction.
- **Over-streaming is the real production bill**: the unit of transfer is the leaf range, not the row. One differing row costs 1/2/4/8 rows streamed at 1/2/4/8 rows per leaf. At Cassandra's default (depth 15 = 32,768 leaves over a range of millions of rows) that is hundreds of rows moved per genuine difference. ScyllaDB's row-level repair exists to kill exactly this; Riak's AAE attacks the *other* cost by keeping hash trees updated on write so no scan is needed.
- **The zombie is the failure that matters most.** Delete-as-absence is unfixable: repair sees "A has no row, C has a row" and cannot tell "A deleted it" from "C got a write A missed", so it copies the row back — measured, the deleted item returns on every replica with no error. Tombstones fix it by making a deletion a *timestamped fact* rather than an absence. But tombstones are purged after `gc_grace_seconds`, and once purged the evidence is gone everywhere: measured with a 6h grace, repair 2h after the delete → stays deleted; repair 8h after → **resurrected on all three replicas**. This converts a storage setting into a hard operational deadline: **every replica must be repaired at least once inside `gc_grace_seconds` (Cassandra default 10 days) or deleted data comes back.** The corollary trap: shortening `gc_grace` to relieve tombstone-scan latency without shortening the repair cycle to match.
- **Anti-entropy is transport; the merge rule is semantics** — and a perfect transport with a broken merge rule converges fast and stably to nonsense. Measured with a non-join rule ("whoever received it wins"), the same three facts repaired in four orders gave 4.40 in two of them and 8.50 in the other two — all four converged. **Convergence is not correctness.** Same shape as the merge-not-a-join bug caught in [gossip-protocols]. Third way to converge on garbage: LWW plus a clock 2h fast — C's write beat a genuinely later one and every replica discarded the newer value *on arrival*, then repair spread it authoritatively.
- Also worth keeping: **you cannot detect staleness with one copy** — at R=1 the read answered the stale value and repaired nobody, so a latency-tuned `ONE` path gets zero convergence from layer 2. Trees built at different instants create phantom work (one write between the two builds → 1 row streamed to fix 0 real differences, or 8 rows at coarse leaves), so repair metrics always show non-zero streaming on a busy cluster. And **anti-entropy is the price of leaderless writes**: Kafka/Postgres followers need none of it because a leader plus an ordered log means a lagging replica is *behind*, not *divergent*.
- Key terms now known: entropy, anti-entropy, divergence (stored vs visible), read repair (blocking vs digesting all replicas; `read_repair_chance` removed in Cassandra 4.0), hinted handoff, hint, hint window (`max_hint_window_in_ms`, 3h), full sync, Merkle tree, root hash, leaf range, pruning, over-streaming, validation compaction, streaming, repair session, merge rule, join, LWW, tombstone, `gc_grace_seconds`, resurrection/zombie, bootstrap vs repair, sub-range repair, incremental repair, row-level repair (ScyllaDB), active anti-entropy (Riak AAE).
- Builds on: [quorums-and-replication] (**this lab is the mechanism that lab named and never opened** — R+W>N makes a *read* fresh, it never removes the stale copy), [eventual-consistency] (the engine behind its promise; "eventual" = "when the anti-entropy pass gets to it"), [gossip-protocols] (the merge-must-be-a-join lesson, reused as a hard prerequisite; same 1987 Demers paper), [crdts] (merge rules that cannot be wrong by construction), [hybrid-logical-clocks] (the fix for the LWW-plus-bad-clock failure), [consistent-hashing] + [partitioning-and-rebalancing] (which replicas own a range at all — abstracted away here).
- Related: [cap-theorem], [raft]/[paxos] (consensus *prevents* divergence by agreeing an order first; anti-entropy *cleans it up* after the fact) · Next: [merkle-trees?] (the structure on its own terms), [compaction?] (where tombstones actually die — this lab treats it as a button), [dynamo-paper?], [vector-clocks?]

## change-data-capture — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 52-change-data-capture/

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). The concept four prior labs queued as `Next:` ([idempotency], [eventual-consistency], [cqrs], [partitioning-and-rebalancing]), and the one that finally cashes in [transactional-outbox]'s throwaway mention of "CDC/Debezium".
- Takeaway: A query captures **data**; CDC captures **change** — and the difference is not pedantic, because a poller over `updated_at` fails five ways of which four are **silent**. The five, worth keeping individually: (1) **deletes are invisible** (a SELECT returns only rows that exist — third costume of *absence cannot be propagated*, after eventual-consistency's resurrecting delete and partitioning's F9 dirty-key delta log); (2) **intermediate states collapse** inside the poll interval, so anything reacting to a *transition* is blind and polling faster shrinks-never-closes the window (SQL Server ships this distinction as two separate products — Change Tracking = net changes, CDC = every change); (3) **the high-water mark silently skips late-committing transactions** — `now()` in Postgres is *transaction start time*, so a txn stamping `updated_at=10:00:00` and committing at 10:00:09 is invisible to the 10:00:05 poll (uncommitted) and below the mark by 10:00:10, skipped **forever**, no error; the generalization is **any column the application writes is stamped at WRITE time, rows become visible at COMMIT time, and a cursor over write-time values skips every txn whose commit lands after the cursor passed its stamp** (identical bug with a monotonic `id` cursor — sequence values are also allocated at write time); (4) ties force a choice between `>` losing rows and `>=` duplicating forever; (5) the interval is a lose-lose dial. **The key idea:** the perfect change feed already exists and is not for you — the **write-ahead log**, complete/ordered/gap-free/resumable *because the database's own crash recovery depends on it*, which is what makes CDC trustworthy rather than clever ("you are tapping a log whose correctness is already load-bearing for something the vendor cares about far more than your search index").
- **The v1→v5 mechanism ladder, each rung a repaired failure:** v1 read the WAL raw → **physical records are useless** (`rel=16384, blk=42, off=3, 61 opaque bytes` — an OID not a table name, a physical address, no column names; this is *why physical replication only works to an identical replica*) → **logical decoding** (server-side in Postgres via `pgoutput`, consulting the catalogs; `wal_level=logical` is the standing WAL-volume tax). v2 emit as decoded → **the WAL contains uncommitted and aborted transactions**, and v2 ships a rolled-back cancellation + ₹125 refund that never existed, plus statement-order interleaving → **buffer per xid, emit at COMMIT, ordered by commit LSN** (hence: huge transactions = a latency spike then a flood; PG14+ `streaming=on` deliberately re-accepts v2's problem to kill the spike). v3 stream only → **empty sink** → snapshot; and the handover has **three protocols, two wrong**: A copy-then-stream-from-now loses every write to an already-read row (= partitioning's v1 copy-then-flip *verbatim*, "protocol-design data loss, no crash needed", gap size = copy duration); B lock the table = correct and an outage (Debezium's `snapshot.locking.mode` is the fossil record); **C = repeatable-read snapshot at LSN X + stream from X**, correct *and* non-blocking because of MVCC ([isolation-levels] cashed in), joining the two pieces with a deliberate **overlap** instead of a gap — safe only because events are keyed by PK and applied as upserts. v4 → **at-least-once is the only thing on offer** (emit-then-confirm = the dual-write problem living inside the connector; confirming first converts duplicates into losses), so: key by PK (natural idempotency, free per-key ordering) + carry `source.lsn` so accumulative consumers can build the [idempotency] lab's one-transaction claim. v5 → **schema**: DDL is not in the logical stream, and MySQL binlog row events identify columns **by position**, so decoding last Tuesday's event needs last Tuesday's schema — which is what Debezium's **schema history topic** is (lose it and the connector cannot resume).
- **Failure catalogue (9), the operationally load-bearing half:** **F1 the slot fills the disk and takes down the SOURCE** — a slot is an *unconditional, non-expiring* promise to retain WAL ([distributed-locking]'s lease held by a dead holder, where the resource is disk on your primary); alarm on `pg_wal_lsn_diff(pg_current_wal_lsn(), confirmed_flush_lsn)` **and** `active=false`, set `max_slot_wal_keep_size` (PG13+) to trade resumability for uptime. **F2 the heartbeat trap — the best fact in the lab:** a *healthy, connected, consuming* connector on a **low-traffic captured table in a high-traffic database** fills the disk anyway, because it confirms its position only when it *emits*, so `confirmed_flush_lsn` freezes while `pg_current_wal_lsn` races on other tables' traffic; every connector-side metric is green and truthful; `heartbeat.interval.ms` (+ `heartbeat.action.query`) exists exactly for this. **F4 failover loses the slot** → connector silently starts from "now" with all-green metrics (PG16 = logical decoding on standby, PG17 = failover slots synced — both documentation-sourced; the alert that catches it in seconds is *"a replication slot was created"*). **F6 changes that never reach the log**: `binlog_format=STATEMENT`, **unlogged tables**, TRUNCATE (`op:t`, ignored by a c/u/d-only consumer), restores/bulk loads, DDL — so the real invariant is *every change that goes through the log*, not *every change*. **F7 `REPLICA IDENTITY DEFAULT`**: the before-image is the PK and `null` for everything else, where `null` means **"not captured", not "was null"** — silently produces plausible wrong data in audit tables and aggregate reversals; `FULL` fixes it and costs the whole old row in WAL on every update/delete. **F9 TOAST**: unchanged oversized columns aren't in the WAL record, so `after` carries `__debezium_unavailable_value` — `after` is *"the row as far as the log knows"*; general rule = **always ask whether a null means was-null, unchanged, or not-captured**. Plus F3 (phantoms — note CDC's other failures *lose* data, this one *invents* it, and reconciliation naturally looks only for what's missing), F5 (duplicates → wrong numbers, a consumer-side bug that presents as a CDC bug), F8 (per-key order survives fan-out; cross-table atomicity does not).
- **The argument the earlier labs were saving — CDC vs outbox, and it is not a tooling preference:** *CDC captures what the rows now say; the outbox captures what the business meant.* Five different business events produce the identical diff `status: 'CANCELLED'` (customer cancelled / payment declined / fraud rule / stock-out / a batch job clearing 14,000 carts), and the loyalty service must do something different for each. Second edge: **CDC on a table makes that table's schema a public API for consumers the owner does not know exist** — a routine column rename is an incident in three teams and the owner's test suite passes. So: **CDC for your own derived stores** (search, cache, read models, warehouse, a legacy DB you must keep in sync, a system whose source you cannot modify — zero application change is the decisive advantage); **outbox for business events to other teams**; **both** = the app writes an outbox row in its transaction and CDC drains it (Debezium's **outbox event router**) — the mainstream answer for a Spring shop. And the retired open question: **the outbox relay is immune to the §4 high-water skip because it tracks per-row state (`processed=false`) rather than a position** — so "optimizing" it to `WHERE id > :last_id` silently reintroduces the bug. *The high-water-mark bug is not about polling; it is about cursors over write-time values.*
- Invariants: I1 log completeness (bounded by F6), I2 **commit-order delivery** (the property no poller can have at any price), I3 per-key order **conditional** on PK-keying and one-key-one-partition, I4 at-least-once never at-most-once, I5 **convergence under replay** — which is what turns [cqrs]'s "disposable read models, rebuild the projection" into an operation you can actually perform on a Tuesday (replay a compacted topic from offset 0; no source involvement, no slot pressure). NOT guaranteed: exactly-once, global order, source atomicity, a complete before-image, a complete after-image, DDL, freshness ([cqrs]'s *consistency window*, measured here as `ts_ms − source.ts_ms`), read-your-own-writes, and **the reason for a change**.
- Production: Debezium in Kafka Connect; `wal_level=logical` + publication + slot + `pgoutput`; MySQL `binlog_format=ROW` + `binlog_row_image=FULL` + GTIDs + schema history topic; MongoDB change streams + resume token over a **capped** oplog (**the mirror of Postgres's trade — Mongo protects the database and sacrifices the consumer; Postgres protects the consumer and sacrifices the database**; know which you run); Oracle LogMiner/XStream + `SUPPLEMENTAL LOG DATA` (its F7); SQL Server CDC vs Change Tracking; DynamoDB Streams `StreamViewType` (F7 as an explicit menu, a better design than a silent default); **Cassandra as the counterexample — CDC is easy when there is one authoritative ordered log and hard when there is not**. First-deploy sequence and its #1 incident: the slot retains WAL for the *entire* single-threaded snapshot, so size `pg_wal` for that duration before pressing go — fixed by **incremental snapshots** (Netflix **DBLog** watermark approach: chunk, low watermark, read, high watermark, reconcile; runs concurrently with streaming, pausable, per-table via a **signal table**). Two lags that are different and both matter: **slot lag = availability risk to the database; consumer lag = correctness risk to the user.**
- Key terms now known: change data capture, write-ahead log / binlog / oplog / redo log (and *MySQL has two logs* — InnoDB redo ≠ binlog), LSN / GTID / SCN / resume token, logical vs physical replication, logical decoding, pgoutput / wal2json / decoderbufs, publication, replication slot, `confirmed_flush_lsn`, `max_slot_wal_keep_size`, snapshot vs streaming phase, before-image / after-image, `REPLICA IDENTITY` (DEFAULT/FULL/USING INDEX/NOTHING), connector, Debezium, event envelope (`before`/`after`/`source`/`op`/`ts_ms`), op codes c/u/d/**r**/**t**, tombstone, log compaction, transaction metadata topic, schema history topic, heartbeat + `heartbeat.action.query`, signal table, incremental snapshot, DBLog watermark, TOAST + `__debezium_unavailable_value`, `binlog_format`/`binlog_row_image`, unlogged table, supplemental logging, outbox event router, high-water mark (as an anti-pattern), reconciliation
