- Lab contents: README (16 stages, 4 depth layers, 7 prediction checkpoints, **till-roll** mental model + 4 analogy breaks — incl. *the roll shows only completed sales, the WAL does not* and *the roll says WHY, the WAL never does* — mermaid pipeline diagram, v1→v5 mechanism ladder with hand-worked traces, 9-failure catalogue, product bestiary, 40-term glossary; every number cited from a prior lab or vendor docs, nothing benchmarked). `visualization.html` ("The Drawer and the Roll", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/8014f89b-38c0-44d4-a6ac-6dd215a0dfd5>) — 4 tabs, 13 predict-then-run scenarios: **Tab 1** runs a poller and a log reader over the *same* writes with two search indexes side by side and a live "changes the poller never saw" counter (the `all` scenario produces exactly 5 losses / 3 wrong rows / 0 errors), poll-interval slider, upsert-vs-running-total consumer switch; **Tab 2** steps the decoder record-by-record with visible per-xid buffers, v1/v2 and DEFAULT/FULL switches; **Tab 3** races the three cold-start protocols with a second-by-second timeline and an append-instead-of-upsert toggle that corrupts the *correct* protocol; **Tab 4** is the slot as a disk gauge with connector stop/start, captured-table share, heartbeat and `max_slot_wal_keep_size`. Verified headlessly in JavaScriptCore (**147 engine + 212 UI = 359 checks**, incl. reducer purity, determinism, step-back-replay equality, no-missing-element-ids, 94 render sweeps, and every scenario × every prediction answer). EXERCISES/SOLUTIONS: 8 rungs; rung 5 = an 8-event snapshot/stream/crash hand trace whose point is that the txn which *started* first has the *highest* commit position; rung 6 plants the 30-second-lag "safe" poller, the outbox relay "optimized" to an id cursor, the two-statement warehouse loader (+ the false claim that an LSN is unique per event — one transaction's 40 row changes share one), the audit log on `REPLICA IDENTITY DEFAULT`, and the cross-team column rename; rung 7 = failover / an undersized `pg_wal` / a re-snapshot you cannot afford; rung 8 = the four-consumer pipeline, an 18-month bidirectional strangler (loop prevention via replication origins, authority partitioning instead of LWW, and a reconciliation that must first prove it compared anything at all), and the paragraph you post when someone proposes CDC topics as a cross-team event API.
- Builds on: [transactional-outbox] (**its "CDC/Debezium" aside made a lab, and its relay finally explained — safe because it claims rows, not positions**), [acid] + [btree-and-lsm] (WAL-before-ack *is* the key idea), [idempotency] (at-least-once + keyed upserts + the one-transaction claim, applied verbatim), [partitioning-and-rebalancing] (**snapshot + delta + cutover is literally the cold-start protocol, and protocol A is its v1 copy-then-flip**; alarm-on-backlog-never-on-activity; sealing/epoch parallels), [isolation-levels] (MVCC repeatable-read is what makes protocol C non-blocking), [cqrs] (consistency window given a measurement; rebuildable projections given a mechanism), [event-sourcing] (states-vs-changes asymmetry; and the arrow reversed — CDC derives events from rows, event sourcing derives rows from events), [eventual-consistency] (every sink is a stale replica; tombstones; the machinery's death produces no event → probe), [distributed-locking] (the slot as a lease nothing revokes), [quorums-and-replication] (a CDC consumer is a logical replica that is not a database), [anti-entropy] (**the scheduled source↔sink reconciliation that catches every silent-loss mode IS anti-entropy pointed across two systems instead of across replicas of one** — same job, same cost problem, so borrow Merkle-per-key-range rather than diffing 4 TB; its zombie is the *fourth* appearance of absence-cannot-be-propagated, alongside F-a here) · Related: [saga], [causal-consistency], [work-pressure-and-deadlines] (ρ/(1−ρ) on the drain) · Next: [kafka?] (**now the most-referenced unlearned concept across eleven labs — this lab assumed its partitions, keys, compaction, tombstones and consumer groups without teaching any of them**), [streaming-materialized-views?], [strangler-fig-migration?], [schema-evolution?]

## google-spanner — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 66-google-spanner/

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). The capstone that composes eight earlier labs: [paxos]/[raft] under every shard, [two-phase-commit] over them, [distributed-transactions]' atomic-visibility problem, [hybrid-logical-clocks]' measured ε bill, [linearizability-vs-serializability]' exact guarantee, [isolation-levels]' MVCC, [partitioning-and-rebalancing]' hotspots, [split-brain]' leases. Deliberately teaches **one** new idea and lets the rest snap onto it.
- Takeaway: Spanner did not build a more accurate clock — it built a clock that **admits how wrong it might be** (`TT.now()` returns an interval `[earliest, latest]` guaranteed to contain the true instant; ε = half its width), and a protocol that **pays that admission off by sleeping**: stamp with `TT.now().latest`, then refuse to acknowledge until `TT.after(s)`. Two rules close external consistency for news that travelled by *any* path, including a telephone call the database cannot see. ε appears **nowhere in the correctness argument and everywhere in the bill** — which is why GPS receivers and atomic clocks in every datacenter are a *latency* purchase, not an accuracy one.
- **The trace that carries the lab** (reproduced exactly by the simulator): Northgate ε=7, Southgate ε=1, **both clocks perfectly accurate (offset 0)**. T1 arrives 100.000 → s₁=**107**, acked **102**; client phones; T2 arrives 104.000 → s₂=**105**. A transaction that began *after* the first was acknowledged carries a **smaller** stamp, and a snapshot read at 105 or 106 (exactly two integer values out of a 45 ms window — hence untestable) reports a mortgage on an unsold property. The error comes from the **asymmetry of the two admissions**, not from any clock being wrong. With commit wait: ack moves 102 → **114** (12 ms of a machine doing literally nothing), s₂=117, verdict holds. Derived inversion condition: **g < ε₁ − ε₂ − P** — only the *difference* in admissions matters, and making Paxos faster makes the bug *more* likely.
- **The v1→break→refine chain** (every odd detail is the scar of a watched failure): stamp-with-latest → *reader on a lagging replica returns a stale-but-plausible answer* → **t_safe** = min(t_safe_paxos = highest applied write, t_safe_TM = min(prepare ts) − 1) → *one prepared 2PC freezes the whole group's readable horizon, unrelated keys included* → **fine-grained safe time in the lock table** → *new leader stamps below the old leader's s_max* → **10 s leader leases + disjointness invariant + wait for TT.after(s_max) before abdicating** (= [split-brain]'s fencing argument applied to time; needs no partition — a GC pause suffices) → *idle group's t_safe never advances* → **MinNextTS, advanced every 8 s**, so idle groups can serve reads up to 8 s old → **LastTS()** as the single-group shortcut that never waits.
- Paper figures worth keeping: ε is a **sawtooth 1→7 ms, avg ~4 ms** (30 s poll interval × 200 µs/s drift = 6 ms, +1 ms network to the time master). Commit wait ≈ **5 ms measured** (write **9.4 → 14.4 ms**, 1 replica, commit wait off→on), less than 2ε̄ because it overlaps Paxos. Reads: RO txn **1.4 ms**, snapshot read **1.3 ms**, and snapshot-read throughput **13.5 → 38.5 → 50.0 K ops/s** at 1/3/5 replicas while **write throughput falls 4.1 → 2.2 → 2.8** — the whole point of charging the writer. 2PC mean latency by participants: **17.0 (1) · 24.5 (2) · 42.7 (50) · 71.4 (100) · 150.5 (200) ms** → keep transactions in one group; that is why interleaved tables exist. Leader-zone kill **without** handoff: throughput ≈ 0, recovers over **~10 s** (lease length); **with** handoff: **3–4%**. Marzullo's algorithm rejects lying masters; machines exceeding the drift spec **evict themselves**; the justification for trusting it is empirical — *bad CPUs are 6× more likely than bad clocks*.
- **The honest boundary, second sighting.** The guarantee is *conditional on hardware*: put the true offset outside ε and commit wait waits for the wrong number — external consistency dies **with commit wait switched on**, no error, no log line (simulator scenario 4). Same shape as [hybrid-logical-clocks]' measured infection (one +60 ms node dragged a ±8 cluster's bound 16 → 67). Corollary the lab insists on: **alert on ε as a correctness signal, not a performance one** — a latency incident with flat CPU/disk/network and raised ε is a *time* incident.
- **The four-row table that is the real takeaway** (all four now traced or measured across labs): same ε, different payer — **Spanner** = writer (commit wait; reads lock-free, local, retry-free; needs the hardware) · **CockroachDB** = reader (HLC + uncertainty restarts; ε ≈ 500 ms on commodity clocks) · **TiDB/Percolator** = network (one global oracle round trip; no clock assumptions) · **MongoDB** = client (signed clusterTime; nothing for strangers).
- Production: universe → zone → spanserver (100–1000 tablets, + lock table and transaction manager where leader) → tablet on **Colossus** → Paxos group; **directory** = contiguous key prefix = the unit of data movement, relocated live by the **placement driver** (~50 MB in a few seconds). Cloud Spanner: regional = 3 RW replicas/3 zones, **99.99%**; multi-region adds **witness** (votes, no data, no reads) and **read-only** (data + reads, no vote — read capacity without enlarging the write quorum), **99.999%**; **writes always go to the leader first**, so leader-region placement is the main latency lever; CPU targets **65% regional / 45% multi-region**. Timestamp bounds: strong / exact staleness / bounded staleness (**≥10 s to be worth asking for**); version retention **1 h default, 1 week max**, older reads fail `FAILED_PRECONDITION`. **Hot split is the #1 real incident**: monotonic first key → one leader → fixes are UUIDv4, `sequence_kind='bit_reversed_positive'`, key reordering, or a hashed ShardId. Read-write transactions are pessimistic with **wound-wait**, so aborts are normal and the client retry loop is part of the contract — the transaction body must be re-runnable ([idempotency] discipline imposed by the database). CP by [cap-theorem], PC/EC by [pacelc]; Brewer's "effectively CA" rests on the 5-nines SLA, not on the theorem.
- Key terms now known: TrueTime, TTinterval/TTstamp, TT.now/after/before, ε (instantaneous error bound), time master / Armageddon master / time-slave daemon, Marzullo's algorithm, commit wait, external-consistency invariant, monotonicity invariant, disjointness invariant, leader lease, s_max, prepare timestamp, safe time / t_safe_paxos / t_safe_TM, false conflict, fine-grained safe time, MinNextTS, LastTS(), read-write vs read-only transaction vs snapshot read, scope expression, wound-wait, universe / zone / spanserver / tablet / directory / placement driver / Colossus, split, load-based splitting, interleaved table, bit-reversed sequence, witness replica, read-only replica, leader region, version retention period, schema-change transaction at a future timestamp
- Lab contents: `README.md` (16 stages, 4 depth layers), `EXERCISES.md` (8 rungs), `SOLUTIONS.md`, `visualization.html` — "The Honest Clock", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/10030776-7351-463a-b99a-0209680dc341> — a **true-time axis on which every transaction is drawn twice**: a bar for when it really happened and a diamond for the timestamp it was given, so an inversion is literally visible as crossed diamonds. Commit-wait toggle, per-region ε and true-offset sliders (the offset slider is the hardware-honesty experiment), a draggable "analytics read as of" timestamp, replication lag, Paxos latency, read-routing and MinNextTS-cadence selectors, step/step-back/play/run-to-end, click-to-inspect every region, group and transaction (live t_safe components, s_max, LastTS), 7 predict-then-run scenarios, and a verdict banner that re-checks the external-consistency invariant over all pairs after every step. Engine verified headlessly before publication (`osascript -l JavaScript`): **45 checks** — all seven scenario traces to the millisecond, plus property sweeps (140 ε-pairings → 0 violations with commit wait on; commit wait off → violations in 16/20; TrueTime honesty ⇔ containment over 820 cases; 0 breaches of the safe-time contract; run-to-run determinism) — and **32 further checks** driving the DOM and every control to its extremes. NOT built: real Paxos, persistence, wound-wait aborts, the lock table, query engine, movedir, version GC, schema changes.
- Builds on: [distributed-transactions] (**this is the answer to its open question** — its "timestamp the world" branch, taken; its Percolator alternative now contrasted row by row), [hybrid-logical-clocks] (**its writer-pays row, cashed in**; its conditional-bound warning re-demonstrated), [two-phase-commit] (**its blocking problem solved a second, different way** — replicate the coordinator's decision through Paxos rather than relocating the commit point into data), [paxos]/[raft] (black box under every group), [linearizability-vs-serializability] (external consistency = strict serializability), [isolation-levels] (MVCC, snapshots), [split-brain] (leases as fencing, applied to time), [partitioning-and-rebalancing] (splits, hot ranges), [pacelc]/[cap-theorem] (PC/EC), [idempotency] (the retry loop) · Related: [causal-consistency], [crdts] (the coordination-free opposite corner), [quorums-and-replication] · Next: [cockroachdb] (**done 2026-08-30 — the same guarantee with the bill sent to readers**), [calvin-deterministic-databases?] (order first, delete 2PC), [jepsen?] (how "strictly serializable" gets falsified), [clock-synchronisation?] (NTP/PTP/GPS — what ε is actually made of)

## zab — learned 2026-08-30 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-09 — lab: 41-zab/

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). Opens the box four earlier labs named and never explained: [cap-theorem] listed ZooKeeper as "Raft/ZAB consensus", [split-brain] said "ZAB, and `zxid` is the epoch" and stopped, [distributed-locking] used `zxid` as a free fencing token, [paxos] called it "a Paxos-family protocol with a stronger leader".
- Takeaway: ZAB is the atomic-broadcast engine inside Apache ZooKeeper, and the thing to know about it is that **it is not a replicated state machine protocol — it is primary-backup.** Raft replicates the *command* and every server computes the answer; ZAB replicates the *answer* one server already computed. Because each transaction is a result computed on top of earlier results, ordering the messages is not enough — **the primaries themselves must be ordered**, which is the property (Primary Integrity) that plain Paxos does not give and that ZAB was designed for.
- **The sentence to be able to say:** *"ZAB guarantees one leader" is false; "at most one leader per epoch, and only a leader a majority has joined can commit" is true* — same shape as the [raft] lab's correction. Second sentence worth keeping: **a new primary's first act is to deliver its entire inherited history before writing anything of its own** — and *that NEWLEADER round is Raft's Figure-8 no-op, made explicit, mandatory and moved to the front.* ZAB must do it eagerly because the very next transaction is computed from that state.
- The four rules, each the scar of one failure the lab makes you watch (all four are toggles in the visualization, each verified to break in 12/12 runs): (1) **elect the maximum zxid, not merely a sufficiently up-to-date candidate** — this is what lets synchronisation be strictly one-directional (a ZAB primary pushes and never pulls); flip it to "lowest id wins" and the shorter history is pushed onto everyone, hatching committed changes out of the whole cluster. (2) **the NEWLEADER round** — skip it and the new primary computes from a stale delivered state and issues the same ticket number twice. (3) **TRUNC** — refuse to delete and one server disagrees with everyone forever, though the damage stays invisible until the *next* write lands at a different position. (4) **the epoch check** — accept an old-epoch proposal and a stale change is inserted *underneath* an already-final one, so a delivered change becomes un-delivered.
- **zxid = one 64-bit number, epoch in the top 32 bits, counter in the bottom 32**, counter restarting at 1 each epoch (so the successor of `1.3` is `2.1`, never `1.4`). Raft keeps term and index as two fields; ZAB welds them, so one comparison answers both "whose is this" and "where does it sit". **acceptedEpoch vs currentEpoch** are two separate on-disk files and the distinction is load-bearing: "the newest chapter I have agreed to join" vs "the chapter my data belongs to" — they differ exactly when a leader died mid-recovery, and merging them lets a restarted server be talked into following a superseded primary.
- Four phases: **0 leader election** (Fast Leader Election — broadcast your opinion, adopt better ones, converge on the max; `finalizeWait` is the settling period), **1 discovery** (`newEpoch = max(acceptedEpoch)+1`), **2 synchronisation** (DIFF / TRUNC / SNAP per follower, then NEWLEADER), **3 broadcast** (PROPOSE → quorum ACK → COMMIT — *two-phase commit with the abort branch deleted and the decision moved from one coordinator to any majority*, which is the direct answer to what [two-phase-commit] said to come here for). The paper lets phase 1 adopt a better history; the implementation cannot need to, because FLE already picked the max.
- **The FIFO channel does real work.** Leader↔follower is one TCP connection, so a gap in proposals cannot mean "a message was lost" — it means the connection broke, and the follower must go back to LOOKING and re-synchronise. This is why ZAB has no per-message repair machinery, and it is an assumption you would break re-implementing it over UDP. (Found by soak-testing the lab's own simulator: at 30% loss a follower was accepting proposals with gaps until this rule was added.)
- What ZooKeeper does NOT give you, each stated in the lab: **linearizable reads** — reads are answered from the local server's memory with no coordination and can be stale, by design; `sync()` (a ReadIndex-style quorum round, not a lease) is the opt-in fix, and this is the single most common ZooKeeper surprise in production. No write scale-out (whole tree in memory on every server, every write through one leader — *coordination services store the pointer, not the payload*). No Byzantine tolerance. No progress below quorum. A request with no answer may still have committed.
- Operational spine: the inequality **GC pause + fsync latency + network RTT < syncLimit × tickTime** — almost every "ZooKeeper is unstable" incident is this violated from the left, and the usual culprit is disk or GC, not the network. Leader elections per unit time is the one alarm. `dataLogDir` on its own physical disk. Odd voting members; 2 servers is worse than 1. Fuzzy snapshots are safe *only* because transactions are results and therefore idempotent. zxid counter rollover makes the leader abdicate, causing an unexplained write blip.
- Key terms now known: transaction (a *result*, not a command), zxid, epoch, counter, acceptedEpoch vs currentEpoch, history, lastZxid, PROPOSE/ACK/COMMIT, accept vs deliver, quorum (consensus flavour), leader/primary, follower, observer, LOOKING/FOLLOWING/LEADING, atomic broadcast, primary-backup vs replicated state machine, DIFF/TRUNC/SNAP, Fast Leader Election, finalizeWait, NEWLEADER round, UPTODATE, primary order (local/global) + primary integrity, znode, ephemeral node, session, watch, tickTime/initLimit/syncLimit, fuzzy snapshot, sync(), jute.maxbuffer, forceSync, snapCount, mntr/zk_synced_followers/zk_outstanding_requests.
- Lab contents: `README.md` (16 sections, four depth layers, hand-worked handover trace with real zxids), `visualization.html` (published artifact, **"The Epoch Ledger"** — servers as rows, zxid positions as columns, so a disagreement is two things in one column and a handover is a colour change; dashed = written down, solid = delivered, hatched = TRUNC'd; **9 predict-then-run scenarios**, step-backward, kill/revive, partition, latency and loss dials, five rule toggles), `EXERCISES.md` (8 rungs), `SOLUTIONS.md`. Simulator verified headlessly: all 9 scenarios reach their intended outcome 12/12; **no invariant breaks in 30% message loss, repeated primary crash+restart, or repeated 2|3 partition+heal** — every safety failure requires switching a rule off, which is the safety-vs-liveness point made executable.
- Builds on: [raft] (**the whole lab is pitched against it** — the side-by-side table is the payoff; ZAB's NEWLEADER round *is* Raft's Figure-8 no-op), [paxos] (ZAB is the *other* answer to that lab's closing line, arrived at three years earlier for a different requirement: Paxos lets a new leader fill an earlier gap with a *different* value, ZAB never substitutes), [quorums-and-replication] (the overlap rule is what makes "the winner cannot be missing it" arithmetic rather than luck), [two-phase-commit] (ZAB's broadcast round is 2PC minus abort), [split-brain] (epoch = the fencing token; two leaders are normal and harmless), [distributed-locking] (the `czxid` fencing token, and why the ephemeral node vanishing is not enough), [idempotency] (why transactions are results, and why fuzzy snapshots work), [pacelc] (ZooKeeper is PC/EC — consistent when partitioned, latency-optimised when not, which is exactly why reads are local)
- Related: [linearizability] (writes yes, reads no) · Next: [zookeeper-recipes?], [viewstamped-replication?] (its "view" is ZAB's epoch), [kraft?] (Kafka replacing ZAB-backed ZooKeeper with Raft — the same job, decided differently), [flp-impossibility?]

## split-brain — learned 2026-08-30 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-09 — lab: 42-split-brain/

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). The capstone that finally opens the box four earlier labs left closed: [cap-theorem] listed "split-brain" as a key term and never explained it, [raft] measured two leaders coexisting routinely, [distributed-locking] measured `overlapTicks > 0` on every run, [redis] watched 500 acked writes vanish at failover.
- Takeaway: Split brain is two nodes both believing they hold an exclusive role and acting on it, and the goal everyone reaches for — *"at most one server believes it is in charge"* — is **impossible**, because belief is a variable in a machine's own memory that survives being unplugged, frozen or unreachable, and the machines you would need to correct are exactly the ones you cannot reach. The achievable goal replaces belief with effect: **at most one authority's actions may ever take effect on the shared resource, and once a newer authority has acted, nothing older may act again.** You cannot stop a machine believing; you can stop its writes counting.
- **The two numbers that must never be confused, and the lab's whole visual thesis:** *servers that believe they are in charge* (cheap, routine, rises constantly in correct systems) vs *writes the database accepted from a stale authority* (the damage). A good design lets the first reach 2 and holds the second at 0.
- **The four-word disambiguation people collapse into one:** **partition** = the network broke (a cause) · **split brain** = two minds (a state of belief) · **divergence** = two histories, both acknowledged (the damage) · **data loss** = someone discarded a branch (a later, usually manual event). All four are independent — partition with no split brain (a good cluster), split brain with no divergence (fencing working), divergence with no partition (a frozen process).
- **Measured, 5 servers 3|2, 400 ticks, write every 5 ticks** (the spine of the lab, v1→v5 each broken before the next appears): heartbeat-only + cut → **2 leaders, 148 accepted, 68 DAMAGED**. Add majority quorum with the incumbent on the MAJORITY side → 1 leader, 79 accepted, **0 damaged** (minority's epoch climbs to 8, never wins). **The surprise, and the lab's best result:** same quorum, incumbent stranded on the MINORITY side → **2 leaders, 147 accepted, 68 DAMAGED — identical to no defence at all.** Because *a quorum rule governs WINNING an election and says nothing about KEEPING the job; not receiving a message is not a resignation letter.* Add a lease → 1 leader, 68 accepted, 0 damaged. Add fencing instead → **2 leaders, 79 accepted, 68 REFUSED, 0 damaged.**
- **The headline comparison, on the freeze test (70-tick freeze, 40-tick limit, perfectly healthy network):** lease = 1 leader / 118 accepted / 0 damaged; fencing = **2 leaders / 133 accepted / 2 refused / 0 damaged**. Fencing serves MORE writes than the lease AND leaves split brain in place AND has zero damage — because the lease buys safety by making everyone wait, fencing buys it by letting everyone proceed and refusing the stale ones. **Safety by arithmetic, liveness by timeout, never the reverse** (same sentence [distributed-locking] reached): the token check has zero timing assumptions and is correct under every schedule; the timeout only decides how fast you recover, not whether you are correct.
- **Split brain does not need a network fault.** The freeze test cuts no cable: a GC pause / VM suspend / disk stall stops the leader executing, everyone else correctly elects a replacement, and the leader wakes with `role=leader, epoch=1` because *nothing tells a resumed process that time has passed*. Damage = 2. And be honest about the mechanism: it is a **race** between the zombie's write and the new leader's heartbeat (won by 7 ticks here) — which is the point, since a defence that works most of the time cannot be reasoned about.
- **The generalisation that makes the whole subject click: a quorum protects the VOTERS. Anything that is not a voter gets no protection from consensus at all unless it independently refuses stale work.** Measured: identical fencing setup, resource unable to check tokens → refusals 2→**0**, damage 0→**2**. All protection gone. The token was correct, was sent, and was ignored — *fencing is a property of the receiver, not the sender*. Hence payment gateways / SMTP / raw block devices / dumb APIs need idempotency keys ([idempotency]), self-fencing watchdogs, or STONITH — never a lock alone.
- **Two things are both called fencing** and this trips people between communities: **fencing token** (distributed systems — the resource refuses stale work, nothing dies) vs **fencing/STONITH** (HA clusters — physically power off the suspected-dead machine). You need the second only when the first is impossible, which is why Pacemaker people insist on STONITH first: their shared resource is usually a raw disk that cannot check anything.
- Failure modes each created/measured: even 2|2 split + lease → **0 leaders, 9 writes in 400 ticks, 0 damaged** (4 healthy machines, total outage — hence **always an odd number of voters across an odd number of failure domains**; 4 tolerates the same 1 failure as 3, 6 the same 2 as 5); witness in a third site + real site outage → rescued (1 leader, 0 damaged) **but placement is the whole trick — a witness inside one main site is not a tiebreaker, it is a rule that that site always wins**; **non-transitive partition** (A↮B, both↔C — the shape nobody draws) → epochs climb to 6 vs 1 healthy, writes drop 158→42, **0 damaged**: safety perfect, liveness destroyed, nothing reports itself broken — *this is what Raft's pre-vote exists for*; one-way cut → a node that cannot receive is invisible to itself and disruptive to everyone; **fence race/death match** (both sides STONITH each other → total outage, or reboot ping-pong; mitigated by quorum-gating the fence and a randomised delay — same trick as Raft's randomised timeouts); **the operator override** (force quorum on the minority while the majority lives → 2 independent clusters, both internally correct, on one database — no protocol defends against a human removing the protocol); **healing** (belief heals in milliseconds via one message carrying a higher epoch — *no timer involved* — while the divergent data stays forever: MongoDB's rollback files).
- Key terms now known: split brain, partition (as cause), failure detector (as a fallible component), heartbeat, epoch (= term = generation = zxid = mod_revision = sequencer = incarnation = fencing token — **one object, seven names, now unified across five labs**), quorum-governs-election-not-incumbency, incumbent, lease (and its two failure halves: clock bet + must be executing to notice), fencing token, effect gate, STONITH, divergence, zombie/stale leader, witness/arbiter/tiebreaker (+ placement rule), non-transitive partition, pre-vote, fence race, rollback file, gray failure, break-glass override, at-most-one-effect-lineage
- Production mapping: etcd `mod_revision` Txn = a fencing token you already have (+ pre-vote); ZK `zxid` + ephemeral sequential, with the caveat that **the ephemeral node vanishing is not fencing**; **Elasticsearch pre-7.0 `discovery.zen.minimum_master_nodes` is the canonical cautionary tale** — a safety parameter a human had to compute, got wrong often enough that 7.0 replaced the discovery layer and DELETED the setting; Kafka leader epoch (KIP-101) + `unclean.leader.election.enable=false` + producer epoch for zombie fencing; K8s `coordination.k8s.io` Lease = a lease and **not** fencing for your controller's external side effects; Patroni gets it right with a hardware **watchdog** = self-fencing for a resource (a PG data dir) that cannot check tokens; Pacemaker `no-quorum-policy` / `two_node` + `wait_for_all` / `pcmk_delay_max`; MongoDB arbiters + rollback files (w:majority default since 5.0); Redis Sentinel async replication = 500 acked writes lost, `min-replicas-to-write` narrows and cannot close. Monitoring ranked: **leader elections per minute** first, then epoch-over-time (a ramp = non-transitive partition), then fenced-write rejections (each one a prevented disaster), then GC pause vs detector limit. GitHub Oct 2018 (43 s of partition → 24 h+ of reconciliation) as the incident — *flagged in the doc as recalled, not verified*.
- Lab contents: `README.md` (16 stages, 4 depth layers with checkpoints, 2 prediction checkpoints, the v1→v5 breaks-then-refine spine, two hand-worked traces, railway single-line-token mental model + 3 ways it breaks, 25-term glossary), `visualization.html` = **"Two Signal Boxes"** (<https://claude.ai/code/artifact/0bb7695b-3ce1-4465-b700-56b9e26eec0a>) — 2/3/4/5-node + witness clusters in two named cities, **the four defences as INDEPENDENT switches not a ladder** (deliberate: you cannot see what one is worth until you can turn the others off), a "database can check tokens" switch for the box-G case, 4 cut shapes incl. one-way and non-transitive, per-node crash/freeze, client→server routing as a control, the operator-override button, step-back, click-to-inspect, a write ledger where each square is accepted/refused/damaged, and **11 predict-then-run scenarios**; EXERCISES + SOLUTIONS (8 rungs; rung 6 = 5 described flaws incl. Redis-SETNX-with-precheck, in-memory epoch counter, write-only fencing, six-nodes-two-DCs, two-node STONITH; rung 7 includes a full break-glass runbook; rung 8 = payments control plane across 3 regions, the two-node retail edge where the answer is **change the frame to AP + disjoint id space**, and a 10-question review checklist for a home-grown election).
- Verified: the page's simulation engine is exercised by a **56-check headless harness** (JavaScriptCore via `osascript -l JavaScript`, stub DOM) covering every scenario's stated outcome, the link model, healing, and the measurements quoted above. It caught three real bugs during the build: a crash delivering writes to the resource, a client that could not follow leadership, and the strict-ladder defence design that made fencing impossible to demonstrate in isolation.
- Builds on: [raft] (**this lab is the direct sequel to its headline sentence** — "Raft guarantees one leader" is false, "one leader per term, and only the newest can commit" is true; also pre-vote, randomised timeouts as the fence-race mitigation, and the `broadcastTime ≪ electionTimeout ≪ MTBF` inequality), [distributed-locking] (**the same conclusion from the other side** — its at-most-one-effect-lineage, its safety-by-arithmetic sentence, its write-only-fencing bug reused as exercise 6.3, its in-memory-token bug as 6.2, its box G as rung 7.1), [quorums-and-replication] (the overlap rule doing the election work, plus the new fact that overlap says nothing about incumbency), [gossip-protocols] (the failure detector as a fallible component — its detection dial and its detector-meltdown result are quoted directly; indirect probes as the one-way-partition fix), [cap-theorem] (**this is the term that lab named and never opened**), [pacelc] (the PA/EC corner = accept on both sides then discard at heal = rollback files), [redis] (its 500-lost-writes and its 190× KEYS stall reused as the freeze-source argument), [idempotency] (**the answer for every resource that cannot check a token**), [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] (a fencing check IS a conditional write), [crdts] (the retail-edge answer in rung 8.2: design so merging is trivial and you never need to decide), [transactional-outbox] (a timeout is not a failure) · Related: [linearizability], [two-phase-commit] · Next: [flp-impossibility] (why none of this can be perfect), [failure-detectors?] (phi-accrual and SWIM in depth — the black box this lab assumed), [clock-synchronization?] (what a lease actually bets on), [zookeeper?], [kafka?]

## acid — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 07-acid/

- Takeaway: **ACID is a contract, not a mechanism, and its four letters come from three different places.** A and D are the same machine — a write-ahead log written before the change, plus a three-pass recovery — I is a separate dial you already own ([isolation-levels]), and **C is not a database mechanism at all**: it is your definition of "valid", and the only part the engine will defend is the part you *declared* as a constraint. The lab derives ARIES rather than reciting it: five versions, each broken by one specific failure. Deliberately lopsided — it skips I entirely and spends its depth on the log, recovery, and the durability ladder.
- **The v1→v5 derivation, each refinement a scar:** redo-only + no-steal works until the buffer pool is full of *uncommitted* pages → allow **steal** → need **before-images/undo**. The 2×2 (steal × force) shows why every real engine sits in the steal + no-force corner needing BOTH undo and redo: no-steal caps a transaction at RAM size; force turns commit into scattered random page writes instead of **one sequential append + one fsync**. Then: recovery scanning from the beginning → **checkpoint**; redo applied twice → **pageLSN on the page** (`apply only if L.LSN > pageLSN` — the same "stored marker lets a repeated operation recognise itself" trick as [idempotency] keys and [event-sourcing] expected-version, third context); undo needing two possible starting states → **repeating history** (redo the losers too, so undo always starts from the pre-crash state and shares ONE code path with ordinary runtime rollback); crash during rollback → **CLRs + undoNext**; interrupted page write → **full-page writes / doublewrite**; one fsync per commit → **group commit**.
- **The worked trace to keep** (verified against the lab's own simulator): log #100 CKPT · #101 T1 BEGIN · #102 p5 700→200 · #103 p9 300→800 · #104 T1 COMMIT · #105 T2 BEGIN · #106 p9 800→700 · CRASH; disk p5=₹700 stamp#101, p9=₹700 stamp#106 (page 9 was *stolen* to disk carrying an uncommitted change), total ₹1400. Analysis → losers {T2}, redo from #102. **Redo leaves the total at ₹900 — wrong on purpose**, because it reproduces the crash instant faithfully. Undo reverts p9 via before-image, writes CLR #107 (undoNext #105), then END. Final 200/800 = ₹1000. Corollary from the rung-5 variant: **a balanced total is not evidence of correctness** — one trace ends redo at exactly ₹1000 with a loser's changes fully present.
- **Why CLRs, stated precisely** (the common explanation is wrong): with *physical* before-images undo IS idempotent, so "we'd double-undo" is not the reason. CLRs buy (a) correctness for *logical/physiological* undo ("give back 100" twice gives back 200), (b) **termination** — a crash-looping rollback of 1M rows resumes from `undoNext` and the remaining work only shrinks, instead of restarting, and (c) a CLR is **never itself undone**. Verified in the simulator: crash mid-undo, recover twice, each update undone exactly once (2 CLRs, not 3).
- **C is the odd letter out, and this is the lab's most useful hour.** "Valid by whose definition?" — yours. The only bridge to the engine is *declared constraints* (`CHECK`, `UNIQUE`, `FK`), and they protect **the table** — including the migration script and the 2 a.m. psql session — whereas app-layer validation protects only the code paths that remember to ask. Directly extends the [isolation-levels] finding that a constraint beats an isolation level whenever the rule is about the data rather than the decision. Also: **ACID's C ≠ CAP's C** (data obeys its rules vs linearizability); when someone says "we need consistency", make them say which one.
- **Durability is always durability *against something*** — a ladder, each rung surviving strictly more: log buffer (nothing) → `write()`/OS page cache (process crash) → `fsync` (power loss on this machine) → redundant local storage (one disk) → **quorum of replicas** (machine + AZ) → remote region. Every rung above fsync costs a network round trip on **every write**, so durability is a PACELC "else" branch, not just a partition-time choice. "We use an ACID database so we can't lose data" is a category error; [redis]'s measured 500 acked / 500 lost across a failover is what the wrong rung looks like.
- Production mapping: **PostgreSQL** WAL + `synchronous_commit` (note `off` risks *losing committed transactions* while `fsync=off` risks *corruption* — different bets) + `full_page_writes` + `commit_delay`; **InnoDB** redo log + a **separate undo tablespace that also serves MVCC reads** (so `History list length` bloat is the same bill as Postgres VACUUM) + doublewrite buffer + `innodb_flush_log_at_trx_commit` 1/2/0 + `sync_binlog`; **SQLite's rollback journal is the OTHER corner of the 2×2** — undo-only with force — and "WAL mode" is the name for moving it to the redo corner; **Oracle** redo+undo/SCN. **MySQL runs an internal 2PC between InnoDB's redo log and the server's binlog on every commit** — coordinator, prepare, commit point, in-doubt-on-recovery, all of [two-phase-commit]'s nouns inside one process on one machine.
- On-call translation table worth keeping: slow startup after a crash = the **redo** pass (checkpoint lag — so *less* frequent checkpoints make it worse); up-but-unusable with one process pinned = the **undo** pass rolling back a huge aborted transaction (wait it out); commit latency doubling with no code change = fsync got slower; WAL volume exploding = full-page writes after checkpoints **or** a stuck replication slot; `invalid page header` = a torn page surfacing at read time, long after the crash.
- Key terms now known: transaction/commit/abort/rollback, page, buffer pool, dirty page, write-ahead log, log record, before-image/after-image, LSN, prevLSN, OS page cache, fsync, the three moments of "written", redo, undo, recovery, checkpoint, steal/no-steal, force/no-force, pageLSN, analysis/redo/undo passes, repeating history, winner/loser, CLR, undoNext, torn page, full-page writes, doublewrite buffer, group commit, ARIES, failure domain, declared constraint vs application invariant, deferrable constraint (by name)
- Lab contents (no runnable code — doc + visualization + exercises): README with 4 depth layers, 4 prediction checkpoints, day-book/ledger + engineering mental models with a 4-item "where the analogy breaks", the v1→v5 derivation, 11 failure scenarios, a four-letter correctness audit, and a 35-term glossary. `visualization.html` = **"Vikram's Morning"** (<https://claude.ai/code/artifact/584af82b-f8f6-4e22-8580-5d955149d4cf>), a crash-and-recover simulator with a pure state+reducer engine, step-back, 6 policy switches, process-kill vs power-cut, click-to-inspect on every page and log line, and 6 predict-then-run scenarios. Verified headlessly in JavaScriptCore: **30 behavioural assertions pass**, plus a DOM-shim render test driving all 6 scenarios. EXERCISES + SOLUTIONS, 8 rungs (rung 5's hand trace was checked against the engine and matches exactly).
- Bugs found by writing the tests, worth remembering: the crash step was wiping a queued recovery; scenarios leaked policy switches into each other; and **the README's own arithmetic was wrong** (₹700+₹300 with ₹500 moved leaves ₹500, not the ₹800 first written) — the falsifying run caught all three.
- NOT covered: log segmentation/archiving/PITR, physical vs logical logging detail, nested top actions for index splits, parallel redo, MVCC internals, replication, backup verification.
- Builds on: [btree-and-lsm] (pages, "WAL-before-ack" and torn write were already vocabulary; this is where the log is taken apart), [redis] (buffer → `write()` → `fsync` re-used as the lab's central distinction; its kill -9 vs power-loss measurement is quoted rather than re-run), [isolation-levels] (the I, deliberately not retaught; its constraint-beats-isolation-level line becomes this lab's §12.2), [two-phase-commit] (MySQL's internal binlog/redo 2PC) · Related: [idempotency] (the lost ack — ACID gives a correct database and says nothing about whether the caller knows it), [distributed-transactions] (atomic durability ≠ atomic visibility), [pacelc] (every durability rung above fsync is an "else"-branch round trip), [cap-theorem]+[linearizability] (ACID's C vs CAP's C) · Next: [wal-replication-and-pitr?], [mvcc-and-vacuum?], [change-data-capture?], [kafka?]

## pacelc — learned 2026-08-30 — lab: 25-pacelc/ (extended 2026-08-30, second and third pass)

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). Cashes in the cap-theorem entry's `Next: [pacelc?]` — the 2×2 that places the whole consistency ladder.
- Takeaway: CAP only governs the rare moment the network breaks; the tradeoff that governs the other 99.99% is **latency vs consistency on a healthy network**, and PACELC (Abadi 2010/2012, IEEE Computer) names both: **if Partition, Availability vs Consistency; Else, Latency vs Consistency.** The else-clause in one sentence: **someone waits — the writer, the reader, or nobody; and if nobody waits, somebody gets stale** (the §4 hand trace: async 3-region design, ack at 1 ms, Virginia reads a 39 ms-stale price with zero failures anywhere). The four classes are not philosophies but shadows of one machine's ack policy: local-ack → PA/EL (Dynamo/Cassandra/Riak); majority write + majority read → PC/EC (Spanner/CockroachDB/VoltDB); per-record home master with local reads → PC/EL (PNUTS: timeline consistency — replicas lag but never fork; lose the master, lose writes only); wait-for-all with a timeout **degraded ack** → PA/EC (classic MongoDB: pays the worst everyday bill, then discards partition-era acked writes at heal — rollback files). Latency bills computed from a real RTT matrix (BOM–FRA 110 / FRA–IAD 90 / BOM–IAD 190 ms) and cited from prior labs, no new experiments: ABD write-back 11.7→24.4 ticks, commit wait 6.7→24.8, DynamoDB strong read = 2×, 3-of-3 vs 2-of-3 tail 27.31% vs 2.85%.
- The two anchors and the unification: CAP is a proven theorem (Gilbert & Lynch; C = linearizability) but PACELC's else-half is a **taxonomy anchored by a lower bound** — Attiya & Welch 1994: linearizable reads and writes cannot both beat a constant fraction of message-delay uncertainty (the speed of light is in the contract). Deepest point (F7, made live in the viz): **a node's only partition detector is a timeout, so "partition" = latency above a configured threshold — unavailability is unbounded latency, and CAP's clause is just PACELC's else-clause priced at infinity.** Drag the RTT slider past the 1000 ms timeout and the PC design starts refusing with no partition set. Also earned: EL/EC is a **per-operation dial**, not a system property (Cassandra per-query levels, DynamoDB ConsistentRead, Cosmos's five contracted levels) — "is this DB EL or EC?" is the wrong question; the else-C is really the whole ladder (lin → bounded staleness → causal (the PA column's ceiling, 600/600) → session → EC). Classifications are dated defaults of a version+config, not destinies.
- Signature failures, each a predict-then-run scenario: EL = stale-on-healthy-network (S1, staleBy 39 ms, self-healing window); EC = the bill arrives (S2, 1 ms → 110 ms); PC = loud scoped minority refusal (S3 — and the refused write was still applied locally: "failed ≠ undone", quorums lab re-worn); PA = silent execution at heal (S4: both sides acked, LWW keeps one, "acked writes lost" counter is the only witness); PC/EL = reads live stale + writes die when home is dark, but never a fork (S5); PA/EC = degraded acks then heal-time discard — the worst of S4 after paying for S2 (S6).
- **Second pass (§11–§12, F7–F9, viz S7–S9): the middle of the dial and the price as a design variable.** The EL/EC binary hides where most production systems live, and two mechanisms fill the gap. **v5 session guarantees** (Terry et al. 1994): the client carries a token of the newest version it saved *or was shown*, and a replica behind that token must catch up (a **parked read**) before answering that client — cost is one lag-wait, paid once, by the only person who needed it (45 ms in S8) versus EC's 90–110 ms for everybody forever. Load-bearing insight, visible on screen as two counters moving independently: **session guarantees are consistency with one client's own history, never with real time** — S8 ends with "your own change vanished" at 0 while stale reads climb. Formally, **causal consistency implies all four session guarantees**; a client's own history is the branch-free special case, which is exactly why one token replaces a dependency graph. **v6 bounded staleness**: a replica cannot know it is lagging (silence carries no information), so the promise must come from the write path as a **closed timestamp** beacon; hand trace (beacon interval 100 ms, one-way 95 ms) gives a provable ≤295 ms answer at ~1 ms, and the cost rule that decides most designs — **the moment the freshness requirement exceeds the one-way delay, coordination becomes free** (§11's five-row cost table: local read / session token / closed-timestamp snapshot / coordinate / quorum+write-back).
- **§12 latency arithmetic:** an EC operation is `client→coordinator + coordinator→quorum→coordinator + back`, and only the middle term is the **consensus tax** — Omar writing a Mumbai-led record pays 95+110+95 = 300 ms, a Virginia-led one 90 ms (3.3× decided by placement, invisible in every profiler). Majority ack = the ⌈(N−1)/2⌉-th nearest peer (3 regions → 110 ms; 5 regions → 130 ms, worse tail). Placement patterns priced: tight quorum (0.1× slider ⇒ **12 ms** majority write — "EC is slow" is largely a claim about your map, paid for with a shared failure domain), geo-partitioned leaders, witness replicas, non-voting replicas + follower reads, Cassandra `LOCAL_QUORUM`. Physics floor: fibre ≈200 km/ms ⇒ Mumbai–Virginia ~65 ms one way minimum (real 95).
- **New failures (renumbered so file order is F1–F10; the slow-partition one is now F10):** **F7** own-change-vanished — write acked at replica A, next read served by replica B; *server-side completely invisible*, found only by a canary that writes then reads through the read path; the Java-shop face is a routing DataSource / `@Transactional(readOnly=true)` replica read inside the same request, or a CQRS projection. **F8** bounded staleness's cliff — past the bound the honest moves are refuse (an EL design acquires PC behaviour with no partition) or fall back to the leader (stampeding the component already behind); Azure documents throttling *writers* instead; mitigations are the caching lab's single-flight/jitter/shed. **F9** the hidden cross-region hop — leadership migrates after a failover and every write silently pays one extra RTT: bimodal histogram with peaks exactly one RTT apart, errors flat; dashboard is **leaders per region**.
- **§14 additions:** the two-axis picture (EL↔EC measures staleness against the clock for everybody; session guarantees measure consistency with a history for one client — they are not points on that line), and **per-path classification** — read path and write path get their own letters, so "majority writes + local reads" is EC on writes and EL on reads (exercise 6's planted flaw, ZooKeeper's documented gap). **§15 additions:** the dial already in the user's own stack — Postgres `synchronous_commit` (off→local→remote_write→on→remote_apply) as the literal EL→EC ladder, `synchronous_standby_names ANY 1(...)` as the quorum ack, LSN-wait on a replica read as a hand-built session token, MySQL `WAIT_FOR_EXECUTED_GTID_SET` (whose `timeout` argument *is* F8's cliff), Kafka `acks`/`min.insync.replicas`; plus monitoring per mechanism (session canary, lag-as-%-of-Δ headroom alarmed at 80%, leaders-per-region).
- **Third pass (§13–§14, F10–F12, viz Tab 2 + S10–S13): the two things a per-operation price hides — capacity, and whether the price was necessary at all.** Sections renumbered (old §13–§18 → §15–§20; the slow-partition failure F10 → **F13**). **§13 the load axis**, closing the first pass's own "load-dependent queueing" omission using [queueing-theory] whole: every bill in the lab until now was *one operation on an idle machine*, and **`L = λW` turns a latency change into a capacity change** — 2,000 reads/s at 2 ms = 4 in flight, at 90 ms = **180** (45×), so a pool of 50 goes from a 25,000 ops/s ceiling to **556** and the flip to strong reads is not "slower reads" but **ρ = 3.6** (queueing F2: throughput pinned at capacity looking healthy while the backlog climbs linearly forever). Sizing rule `c ≥ λW/ρ_target` = 225 connections; and at ρ=0.8 the EC path delivers ~450 ms, not 90 (5.1× measured) — **RTT is the floor of the EC bill, never the bill.** Contention adds a per-record ceiling nothing can buy off (~9 conflicting writes/s at a 110 ms majority; flagged as model arithmetic, not measured), corroborated by [google-spanner]'s measured curve (snapshot reads 13.5→38.5→50.0 K ops/s while writes *fall* 4.1→2.2→2.8 at 1/3/5 replicas; 2PC 17.0→150.5 ms at 1→200 participants) and [cockroachdb]'s push-and-retry, where contention **discards completed work** (40001) and the *second-in-real-time* transaction starves. Ends on the asymmetry worth keeping: **EL degrades, EC collapses** — timeouts → retries → queueing F3's metastable loop (37,115 queued at 20 min; removing the trigger does not restore service), which EL cannot have because nothing waits.
- **§14 coordination avoidance — the question that comes *before* PACELC's two.** Meera stops selling a price and starts selling **ten necklaces**: staleness now breaks an **invariant**, and the sharpened rule is **a stale read that *informs* can be repaired by a later read; one that *authorizes an irreversible act* cannot be repaired at all** (five necklaces are in vans). The everyone-jumps-to conclusion "invariants ⇒ coordinate" is half right: **v7 escrow** (O'Neil 1986) divides the ten into per-branch allowances 4/3/3, each branch enforcing a purely local rule whose conjunction *implies* the global one — 1 ms sales, zero messages, keeps selling through a partition, and the eleventh sale is **arithmetically unavailable**. So: **a PA/EL system that never violates the invariant** — and the category error to be loud about is that **PACELC classifies replicas, not invariants** (a PC/EC system oversells fine via an unguarded read-modify-write — [linearizability]'s lost increments). The general rule: **I-confluence** (Bailis et al., VLDB 2014) — coordination-free iff merging independently-valid states is always valid (set-append/increment-only/per-row CHECK/FK-insert yes; uniqueness, `SUM ≥ 0` with decrements, "at most one active" no) — added as the **third anchor** beside Attiya–Welch: *one bounds the price of coordination, the other bounds the need for it*. Escrow reframed precisely: it does **not** make `SUM ≥ 0` I-confluent, it **replaces** it with a stronger local invariant that implies it ⇒ **divide the constraint, don't weaken it — quantities divide, identities do not** ("no way to give Mumbai 40% of the username asha"); CALM stated with an honesty flag (coordination-free iff monotonic; negation = a claim about absence, which never travels — the tombstone rule again). Costs named: **stranding** (4/3/3 vs demand 6/2/2 ⇒ 7 sold vs 8, refusals *on a healthy network with stock on a shelf*), fixes in order (split by demand → background rebalance → coordinate only at zero allowance → central reserve), and **transfer must be fenced** (epoch/lease — [distributed-locking] + [partitioning-and-rebalancing], now with physical goods attached). Closes with **the three questions, in order**: informs or authorizes? divisible? — only then pay §12's bill and size it by §13. Already-built-it links: rate-limiting's **lease/batch + stranded quota** (coordination *per key per node per interval* vs *per request*; measured 1:20 vs 1:1 in exercise 4.7), Hibernate **HiLo** `allocationSize`, seat pools.
- New failures: **F10** the consistency change that ate the capacity (every component behaving exactly as specified while the service is down; the alarming fact is *flat throughput*; fix = treat consistency as a resize, bulkhead the EC path, retry budget), **F11** the oversell that reconciliation cannot repair (the merge is *perfect* and the rule still broke; distinguishes it from F4's lost write), **F12** stranded allowance (a refusal indistinguishable from a genuine sell-out unless you count *refusals while global stock > 0*; plus the rebalancer-racing-a-slow-region double-sell). Monitoring added: in-flight vs pool, queue-time vs service-time split, goodput; refusals-with-stock, allowance headroom, an oversell counter alarmed as a **correctness** signal.
- **Viz Tab 2 — "The Last Ten Necklaces"** (same artifact/page, tabbed): a second pure engine — ten necklaces, three branches, three designs (sell-now-tell-later / head-office approval / split-the-stock with a 4-3-3 or 6-2-2 division), per-branch sell buttons, cut control, **hand-driven allowance transfer** (deliberately not automated, so the fencing problem stays visible; blocked across a cut, and in-flight allowance belongs to *nobody* — always leave a gap, never an overlap), counters **sold / sold-that-don't-exist / turned away / turned away while necklaces remained / ms spent waiting for permission**. Scenarios **S10–S13** on identical demand (6 Mumbai + 5 Virginia, Virginia cut): **11 sold with 1 oversold and a perfectly converged merge** · **6 sold, 5 refused, 0 oversold, 110 ms each** (Frankfurt 220, Virginia 300 — §12's decomposition charged to a customer at a counter) · **7 sold, 4 turned away with 3 unsold** · **8 sold, 3 turned away** — the constant across the last three being **oversold = 0**, i.e. the division trades utilization, never correctness. Also verified: the local design **oversells with no partition at all** (95 ms one-way vs customers every 20 ms). Re-validated headlessly in JavaScriptCore: **38 new engine checks** (including a 16-config sweep proving no coordinated or divided configuration ever oversells, genuine-sellout vs stranded-refusal separation, transfer fencing, held-message re-travel, determinism) + **50 UI checks** driving the real UI against a DOM shim (every control, every scenario × every answer) + a 4-check spot re-verification of the Tab-1 engine.
- Exercises extended: 1.5/1.6 (informs vs authorizes; "we need strong consistency for inventory" is not a finished argument), 2.5/2.6 (where the rule is enforced in each design — the fast one has *nowhere*; who owns allowance in flight), 3.6/3.7 (predict all four scenarios' counters, then the no-partition oversell), 4.7 (coordination ratio 1:20 vs 1:1), **4.8** (the capacity change nobody wrote down — full `λW`/`c/W`/ρ/pool-size arithmetic), 5.3 (escrow ledger hand-trace with the dishonest "sold out" line), **6.3** (planted: "CRDT counters can't oversell" + a 30-second-quiet auto-rebalancer — convergence ≠ invariant, and a timeout is not a death certificate), **7.3** (the 09:14 incident: why flat throughput is the worst news, why rollback doesn't recover, what does), **8.4** (promotional stock via blocks + the never-under-sell inversion; per-customer spend limits routed to a home region instead of divided — divide along the axis demand partitions on, or own it in one place).
- Key terms now known: PACELC, else clause/baseline mode, EL, EC (else-consistency — acronym collision with eventual consistency called out), PA/PC (as clause answers), the four classes, staleness window (≈ one-way delay for async), ack policy, degraded ack, per-record master, timeline consistency, per-operation dial, recency judge (read must not return a value older than the newest write acked before the read began), Attiya–Welch bound, the unification (partition = far end of the latency dial; timeout defines P), session guarantee, session token, read-your-writes, monotonic reads/writes, writes-follow-reads, parked read, closed timestamp, follower / stale-at-timestamp read, consensus tax, quorum arithmetic, tight quorum, geo-partitioned leaders, witness replica, non-voting replica, leaseholder placement, RTT floor, per-path classification, Little's Law `L = λW`, in-flight count, throughput ceiling `c/W`, bulkhead, metastable failure, contended-record ceiling, invariant (vs consistency), informing vs authorizing read, escrow/reservation, allowance, stranded allowance, I-confluence, CALM, divide-the-constraint, fenced handover of an allowance
- Lab contents: README (20 stages, 4 depth layers, 7 prediction checkpoints, Meera's-two-branch-shop / ack-policy mental models + 4 analogy breaks, mermaid quadrant + decision flowchart, 39-term glossary). `visualization.html` ("The Price of Now", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/89e61b7c-028e-45f2-8ed7-ee3a2e8793c6>): three-city SVG stage with crawling messages and real RTT labels, 4 designs (one per class, plain-word names), per-city clients, isolate-a-region / move-home / RTT-scale controls, live recency judge with full-sentence verdicts, PACELC meter, casualty counter (an acked write is lost iff the final winner never saw it — causal test via per-replica applied-sets), step-back via snapshots, 6 predict-then-run scenarios. Second pass added a **fifth design** (session tokens), **roaming reads** (send any client's reads through another city — the load-balancer decision that causes F7), two violation counters (your own change vanished / price went backwards), ⏳ parked-read markers, scenarios **S7–S9** (RYW break → session fix → monotonic-reads break), and an RTT slider down to 0.1×; re-verified headlessly (**52 engine checks + 14 UI-load checks + all 9 scenario predicates**, JavaScriptCore). Engine is a pure reducer verified headlessly in JavaScriptCore (**47 checks**: all six scenario outcomes incl. exact latencies 1/90/110/190/220 ms, F7 slow-network refusal, blocked-message re-travel at heal, determinism, sequential-overwrite-is-not-a-casualty) + UI load path smoke-run against a DOM stub. EXERCISES/SOLUTIONS: 8 rungs, extended in the second pass with 1.4/2.4/3.4/3.5/4.4/4.5/4.6/5.2 (beacon trace + max-age formula)/6.2 ("session tokens ⇒ consistent" planted, broken by a fraud-check run)/7.2 (metastable fallback stampede)/8.2 (placement + data residency)/8.3 (checkout page, four freshness requirements) — rung 5 hand-trace of a majority write+quorum-read interleaving; rung 6 plants "majority writes + local reads = consistent" (ZooKeeper's documented gap); rung 7 redesigns the PA/EC degrade path with outbox+idempotency-keys+no-LWW (accepted ≠ finalized); rung 8 = 3-region payments platform, flow-by-flow, EC paid only at the ledger write via the per-operation dial + home-region account mastering.
- Builds on: [cap-theorem] (**the direct sequel** — its P-clause becomes half the story), [linearizability] (C = recency, its real-time rule is the sim's judge; ABD bill reused), [quorums-and-replication] (R/W dial = the per-request PACELC selector; acks=all trap; LWW 1000/1000 as PA's casualty rule; tail numbers), [eventual-consistency] (EL's contract given its own lab; F7 display-vs-decide rule closes rung 8; async SQL replica = PA/EL in costume), [causal-consistency] (ceiling of the PA column, 600/600), [distributed-transactions] (commit wait = EC's bill at txn scope), [distributed-locking] (slow-vs-dead undecidability ⇒ the timeout defines P), [crdts]/[idempotency]/[transactional-outbox]/[saga] (rung 7's honest PA degrade path) · Related: [hybrid-logical-clocks] (the sim's one-perfect-clock luxury priced), [linearizability-vs-serializability] · Next: [kafka?] (acks/min.insync.replicas = this ack-policy ladder as config), [jepsen?], [coordination-avoidance?] (§14 given its own lab: I-confluence, CALM, RAMP). Since learned and now wired in: [partitioning-and-rebalancing], [queueing-theory] (§13's whole load axis), [google-spanner] + [cockroachdb] (PC/EC shipped twice, bill sent to opposite ends), [flp-impossibility] (why the partition detector can only be a timeout).

## partitioning-and-rebalancing — learned 2026-08-30 (extended same day, second pass) — lab: 36-partitioning-and-rebalancing/

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). Cashes in the consistent-hashing lab's open box: "the ring is a pure placement function and moves nothing — joining a node is a separate throttled streaming operation with its own state machine." This lab IS that state machine, plus everything placement alone can't answer.
- Takeaway: Splitting data forces three questions replication never asked — where does each record live, how do queries find it, what happens when the machine set changes — and the key idea answering all three is: **cut the data into many more pieces than machines (by key order for range scans, by hash for balance) and make the piece, never the record, the unit of ownership and movement**. Then rebalancing decomposes into reassigning whole pieces (minimal-movement table edits: 16 partitions 3→4 nodes = exactly 1/4 of keys, all onto the newcomer, zero sideways — monotonicity by arithmetic instead of ring geometry) plus a per-piece **handover protocol**. The two-hop picture `key → partition → node` organizes everything: hop 1 is about queries and skew, hop 2 about balance and movement; mashing them into one hop is `mod n` (99%-moved corpse, cited from consistent-hashing).
- **The migration state machine (the genuinely new deep content):** v1 copy-then-flip silently shreds every acknowledged write landing behind the copy cursor (no crash needed — protocol-design data loss); v2 = snapshot + **delta log** + catch-up drain + brief write-pause cutover, whose invariant is *every write made the snapshot, the delta, or arrived post-flip* — and whose liveness condition is drain rate > write rate (a queue; throttle tuning, not protocol); v3 adds an **ownership epoch** per map entry because a GC-paused old owner wakes believing it still owns the partition and accepts writes (split-brain) — the distributed-locking lab's stale-holder + fencing-token mechanism verbatim, with "lock" renamed "ownership" (production: Kafka leader epochs, Raft terms, HBase ZK+WAL fencing). Skew taxonomy: **data skew** (uneven bytes — fixable by boundary moves/splits) vs **load skew** (uneven requests — a single hot key defeats every placement scheme, since one-key-one-home is the load-bearing agreement; fix the key: salting write-random-read-ALL, or cache). **Sequential keys** (timestamps/auto-increment) + range partitioning = all writes on the last partition forever, auto-split chases but never catches the frontier; fix is key design — compound key (hashed partition component + range-ordered clustering component, Cassandra's split) or bucketing.
- Rest of the mechanism: fixed P (`hash mod P`, safe because P never changes — Redis 16384 recognized as a standard answer, not an oddity; P is a forever decision, Elasticsearch's regret; Kafka increase-only breaks key→partition history) vs dynamic split/merge at median (HBase ~10 GB, CockroachDB ~512 MB, incl. load-based splits; pre-split or bulk loads hammer one node) vs vnodes (recalled). Routing: ask-any-node / routing tier / partition-aware client; stale maps are NORMAL — design goal is wrongness-costs-one-hop via **MOVED** (permanent, repair your cache) vs **ASK** (mid-migration, don't); the authoritative map is small must-agree state = where consensus re-enters (ZK/etcd/PD/meta-ranges; Cassandra's gossiped map is the exception quorum-overlap forgives). Secondary indexes: **local** (cheap co-located writes, scatter-gather reads) vs **global/term-partitioned** (one-hop reads, multi-partition or async writes — DynamoDB GSI lag); scatter's compounding cost = **tail latency amplification**: per-partition 1% p99 tails → 0.99^100 ≈ 36.6% all-fast, so ~63% of 100-way scatter queries eat the tail.
- Failure catalogue: celebrity key (F1), sequential frontier (F2), stale map with dead old owner (F3 — no redirect possible; map authority joins the data path; single-flight + jittered backoff against the herd), naive-handover loss (F4), **rebalance storm** (F5 — auto-rebalancer × failure detector = gossip lab's detector meltdown *with a payload*: movement→load→silence→verdicts→movement; hence throttles, sustained-evidence thresholds, route-around-before-move, human gates — DDIA's "don't fully automate"; Cassandra separates down from removenode, ES delayed_timeout), dual ownership (F6). Invariants: complete coverage no overlap; one authoritative owner per epoch; handover completeness; routing safety (stale = slow, never wrong). NOT guaranteed: perfect balance (statistical), cross-partition atomicity (distributed-transactions' bill), global-index freshness, availability during cutover (ms per partition done right).
- Production frame worth keeping: every real partition is itself a replication group, so **rebalancing moves replicas, and leader transfer is zero-data-motion rebalancing** (cheapest move there is); quorums-lab machinery lives inside each partition, this lab's decides where replicas live. Bestiary table in README (Kafka/Cassandra/HBase/CockroachDB/TiDB/Redis/ES/MongoDB/DynamoDB); Kafka consumer-group rebalance = same concept applied to *consumption* (eager stop-the-world → cooperative sticky KIP-429); DynamoDB adaptive capacity = load-based splitting as a service. Dashboards: size + heat histograms (busiest:median ratio), migration ETA trend + throttle utilization, MOVED/epoch-rejection rates (rejections = the fence working), movement-per-hour vs verdicts-per-hour on one graph (F5 tripwire).
- **Second pass added the operational-lifetime layer** — the three things the first pass listed as omissions and then owed. (1) **10.6 "two things the migration protocol still gets wrong"**: v3 was verified against *writes only*, so (a) **reads are unfenced** — the deposed owner keeps a complete frozen copy on disk and a stale client reading it gets a plausible answer with no error that grows staler forever; fix is **sealing** (refuse everything for the partition, answer MOVED — Redis MOVED-for-unowned-slot, Kafka fetch leader-epoch rejection, HBase close-region-before-reassign), stated as *fencing writes protects the future, fencing reads protects the truth of the present*; and (b) **deletes resurrect** if the delta log records *dirty keys* rather than *operations* — draining "what is asha now?" returns nothing, which reads as "no update", so the snapshot's copy survives cutover; absence cannot be shipped (eventual-consistency/CRDT tombstone rule inherited by migration, which is short-lived replication), and deltas need explicit tombstones plus per-key order. Diagnosis that generalizes: *a protocol is only correct with respect to the operations it was checked against.* (2) **10.8 changing P** — the arithmetic the first pass owed after calling P "a forever decision": `h mod 2P ∈ {h mod P, h mod P + P}`, so **doubling splits every partition locally in two and moves ZERO bytes** (measured Tab 3, 420 keys: 48.8% re-addressed / **0%** crossed the network) — this IS Elasticsearch `_split`'s multiple requirement and its hard-linked-segments implementation; `_shrink` is the mirror (factor). Non-multiple 16→24 = **68.8% re-addressed / 68.8% crossed** (`lcm(16,24)=48`, no parent-child relation to exploit). Escape hatch = **hash-RANGE partitioning** (each partition owns a slice of hash space, P appears nowhere in the rule): split any partition any afternoon — measured 6% re-addressed / 0% network, P 16→17, *not a multiple*. So the real table is: mod-P = doublable-and-otherwise-frozen; hash-range = revisable forever at the cost of a boundary list; and Redis's 16384 is reframed as *the* escape (pick P so large it never needs revisiting). Sizing method: target partition size (512 MB–10 GB) → horizon volume / that → round to a power of two → floor-check against max node count, ceiling-check against per-partition overhead × P and scatter fan-out. Kafka's increase-only moves **zero bytes but breaks per-key history** — free in bandwidth, expensive in semantics; ES `_split` is the opposite trade. **Non-obvious catch (exercise 5.3, hand-verified): "doubling is free" needs the arithmetic AND a placer that keeps the child where the parent was** — re-deal 16 partitions by `p mod 3` and 4 of 8 keys cross after all; hence real APIs are deliberately "split in place, rebalance later" = the two hops again. (3) **10.9 placement constraints** — balance demoted from objective to tie-breaker: rack/zone diversity (the consistent-hashing lab's *failure domain* finally given a job: 3 copies in 1 rack = 1 copy plus 2 spares sharing a power supply), data residency (placement driven by partition *content*, not load), latency locality, heterogeneous weights. A constrained placer with no legal solution **does not compromise — it stops moving**, and that is indistinguishable from an idle healthy cluster; this is literally what ES YELLOW/UNASSIGNED is, and why `_cluster/allocation/explain` exists. Hard-constraint vs soft-preference is the distinction to get right in both directions (hard-when-should-be-soft = outage for 100 ms; soft-when-should-be-hard = silent residency violation). Real names: NetworkTopologyStrategy, ES shard allocation awareness, CockroachDB zone configs, MongoDB zone sharding, Kafka rack-aware assignment, k8s topology spread.
- New failures **F7 unsatisfiable constraint** (rebalancer goes quiet; alarm on under-replicated partitions, NEVER on rebalancing activity — the dangerous state has no activity), **F8 stale read from the deposed owner** (write fence without read fence), **F9 resurrected delete** (durability of an acknowledged *delete* = compliance incident, found by an auditor not a customer). Invariant **5 — rules before balance** added; invariant 3 corrected to "every acknowledged **operation**, deletes included"; invariant 4 corrected to hold *only if the epoch check guards reads*. All three new failures present as **silence**, which is the framing the engineering checkpoint now uses.
- Key terms now known: partition/shard/region/chunk, partition key, range vs hash partitioning, data skew vs load skew, hot partition, rebalancing, partition map/routing table, scatter/gather, dynamic splitting, pre-splitting, sequential-key hotspot, compound key (partition + clustering key), salting/bucketing, fixed partitions, routing tier, partition-aware client, MOVED/ASK, migration (snapshot/catch-up/cutover), delta log, ownership epoch, split-brain on ownership, rebalance storm, local vs global secondary index, tail latency amplification, jumbo partition, leader epoch, consumer group rebalance (eager vs cooperative), adaptive capacity, tombstone, sealing a partition, read fencing, hash-range partitioning, partition doubling, `_split`/`_shrink`, failure domain (now with a job), placement constraint, hard constraint vs soft preference, data residency, shard allocation awareness, under-replicated/UNASSIGNED
- Lab contents: README (16 stages, 4 depth layers, 4 prediction checkpoints, library-on-wheels / map-table-protocol mental models + 4 analogy breaks, 2-hop mermaid + migration state diagram, 33-term glossary; all mod-n numbers cited from consistent-hashing, no re-runs). `visualization.html` ("The Moving Shelves", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/745d4c50-6214-4989-95d9-b90f3a6fb356>) — **now 4 tabs, 12 scenarios, 42 headless checks** (second pass added Tab 3 "The Recount": mod-P vs hash-range, doubling / non-multiple / split / add-node / rebalance, with the two counters *keys re-addressed* vs *keys crossing the network* deliberately separated so the two hops are visibly independent; Tab 4 "The Rules": nodes with zone+rack, RF 2/3, rack-diversity and EU-residency toggles, greedy placer reporting UNASSIGNED with a named reason; scenario 7 "the read that lies" via a new seal-reads tick-box on Tab 2; scenarios 8–11 on Tab 3, 12 on Tab 4. Validation harness now boots the real UI against a DOM shim and clicks every control and every scenario × every answer). Tab 1 = buildings/shelves map with 4 schemes × 3 workloads (names/dates/celebrity) × add-remove buildings, moved-vs-minimal counts, live data+heat balance and range-scan cost; Tab 2 = p11 migrating B→D in slow motion with per-record versions, delta log, protocol switch v1/v2/v3, mid-copy write button, GC-pause, stale-write and stale-client probes; 6 predict-then-run scenarios; deterministic, step-back via snapshots. Engine verified headlessly (JavaScriptCore, **34 checks**: moved fractions, zero-sideways, frontier-in-new-last-range, heat survival, naive-loss/catchup-save/fence-reject/unfenced-loss, MOVED repair, reducer purity, determinism). EXERCISES/SOLUTIONS: 8 rungs; rung 5 = 8-event migration hand trace; rung 6 plants check-then-act cutover, in-memory still_owner flag, read-one-salt, and the 3-second auto-drainer; rung 7 = D-crash-mid-catchup + dead-owner routing loop; rung 8 = payments-ledger design (co-locate balance+entries, refuse query (c) via CDC copy, whale handling, idempotent posts before rebalancing anything).
- Builds on: [consistent-hashing] (**the direct sequel** — its open box closed; ring/vnodes/fixed-slots/monotonicity/hot-key/disagreement-window all reused as known vocabulary, its mod-n and 9.5%-window numbers cited), [distributed-locking] (ownership epoch = fencing token, stale holder = paused owner, at-most-one-effect-lineage phrasing reused), [quorums-and-replication] (replication inside each partition; lag-is-a-queue → delta-drain convergence; tail-latency shape), [gossip-protocols] (map spread, detector meltdown → F5's payload), [raft] (the map is exactly the small must-agree state consensus was built for), [distributed-transactions] (cross-partition writes priced there; co-location as the dodge), [eventual-consistency] (async global indexes), [idempotency] (retries across MOVED/epoch-refusal must be harmless before you rebalance money), [caching]/[http-caching] (single-flight + jitter for the map herd), [distributed-rate-limiting] (sub-key splitting = salting), [btree-and-lsm] (sortedness within partitions), [database-landscape] (partition key / hot partition, now with mechanism) · Related: [linearizability-vs-serializability] (composition asymmetry is why partitioned linearizable stores scale), [work-pressure-and-deadlines] (ρ/(1−ρ) on the drain race) · Next: [kafka?] (most-suggested across ten labs, every prerequisite now in place), [change-data-capture?] (the delta log as a product), [geospatial-indexing?]

## idempotency — learned 2026-08-29 — lab: 49-idempotency/

- Takeaway: Retries are mandatory (silence carries no information about what the other side did), so every reliable system generates duplicates — and a duplicate is indistinguishable from a new request by content alone; only the client knows two sends are one intention. The fix has three parts, each of which fails specifically when done naively: the client **mints an idempotency key** before the first send and reuses it verbatim (mint at screen-render, persist with the pending intention — a fresh-key-per-retry client defeats everything and the server can't tell); the server makes **claiming the key atomic with the effect** — a dedup check-then-act dies in its own gap, so the claim is an INSERT under a UNIQUE constraint (the **seventh costume** of the conditional-write primitive), in the SAME transaction as the effect when the effect is local (this single-transaction shape — a processed_messages table in the consumer's txn — IS the honest "idempotent consumer" eight prior labs demanded, and Kafka-offset-in-your-DB is it verbatim); and the server **stores and replays the response** — a dedup that answers "duplicate!" leaves the retrying caller as ignorant as before.
- **The crux and the central insight:** an external call cannot live inside the transaction (ROLLBACK cannot unsend a network request — crash after bank-OK before COMMIT erases the claim but not the money → double charge; the dual-write problem one hop down). Fix = outbox's move applied here: **claim commits first, alone** (durable RUNNING evidence), effect second, outcome txn third; concurrent duplicate on RUNNING → 409 (waiting = the lunch problem on the request path); crashed keys re-driven by a reaper — safe only because the re-driven bank call carries the same downstream key and the bank **replays**. Hence: **idempotency is a per-hop contract that recurses** — each hop's retry-doubt is absorbed by the next hop's key table, dying only at a hop that can claim-and-act atomically; an unkeyable bottom hop (email, dumb API) is distributed-locking's box G, closed with at-most-once-or-at-least-once per operation + reconciliation + a measured residual. Multi-step effects add recovery points per step (= Stripe's published architecture, Brandur Leach's write-up ≈ this lab's v4 exactly).
- Boundaries, each one sentence: exactly-once **delivery** impossible (two generals); **exactly-once processing** = at-least-once + idempotent processing (what honest vendors mean); replay is a snapshot of the outcome, never current state (refund doesn't change it — the stale-read-as-non-event lesson again); different keys are different intentions **by definition** (cross-key business duplicates get only an advisory heuristic layer); idempotency handles duplicates only — reordering/interleaving of different intentions still needs versioning (expected-version/HLC) and concurrency control, three orthogonal defenses that stack. **Fingerprint** (hash of body stored at claim) catches key-reuse-with-different-body → 422, never execute — same key + new content is a client bug, and a rising 422 rate is a client-regression detector. **Fencing vs idempotency**: refuses stale writers vs duplicate writers; orthogonal; both conditional writes at the resource.
