- Failure catalogue worth keeping: keys-in-Redis + effect-in-Postgres = dual-write rebuilt at the point of its own solution, plus restart-from-empty forgets every claim (quorums' rebuilt-empty-replica trap in a cache costume) — **the key row lives in the same database as the effect**, Redis dedup only as untrusted accelerator; success-only key rows die on ambiguous failure (a bank timeout is not "didn't happen" — must stay RUNNING for the reaper, terminal failures settle as replayable FAILED); retention window (Stripe 24h, SQS FIFO **5 min**) is the guarantee's explicit boundary — pick ≫ retry horizon. **Oldest-RUNNING age is the page-worthy alarm**; replay/409/fingerprint-mismatch rates are dashboards; "duplicate charges with a healthy key table" ⇒ check the two charges' keys first — differing keys = client bug (failure 8).
- Production: Stripe `Idempotency-Key` (24h, 409 in-use, 422 mismatch, `Idempotent-Replayed`); IETF draft-ietf-httpapi-idempotency-key-header; RFC 9110 GET/PUT/DELETE idempotent-by-contract → infrastructure auto-retries them, so never hide a non-idempotent effect behind one (and `PUT /orders/{client-id}` = key-as-resource-name, the lightweight creation alternative); Kafka idempotent producer = PID + per-partition seq, **producer→broker hop only** (google-docs' per-client edit number at scale), EOS/read_committed = Percolator-shaped, consumer side still yours; DynamoDB conditional PutItem = the claim without SQL.
- Key terms now known: idempotency key, natural idempotency, claim/settled/RUNNING, request fingerprint, response replay, dedup vs idempotent processing, dedup window/retention, recovery point, re-drive, reaper (idempotency form), 409/422 contract, exactly-once processing vs delivery, effectively-once, per-hop recursion, key-as-resource-name, idempotent producer, MessageDeduplicationId, reconciliation residual
- Lab contents (no-code format): README (4 depth layers, 5 prediction checkpoints, teller-with-numbered-forms mental model + 4 analogy breaks, key-lifecycle state diagram, v1→v4 mechanism ladder with hand-worked crash traces, 8-failure catalogue, 24-term glossary). `visualization.html` ("The Second Tap", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/b63fab62-7d79-4256-95b3-e05ac7f53598>): Asha's phone / PayEasy server / bank three-panel stage; design switch A none · B check-then-act · C one-transaction · D claim-first+replay+keyed-bank; local-vs-external effect toggle; lose-response, double-tap, re-price-same-key, crash/restart/reaper controls; step-back via snapshots; live verdict "Asha has been charged N times"; 6 predict-then-run scenarios. Engine verified headlessly (JavaScriptCore, **31 checks**: all scenario outcomes, C-blocked-duplicate, D-concurrent-409, pre/post-effect crash re-drives, step-back equality). EXERCISES/SOLUTIONS: 8 rungs; rung 5 = 9-event hand trace incl. the 422-that-feels-wrong; rung 6 plants claim-committed-separately-from-local-effect, Redis-claim, and success-only-rows; rung 7 = unkeyable partner bank (SENT_UNCONFIRMED/HELD design); rung 8 = checkout with Stripe + seats + unkeyable email.
- Builds on: [transactional-outbox] (**its "consumers must be idempotent" is finally a mechanism**, and its claim-first move is reused for the external hop), [networking-for-developers] (the doubt is physical: TCP acks bytes, not outcomes), [distributed-locking] (**box G's other half cashed in**; check-then-act gap re-used; fencing-vs-idempotency distinction), [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] (claim INSERT = seventh costume of the conditional write; lunch problem prices the wait-on-RUNNING option), [isolation-levels] (unique constraint beats any level, again), [google-docs-system-design] (its per-client edit number + resent confirmation was this in miniature), [quorums-and-replication] ("failed" ≠ "nothing happened" → ambiguous failures must not settle), [event-sourcing]/[cqrs] (idempotent projections = v2), [distributed-transactions] (commit-record retries; Kafka EOS shape), [saga]/[uber]/[whatsapp] (at-least-once + idempotent receiver, now implemented), [crdts] (natural idempotency as algebra; idempotent-alone ≠ reorder-safe), [raft] (retry-on-timeout must be idempotent — client session dedup) · Related: [linearizability-vs-serializability] (replay staleness as a non-event), [hybrid-logical-clocks] (ordering, the orthogonal defense) · Next: [kafka?] (most-referenced unlearned concept; idempotent producer + EOS at home), [change-data-capture?], [flp-impossibility]

## eventual-consistency — learned 2026-08-29 — lab: 28-eventual-consistency/

- **No-code lab** (doc + visualization + exercises). The bottom rung of the ladder finally given its own precise treatment — the concept every prior consistency lab was an upgrade *from*.
- Takeaway: "Eventually consistent" is not a weak version of consistent — it is a **different kind of statement**: a **liveness property** (promise about the limit, silent about every present moment), so **no finite observation can ever falsify it** ("wait longer" answers every accusation) and it cannot be tested, only its companions can. Unpacked it has exactly two clauses: **delivery** (every update keeps being propagated until present everywhere — a forever-obligation whose death produces *no event*, hence must be monitored via machinery heartbeats: lag AGE, time-since-last-repair, injected t-visibility probes) and **decision** (what a replica serves must be a pure function of the *set* of updates received — the audit question for any hand-rolled sync; an arrival-order resolver breaks it and yields the ONE provable EC breach: same set, different display, permanent divergence with a finite witness). Everything users actually rely on — freshness, monotonicity, which write wins — is **un-promised**; engineering an EC system = engineering its un-promised behavior.
- The frame that organizes the whole prior ladder: **SEC ([crdts]) is the safety upgrade of the decision clause** (same set ⇒ same state *immediately* — finite witness, which is exactly why the crdts lab could fuzz its laws while EC per se is untestable); **session guarantees / causal ([causal-consistency]) strengthen the path**; **bounded staleness** (Cosmos's menu rung, now earned) = EC + a *promised* ceiling where EC only ever has a *measured* one; linearizability = both + real time. **Converged and wrong are compatible**: the LWW worked trace (skewed replica stamps a 15-s-older write into the future → every clause satisfied, newer write silently dead — and the mirror wrinkle from exercise 5: a SLOW clock back-dates your write into losing to the ancient default, RYW "satisfied" while your own board out-votes the write it just accepted). The resolver is the part that decides which writes die: LWW (clock = resolver — [quorums-and-replication] 1000/1000, [crdts] 67% measurements reused), siblings, or CRDT join. Deletes: EC certifies convergence only over updates the design chose to *represent* — delete-as-row-removal resurrects everywhere while remaining perfectly EC (tombstone must be data; lifetime ties to the repair deadline, Cassandra `gc_grace_seconds` + repair-within-window; re-admission past the deadline forfeits the set).
- Production reframe worth keeping: **the most-deployed EC system in a Java shop is an ordinary async SQL read replica** (`@Transactional(readOnly)` routing; lag = the window; F1/F2/F7 in costume); DynamoDB prices the trade (EC read = half); S3's Dec-2020 switch to strong = Hyrum's law priced; DNS = TTL-bounded EC everyone accepts because the *data* tolerates it (EC is matched to data, never believed in). **F7 rule: EC reads are for display, never decisions** — read-modify-write on an EC read is the [linearizability] 87%-lost increment with a bigger window; the write path needs conditional writes ([optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking]). Monitoring table (exercise 7.1): six failure modes vs why "compare replicas, alert on divergence" misses each; the falsifying query for "healthy" is the t-visibility probe **under injected network stress** (calm-network-proves-nothing, 5th appearance).
- Key terms now known: eventual consistency (full treatment), delivery clause vs decision clause, liveness vs safety **applied to consistency contracts** (finite witness; why EC is untestable and SEC/lin are checkable), quiescence / quiescent consistency, staleness, t-visibility, k-staleness, PBS (Bailis; paper-reported: N=3 R=W=1 fresh at 99.9% within tens of ms), BASE / soft state, resolver as "who dies", arrival-order resolver (the broken one), bounded staleness (contract vs measurement), resurrection, consistency window (edges at last), machinery-heartbeat alarms
- Lab contents: README (16 stages, 4 depth layers, 4 prediction checkpoints, noticeboards-of-Deodar / sets-plus-resolver mental models + 3 analogy breaks, ladder diagram, 28-term glossary; all numbers cited from prior labs — no new experiments, per the no-code rule). `visualization.html` ("The Weakest Promise", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/a134ee1f-49c6-4130-abe8-44d450c28ba3>) — three noticeboards with **Holds vs Displays kept visibly separate**, a 4-judge panel where **the EC judge has no red light during a run** (only "cannot be violated… yet" → Stop-&-settle forces a ruling; its sole red = the same-set-different-display witness), Lost-updates judge with causal-supersession rule (context lists inspectable per chip), staleness meters + read-staleness histogram (the measured window beside the contract that refuses to state one), resolver/skew/cadence/flood/routing all controls, 6 predict-then-run scenarios; engine + full UI path verified headlessly in JavaScriptCore (35 engine + 37 UI checks, incl. settle idempotence, snapshot/restore determinism, duplicate-delivery idempotence). EXERCISES/SOLUTIONS: 8 rungs incl. the slow-clock trace, the no-tombstone spot-the-flaw, the 6-failure monitoring page, and the 4-flow 3-region design (cart = the flow where strong-everywhere is the error).
- Builds on: [linearizability] + [causal-consistency] (**the ladder those labs climbed, now given its floor**; 0/600-calm and 600/600-partition numbers reused as the price/prize), [crdts] (SEC as the safety upgrade; OR-Set as the cart answer; the −2-flour invariant impossibility), [quorums-and-replication] (anti-entropy/read-repair/hinted-handoff = the delivery machinery; LWW 1000/1000; lag-is-a-queue → alarm on age), [gossip-protocols] (rounds math prices "eventually"; the stop-rule 9/1000 as the permanent-orphan shape), [cap-theorem] (BASE = its available half named), [cqrs]/[caching] (their "consistency window" finally given edges), [isolation-levels] (stale-replica ≡ read-skew symptoms, third repetition), [hybrid-logical-clocks] (relative-vs-absolute skew under LWW) · Related: [event-sourcing] (refusing the conflict instead of resolving it), [raft] (the referee EC's definition is the absence of) · Next: [jepsen?] (testing the untestable via its companions), [change-data-capture?], [local-first-software?], [partitioning-and-rebalancing?]

## linearizability-vs-serializability — learned 2026-08-29 — lab: 27-linearizability-vs-serializability/

- **First no-code lab** (user directive 2026-08-29: /learn labs are doc + visualization + exercises only — no src/, no tests, no experiments; the skill file now enforces it). Contents: README (4 depth layers, 2 prediction checkpoints, notary/auditor mental models + where they break), `visualization.html` ("One History, Three Judges", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/db063242-8532-475f-9c60-ace03d84f0f0>) — one draggable operation-bar timeline judged **simultaneously** by three verdict panels (Notary = linearizability per object, Auditor = serializability, Both Jobs = strict serializability), a grouping toggle that flips the same bars between single-op and transaction scope, 6 predict-then-run cards, per-order rejection lists, click-to-inspect real-time constraints; engine verified headlessly (JavaScriptCore, 25 checks) including both exercise variants. EXERCISES/SOLUTIONS: 8 rungs, pencil-and-paper (rungs 5/6 recast as Trace / Spot-the-flaw).
- Takeaway: "Is the system consistent?" hides **two independent questions** — *scope* (one operation on one object vs a declared group over many) and *time* (must the explaining order respect the wall clock, or is any order acceptable). **Linearizability = single-operation scope + real time** (CAP's C, a recency guarantee); **serializability = transaction scope + ANY order** (ACID's I, clock-free by definition); they sit off-diagonal in the 2×2 and **neither implies the other**, proven by two counterexamples each walking through the other's judge: the stale read (serializable via the time-reversed order T2→T1; not linearizable, not strict) and the ungrouped lost update / write skew (every operation linearizable; the group unserializable — or unprotected entirely when never declared). **Strict serializability = both axes** = Spanner's external consistency; restricted to single-op transactions it collapses to linearizability.
- The two axes have different prices and different structure: recency (right column) costs coordination per read/ack (ABD write-back 11.7→24.4, commit-wait 6.7→24.8 — both prior-lab measurements); grouping (bottom row) costs conflict detection (2PL waits / SSI refusals / SI versions). **Composition asymmetry**: linearizability is local (per-key ⇒ global, 300/300), serializability is NOT — two locally-serializable banks + a cross-bank transfer have no global serial order (each bank orders T1,T2 oppositely), which is exactly why cross-shard transactions need 2PC/Percolator while linearizable stores scale by sharding.
- The five confusions, each a viz card: **F1** stale read (serializable ✓ strict ✗ — a serializable DB showing yesterday's balance is *working as specified*; freshness was never in the contract; fix recency, not isolation); **F2/F3** the same ATM bars all-green ungrouped and auditor-red grouped — the guarantee cannot protect a group it was never told about (`@Transactional`/conditional write is a *declaration*, and the boundary is the app's to draw); **F4** write skew (per-object notary structurally blind to a predicate spanning objects); **F5** causal reverse — serializable via T2→T3→T1 *only because disjoint keys never conflict* (weld a second write onto T2 and S dies — ex 4.2); CockroachDB's documented not-strict gap, closed by Spanner's commit-wait. Card 6: the torn read (L ✓✓ S ✗) — atomic visibility as a transaction-scope property that per-object judges catch only by luck of timing (the distributed-transactions lab's fractured read, re-derived from the judges' definitions).
- Deciding vs providing: serializability-checking of a recorded history is NP-complete (Papadimitriou), matching the linearizability lab's 79M-state checker blowup — real systems provide by construction (single-node commit order makes Postgres SSI *behave* strict until a replica serves the read) and Jepsen/Elle checks by cycle inference, not permutation. Vendor-page reading list: Oracle SERIALIZABLE = SI (wrong grid cell), CockroachDB serializable-not-strict, FoundationDB/Spanner strict, ZooKeeper straddles cells (lin writes, stale reads), any replica/cache = "minus the right column, never minus isolation."
- Key terms now known: scope axis vs time axis, serializability (full treatment — some-serial-order, view-style evidence), strict serializability (earned), recency guarantee, stale read as an isolation *non-event*, one-copy serializability (1SR), causal reverse, torn read at transaction scope, C-in-CAP vs I-in-ACID (two research communities, two threats), the 2×2 grid, composition asymmetry
- Builds on: [linearizability] (**the direct sequel** — its AtomicInteger 87%-lost result is now the scope counterexample; its locality measurement becomes half of the composition asymmetry; its checker cost result generalizes), [isolation-levels] (its ladder is the bottom-left cell's interior; H2/Oracle SERIALIZABLE facts reused; "constraint beats level" unchanged), [distributed-transactions] (external consistency = strict serializability now earned; atomic visibility = card 6; stale-snapshot measurement = F1 at scale), [cap-theorem] (C is linearizability, per Gilbert & Lynch), [causal-consistency] (the ladder below the grid; session tokens as the cheap F1 fix), [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] (conversations sit above every cell — SERIALIZABLE included) · Related: [hybrid-logical-clocks] (external consistency for strangers), [crdts] (the no-referee corner) · Next: [jepsen?] (Elle finds F1–F5 in the wild), [deterministic-databases?], [mvcc-and-vacuum?]

## distributed-locking — learned 2026-08-29 — lab: 43-distributed-locking/

- Takeaway: A lock between machines must be manufactured out of messages, and the two facts that make it hard are: **a process cannot observe its own delays** (between any two instructions the world may have moved — a paused holder wakes and acts as if nothing happened), and **death and slowness are indistinguishable from outside** (silence is the only evidence, with two meanings needing opposite responses). Consequences, each measured in a deterministic tick simulator: a lock with no expiry turns one crash into a permanent error-free outage (recovery = NEVER); a **lease/TTL** fixes that (recovery ≈ TTL, every value) and creates the stale holder — pause > TTL = two believed holders and a lost update. **No TTL is safe** (the `dilemma` sweep: every row a compromise, and "longer than my longest pause" is a number nobody has); **renewal doesn't move the cliff** (a paused holder cannot heartbeat — the heartbeat's absence IS why it expired); **the defensive pre-write check dies in its own check-then-act gap** (all three measured). The fix that works: **fencing tokens** — strictly monotonic grant numbers, checked on EVERY resource operation, lower-than-seen refused → 0 lost at every pause length, stale writers pay with a redo.
- **The two honest sentences about fencing:** (1) it does NOT restore mutual exclusion — split-brain still happens (overlapTicks > 0 every time); it makes overlap *harmless* by refusing stale EFFECTS: at-most-one-effect-lineage, not at-most-one-holder. (2) **Safety by arithmetic, liveness by timeout, never the reverse** — the token check has zero timing assumptions; the TTL is pure timing and must be, since slow-vs-dead is undecidable from silence.
- **The bug this lab found in itself (planted as bug #4, `write-only fencing`):** pause 25 < TTL 30 and an update STILL died with fencing "on" — the hold includes the write's TRAVEL time, so the write was in flight when the lease expired, the new holder READ before the ghost packet landed, and both writes arrived with valid increasing tokens, zero rejections. Fix: **fence reads too** — a read presents its token and raises the resource's floor, so older in-flight writes bounce (Chubby checks sequencers on every request). The most invisible failure in the lab.
- **The unification with [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking]:** the resource's fencing check IS a conditional write — the token is a version column issued by a lock service (`WHERE token >= ?`). Sixth costume of one primitive (expected-version, WATCH, If-Match, @Version, incarnation). Corollary = **Kleppmann's efficiency-vs-correctness distinction, made quantitative** (`efficiency` experiment: identical faults, 2 overlaps = corruption if an invariant, a shrug if idempotent duplicate work): if the lock is for correctness the guarantee must live at the resource anyway, so a correct distributed lock system = optimistic concurrency at the resource + a pessimistic hint on top to avoid duplicate cost.
- Other measured results: **herd** — 20 waiters: 2,740 poll messages vs 99 watch messages, same finish time; the watch queue (ZK's watch-the-previous-node recipe) is one notification per handover + FIFO. **Redlock** (5 servers, majority 3): availability works (2 unreachable, still locks), but a client pause corrupts identically (five servers agreed; the pause didn't ask them — and there's no token to check), and ONE server's clock jump inside the holder's majority creates two overlapping majorities (quorum-overlap working AGAINST safety: the shared server is where the intersection argument breaks). That pair IS the Kleppmann–antirez debate with numbers. **Planted bugs** (4, each caught): token-reused-on-expiry (both holders equally credentialed → fencing defeated, 1/2 survive), token-counter-in-memory (restart resets to 0 → legitimate writers fenced off until it climbs back — safety kept, availability lost; tokens must be durable, why etcd/ZK use log positions), dedup-instead-of-ordering (a stale holder's FIRST write always carries an unseen token), write-only-fencing.
- Production mapping worth keeping: ZK zxid / etcd `mod_revision` / Chubby sequencer / DynamoDB RVN are the fencing token; etcd `Txn(Compare(mod_revision))` is the fenced write; **Redisson RLock has TTL + watchdog renewal and NO fencing token** — an efficiency lock with correctness marketing; **ShedLock is explicitly an efficiency lock** (its docs disclaim correctness) — right for don't-email-twice, wrong alone for don't-pay-twice; `pg_advisory_xact_lock` special case: work inside the same transaction makes release and commit atomic, deleting the stale-holder problem; renewal must be compare-and-extend (a bare `SET k me PX` renew is lock theft). Leader election = the same lock held long + fencing on every side effect (K8s Lease, etcd Campaign). The honest corner (box G): a resource that cannot check conditions (email, dumb API) is safe under NO lock — idempotency keys, a single fenced owner, reconciliation, and a measured residual. Chubby extras: sessions (one heartbeat, many claims, coarser blast radius), lock-delay, coarse-grained design point.
- Key terms now known: distributed lock, lock service, lease (as the only sane lock), TTL's two opposed jobs, renewal/heartbeat (compare-and-extend), stale holder, fencing token (full treatment at last), sequencer, read fence, ghost packet, check-then-act gap, split-brain as overlapTicks, at-most-one-effect vs at-most-one-holder, herd effect, watch, Redlock, clock jump, efficiency lock vs correctness lock, ephemeral node/session, lock-delay, advisory lock, leader election as long lease
- Lab contents: 4 Java files, no deps (`java src/World.java` = narrated four-act story: stuck-forever → TTL rescue → pause disaster → fencing redo; `java src/Tests.java` = 14 scenario checks incl. the Redlock clock-jump double-majority; `… broken` = 4 planted bugs; `java src/Experiments.java [crash|pause|dilemma|check|herd|redlock|efficiency]` = 7 measurements in ~1s; `java src/YourLock.java` = grant rule + fencing rule blanked out, self-checking 2/6 → 6/6). `visualization.html` ("The Spare Key", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/8e3561c0-8249-45dd-9b04-52ab6e0f5faa>) — darkroom-key framing on the page, lock service with live lease bar + key counter, resource with token dial, writes-in-flight wire strip, pause/crash buttons per client, fencing off/writes-only/all switch, watch/poll, 6 predict-then-run scenarios verified headlessly (JavaScriptCore), step-back. README: 4 depth layers, 4 prediction checkpoints, darkroom/logbook mental models + where they break, box-G flowchart, 23-term glossary. NOT built: consensus internals (raft lab), sessions multiplexing locks, real independent partitions client↔lock vs client↔resource, reentrancy/RW locks/fairness, redlock in the viz.
- Builds on: [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking] (**the unification target** — fencing = its conditional write with the version minted by a lock service; its offline-lock exercise 7 was this lab's claim-row design), [redis] (its measured SET-NX-PX pause failure is this lab's Act 3; its "fencing token checked by the resource" one-liner is now a full mechanism), [uber-system-design] (lease with deadline, claim/CAS, offer id), [gossip-protocols] (slow-vs-dead undecidability; incarnation ≈ token), [raft] (consensus-backed lock services; lease reads as clock bets; quorum overlap — here working against safety in Redlock), [two-phase-commit] (the no-TTL blocking is its lesson re-worn), [quorums-and-replication] (majority-overlap arithmetic), [whatsapp-system-design] ("the queue is the guarantee, the push an optimization" → "the token is the guarantee, the lock an optimization"), [linearizability] (fencing named there for Redis locks), [transactional-outbox] (idempotency for box G) · Related: [isolation-levels] (the in-database twin), [google-docs-system-design] (ownership lease + fencing for a document owner — this lab's mechanism at work) · Next: [idempotency-keys?] (the other half of box G), [zookeeper?], [clock-synchronization?], [leader-election-patterns?]

## optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking — learned 2026-08-29 — lab: 10-optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking/

- Takeaway: The conflicts that matter most span **conversations, not transactions** — read in one request, write in another, a human in between — and no isolation level (SERIALIZABLE included) can see them, because each half is individually harmless; the protection is application code, and there are exactly two shapes: **optimistic** = carry proof-of-what-you-read (a version column) and make every write conditional on it (`UPDATE … SET version=version+1 WHERE id=? AND version=?` — first-committer-wins hand-built one level above the engine), **pessimistic** = take the row lock before reading (`SELECT … FOR UPDATE`) and hold to commit. Detect at write time vs prevent at read time; the third option that beats both when it fits: a single UPDATE statement or a constraint (isolation lab's 320/320-in-26ms result, still the champion).
- **The trap of the whole technique, measured:** a conflicting optimistic save is NOT an error — it is an UPDATE matching **0 rows**, unremarkable to SQL. The update COUNT is the only witness. Three planted bugs, each caught by `Tests.java broken`: `no-count-check` (conflicts become silent no-ops — user sees "Saved", nothing saved), `no-version-bump` (consumed version stays current → every stale committer wins), `stale-reapply` (retry refreshes only the version and re-sends the old value: **40/320** increments survive DESPITE detection and retry — a retry must re-read and RE-DECIDE, the event-sourcing rule re-proven).
- The measured asymmetries that make the choice: **lunch** — one client pausing 2s mid-save under pessimistic: 5 neighbours land **0** saves and hit **15** lock timeouts; same pause under optimistic: **170** neighbour saves, 0 timeouts, only the sleeper pays (one conflict). A held lock converts one client's delay into everyone's failure — never hold a row lock across I/O or a human. **deadlock** — opposite-order transfers: 59 victims (40001) pessimistic, **0** optimistic (never wait-while-holding → no cycle), but optimistic buys **starvation** instead: a 30ms thinker vs 6 instant writers = **0/5 saves in 8s, 263 lost races, no error anywhere** (liveness failure, safety intact); fix = **escalation** — after 3 losses take FOR UPDATE → 5/5 in 603ms (the lock's queue IS the fairness optimism lacks). **crossover** — 8 clients × 25 ops × 2ms work on 1 hot row: wall clock near-ties (opt 511ms/862 retries vs pes 434ms) because idle cores absorb the redone work — the waste is real (2124ms burned vs 400ms useful, 5×) but invisible in latency until the cores aren't spare, which in production is exactly when contention peaks. In-process H2 also flatters pessimistic: real deployments hold locks across network round trips per statement.
- **The job queue result:** 120 jobs × 10ms, 4 workers claiming "first PENDING … FOR UPDATE" = **1397ms convoy** (serial floor 1200 — workers contend on *position*, not data); `FOR UPDATE SKIP LOCKED` = **312ms**; optimistic claim (`UPDATE … WHERE status='PENDING'`, count=1 means mine — the status column IS a version column with named values) = 317ms with 121 cheap lost races. SKIP LOCKED costs only strict FIFO.
- **Write skew finally fixed in the app:** H2 allows the two-doctors anomaly at every level incl. SERIALIZABLE (isolation lab); `FOR UPDATE` on your *own* row does nothing (verified: 0 doctors left). Fix: every rule-dependent transaction bumps ONE shared version row (`UPDATE roster SET version=version+1 WHERE id=1 AND version=?`) → collisions on purpose, second committer refused (verified: 1 doctor left, 1 rejection). This is JPA `OPTIMISTIC_FORCE_INCREMENT` on an aggregate root — a version column converts invisible read-dependencies into visible write-conflicts.
- Reach and limits: the version is **data**, so it survives connection close, server switch, weeks (verified across separate connections) — the only strategy that works across requests; Fowler's *Optimistic/Pessimistic Offline Lock*. But it binds only writers who follow the protocol — a raw data-fix UPDATE bypasses it and (worse) doesn't bump the version, so a later stale save silently reverts the fix; constraints still protect the table itself. Spring traps: a POST handler that re-loads the entity and copies form fields onto it silently DISABLES @Version (the stale version never took part — the form must round-trip it); catch-and-ignore of OptimisticLockingFailureException = hand-built silent no-op; the @Retryable must wrap the whole service method. Same protocol elsewhere: ETag/If-Match/412, DynamoDB ConditionExpression, Kubernetes resourceVersion + 409 + RetryOnConflict, Elasticsearch if_seq_no, Redis WATCH, CAS.
- Key terms now known: optimistic locking (contains no lock), pessimistic locking, version column, stale, conflict, update count, retry re-decide vs re-send, lock queue, lock timeout (H2 50200/HYT00), NOWAIT, SKIP LOCKED, convoy, starvation, escalation, offline lock (optimistic/pessimistic), force-increment, @Version, OptimisticLockingFailureException, LockModeType.PESSIMISTIC_WRITE, jakarta.persistence.lock.timeout (0=NOWAIT, -2=SKIP LOCKED), conditional write, claim column
- Lab contents: 5 Java files on H2 (`java -cp ../lib/h2.jar src/Tests.java` = 14 scenario checks; `… broken` = 3 planted bugs; `src/Experiments.java [overwrite|crossover|waste|lunch|queue|deadlock|starvation]` = 7 measurements ~25s; `src/YourStrategy.java` = optimistic save + job claim blanked out, self-checking 2/6 → 6/6). `visualization.html` ("The Second Save", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/fafcd865-f069-4619-9861-db4df13d8944>) — deterministic tick simulator, two tabs (edit page / job board), clients' stale copies always on screen with STALE flags, 7 predict-then-run scenarios verified headlessly (JavaScriptCore), per-client start/think controls + "phone rings" mid-edit button, step-back via snapshot history. README: 4 depth layers, 5 prediction checkpoints, git-vs-locked-Excel mental models, decision flowchart, 26-term glossary. NOT built: offline-pessimistic claim table (exercise 7, with fencing generation), connection-pool dynamics, replicas/caches interaction, gap locks, advisory locks.
- Builds on: [isolation-levels] (**the direct sequel** — its lost update stretched across requests where the dial can't reach; its 8×40=320 experiment re-run under both strategies at read committed: 320/320 both; its write-skew doctors finally fixed; its "constraint beats level" now "constraint beats strategy"), [event-sourcing] (expected-version IS optimistic locking; re-decide-never-re-send re-proven by bug 3), [linearizability] (the conditional UPDATE is compareAndSet in SQL; read-then-write is two operations), [redis] (WATCH = same bet, same starvation; fencing token returns in exercise 7), [http-caching] (ETag/If-Match = the protocol over HTTP), [transactional-outbox] (idempotent claims; reaper for crashed workers) · Related: [two-phase-commit] (2PL's blocking is the lunch problem writ large), [crdts] (merge instead of refuse — the third answer to concurrent writes), [saga] (semantic locks are offline locks) · Next: [mvcc-and-vacuum?], [distributed-locking?], [database-indexing?], [queueing-theory?]

## hybrid-logical-clocks — learned 2026-08-29 — lab: 31-hybrid-logical-clocks/

- Takeaway: A timestamp has two jobs that ordinary clocks split between them — **order** (cause must stamp before effect) and **meaning** (a stamp should answer "as of 5 p.m."). Measured against a vector-clock ground truth over 28,720 happens-before pairs: the wall clock inverts cause and effect **65 times at skew 8, 341 at 20 — and still TIES 79 pairs at skew ZERO** (finite resolution); the Lamport clock never errs but drifts **+460/−13** from wall time, with one counter value spanning 34 ticks of real time. HLC fuses them as one stamp **(l, c)**: *l may only ever be a wall reading some clock actually produced* (the freshest on your causal path — no `+1` may ever touch it), and *c counts the events l cannot tell apart*, resetting whenever l advances. Result, same fuzz: **0 inversions, 0 ties, 0 torn snapshots, drift ≤ clock spread (measured spread−1, exactly), in 64 bits (48+16)**.
- **The funeral that derives the design:** the obvious hybrid — ONE merged value `max(l+1, pt, incoming+1)` (the HLC paper's own strawman) — is perfectly ordered and drifts **118→244→470→898** as the run doubles, because every event's `+1` lands on a wall-valued number and `max` never lets it back down. The two-character diff (`l+1` vs `l`) is the whole invention: never let logical time masquerade as physical time.
- **The receive rule's trap** (planted bug `recvIgnoresMsgC`, 8 real inversions): when l ties with the incoming message, the new c must beat **both** counters — `max(c, mc)+1` — or the receive stamps below its own send. Fourth branch: own wall won outright → c=0. `YourClock.java` blanks all four branches (1/6→6/6). Other planted bugs: `recvIgnoresPt` → l falls behind your own wall (minDrift −14; "as of 5 p.m." misses local 5 p.m. events), `compareIgnoresC` → 124 unorderable pairs, `neverResetC` → **zero anomalies, guaranteed crash**: maxC 31→718, and 16 bits at 1M chained events/s die in ~65ms — the reset-on-advance rule is what makes 16 bits a fortune.
- **The three honest boundaries.** (1) One-way, like Lamport: smaller stamp never proves causation — concurrency detection still costs a vector clock. (2) The drift bound is **conditional on honest clocks**: one node +60 in a ±8 cluster infects everyone downstream (measured bound 16 → 67, l never comes back down) — hence CockroachDB's node suicide past max-offset and MongoDB **signing** clusterTime (an unsigned token of MAX_VALUE/2 is that experiment weaponized, permanently). (3) No external consistency for strangers: fast-clock writer + slow-clock reader with no causal path = **86/86 stale**; the fix costs ε somewhere — client carries a **causality token** absorbed via the ordinary receive rule (**0/86**; = MongoDB clusterTime/afterClusterTime, the causal-consistency lab's session token now implemented), or CockroachDB **uncertainty restarts** (reader retries within [ts, ts+max_offset]), or TrueTime commit wait (writer waits 2ε). Same ε, three different bills — writer, reader, or client.
- Production: **CockroachDB** — every MVCC version is an HLC (int64 wall ns + int32 logical); HLC + uncertainty restarts is how it skips commit wait (the distributed-transactions lab's open question, answered); restart-wait on reboot; 500ms default max-offset. **MongoDB** — $clusterTime (32-bit secs + 32-bit counter) piggybacked on every message, HMAC-signed. **YugabyteDB** (HLC + leader leases), Kudu. On-call: NTP offset gauges with eviction, `l − pt` drift per node (downstream-of-a-liar detector), uncertainty-restart rates (skew made visible), c-distribution.
- Key terms now known: hybrid logical clock, l (wall part) / c (logical part), inversion, tie, torn cut, drift (l−pt), clock spread, naive hybrid, causality token, clusterTime/afterClusterTime (implementation), uncertainty restart, max-offset, 48+16 packing, one-way property (reaffirmed), conditional bound
- Lab contents: 7 Java files, no deps (`java src/Tests.java` = **25** checks incl. all six receive-rule cases and the vector-clock-as-judge fuzz; `broken` = 4 planted bugs; `java src/Experiments.java [1-8]` = lies/lamport/naive/bound/cuts/token/badclock/pack; `java src/Scenes.java [1-5]` = the newsroom (Asha +5, Ben −5): the correction filed before the article, Lamport burning the map, HLC event-by-event, the cut, the token; `java src/YourClock.java`). `visualization.html` ("Dated Pages, Numbered Lines", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/d3546629-3719-479e-b29a-25ce29f263d0>) = space-time diagram, 4 designs × 3 per-node skew sliders recomputing every stamp live (pure function → free step-back), **a draggable "edition as of T" cut that tears red under WALL and never tears under HLC at any slider position**, burst toggle, per-lane drift meters, click-to-inspect, 5 predict-then-run cards; engine verified headlessly vs the Java on all six rule cases + 11 property checks (`validate-visualization.sh`, node or JavaScriptCore). NOT built: real NTP drift rates, ns resolution, token signing, restart-wait, the full uncertainty-restart read machinery.
- Builds on: [causal-consistency] (**this is its `hybrid-logical-clocks?` suggestion cashed in** — happens-before, Lamport's one-way-ness, vector clocks (promoted to the lab's ground-truth judge), and the session token, now implemented), [distributed-transactions] (consistent cut at a wall time = its snapshot requirement met without an oracle; TrueTime/commit-wait as the alternative bill; "crash the lying clock"), [linearizability] (external consistency for strangers is exactly what HLC alone does not give; calm-network lesson, fourth appearance: 0 violations at skew ≤2), [crdts]/[gossip-protocols] (Lamport counter, max-merge monotonicity) · Related: [event-sourcing] (ordering an audit log), [cqrs] · Next: [jepsen?], [deterministic-databases?], [change-data-capture?], [clock-synchronization?]

## distributed-transactions — learned 2026-08-29 — lab: 46-distributed-transactions/

- Takeaway: **2PC alone is not a distributed transaction.** It makes cross-shard writes all-or-nothing *on disk* while readers still catch them half-applied, because "commit" across shards is a **window, not an instant** — measured: perfect 2PC, zero crashes, **16 fractured audits in 40 runs** (auditor's sum ≠ true total; the naive dual write scores 14, barely worse). A distributed transaction = atomic durability + **atomic visibility** + isolation. The two ways to buy visibility: **lock the readers** (distributed strict 2PL — correct, serializable, but readers queue behind every commit and inherit 2PC's blocking) or **timestamp the world** (snapshot reads at a globally-ordered timestamp). The two key ideas that generate everything: give every transaction *one position in a single global order of time*, and make the commit *one local durable write* that any reader can reach.
- **Percolator (= TiDB's core), the design worth carrying in your head:** oracle hands out start-ts/commit-ts; prewrite stages a value + lock on every written key, each lock pointing at one appointed **primary** key; the commit point is *replacing the primary's lock with commitRecord{startTs→commitTs} in one shard-local write*; secondaries commit lazily. A reader hitting a lock resolves it itself: consult the primary → roll forward (cts≤snap) / read below (cts>snap or lock younger than snapshot) / wait (fresh lock, primary still held) / TTL roll-back. Measured: kill the client at every one of the 7 steps → **rolled back through step 5, rolled FORWARD from step 6**, money correct in all 7; the same crash storm strands **281 2PL readers forever vs 0 percolator readers** (readers did 47 roll-forwards + 272 roll-backs instead). **The blocking problem is deleted by relocating the commit point from coordinator memory into data.** Safety of reader-vs-writer races: both act on one primary cell; the committer finds its lock gone and knows it lost.
- **The clock act:** replace the oracle with client clocks → fractured stays 0 (the lock check never cared where timestamps come from) but **external consistency dies**: a reader starting *after* an acked commit gets a smaller snapshot — 10 stale snapshots at skew 8, 25 at 20, and **0 at skew ≤2** (the linearizability lesson yet again: a guarantee-free design is indistinguishable from a correct one until conditions misbehave). **TrueTime commit wait** (commit-ts = now().latest; hold the ACK until now().earliest > commit-ts) → 0 stale at every skew, paid as ~2ε on every write ack (latency 6.7→24.8 ticks at ε=8, 51.0 at ε=20) plus longer lock holds taxing readers. *Nobody's clock got better; the ack got slower.*
- Still snapshot isolation: **write skew survives distribution untouched** (two doctors' on-call flags on different shards, both commit, invariant dead — 2PL prevents it; CockroachDB closes it with SSI read-tracking). **Distributed deadlock is invisible to every local detector** — each shard holds half the cycle; a timeout kills one victim and the survivor commits; the isolation-levels lock-ordering fix still works across shards (632→320 timeout aborts). Optimistic vs pessimistic on hot keys: perco 255 conflict aborts vs 2PL 632 timeout aborts — retries vs waiting, the same currency pair as isolation-levels. Removing first-committer-wins doesn't just legalize lost updates: with delta-staged transfers it **loses money** (5 tests go red).
- Production mapping: **TiDB** = this percolator + Raft under every shard + PD as a Raft-replicated batched oracle; **Spanner** = 2PC over Paxos groups + TrueTime hardware (GPS+atomic, ε≈ms) + commit wait; **CockroachDB** = HLC + read-side uncertainty restarts (same ε, charged to reads) + parallel commits + SSI; **Kafka transactions** are percolator-shaped (commit markers in partitions = commit records; read_committed consumers = the lock check). **XA/JTA** is the lock-based design living in the JVM app tier — works, blocks, and pages humans to invent *heuristic outcomes* for *in-doubt* transactions. The decision ladder: one DB > co-locate keys on one shard > outbox > saga > distributed txn — reserved for cross-shard invariants that must never be *seen* broken (ledgers, uniqueness, holds).
- Key terms now known: atomic visibility vs atomic durability, fractured read, distributed transaction, consistent cut, timestamp oracle, distributed 2PL, distributed deadlock/victim, Percolator, prewrite, primary/secondary, commit record, commit point (relocated), lock TTL, resolution/roll forward/roll back, first-committer-wins (per-shard), stale snapshot, external consistency (= strict serializability), uncertainty interval ε, TrueTime, commit wait, HLC (by name), parallel/async commit + 1PC (by name), XA/JTA, in-doubt transaction, heuristic outcome
- Lab contents: 7 Java files, no deps (`java src/Tests.java` = **31** scenario checks; `broken` mode = 4 planted bugs each breaking exactly one guarantee — readerIgnoresLocks→visibility, commitSecondariesFirst→atomicity (money 250), resolveWithoutPrimaryCheck→destroys a COMMITTED write (money 150; the dangerous-because-invisible one), earlyReadLockRelease→strictness; `java src/Experiments.java [1-10]` = torn/fractured/blocking/deadlock/oracle/clocks/writeskew/latency/resolution/contention; `java src/Sim.java [1-5]` = narrated scenes; `java src/YourResolver.java` = the read rule blanked out, 1/6→6/6). Six engines (NAIVE, TPC, TPC_2PL, PERCO, PERCO_CLOCK, PERCO_CLOCK_WAIT) behind one step-driven deterministic scheduler, so every number is seed-reproducible. `visualization.html` ("Two Branches, One Instant", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/f4965258-05f4-4bcd-8eb2-a7984d745b85>) = two ledger shards + oracle + Priya/Omar actor cards; you interleave transfer steps and audit steps BY HAND (the interleaving is the lesson), kill the client at any step, drive clock skew, 6 predict-then-run cards; its engine is verified headlessly against the Java via `validate-visualization.sh` (11 checks; runs on node or macOS JavaScriptCore). NOT built: replication under shards, persistence, oracle failure, SSI, parallel/async commit, version GC, multi-key txns beyond 2, MVCC read-refresh.
- Builds on: [two-phase-commit] (**this lab is what that one left open** — its atomic commit is stage-3 background here, its blocking problem gets deleted by the primary-flip, and its "commit point" concept is literally relocated), [isolation-levels] (fractured read = read skew across machines; snapshot/2PL/write-skew/first-committer-wins all reappear distributed and unchanged — *distribution changed nothing about that sentence*), [linearizability] (external consistency IS its real-time rule for transactions; the calm-network trap re-measured a third time), [raft]/[paxos] (every real shard + the oracle are consensus groups; Spanner = 2PC *over* Paxos — the composition both labs promised), [saga] + [transactional-outbox] (the avoid-it patterns, now priced: a saga is *chosen* fractured visibility with compensations), [event-sourcing] (idempotent retry against the commit record = expected-version thinking), [cap-theorem] · Related: [causal-consistency] (weaker cross-key ordering without transactions), [crdts] (the coordination-free corner), [cqrs] (read models accept staleness this lab refuses) · Next: [hybrid-logical-clocks], [deterministic-databases?] (Calvin: order first, no 2PC), [change-data-capture?], [jepsen?]

## causal-consistency — learned 2026-08-29 — lab: 29-causal-consistency/

- Takeaway: Causal consistency is one rule — **no replica may show a write without also showing everything that happened before it** — where "before" means Lamport's happens-before (same-client order + read-from + transitivity), not a wall clock. It is the [linearizability] ladder stepped down twice: delete real-time order and you get sequential consistency; delete *one agreed order* and you get this, which orders only the pairs where one thing actually caused another and leaves concurrent pairs free. The mechanism (**causal delivery**) is three lines: a write records what its author had seen, a replica holds a received write until all of that is applied, and reading puts the write and its whole past into your context. Measured over 300 random workloads with one slow route: apply-on-arrival is anomalous in **160/300** runs; every dependency-based design is **0/300**.
- **The gap this closes from [quorums-and-replication] and [crdts].** Version vectors order the copies of ONE key and detect conflicts on it; CRDT merges make one key converge. **Causality is a statement ACROSS keys** and neither addresses it. The lab plants that exact mistake as a bug (`same-key-only`): anomalies in **99/200** runs, and it reproduces the canonical story exactly. This is the most common misunderstanding in the area and it is made by people who can defend it.
- **The formal reason it matters:** causal consistency is the **strongest model implementable in a system that stays available under partition** (Attiya-Welch's impossibility above it; Mahajan-Alvisi-Dahlin prove it is the ceiling). Measured: **600/600** operations answered on the minority side of a partition where a linearizable store answers 0. It is the top of the available half of [cap-theorem], not "eventual consistency with extra steps".
- The mechanism ladder, each rung a measured failure: apply-on-arrival (160/300) → wall-clock timestamps (dead on arrival: clocks lie — 1000/1000 from two earlier labs — *and* a timestamp never tells you the order is complete) → **Lamport counter** (causal ONLY over FIFO links: **3/300** anomalies with reordering links, **0/300** with FIFO — so "we use TCP so we're causal" is false the moment you shard; and it forces a total order, so a quiet region stalls everyone: **89,509** hidden write-ticks vs 16,279) → **explicit dependency lists** (0 anomalies, no network assumptions, but **102.5** entries/write at 320 ops and growing) → **nearest-only pruning** (COPS: **1.4** entries flat, provably identical visibility) → **vector clock** (3 counters flat) → **causal+** → **session token**.
- **The trade that is the real content: precision costs bytes, imprecision costs latency.** Three answers to "what does this write depend on", all correct, none ever showing an effect before its cause: what the CLIENT saw (exact, 0% false ordering, a list); what the REPLICA had applied (safe superset, **68.9%** of genuinely-concurrent pairs falsely ordered, 1 counter per replica); a single Lamport counter (**87.2%** falsely ordered). The hidden-write-ticks column tracks it exactly: **7,428 / 16,279 / 89,509**. Every falsely-ordered pair is a write waiting for something its author never looked at.
- **Two things causal consistency does not give you, both measured.** (1) **Convergence** — with causal delivery working perfectly, two concurrent writes leave the replicas disagreeing in **400/400** runs with the wire empty; adding an agreed tie-break (timestamp, or a CRDT merge from [crdts]) makes it **0/400**. That plus is what "causal+" names and what every real system ships. (2) **Anything about the client** — a roaming client misses its own write **334/500** times while the regions are ordered correctly the whole time; the fix is a session token (MongoDB's `afterClusterTime`) presented on **both** reads and writes, verified to take a roaming workload from R-Y-W 3759 / mono-R 1611 / WFR 356 / 726 anomalies to **0 / 0 / 0 / 0**.
- **Session guarantees decompose the property, and two of the four are free.** read-your-writes, monotonic reads, monotonic writes, writes-follow-reads — all four together ARE causal consistency. Measured: with a sticky client, R-Y-W and monotonic reads are violated **0** times *even on the completely unordered design*. Stickiness gives you the two guarantees users actually notice, for free; what it cannot give you is writes-follow-reads, i.e. ordering against other people's writes on other replicas. **Corollary: turning off session affinity "for better balancing" is a correctness change disguised as an infrastructure tweak** — flipping sticky→false in the lab fails 11 of 32 tests.
- Also worth keeping: **causal delivery is a WHEN rule, never a WHETHER rule** — healing a partition delivers nothing; anti-entropy from [gossip-protocols] is mandatory underneath (both asserted). The **slowdown cascade** is why causal stores are offered as an option rather than shipped as the default: one slow route takes hidden write-ticks from 351 to **31,659**, delaying writes about entirely unrelated keys — and Facebook's *Existential Consistency* found their eventually-consistent store was already causal 99.9%+ of the time, so they declined to pay. The one alarm to have: **oldest blocked dependency age**. And: a vector clock's width is your replica count, so "add a region" rewrites every stored clock — a real reason explicit dependency lists survive in systems that reshard.
- Repeat of the [linearizability] lesson, re-measured from the other side: the identical broken design is anomalous in **30/300** runs at zero extra route delay and **214/300** at 20 ticks. *A consistency test without injected network asymmetry is close to worthless.*
- Key terms now known: happens-before (→), concurrent (∥), partial order, causal past/future, causal consistency, causal delivery, causally closed, dot, dependencies, nearest dependencies (COPS pruning), Lamport clock (one-way: a→b implies smaller, never the converse), vector clock vs version vector (process history vs one object's copies), FIFO channel, over-ordering, causal+, convergent conflict handling, session guarantees (read-your-writes / monotonic reads / monotonic writes / writes-follow-reads), sticky session, session token / afterClusterTime, slowdown cascade, causal stability, hybrid logical clock, existential consistency, consistent prefix
- Production facts: **MongoDB** 3.6+ causally-consistent sessions via `afterClusterTime` over a hybrid logical clock — per-session and **opt-in**, not automatic. **Azure Cosmos DB** exposes *Session* (≈ the four guarantees) and *Consistent Prefix* as named levels. **COPS/Eiger** are the origin of causal+ and nearest-dependency pruning; Eiger adds read-only/write-only transactions. **AntidoteDB** is causal+ with CRDTs end to end. **Bayou** (1994) named the session guarantees. **Riak/Dynamo per-key version vectors are NOT causal consistency**; **Cassandra QUORUM is not either**. Causal ordering is never atomic — ordered ≠ transactional.
- Lab contents: 8 Java files, no deps (`java src/Tests.java` = 32 scenario checks; `java src/Tests.java broken` = three planted delivery rules — `same-replica-only` (the "TCP is FIFO so we're causal" belief, 98/200), `same-key-only` (99/200), `vv-no-gap-check` (10/200, and the low number is the lesson); `java src/Experiments.java [1-10]`; `java src/Sim.java` = five narrated scenes; `java src/YourCheck.java` = the delivery rule blanked out, self-checking 2/6 → 6/6). `Cluster.java` holds every design behind one `deliverable()` switch plus `fifoChannels`, per-link delay, per-write delay override, partitions and an `antiEntropy()` round. `visualization.html` ("The Comment Before the Post", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/497ca160-d05d-4b21-8e7e-dca6e71a732e>) is a space-time diagram — **click any change and its causal past goes indigo, its future amber, and everything else greys out**, which is the definition of a partial order in one gesture — plus held-write dashed tails, hand-delivered messages, a which-route-is-slow control, sticky on/off, a repair button, and 6 predict-then-run situations verified headlessly. README with 4 depth layers, 5 prediction checkpoints, a model ladder and a which-one-do-you-need flowchart, and a 29-term glossary. NOT built: sharding, dependency GC, session tokens (exercise 7.1), hybrid logical clocks, transactions, persistence, membership changes.
- Builds on: [linearizability] (**this lab is that ladder stepped down twice**; sequential consistency and the "calm network proves nothing" lesson come straight from it), [quorums-and-replication] (version vectors — per key, which is the trap), [crdts] (causal delivery met there as the price of op-based CRDTs; its convergence is exactly the "+" this needs, and the tombstone-GC problem is structurally identical to dependency GC), [cap-theorem] (this is the ceiling of the available half), [gossip-protocols] (anti-entropy underneath, and membership for causal stability), [java-memory-model] (happens-before, same name and same job, one machine instead of many) · Related: [raft]/[paxos] (one agreed order, bought with a majority — the contrast), [cqrs] (eventual consistency, weaker), [distributed-rate-limiting] (the invariant that turns out not to be one) · Next: [hybrid-logical-clocks], [isolation-levels?], [local-first-software?], [jepsen?]

## isolation-levels — learned 2026-08-29 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-08 — lab: 08-isolation-levels/

- Takeaway: `BEGIN…COMMIT` buys you **atomicity and durability, not isolation** — isolation is a separate dial whose default setting permits silent data loss. Measured: 8 threads × 40 `SELECT`-then-`UPDATE` increments on one row, every transaction committing successfully, ended on **~60 of 320 — about 80% of the work gone with zero errors raised**. The ladder of levels is defined by *which bad endings it forbids*, never by *how it works*, which is why two databases can implement the same level name with different machinery and different behaviour. **Learn the anomalies, not the level names, and run the table against your own database.**
- **The lab's central and most reusable artifact: `Matrix.java`** — an engine-agnostic JDBC harness that plays scripted two-transaction interleavings and can tell *waiting* apart from *failing*, printing 10 anomalies × 5 levels for any `-Ddb.url=`. Verdicts: `ALLOWED` / `safe` / `safe(wait)` / `safe(err)`. Deterministic across 5 runs. **The `ALLOWED` vs not-`ALLOWED` verdict is timing-independent** (computed from returned values + final state); only the wait/err/plain distinction depends on the step timeout, so only that part needs a quiet machine.
- **Measured on H2 2.4.240** (RU · RC · RR · SNAP · SER): dirty write `ALLOWED` at RU (H2 takes no locks there — the one anomaly every standard forbids at every level); dirty read `ALLOWED` at RU only; non-repeatable read / phantom / read skew `ALLOWED` at RU+RC, safe from RR up (H2's RR is snapshot-based, so it stops phantoms — which ANSI says RR may permit); lost update `ALLOWED` at RU+RC then `safe(err)`; **write skew and double-booking `ALLOWED` at ALL FIVE LEVELS including the one named SERIALIZABLE.** Vendor-confirmed, not just measured: H2's own PR says its SERIALIZABLE "prevents all three read phenomena, but doesn't ensure equivalence of sequential and concurrent execution" (h2database#2148). Unscripted racing: **196–200 of 200 rounds broken at every level**.
- **The three fixes for lost update, measured side by side** — and the winner is not an isolation level: `UPDATE SET n = n + 1` (arithmetic in SQL) gives **320/320 with 0 retries in 26 ms**, correct at *every* level, because a single statement takes the lock and *then* reads. `SELECT … FOR UPDATE` also 320/320, pays in waiting. Raising to snapshot/serializable gives 320/320 but costs 75–250 retries and **only works if you wrote the retry loop**. *Before reaching for the dial, check whether the read can move into the write.*
- **Contention is a data-access-pattern property, not an isolation property.** Identical work at an identical level, spread over more rows: **282 retries / 247 ms on 1 hot row → 1 retry / 17 ms on 16 rows.** "We turned on serializable and the app fell over" is nearly always a hot-row problem wearing an isolation costume. Separately: **deadlocks are a lock-ORDERING bug** — 57 retries with arbitrary order, **0** when both transactions always lock the lower id first, same level, same work.
- **The three mechanisms, and each one's price** (all four implemented in `MiniDb.java`, a 250-line MVCC engine): **2PL** pays in *waiting* (readers block writers — the reason MVCC won); **snapshot isolation** pays in *keeping old versions* (one open snapshot pins cleanup for the whole database — this is what VACUUM is for, and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` exists for it); **SSI** pays in *refusals at commit time*. There is no fourth option that costs nothing. The difference between snapshot and serializable is literally one method: delete `noteReadWriteConflicts` and exactly one matrix cell flips (write skew @ SSI → ALLOWED) — snapshot checks **what you wrote**, serializable also checks **what you read**.
- **Why write skew is the hard one:** the two transactions write *different rows*, so no write-write check can fire; the thing they shared was a **rule they both read**, and that rule lives in an `if` statement in the application where the database has never looked. SSI catches it by recording an **rw-antidependency** arrow from reader to writer and refusing any transaction with an arrow **in AND out** (a *pivot*). Aborting only the pivot matters: filtering out already-aborted transactions leaves 1 doctor on call; not filtering refuses both and leaves 2 — safe but wasteful, and under load the waste compounds.
- Corollary worth keeping: **read skew is a property of the READER, not the writer** — the transfer is already atomic and it happens anyway, because the reader took two separate looks. Fix by giving the reader one snapshot, or by making it ask one question (`SELECT SUM(...)` — a single statement is a single moment at every level). Measured: a report reading 4 balances while transfers ran got the wrong total **96 of 300 times at read committed, 0 of 300 under snapshot**.
- **A constraint beats an isolation level whenever the rule can be stated about the data rather than the decision** — `UNIQUE (room, day)` ends the double-booking argument at every level, for free. Reach: an isolation level protects the code paths that remember to ask; a constraint protects the table, including the migration script and the support engineer's psql session.
- Key terms now known: transaction/ACID, isolation level, schedule, serial vs serializable, anomaly/phenomenon, two-phase locking (2PL), deadlock + lock ordering, MVCC, version, snapshot, snapshot isolation, first-committer-wins, serialization failure (SQLSTATE 40001) vs deadlock (40P01), dirty write, dirty read, non-repeatable read, phantom, predicate lock / range lock / gap lock, lost update, read skew, write skew, SSI, rw-antidependency, pivot, vacuum/bloat, cursor stability (by name), retry loop, optimistic locking (= first-committer-wins in the app)
- Production facts worth keeping: **PostgreSQL** `READ UNCOMMITTED` silently means read committed (MVCC cannot produce a dirty read); its `REPEATABLE READ` is snapshot isolation and **allows write skew**; its `SERIALIZABLE` is real SSI. **Oracle** offers only RC and SERIALIZABLE, and **its SERIALIZABLE is snapshot isolation — write skew permitted**. **MySQL/InnoDB** defaults to RR where plain `SELECT` reads a snapshot but **`SELECT … FOR UPDATE`/`UPDATE`/`DELETE` read the latest committed row** (a split personality that makes read-modify-write behave differently from Postgres at the same level name); its `SERIALIZABLE` turns plain SELECTs into locking reads, i.e. back to 2PL, so it fails with lock timeouts/deadlocks rather than 40001. **SQL Server** RC is lock-based unless `READ_COMMITTED_SNAPSHOT` is on (a database-wide switch that changes app behaviour with no code change); its SERIALIZABLE is 2PL with range locks. **CockroachDB** is serializable by default. **Spring:** `@Transactional(isolation=…)` without a retry is a bug — and the retry must re-run the whole method, because a serialization failure invalidates everything the transaction *read*, not just the failed statement.
- Lab contents: 11 Java files (`java -cp ../lib/h2.jar src/Tests.java` = 108 checks incl. every cell of both matrices; `src/Matrix.java [anomaly]` = the real-database table + step-by-step logs; `src/MiniLab.java [anomaly]` = the same anomalies on the toy engine with the internals printed; `src/Experiments.java` = 6 measured experiments: increment/contention/skew/stability/versions/deadlock). Planted bugs verified: read-tracking removed (1 cell flips), RC reading from startTs (3 cells flip, lost update conspicuously does NOT), pivot ignoring aborted txns (2 tests), dirty-write check on COMMITTED (3 tests), and one *unreachable* bug (`>=` vs `>` in first-committer-wins — nothing changes, because timestamps are unique). `visualization.html` (published artifact, "Two Clerks, One Ledger") draws the textbook two-column schedule with version chains, each clerk's view side by side, SSI arrows appearing as they form, ▲▼ to reorder the schedule (moving a COMMIT earlier makes every anomaly vanish — concurrency is the input), and a strip showing the same schedule's verdict at all 5 levels at once. `validate-visualization.sh` cross-checks the browser engine against the Java one — all 40 shared cells agree.
- Builds on: [linearizability] (its `AtomicInteger` 87%-lost experiment IS the lost-update anomaly; that lab's closing line "read-then-write is two operations" is this lab's stage 2 — **linearizability = many machines, one operation; isolation = one machine, many operations**, and Spanner's external consistency is the corner where you buy both), [two-phase-commit] (its blocking problem is 2PL's, one level up), [btree-and-lsm] (MVCC version chains live in those pages; its `mvcc-and-vacuum?` suggestion is now earned), [event-sourcing] (expected-version IS first-committer-wins in the application), [saga] ("trades atomicity and isolation for availability" now has content: visible intermediate states = dirty reads and read skew by design; semantic locks claw some back), [database-landscape] · Related: [caching] + [quorums-and-replication] (a stale replica or cache produces IDENTICAL symptoms to read skew and no isolation level touches it — the most expensive misdiagnosis in this area) · Next: [mvcc-and-vacuum?], [database-locking?], [optimistic-vs-pessimistic-locking?], [distributed-transactions], [database-indexing?]

## raft — learned 2026-08-29 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-08 — lab: 40-raft/

- Takeaway: Raft keeps several machines holding **the same list of changes in the same order** by electing one leader per numbered **term** and funnelling every write through it; a write is committed the moment a **majority** have stored it, and the only servers allowed to win the next election are those whose log is at least as complete as the majority's — which is what makes a committed write impossible to lose. It is [paxos]'s "bolt a leader on top" designed on purpose, and the paper's actual research goal was *understandability*.
- **The sentence to be able to say:** *"Raft guarantees one leader" is false; "one leader per term, and only the newest can commit" is true.* Two leaders coexist routinely (measured every time you split 2|3) and Raft does not prevent split-brain — it makes the losing half **harmless**, which is far cheaper. Second sentence worth keeping: **"a majority has it" and "it can never be removed" are not the same statement** (Figure 8).
- The four rules that look arbitrary, each the scar of one measured disaster — this is the spine of the lab: (1) **randomised election timeouts** — identical ones give *term 40 in 6000 ticks, leader NEVER, 0 safety violations*; random gives 200/200 cold starts at 155/174/332 ticks (min/p50/max), 1 split resolved next term. Randomisation doesn't prevent split votes, it makes them **self-correcting**. (2) **the prevLogIndex/prevLogTerm prefix check** — one (index,term) comparison certifies unlimited history by induction (**Log Matching**); disable it and a follower reports "I'm at index 3" holding nothing, leaving a permanent hole. (3) **the up-to-date vote check** — disable it and a *committed* write is deleted (`index 1 was committed as committed-write@t1 but S2 has committed overwrite@t2`); it works because two majorities overlap, so some voter holds the entry and refuses. (4) **the own-term commit rule** — Figure 8, driven by hand in `Tests.figure8`: an entry on 4 of 5 servers, reported committed, is overwritten by a server holding a *later-term* entry at the same index → **12 distinct violations**. Old entries commit for free *underneath* a new one, which is why real leaders append a **no-op** on winning.
- Measured behaviour worth remembering: leader crash → leaderless for **154/182/243/488 ticks** (min/p50/p95/max over 200 runs), **0** new leaders missing the committed write. Message loss: **safety never degrades at any rate including 90%**; liveness degrades smoothly then collapses (30/30 commit at 30% loss, **7/30** at 50%, 0/30 at 70%) — election churn is always a *timing* problem, never a correctness one, which is why "leader elections per minute" is the one Raft alarm. Heartbeat vs timeout: stable to 140 against a 150–300 timeout, **48 terms burned per 10k ticks** at 200 (`broadcastTime ≪ electionTimeout ≪ MTBF`; etcd ships 10x). Log repair cost depends only on where `nextIndex` was at election time: a follower **6 entries behind** costs 6 rejections, a follower with **5 wrong entries** costs 0 (one conflicting entry truncates the whole tail) — being behind is expensive, being wrong can be free. Soak: 20 clusters x 300 fault rounds, 898 writes, 2550 elections, **0** violations, checked after every tick.
- Two modifications that fail instructively (both verified by running): `majority() = N/2` instead of `N/2+1` → two *disjoint* groups of 2 in a 5-cluster → `LOG MATCHING broken` + `STATE MACHINE SAFETY broken`; half-plus-one is the smallest number that **forces intersection**. And scanning `advanceCommit` *upward* stopping at the first failure → **permanent stall** (`commitIndex=0` where downward gives 2), because the own-term rule makes committable indices **non-contiguous**.
- Key terms now known: log/entry/index, state machine (replicated state machine), term (a logical clock costing one integer; the successor to paxos's proposal number), follower/candidate/leader, election timeout, heartbeat (= an empty AppendEntries; silence is the only failure detector), RequestVote, AppendEntries, votedFor, prevLogIndex/prevLogTerm, nextIndex (a guess) vs matchIndex (a fact), committed vs applied, commitIndex/lastApplied, Log Matching, Election Safety, Leader Append-Only, Leader Completeness, State Machine Safety, up-to-date check, own-term commit rule, Figure 8, split vote, safety vs liveness (independent — proven both ways in the lab), no-op entry, pre-vote, ReadIndex vs lease read, snapshot/InstallSnapshot, joint consensus, learner, multi-raft
- What Raft does NOT give you, each stated in the lab: linearizable **reads** are not free (leader reads can be stale in a partition — needs ReadIndex or a lease, and a lease is a bet on bounded clock drift); no Byzantine tolerance; **no write scale-out** (more servers = more fault tolerance and more latency, never more throughput — scale means many Raft groups, i.e. multi-raft + [consistent-hashing]); no progress below a majority, by design.
- Lab contents: 12 Java files, no deps (`java src/Tests.java` = **56** scenario checks incl. all five planted-bug pairs; `java src/Experiments.java` = 11 measured experiments — election/replication/leader-crash/split-vote/partition/log-repair/loss/stale-candidate/figure8/heartbeat/soak). `Cluster.checkSafety()` asserts all five properties **after every tick**. `Bugs.java` = five switches, one per rule. `visualization.html` (published artifact, "The Raft Log Grid") lays servers out as **rows and log indices as columns**, so divergence is literally two colours in one column; dashed = stored, solid = committed, colour = term; step/step-back/play, click a row to crash or inspect it, a **tear-the-network selector** (the partition point is a control, not a hardcoded choice), drop-rate and delay sliders, a random/identical timeout switch, and six predict-then-run cards. Its protocol port is verified by 10 headless behavioural tests. README with 4 depth layers, 5 prediction checkpoints and the v1-breaks-then-refine loop for all four rules; EXERCISES + SOLUTIONS (8 rungs; rung 5 = implement three rules in `ImplExercise.java`, rung 6 = three planted bugs in `DebugMe.java` where the *invisible-on-a-healthy-cluster* one is the safety bug; rung 8 covers a 3-datacentre payments config store and where Raft stops in a payment flow).
- Builds on: [paxos] (**this is the answer to that lab's closing line** — Raft *is* "bolt a leader on top", and its term is paxos's proposal number spent on shifts rather than single values; Multi-Paxos permits log holes and out-of-order commits, Raft forbids both and that is most of why it is easier), [quorums-and-replication] (its **overlap rule is used twice here** — no two leaders in a term, and no leader missing a committed entry; also its *consensus quorum decides / read-write quorum merely intersects* distinction, where Raft is emphatically the first), [two-phase-commit] (a coordinator whose death **blocks**; Raft's leader dies routinely and costs one election timeout, because leadership is a lease decided by vote, not an appointment — and the two compose: real distributed DBs run 2PC across shards, each shard replicated by Raft), [cap-theorem] (the CP corner made concrete: the minority side *refuses* rather than guesses), [event-sourcing] (the log **is** an event stream folded into state; Raft supplies the replication that lab assumed), [crdts] (**the same boundary from the other side** — CRDTs make everyone agree without deciding, and cannot enforce an invariant; Raft decides, and makes the minority stop), [transactional-outbox] (a timeout is *not* a failure — an uncommitted entry may still commit, so client retries must be idempotent), [tls] (peer transport) · Related: [gossip-protocols] (spreads facts that will eventually agree, cheaply and leaderlessly; Raft decides facts that must agree immediately, expensively), [consistent-hashing] (multi-raft: one group per shard is how Raft scales), [redis] (its Cluster failover election is Raft-shaped but not Raft) · Next: [linearizability?], [multi-raft?], [raft-membership-changes?], [kafka?] (KRaft is Raft; ISR is not), [flp-impossibility]

## crdts — learned 2026-08-29 — lab: 32-crdts/

- Takeaway: If two machines must both accept writes while unable to talk, the fix is not a better conflict resolver — it is a data type whose **merge** is commutative, associative and idempotent, so that reordering, rebatching and duplicate delivery have nothing left to break. Formally the state is a point in a join-semilattice, local updates only ever climb it, and merge is the least upper bound; that single picture *derives* the three laws rather than assuming them. The promise is **strong eventual consistency**: replicas that received the same *set* of updates are identical immediately, with no repair job and no coordination. Measured: 7 CRDT types pass all three laws, 7 pending messages delivered in all **5,040** orders give **1** final state, and 500 random 3-editor RGA sessions at 30% loss + 30% duplication diverged **0** times.
- **The sentence to be able to say:** *CRDTs guarantee that everyone ends up agreeing. They do not guarantee that what everyone agrees on was ever allowed.* 10 bags of flour, two partitioned warehouses each selling 6, merged stock = **−2** — no global invariant (`stock ≥ 0`, uniqueness, `balance ≥ 0`) is enforceable without coordination. That is the CRDT/consensus boundary, and it is drawn in §12 as a decision tree.
- The design ladder, each rung the scar of a measured failure: single number (two +1s = 1, the *state* threw away what merge needed) → **G-Counter** (row per replica, merge = per-row max) → decrement by lowering your row is undone by merge → **PN-Counter** (two grow-only piles, value = P−N) → **G-Set** (union, cannot remove) → **2P-Set** (blacklist by value; re-add impossible — item present in **0/100** carts, converging *perfectly onto the wrong answer*) → **OR-Set** (every add mints a **dot** = `(replica, counter)`; remove tears up only *observed* dots → re-add works **100/100**, and concurrent add-vs-remove is **add-wins 1000/1000** in both merge directions, as a consequence of "you may only remove what you observed", not as a tie-break) → **LWW-Register** (legal CRDT; the replica-id tie-break is load-bearing) vs **MV-Register** (keeps siblings, version-vector dominance drops ancestors) → **RGA** (name an identity, never a position).
- Numbers worth remembering: last-write-wins destroyed **67%** of 8,992 offline edits (2,971 survived); G-Counter lost 0. A clock **60 s fast destroyed 1,000/1,000** genuinely-newer writes, silently — under LWW *your clock is the conflict-resolution algorithm*. Sum-merge instead of max-merge reads **11** where the truth is 2 after ten deliveries (idempotence is the only thing between you and a counter that inflates on retry). Metadata tax: a cart with **10 visible items stored 500 records (50/item, 250 tombstones)**; a 206-char RGA document stored 403 cells (**2.0 cells/char**).
- **State-based vs op-based** is the axis that matters operationally: state-based (this lab) needs *nothing* from the network and a wiped replica repairs itself in one ordinary sync (measured: 0 → 60); op-based ships one operation instead of a snapshot but **requires causal delivery** — delivered backwards, add/remove diverges permanently (`[]` vs `[milk]`), which is exactly the bug the google-docs lab hit in its own CRDT. δ-state CRDTs are the modern middle (Riak, Akka). Also: RGA *converges* with any dot numbers but only preserves **intention** with Lamport-numbered dots (plain 1,2,3 gives everyone `HATSC` instead of `CHATS`) — convergence and intention preservation are two properties and only one is free.
- The bug the lab found in itself, worth generalising: the hand-written law check used five sample states and passed; a **randomised** law fuzz (50,000 triples sharing a history then diverging) immediately failed `a.merge(a) == a` on 995 of them — `OrSet.add` was re-inserting a dot that was already tombstoned. **For a CRDT the laws ARE the specification, so fuzz them on generated states.** Same fuzz proved a naive "remove-wins" patch breaks associativity on 1,696/50,000 while the fixed-sample check saw nothing. Real remove-wins is not a merge change at all: it needs a causal claim per removed element (a version vector), which is what Riak's ORSWOT stores.
- Operational reality: nothing frees tombstones by itself; freeing one needs **causal stability** (every replica has seen the delete), which needs membership + liveness (gossip) and stalls forever on one unreachable replica — so eviction is a *coordinated* decision wrapped around a coordination-free type. Corollary rule: **never restore a CRDT replica from backup into a live cluster** — bootstrap it empty and let it re-merge; a restore into a GC'd cluster resurrects deleted data permanently. Watch: metadata size per key, sync lag, GC backlog, sibling count, clock skew (only if LWW).
- Key terms now known: replica, merge, commutative/associative/idempotent (the three laws), converge, strong eventual consistency (SEC), CRDT, state-based/CvRDT vs op-based/CmRDT, join-semilattice, least upper bound/join (⊔), monotonic, G-Counter, PN-Counter, G-Set, 2P-Set, dot, tombstone, OR-Set/observed-remove, add-wins vs remove-wins, LWW-Register, MV-Register, sibling, RGA, Lamport counter, intention preservation, causal delivery, δ-state CRDT, ORSWOT, causal stability, global invariant
- Lab shape: 17 Java files, no deps (`java src/Tests.java` = 46 guarantees incl. a 50k-triple law fuzz and a 500-session RGA fuzz; `java src/Tests.java broken` = 3 planted bugs, each breaking exactly one law, 3/3 caught by the laws alone; `java src/Experiments.java [1-10]` = ten measurements; `java src/Sim.java` = five narrated scenes; `java src/YourMerge.java` = blanked-out OR-Set merge, self-checking 2/5 → 5/5). `visualization.html` ("Merge Without a Referee", <https://claude.ai/code/artifact/fea9c00f-2004-4f4f-a867-7d6048751fdc>) shows *what the user sees* vs *what is stored* per phone, four competing designs as tabs, hand-delivered messages you can duplicate/drop/reorder, partition + clock-skew controls, step-back, and 8 predict-then-run situations verified headlessly against the Java. README with 4 depth layers, 6 prediction checkpoints, a CRDT-vs-consensus decision tree, and a 33-term glossary. NOT built: deltas, tombstone GC, persistence, real transport, membership changes, nested maps of CRDTs, undo, compression — all named as omissions in §15.
- Builds on: [quorums-and-replication] (version vectors, siblings, and the 1000/1000 lost updates under LWW — this lab is the fix for that measurement; also its wiped-replica failure, which is a non-event here), [google-docs-system-design] (OT as the other answer; RGA already met there; its causal-delivery bug reproduced here as experiment 9), [gossip-protocols] (anti-entropy is the transport, and membership is what tombstone GC needs), [cap-theorem] (this is the AP corner, chosen deliberately), [event-sourcing] (the opposite strategy: *refuse* the conflicting write via expected-version, which is why ledgers are not CRDTs), [btree-and-lsm] (tombstones, compaction), [paxos]/[two-phase-commit] (the referee you buy when you need to say no) · Related: [redis] (Redis *Enterprise* CRDBs; open-source Redis has none), [distributed-rate-limiting], [cqrs] (eventual consistency, weaker than SEC) · Next: [lamport-clocks?], [causal-consistency?], [local-first-software?], [raft]

## linearizability — learned 2026-08-29 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-09 — lab: 26-linearizability/
