- Takeaway: Linearizability is the precise meaning of "consistent": the system must be **indistinguishable from outside from one copy handling one request at a time**, which means you can pick a single instant inside each request's `[sent, answered]` stretch such that reading them in instant-order reproduces every answer clients actually got. It is a property of the **observed history** — clients, times, answers — never of the implementation, which is why it can be *checked* against a black box. The one rule that makes it strict is real-time order: if operation A was already answered before B was sent, A must come first in any explanation. Delete that one comparison and you have sequential consistency.
- **The gap this lab closes from [quorums-and-replication].** R+W>N stops a read missing a **completed** write. It says nothing about a write **in flight**: reader X reads {A,B} and sees the new value; reader Y, starting *after X was answered*, reads {B,C} and sees the old one. Both quorums are legal, nothing is misconfigured, and time ran backwards. **The fix is the ABD read write-back**: before returning a value, push it to a quorum so no later reader can un-see it. One extra round trip, measured mean read latency 11.7 → 24.4 ticks.
- **The measured numbers (600 histories each, N=3, R=W=2, 1 writer + 3 readers, 15% message hiccups):** SINGLE **0**, LEADER **0**, QUORUM_ABD **0** · QUORUM_NAIVE **36 (6%)**, ASYNC **587 (97.8%)**, CACHED **560 (93.3%)**. The most important row in the whole lab is the jitter sweep: the broken quorum design produces **0 violations in 600 runs on a calm network** and 109/600 at 40% hiccups — *a design with no guarantee at all is indistinguishable from the correct one until the network misbehaves*. A green consistency test without injected latency is close to worthless.
- Results worth keeping: **a cache of ANY duration destroys it** — the store is 0/600 at every TTL, a **one-tick** client cache makes it 332/600 and a 10-tick cache 555/600, and **560 of those 560 violations are still sequentially consistent**, which is why they feel harmless. **Wall-clock write timestamps**: 0 violations at zero skew, 112/600 at 20 ticks, 270/600 at 60 — same quorum, same write-back, only the source of the ordering changed. **Making writes wait for everyone barely helps** (W=3 → 32 violations vs baseline 36) because the culprit is an in-flight write; **making reads ask everyone does fix it** (R=3,N=3 → 0) because a replica's timestamp never goes down, but it costs all your read availability, and R=3 of N=5 still leaves 15. **Lazy write-back** (skip it when the whole read quorum already agreed) is correct iff **2R > N** — verified: N=3,R=2 → 0 violations; N=5,R=2,W=4 → 6/600. Two read quorums that don't intersect each other can't keep each other in order.
- **Linearizable ≠ atomic sequence.** `AtomicInteger` is a linearizable register shipped by the JDK; 8 virtual threads × 20,000 get-then-set increments produce **21,051 of 160,000** (87% lost), and `compareAndSet` produces exactly 160,000. Nothing was violated. Read-then-write is two operations. That is the boundary between linearizability and serializability, and it is why `expected-version` in [event-sourcing] is the right tool.
- **Locality is why this property and not another.** A multi-object history is linearizable iff each object's projection is — measured 300/300 merged pairs. Sequential consistency does NOT compose (two individually-SC objects combine into a history that is not SC). So you can make every key linearizable independently, with zero coordination, and the whole store is linearizable. Nothing else on the ladder does that.
- **Checking is NP-hard; being linearizable is free.** On 18 fully-concurrent operations the search explores **79,457,057** states without memoisation and **239,506** with (330×, identical verdicts). This is why Jepsen chops a run into one small history per key, and why a leader answering reads — which does no searching at all — is the cheap way to *provide* what is expensive to *verify*.
- Key terms now known: history, invocation/response, concurrent vs sequential history, linearization point, sequential specification, legal history, real-time order, linearizable, linearization, pending/indeterminate operation, sequential consistency, serializability, strict serializability, locality/composability, non-blocking, ABD, read write-back, (timestamp, writer) tie-break, lease, read-index, fencing token, external consistency (Spanner), violation minimiser, Jepsen/Knossos/Porcupine/Elle
- Production facts worth keeping: **ZooKeeper reads are NOT linearizable** (served locally, arbitrarily stale) — `sync()` before a read is what makes one linearizable; writes always are. **etcd** reads ARE linearizable by default via read-index; `--consistency=s` opts down. **Spanner's "external consistency"** = strict serializability, bought by *waiting out* TrueTime's uncertainty interval. **DynamoDB** `ConsistentRead=true` costs 2× read capacity. **Cassandra QUORUM is not linearizability** — lightweight transactions (a Paxos round per operation, ~4× latency) are. **Redis**: a single node is linearizable because it is single-threaded; async replication means failover can lose acknowledged writes, and Redlock is unsafe across pauses — use a **fencing token** checked by the *resource*. **Postgres** `synchronous_commit=remote_apply` is the write-back idea in a different costume.
- Lab contents: 7 Java files, no deps (`java src/Tests.java` = 41 scenario checks; `java src/Experiments.java` = 10 measured experiments: stores/jitter/writeback/realtime/cost/cache/clock/lostupdate/checkercost/locality). The centrepiece is a real Wing & Gong linearizability checker with memoisation, a sequential-consistency mode behind one flag, a per-object model (locality), pending-operation handling, and a **violation minimiser** that shrinks a 20-operation history to the 3 operations that actually contradict. Two planted bugs (`Checker.Opts.dropPending`, `Sim.noTiebreak`) and one real optimisation with a hidden precondition (`Sim.lazyWriteBack`). `visualization.html` (published artifact, "The Single-Copy Illusion") draws the history as draggable bars — **drag a bar and the verdict flips with no answer changed**, which is the whole concept in one gesture — plus draggable linearization dots, a live single-notebook replay that reddens the first contradicting step, a real-time-rule switch that turns it into a sequential-consistency checker, 9 predict-then-run situations, and a generator that runs six real replication designs and reports how many runs it had to search to find a broken one.
- Builds on: [quorums-and-replication] (the in-flight write R+W>N cannot see; version vectors/read repair/anti-entropy are *convergence*, never *ordering*), [cap-theorem] (CAP's C **is** this property — Gilbert & Lynch state the theorem about linearizability specifically), [paxos] (now has a purpose statement: consensus exists to make a single ordering authority survive the death of the authority), [java-memory-model] (`AtomicInteger`/`volatile` are linearizable objects; happens-before is this idea inside one machine), [caching] + [redis] (a cache in front of a correct store gives an incorrect system), [event-sourcing] (expected-version is the compare-and-set the lost-update experiment proves you need), [two-phase-commit] · Related: [consistent-hashing] (which replicas a quorum is drawn from), [distributed-rate-limiting] (a read that should NOT be linearizable) · Next: [raft?], [logical-clocks?], [isolation-levels?], [crdts?], [jepsen?]

## quorums-and-replication — learned 2026-08-29 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-08 — lab: 34-quorums-and-replication/

- Takeaway: Keeping N copies of a key means a reader can silently get an old one, and the fix is one counting argument: tell **W** replicas, ask **R** of them, and if **R + W > N** the two sets cannot avoid each other, so the read must touch a replica that has the last successful write. Measured over 4,000 rounds at 20% independent replica failure, the boundary is exact and binary — every configuration with R+W>N had **0** stale reads, every one without had some (R=W=1 → 784; R=2,W=1 → 119, i.e. "correct 97% of the time", which is the configuration that ships by accident). The same table is the price list: W=1 writes succeed 99.2% vs W=3 at 51.2%, R=1 reads 99.2% vs R=3 at 50.3% — which is why R=W=2 at N=3 is the universal default (cheapest crossing of the line) and why the right way to buy more failure tolerance is to raise **N**, not R and W (N=5,R=3,W=3 = same guarantee, tolerates two dead replicas).
- **The confusion this lab exists to kill: there are two different things called "quorum".** The [paxos] one is a *majority* used to make nodes **agree** on a value and an order. This one is any R,W with R+W>N, used to make two sets **overlap**. They coincide at "2 of 3", which is why they get merged in people's heads. *A consensus quorum decides; a read/write quorum merely intersects.* R+W>N is **not** linearizability: no global order, concurrent writes unordered, two concurrent readers can disagree.
- Five situations where R+W>N still holds and the guarantee does not — the real content: (1) **a failed write is never rolled back** — 500/500 "failed" writes were readable afterwards, so "failed" means "not enough replicas confirmed", never "nothing happened" (⇒ writes must be idempotent); (2) **concurrent writes** — overlap says you'll SEE a value, not who won: over 1,000 pairs, last-write-wins silently discarded **1000** updates and version vectors discarded **0** (surfacing 1000 sibling conflicts instead); (3) **a replica that lost its data** — the argument counts replicas, not copies, so two rebuilt-empty replicas satisfy R=2 and return nothing (⇒ bootstrap before serving reads); (4) **sloppy quorum** — acks collected from nodes that don't own the key, so the read set has nothing to intersect: 300/300 writes accepted during a partition, only **200/300** visible to a strict read until hinted handoff ran; (5) **nothing converges by itself** — read repair only fixes what is read (100 of 200 keys), anti-entropy fixes the rest (200/200), and read repair does **not** rescue a bad R/W choice (measured: stale 784→784, 119→118, because it repairs *after* returning the stale value).
- Corollaries worth keeping: under last-write-wins **your clock IS the conflict-resolution algorithm** — one node 60 s fast threw away 1000/1000 genuinely-newer writes with no error. A quorum is also a **tail-latency** device: with replicas stalling 10% of the time, waiting for 3 of 3 makes **27.31%** of requests slow vs **2.85%** for 2 of 3. In the leader-based family, W's twin is the ack mode, and the trap is that "wait for all *live* followers" refuses **0** writes when both followers are down — `acks=all` silently degrades to `acks=1`, which is exactly what `min.insync.replicas` exists to stop (`acks=all` + `min.insync=2` on RF 3 **is** W=2,N=3). Replication lag is a **queue**: apply rate below write rate ⇒ backlog grows without bound (300 and climbing at 2:1), and read-your-own-writes / monotonic reads / consistent prefix are *routing* fixes, not storage ones.
- The bug the lab hit, worth remembering: writing with an **empty version context** made every write's vector identical, so `compare` said EQUAL and replicas ignored every new write — i.e. in a version-vector store, a client that does not carry forward the version it read is writing something indistinguishable from a stale retry. **Read-before-write is how a write acquires its place in the order.**
- Key terms now known: replica, replication factor N, W, R, coordinator (no authority — unlike 2PC's), consensus quorum vs read/write quorum, overlap rule, stale read, partial write, concurrent writes, version vector (BEFORE/AFTER/CONCURRENT/EQUAL), sibling, last-write-wins, read repair, anti-entropy, Merkle tree, sloppy quorum, hinted handoff (best-effort, not durable — the holder can die with the only copy), primary-ack count (`pw`), leader/follower, sync/async/semi-sync, in-sync replicas (ISR), min.insync.replicas, replication lag as a queue, read-your-own-writes, monotonic reads, consistent prefix, bootstrap, tombstone, gc_grace_seconds, LOCAL_QUORUM/EACH_QUORUM, linearizability (as the thing this is NOT)
- Lab contents: 8 Java files, no deps (`java src/Tests.java` = 42 scenario checks; `java src/Experiments.java` = 10 measured experiments: overlap/availability/partial/concurrent/clockskew/sloppy/repair/leader/lag/latency; three planted bugs behind `bug=` flags, each disabling one invariant). `visualization.html` (published artifact, "The Overlap Rule") renders the write set and read set as two bands with the replicas between them — the overlap column is literally the blend of the two colours; R/W/N sliders, step/step-back, kill a replica, cut the network, sloppy + read-repair + conflict-mode switches, and a "which replica does the coordinator ask first" control that turns the hidden routing assumption into an experiment. README with four depth layers and 6 prediction checkpoints; EXERCISES + SOLUTIONS (8 rungs — incl. the instructive failure that two concurrent compare-and-sets BOTH succeed, which is where you discover you need consensus, and a payments rung on where a quorum is and is not acceptable for a ledger).
- Builds on: [paxos] (the other quorum, and the tool you buy when overlap is not enough — Cassandra's lightweight transactions are a Paxos round per operation), [cap-theorem] (R and W turn the CP/AP switch into a per-request dial), [consistent-hashing] (which N machines are the replicas at all; its 1/n disagreement window is a window in which two clients' quorums may not intersect), [database-landscape] (masterless + consistency window, now with the mechanism), [event-sourcing] (expected-version solves the same conflict by *refusing* rather than preserving; append-only data has no conflicts, which is why a ledger append is the best case for a quorum), [btree-and-lsm] (what a replica is underneath; tombstones), [transactional-outbox] (idempotent writes, which the unrolled-back failed write makes mandatory), [two-phase-commit] (a coordinator with authority whose death blocks; this coordinator's death costs a retry), [caching] (a stale replica and a stale cache entry are one object with two names) · Related: [gossip-protocols] (how nodes learn the replica set and liveness this lab assumes; also anti-entropy's transport), [whatsapp-system-design] ("store before you acknowledge" is W≥1 in words), [uber-system-design] (its geo index AP / driver claim CP is this lab's two halves in one product) · Next: [raft], [linearizability?], [crdts?], [partitioning-and-rebalancing?], [change-data-capture?]

## work-pressure-and-deadlines — learned 2026-08-29 — lab: 61-work-pressure-and-deadlines/

- Takeaway: The only useful version of this topic is quantitative, because advice loses to advice — the manager also has advice, and theirs is "just get it done". Two sentences generate everything: **a date is a bet, not a fact about the work** (say the odds out loud or the room assumes 100%), and **there are only four levers — scope, time, people, quality — of which exactly one moves fast and honestly: scope.** The lab is a runnable team simulator (Java, no deps) that prices every move people actually make. Headline: an honestly-estimated 5.1-week plan ships in **15.7 weeks (2.6×)** with nobody underperforming, and the decomposition of paid time is the killer exhibit — **57.4% became features**; the rest was support/interrupts (23%), coordination (8%), idle-at-the-tail (6%), the team's own rework (5%). The spreadsheet assumed 100%.
- The plan was wrong on day one, before reality intervened: with **no interrupts, no bugs, no meetings and zero task variability** the same project still takes **9.6 weeks against a 5.1-week estimate**. Two causes, and neither is fixable by effort: an estimate is the **median** of the happy path while a schedule pays the **mean** (measured 28% undercount at σ=0.7, theory `median·e^{σ²/2}` confirmed to 0.01d), plus ~20% happy-path optimism. Corollary that surprises everyone (**Ex 4.1**): turning off task variability *entirely* — perfect estimation of all 40 tasks — saves **0.4 weeks**, because independent overruns cancel. Precision does not fix bias; "estimate better" is aimed at the wrong target. (Caveat stated in-lab: no task dependencies, so variance cancels here and accumulates on a real critical path.)
- Every intervention priced, all averaged over 40 seeded universes with **common random numbers** (both plans face the identical universe of work, so the measurement is the decision and not the luck): **cut 40% of unstarted work at wk4 → 12.0** · crunch 60h → 14.6 · +2 engineers → 14.9 · hold → 15.7. Scope cut early is worth **5.1 weeks at wk1 and 0.7 at wk12** — the lesson is not "cut scope", it is *cut early or do not bother*. Crunch is honestly nuanced: the 60h team is **36% ahead at week 4** and stays ahead ~12 weeks, then loses (ships **+0.7 wks**, **2.7× escaped defects**, **57% more hours**); but a **bounded last-two-weeks push ships 0.6 wks earlier than not crunching** (Ex 4.4) — a short push is a legitimate decision with a known price, a quarter of them is paying more for less. Brooks has a crossover: +2 engineers at wk0 = **−3.0 wks**, wk8 ≈ break-even, wk12 = **+0.9**. Two separate traps: 7 engineers with WIP still 5 is **worse than 5 engineers** (16.4 vs 15.7) — capacity you cannot split into parallel work is not capacity.
- The two formulas worth memorising: **interrupt tax is `1/(1−f)`, a division not a subtraction** — 25% of a team to support is a **33%** delay, 50% is double (sim tracked 1.31 and 1.93 vs theory 1.33/2.00); and **queueing delay is `ρ/(1−ρ)`** — at 80% busy a request takes **5×** its own work time, at 95% **19×**, at 98% **58×** (worse than Kingman's 50 because service times are fat-tailed). "We're all fully allocated" is a diagnosis, not a status report. **WIP is a U-curve** with both walls fatal: WIP 1 → 72 weeks (994 idle engineer-days), WIP 5 (=team size) → 15.7, WIP 40 → 26.3 and **1.4 working features by week 6 against 17.1** — and the date is the lesser damage, because half-built work cannot be cut, so high WIP destroys your ability to negotiate. **Scope creep is the largest single term in most real forecasts**: 1.5 items/week against a throughput of 5 moved P50 from **week 12 to week 18**.
- The tool to actually use: **Monte Carlo from throughput history** (`Forecast.simulate`, bootstrap over the team's own last 10 weeks). Average velocity said week 12 and week 12 came true **53%** of the time; P85 was week 14; the promised week 10 was a **4%** bet. Then the sentence that changes the meeting: **cut 20 of 60 items and week 10 goes from 4% to 97%**. Never "we can't do it" — always the exact size of the gap in units of work plus the decision that closes it. Ex 5.3 measured Goldratt: per-task padding + Parkinson ships **18.6 vs 15.7** and meets an honest week-16 promise **10% vs 60%** of the time — a padded task absorbs its buffer and can never give it back, so buffer once, at project level, in public.
- Human half (§15 + PLAYBOOK.md, 12 paste-ready scripts): diagnose which of four deadlines you have (**anchor** / **hard external** / **manufactured urgency** / **real business constraint**) with the question *"what happens if this lands two weeks later?"* — evidence only works on the first. Never accept and never refuse; convert to a forecast and hand back a decision with three dated options. The highest-value habit is boring: **the same four-line forecast every week whether or not it moved**, which turns a courage problem into a calendar problem and makes the week-9 crisis structurally impossible. Price new work instead of refusing it ("4 days, moves wk12→wk13, in or out?"). When overruled: say it once in writing, then execute. Watch **the ratchet** — heroics silently become the baseline, so hours must be reported next to features *while* being paid.
- Lab shape: 11 Java files, no deps (`java src/Tests.java` = 17 scenario checks named after the doc's claims; `java src/Experiments.java <name>` = 8 measurements in ~1.4s; `BrokenPlan.java` = a capacity sheet with 4 planted mistakes for Ex 6, incl. the defensible answer that you should NOT raise the estimation-bias one in the meeting — fix bias by changing the method, not by winning an argument about people's judgement). `visualization.html` (published artifact) runs the **identical model in JS with the same mulberry32 PRNG** — verified digit-for-digit against Java on 7 scenarios — with a luck slider showing the same plan shipping between **week 12.6 and 18.6** on luck alone. Exercises 7.1 (a 1-review/day bottleneck flattens the date at ~13 weeks whether the team is 12 or 16 — capacity past the constraint buys nothing) and 7.2 (bursty vs smooth interrupts measured *identical*, because the model's boundary hides the effect in cycle time) are the two that teach the most.
- Builds on: [cap-theorem] (same shape of argument — a set of properties you want all of, where the world forces a public trade), [io-multiplexing] + [distributed-rate-limiting] (the `ρ/(1−ρ)` collapse is the same maths; rate limiting is backpressure in code, "yes, and here is what comes out" is backpressure in a person), [caching] (a crunch is a transient that vanishes from the quarterly average, like a miss storm) · Related: [consistent-hashing] (statistical guarantees vs hard ones) · Next: **[queueing-theory] — DONE 2026-08-30**, which derives ρ/(1−ρ) and corrects this entry's pairing of that formula with the 5× figure (5× is 1/(1−ρ); ρ/(1−ρ)=4 at ρ=0.8), [technical-debt?] (the quality loop as a long-run balance sheet), [on-call-and-toil?] (where the interrupt tax comes from), [kafka?]

## gossip-protocols — learned 2026-08-29 — lab: 37-gossip-protocols/

- Takeaway: The whole protocol is one sentence — **every node, once a second, picks a couple of peers at random and tells them everything it knows** — and its power comes from multiplication: the informed set grows by a FACTOR each round, so rounds track log_(fanout+1)(n). Measured, fanout 3 push-pull: 2.0 rounds at n=10, 5.0 at n=1,000, 6.0 at n=5,000, while each node sends a flat **6 messages per round at every size** against all-to-all's 999 at n=1,000. The second half of the idea is what makes it correct rather than merely fast: the **merge rule must be a join** — keep whichever state is larger comparing `(incarnation, status, heartbeat)` in that fixed order — which is commutative, idempotent and monotonic, and that is precisely why gossip needs no ordering, no acknowledgements, no deduplication and no retries. I got this wrong while building the lab (took the max heartbeat and separately the worse status, which reads perfectly sensibly) and the order-independence test caught it: 40 orderings of the same five facts produced two permanently different states.
- The failures that shaped it: **the tail** (push reaches 90% of 500 nodes in 11.9 rounds and needs 16.5 for the last node, pull is the mirror image, push-pull does it in 8.3 — hence push-pull as the default); **the stop rule** (rumour mongering that stops after hearing a fact back once left 9 of 1,000 nodes never informed AND never able to recover, for a 6.4x message saving — so anti-entropy must run underneath); **loss** (80% packet loss costs 4.3x the rounds with zero retry logic — redundancy IS the retry); **the detection dial** (no setting is good at both columns: suspectAfter=1 spots a crash in 3.6 rounds and falsely accuses 98% of the cluster on a 4-round pause; suspectAfter=5 has no false alarms and takes 7.9 rounds); **refutation** (a node declared dead can only be revived by itself, via an incarnation number only it may raise — without it a GC pause is a permanent death); **one cut cable** (heartbeat-via-anyone never notices and keeps routing into a broken link; direct-evidence-only has 9.7 of 20 nodes bury a healthy peer; indirect probes fix it — "is n5 up?" is unanswerable, "can anyone reach n5?" is not).
- **The headline failure, and the one to remember: the spreading is robust and the JUDGING is fragile.** At 90% packet loss, 100 nodes with **zero crashed processes** ended with **100% of all beliefs saying DEAD** — every node wrote off every other node, stopped gossiping to them, and the cluster went permanently silent. A total outage manufactured entirely by its own failure detector, because silence is its only symptom and a bad network supplies unlimited silence.
- Key terms now known: gossip/epidemic protocol, round (gossip interval), fanout, convergence (eventual + probabilistic), push vs pull vs push-pull, the tail problem, heartbeat counter (why counters not clocks), incarnation number (only the node may raise it), refutation, failure detector as a fallible component, suspicion as an appealable verdict, ALIVE<SUSPECT<DEAD precedence, merge rule as a join (commutative/idempotent/monotonic), anti-entropy vs rumour mongering, stop rule / feedback counter, piggybacking, digest exchange, seed nodes, phi-accrual, SWIM (direct probe → indirect probe → suspicion → refutation), Lifeguard (self-doubt when you look unhealthy), PFAIL/FAIL quorum-gated verdicts, detector meltdown, Byzantine vs crash-stop
- Lab contents: 8 Java files, no deps (`java src/Sim.java` narrated 8-node afternoon, `java src/Experiments.java` ten measurements, `java src/Tests.java` 12 asserted guarantees, `java src/Tests.java broken` runs the planted-bug merge for the debug exercise), `visualization.html` = the Rumour Mill (20 nodes, every rule a switch, a belief matrix showing exactly who disagrees with whom, 7 predict-then-run situations), README with four depth layers, EXERCISES + SOLUTIONS.
- Builds on: [consistent-hashing] (**this lab is the box that one left closed** — it measured a 9.5% keyspace disagreement window during membership propagation and assumed the propagation away; experiment 9 measures the window itself: one crash leaves a 100-node cluster with peak 45% of nodes disagreeing for ~10 rounds, and a healed partition reconverges in 1.8), [cap-theorem] (membership gossip is the AP corner, and gossip cannot elect anything), [redis] (Cluster bus PFAIL→FAIL is gossip with a quorum gate; fencing token ≈ incarnation number), [paxos] (the CP contrast: consensus decides one value and knows it decided; gossip spreads values and never knows it finished), [networking-for-developers] (timeout-vs-retry, MTU limits on message size, loss), [uber-system-design] (Ringpop = SWIM + consistent hashing, the pairing that answers "which node owns which driver"), [cqrs] (consistency window renamed), [event-sourcing] (expected-version ≈ incarnation) · Related: [distributed-rate-limiting] (its one "periodic sync/gossip" bullet, expanded) · Next: [crdts?] (the merge rule generalised from membership to data), [quorums-and-replication?], [raft], [vector-clocks?] (gossip's version numbers cannot order concurrent updates), [kafka?]

## whatsapp-system-design — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 69-whatsapp-system-design/

- Takeaway: A chat system is hard for one reason that is invisible from outside — **the person you are messaging is usually not there** — and the whole design is the consequence. Two sentences generate it: *turn the connection around* (the phone holds one open connection so the server can speak first; polling is not a tuning problem but a shape problem — 2B phones at one poll per 2 s is **1 billion req/s** of "nothing", against ~1.2M actual messages/s) and *the server is a queue, not a database* (one durable FIFO **per device**, written before the sender is acknowledged, deleted only when that device confirms). The capacity arithmetic is the punchline and the interview tell: 500M concurrent connections but only ~**200 GB** of pending messages — **this is a connection problem wearing the costume of a database problem**, and "we shard the messages table" is the wrong opening.
- The five invariants, each earned by a failure the lab watched: (1) never acknowledge what is not yet durable — swap the two lines in `Server.onSend` and a grey tick becomes a lie about a message that exists nowhere; (2) delete only on the *device* ack, never on a successful push — planted bug `delete-on-push` fails 4 checks and produces silent loss that *looks like an offline user*; (3) **message identity belongs to the sending device** — bug `new-id-on-retry` fails only 2 checks and only under loss, because on a healthy network retries never execute; (4) a device accepts each id at most once — at-least-once + client dedup = exactly-once *on screen only* (80 copies reached the device, 10 lines appeared); (5) one ordered connection + FIFO queue = per-conversation ordering, which is **inherited, not implemented** — turn overtaking on and order goes `[12,2,5,1,4,7,8,6,10,11,3,9]` with no code changed and no test failing.
- The lab's best moment is a real bug it hit, not an invented one: at 30% loss, **4 of 20 messages stranded forever on a network that had become perfect**, both phones online. Cause: the push-retry timer is created by the server that *receives* the server-to-server hand-off, so if that hand-off packet dies, no timer exists anywhere. Fix is not a better push — **the queue is the guarantee and the push is only a latency optimisation**; a periodic mailbox sweep takes it to 20/20. Generalisation worth carrying: any design whose delivery guarantee depends on a push succeeding has a hole, and "what if the internal hand-off is lost?" is the interview question that finds it.
- Other measured shapes: fan-out on write is linear and paid per message (256-member group = **255 mailbox writes, 1,244 packets** for one typed message; sender keys make it 255 *encryptions once* rather than per message); multi-device means 3 mailbox copies for 1 typed message and makes "delivered" genuinely ambiguous (first device vs all devices — no correct answer, most products over-promise because a stuck tick costs more support than an optimistic one); a 50k-phone reconnect storm does **50,000 registry writes with zero messages sent**, so connection churn, not message volume, sizes the fleet; the ticks are three different facts and one grey tick says nothing whatsoever about the recipient.
- Key terms now known: persistent connection, connection server (holds sockets, zero durable state, disposable), routing table / session registry (device→server, lease with TTL; a crashed server leaves rows that are **lies**), mailbox / per-device durable FIFO, catch-up drain, server ack vs device ack vs read receipt (the three ticks, stated precisely), at-least-once, deduplication by sender-minted id, fan-out on write vs fan-out on read (chat vs feeds/Discord channels), delivery sweeper, stale session row, thundering herd / reconnect storm, exponential backoff with jitter, presence (ephemeral, never queued), end-to-end vs transport encryption, sender key, retention window (~30 days, the one deliberate data loss), multi-device, connection pinning as the source of ordering.
- Lab contents: 9 Java files, no deps (`java src/Tests.java` = 31 scenario checks; `java src/Experiments.java` = 9 measured failures: trace/offline/crash/partition/dup/group/ordering/storm/capacity; three planted bugs behind `bug=` flags, each disabling exactly one invariant), `visualization.html` (published artifact — routing table and mailboxes as live tables, step/step-back, crash a server, cut the network, drop/duplicate sliders, sweeper and ordered-connection switches, four predict-then-run scenarios), README with four depth layers and 4 prediction checkpoints, EXERCISES + SOLUTIONS (8 rungs, incl. 3-datacentre design, adding server-side history, 1M-subscriber broadcast, and a payments/outbox twist).
- Builds on: [communication-protocols] (push vs pull made into a product; WebSocket/long-poll choice), [io-multiplexing] (how one box holds ~1M idle sockets — the connection server IS that event loop), [transactional-outbox] (the mailbox is an outbox; the phone is the idempotent consumer; the phone's own send queue is the same pattern with the device as the database), [cap-theorem] (partition = queue and wait; chat gets an easy ride because it never needs two machines to agree), [caching] (the routing table is a cache of a fact that is briefly true), [btree-and-lsm] (append/read-once/delete workload; tombstone pressure), [tls] (transport vs end-to-end — the server holds ciphertext it cannot read), [consistent-hashing] (sharding the queue store by device id), [distributed-rate-limiting] (fan-out abuse, group caps), [two-phase-commit]+[paxos] (the coordination this design proudly avoids — the contrast is the lesson) · Related: [uber-system-design] (same genre, opposite hard part: claiming one moving resource vs never losing a message for someone absent; both land on at-least-once + idempotent receiver and a saga pivot for payment) · Next: [kafka?] (the shared-log model this design deliberately is not), [push-notifications?] (APNs/FCM — the missing half of mobile delivery; iOS cannot hold a background socket), [news-feed-design?] (fan-out on read, the mirror image), [load-balancing?], [end-to-end-encryption?] (Signal protocol, sender keys)

## consistent-hashing — learned 2026-08-28 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-09 — lab: 35-consistent-hashing/
- Takeaway: The whole subject is one bug and one substitution. The bug: `hash(key) % n` makes every key's location a function of a **global property** of the cluster, so changing the machine count changes almost every answer at once — measured at 100→101 machines, modulo moves **99.0%** of keys where only **1.0%** had to move, and it gets worse as you grow. The substitution: hash keys AND machines into one fixed circle and give each key to the first machine clockwise, so the machine count appears nowhere in the rule and there is nothing global left to change. The guarantee this buys has a name, **monotonicity** — adding a machine moves keys only ONTO the newcomer, never sideways between existing ones — and it is a hard zero, measured: of the 181,668 keys modulo moved on a 10→11 change, **163,679 went sideways** achieving nothing, against the ring's 0.
- The thing that actually matters in production is not tidy data movement, it is that **your source of truth survives a deploy**: every key that moves is a cache entry now on the wrong machine, so they all miss at the same instant. Measured on a 10-machine cache adding an 11th mid-traffic, hit ratio per 25k-request window: modulo `92% → 59%` (a cold cache, instantly, fleet-wide) vs ring `92% → 90%`; the database took 10,313 misses vs 2,622. And the storm is a **transient** — both recover in a minute — which is exactly why it vanishes from a 5-minute average and takes down the database rather than the cache. Same number governs the operational failure nobody mentions: membership propagates, so during that window clients hold different lists and disagree about ownership — ring 9.5% of the keyspace, modulo 90.8%.
- **Virtual nodes** exist for two reasons and the famous one is the lesser one. Balance: 8 machines at one position each gave the busiest **4.74×** its fair share (random points do not cut a circle into equal arcs, and the arc IS the share); 100 positions → 1.06×; past ~500 the curve is inside its own noise (2,000 positions measured *worse* than 500 — the guarantee is statistical) while rebuild cost keeps climbing, which is why Cassandra went 256 → 16 tokens with deliberate allocation. The reason that would survive a design review: with one position each, a dead machine's ENTIRE share lands on one arbitrarily-chosen neighbour — measured +483%, and the inheritor happened to be the quietest machine in the cluster, so it is a cascade with a mechanism. With 200 positions it is a uniform +10–19% on everyone. Weight = position count (a 4× box gets 4× the positions), but ratios need enough positions to mean anything: at 5 positions a weight-1 machine took 1.68× its entitlement.
- Alternatives worth knowing, all measured on the same workload: **rendezvous hashing** (score every machine by `hash(key+machine)`, take the max) predates consistent hashing by a year, needs no ring/vnodes/tuning, balances better (1.06× vs 1.16×) and spreads a dead machine's keys perfectly — but is O(machines): 0.384 µs at 10 machines, 3.5 µs at 100, **34 µs at 1,000** vs the ring's flat ~0.1–0.25 µs, so the crossover is ~30–50 machines and small clusters should probably use it. **Jump hash**: zero memory, best balance, and structurally cannot express "machine 3 died". **Bounded loads** caps the tail (HAProxy ships it) but is stateful, so it is a load balancer's tool and never a database's. Balance was never the hard part — modulo wins that column too.
- The correction that matters given the sibling labs: **Redis Cluster and Kafka do NOT use consistent hashing.** `CRC16(key) % 16384` is plain modulo, safe only because 16,384 never changes, plus a coordinator-published slot→node table. That is the other legitimate answer to §4's bug, and the trade is **coordination for control**: with a coordinator you get exact placement, hand-movable slots, and no disagreement window; without one you get statistical balance and a window bounded by 1/n. Pick fixed slots if you already run a coordinator and control your clients; pick the ring if the clients are not all yours.
- Failure modes that are NOT solved by any of this: a **hot key** (every scheme agrees one key has one owner — that agreement is the feature), **data movement** (the ring is a pure placement function; it says where things belong and moves nothing, so joining a node is a separate throttled streaming operation with its own state machine), and **client incompatibility** (a ring is determined by the hash + the vnode naming format + the position count; differ on any one and two clients silently disagree about every key forever — this is what ketama compatibility actually means). Also a **range mismatch** kills it silently: a 32-bit key hash on a 64-bit ring put all 20,000 keys on ONE machine with the other seven idle, and nothing collided — the keys simply never reach most of the circle.
- Lab shape: runnable Java (`src/`, no deps) with all five strategies behind one `Placement` interface — 22 scenario tests, 9 experiments, 3 planted bugs. Two exercises worth doing above the rest: **6.4** (planted bug 1 destroys monotonicity while the test *named after monotonicity* passes — because appending a machine never renumbers anyone, so a test named after a property only tests it under one operation) and **8.4** (migrate a live memcached fleet off modulo without a storm; the naive cutover remaps 94.9%). The shipped `Ring` also contains one disclosed latent bug the suite misses — a weighted machine silently drops to weight 1 if it re-registers — left in because the uncovered surface is *sequences of operations nobody tested*, here re-add.
- Key terms now known: modulo hashing, hash space/ring, node, key, clockwise owner/successor, the wrap, remapping fraction, monotonicity, virtual node/vnode/token, imbalance, weight-as-position-count, replication factor, failure domain, bounded loads, rendezvous hashing (HRW), jump consistent hash, hot key, miss storm, membership propagation window, ketama, hash slot, range partitioning, Maglev hashing, Karger et al. 1997
- Builds on: [redis] (its 16,384 slots are the *contrast* — this lab corrects the assumption that they are consistent hashing; the hot-key lesson reappears unchanged), [caching] (the miss storm is that lab's cold cache with a specific trigger — your own deploy — and single-flight is the mitigation), [distributed-rate-limiting] (hot key and sub-key splitting, identical remedy), [database-landscape] (partition key, masterless, hot partition), [cap-theorem] · Related: [btree-and-lsm] (hash destroys ordering, which is why range partitioning exists) · Next: [kafka?] (partitions are the fixed-slot answer, and consumer-group rebalance is this lab's F4 with teeth), [gossip-protocols?] (how the membership list this lab assumes actually propagates), [quorums-and-replication?], [load-balancing?]

## uber-system-design — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 70-uber-system-design/

- Takeaway: A ride-hailing system has exactly one hard problem — **matching a moving supply to a moving demand in milliseconds without ever promising the same car to two people** — and it is solved by two ideas plus a deadline. First, stop *searching* for nearness and *index* by it: file drivers into map cells so "who is near me?" is a handful of key lookups, which took 100k drivers from 3.37 ms/request (full scan) to 0.01 ms with ring expansion that stops once it holds 10 candidates — 674×, and the cost now tracks nearby supply rather than city size. Second, **a match is a claim, not a query result**: "nearest free car" stops being true the instant somebody acts on it, so the match is *taken* with one `compareAndSet(AVAILABLE→OFFERED)` — measured, read-then-write promised ~98 of 200 riders a car somebody else also got, and CAS promised 0, with no lock anywhere. Third, every reservation and every driver position is a **lease with a deadline**, which is what makes one code path recover from a driver ignoring the popup, a dead phone, and a crashed matcher alike; the freshness half of it stopped 65 of 200 dispatches to cars that were not there.
- The failures that shaped it: cell-edge bug (own-cell-only search → 44.9% of requests find nobody while a car sits across the line, and a further 13.3% silently return a worse car); hot cell (whole fleet at one airport → 15–25× slower, 6,399 cars in one 500 m square — the same hot-key shape as Redis/rate-limiting); greedy vs batched matching (batching improved mean pickup distance 12.9%, paid for with seconds of everyone's time); ghost drivers; retry/double-tap; partition (the claim is CP per driver id, the geo index is AP); payment-after-trip (trip = saga pivot step).
- Key terms now known: dispatch/matching (DISCO), supply/demand/marketplace, geospatial index, cell, ring search + early stopping, cell size as a U-shaped cost curve, ETA vs distance ranking, road factor, offer (reservation with a deadline), lease, claim/compare-and-set, fencing via offer id, heartbeat/ping TTL, freshness, ghost driver, idempotency key, surge as a control signal, hot cell, location firehose (~250k writes/s ≈ 150× the dispatch rate), batched matching, greedy vs global assignment, trip state machine, geo index as a cache vs trip store as source of truth, H3/S2, Ringpop, Schemaless, on-call signals (stale-pin ratio, ingest lag, offers-per-trip, acceptance rate)
- Lab contents: 13 Java files (no deps, `java src/Sim.java` narrated evening, `java src/Experiments.java` six measured failures, `java src/Tests.java` 14 asserted guarantees, `java src/Tests.java broken` runs the planted-bug index for the debug exercise), `visualization.html` = the Dispatch Console (every matcher rule is a switch; predict-then-run situations), README with four depth layers, EXERCISES + SOLUTIONS.
- Builds on: [cap-theorem] (claim CP / index AP, stated explicitly), [caching] (the geo index IS a cache — derived, rebuildable, allowed stale; TTL reused), [redis] (lease + fencing token + hot key, third context), [transactional-outbox] (trip event and trip commit together), [saga] (trip is the pivot step; payment can only go forward), [distributed-rate-limiting] (shedding, breakers, fail-open with degraded quality), [software-architecture-patterns] (the service boundaries and the metastable-failure toolkit), [communication-protocols] (at-least-once + idempotent consumer), [java-memory-model] (the CAS itself), [cqrs] (out-of-order updates → versioned, idempotent projections), [btree-and-lsm] (index-vs-scan, different question), [consistent-hashing] (which node OWNS a driver id, so the claim has a single owner; both of its answers — ring+gossip like Ringpop, or coordinator-published slots — are live options for dispatch, and its 1/n disagreement window is exactly the window in which two nodes must not both think they own one driver) · Related: [two-phase-commit] (the claim is its cheap single-resource cousin) · Next: [kafka?], [geospatial-indexing?] (H3/S2/quadtree/R-tree), [load-balancing?], [idempotency-keys?]

## google-docs-system-design — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 71-google-docs-system-design/
- Scope: HLD of Google Docs, with the collaborative-editing core built and broken in full. Doc §1-9 beginner, §10 mechanism, §11-14 engineering, §15 the production architecture + capacity method + a 45-minute interview script.
- Takeaway: Collaborative editing is one product decision — **your keystroke appears before any server has agreed to it** — and every other piece of the design is the bill for it. Sending whole documents loses updates silently (last save wins); sending `insert('S' at 3)` and applying it as sent diverges, because the instruction was correct when written and wrong when run. The fix is to **rewrite each operation against the operations it was written without knowing about** (operational transformation), with one server per document deciding the official order. Measured on identical harnesses: the naive design diverged in **500 of 500** random schedules; OT in **0 of 1000** (3 clients x 30 edits). The spine example is `HAT` -> Alice inserts C at 0, Bob inserts S at 3 -> naive gives Alice `CHAST` and Bob `CHATS`; OT rewrites Bob's op to `insert('S' at 4)` and both read `CHATS`.
- Four rules, each a scar from a failure that was actually run: (1) stamp every edit with the revision it was written against, and the server transforms it against the log tail; (2) **the client transforms too, in both directions** — when Bob's edit reaches the server first, the server cannot know about Alice's unconfirmed C, so only Alice's own machine can fix her copy; (3) **one edit in flight per client**, the rest queued — two in flight both claim the same base revision and the server double-transforms the second (`server THREW: insert at 5 but doc is only 4 long`); (4) concurrent inserts at one position ordered by a **fixed tie-break** (site id) — deleting that single line diverges **493 of 500** runs while nothing crashes and every individual edit looks correct. Fifth, unstated-but-required: per-client **ordered** delivery — replaying two broadcasts backwards throws immediately, which is what buys the one-WebSocket-per-client transport.
- Failure lessons that generalise far beyond documents: a lost *request* only stalls a client (nothing corrupts, because revision never advances), but a lost *confirmation* + retry duplicates the keystroke (`CCHAT`) unless the server keeps a per-client edit number and **re-sends the original confirmation** — an idempotency key in its smallest form, identical to the payments duplicate-charge problem. Confirming an edit that is not yet durable is silent permanent corruption: after a restart that lost 2 log entries, clients hold revision 4 against a log of 2 and every later edit is transformed against a history that no longer exists. Healing after an absence costs ~(edits missed x edits made) transforms (200 x 20 = 8,200 measured), which is why real clients get "your revision is too old" and reload. One stalled client's backlog is held by the *server* (100 queued messages, measured), so per-client queue caps + forced reload are mandatory.
- Architecture: the only non-negotiable line is **documentId -> one owning process** (two logs = two revision-4s = no shared meaning), which forces sticky routing, an ownership lease with a fencing token, careful handover on deploy, and makes a busy document a textbook hot key that sharding cannot help. Storage is snapshot + log (event-sourcing, third context) and version history falls out of it for free. CRDTs are the other answer — character identities instead of positions, so ops commute and no sequencer is needed (measured 0/500 divergence with no server, duplicates included) — paid for in metadata + tombstones (1,000 cells for 100 visible chars) and needed for offline-first; OT is cheaper on the wire and in memory, which is why a product that already has a central server picks it.
- Key terms now known: lost update, operation, apply, revision, operation log, convergence, intention preservation, operational transformation (OT), transform function, site id tie-break, outstanding vs buffer, one-in-flight rule, sequencer, no-op (delete-vs-delete), late joiner, duplicate suppression, idempotency key, ordered per-client channel, snapshot, delta/retain-insert-delete, presence, hot document, fencing token, ownership lease, CRDT, RGA, tombstone, causal delivery, semantic conflict, Jupiter/Wave lineage
- Lab shape: `src/` = Ot (transform, ~60 lines of logic), Collab (server+client+hand-driven network, swappable transform), Demo (4 designs narrated), Tests (10 scenario tests incl. a 1000-schedule fuzz), Experiments (8 failures: two-in-flight, raw-vs-ot, lost-request, lost-ack, out-of-order, offline-burst, stale-server, slow-client), Crdt (RGA, no server), BrokenOt (planted bug), YourTransform (blanked-out rules, self-checking: 0/5 -> 5/5). Viz "Two Cursors, One Sentence" has 3 designs x hand-delivered messages, 6 predict-then-run scenarios, verified headlessly against the Java results. NOT built: rich text, range deltas, persistence, presence, access control, undo — all named as omissions in §15. A CRDT bug was found by its own fuzz mid-build (a delete arriving before its character was silently lost, 428/500 divergence) and fixed.
- Builds on: [event-sourcing] (log + snapshot + fold is literally the storage design), [communication-protocols] (WebSocket as the ordered channel, at-least-once, idempotency), [cap-theorem] (one owner = CP for the shared doc, AP for your own screen), [io-multiplexing] (a million idle connections at the edge), [transactional-outbox]/[saga] (at-least-once -> idempotent consumer, same mechanism), [redis] (single-threaded-per-document = no races; fencing tokens over leases), [two-phase-commit] (locking the document = the blocking problem), [distributed-rate-limiting] (hot key: sharding spreads keys, never one key), [whatsapp-system-design] (same connection tier, and the same never-ack-what-is-not-durable rule reached from the other side), [consistent-hashing] (documentId -> owner, but exclusive rather than preferred) · Related: [cqrs] (search index as a read model), [uber-system-design] · Next: [crdt?], [lamport-clocks?], [idempotency-keys?], [websocket-at-scale?]

## redis — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 13-redis/
- Takeaway: Redis's whole design follows from one sentence — **every command runs to completion on one thread, one at a time** — and that sentence is simultaneously the source of its best guarantee and its worst failure. Atomicity is not a feature Redis implements but concurrency it refuses to have: verified by running the identical command code both ways, 8 clients × 2,000 INCR lost **0** on one thread and **4,876 of 16,000 (30.5%)** on four, with no lock anywhere in either. The second invention is that the server knows the value's TYPE, which is what makes `INCR`/`LPUSH`/`ZADD` expressible as one command at all — that, not speed, is the real gap between Redis and memcached, because an opaque-bytes cache forces get-modify-set in the client and the gap that loses updates. The price of the one thread is total shared fate: one `KEYS` over 200k keys took innocent GETs on other connections from **p99 1,011 µs to 191,661 µs**, a 190× collapse, safety fully intact and liveness gone.
- Every mechanism is a scar from a failure, and the numbers are the lesson: exact LRU costs 2 pointers/key so Redis **samples 5** instead — measured curve 2→70.4%, 5→73.8%, 10→74.9%, 50→75.6% against exact 75.7% and random 66.6%, i.e. the shortcut costs 1.9 points and having any policy at all is worth 7. There is no timer per key either, so expiry is lazy (on touch) **plus** a sampled sweep, which means `DBSIZE` reports RESIDENCY not liveness and an instance can sit at maxmemory holding only corpses (50k dead keys needed 157 sweeps). Durability has three distinct moments — buffer → `write()` → `fsync()` — and conflating them causes the biggest surprise in the lab: **`kill -9` with `appendfsync no` loses NOTHING** (the page cache is the kernel's, not the process's), while power loss costs one fsync window (measured 530 ms of traffic), and `always` buys zero loss at **3.4× lower throughput**. Replication is async, so "Redis said OK" and "this survives a failover" are different statements separated by whatever the lag happens to be: 500 acked writes, 500 lost. `WAIT` measures that window and cannot close it — Redis has no rollback.
- The three things most likely to bite in production code: a Redis "transaction" (MULTI/EXEC) batches so nothing interleaves but **cannot roll back** — a failing command mid-batch leaves the others committed; `SET k v NX PX` is a good way to make duplicate work RARE and not a correctness mechanism, because a GC pause longer than the TTL puts two holders in the critical section (measured) and the only fix is a **fencing token checked by the resource**, not by Redis; and WATCH is optimistic concurrency that degrades badly under contention (4.8 aborts per success, 30× slower than the equivalent single command) so if one command expresses what you want, use it. Also worth carrying: propagate the EFFECT not the REQUEST — Redis rewrites `EXPIRE`→`PEXPIREAT`, `SPOP`→`SREM`, and `SET ... NX PX`→`SET ... PXAT` before anything reaches a replica or the AOF, and turning that last one off makes the live server and the recovered server disagree about who holds a lock (demonstrated).
- Lab shape: a working single-threaded RESP server in Java (`src/`, no deps) — 25 scenario tests, 9 experiments, 3 planted bugs. **One planted bug is caught by no test**, deliberately left in as exercise 6.5: every test asserts one command's behaviour and none asserts that two commands AGREE about the same key, which is exactly the shape the quietest bug hides in.
- Key terms now known: key-value store, in-memory, single-threaded command execution (execution only — I/O and fsync are not), atomic (Redis vs ACID vs JMM senses), data type as the delivery mechanism for atomicity, RESP, framing, pipelining, round trip, TTL, lazy vs active expiry, resident vs alive, DBSIZE lies, maxmemory, eviction policy (noeviction/allkeys-lru/lfu/volatile-*), sampled LRU, AOF, appendfsync (no/everysec/always), page cache, RDB, fork + copy-on-write memory spike, replica, async replication, replication lag, failover, WAIT, min-replicas-to-write, split brain, MULTI/EXEC (no rollback), WATCH, fencing token, Redlock, hot key, big key, hash slot (16384), hash tag, Sentinel, Cluster, Pub/Sub (fire-and-forget) vs Streams, OBJECT ENCODING (listpack/intset/skiplist/embstr), SLOWLOG, UNLINK vs DEL, SCAN vs KEYS, Lettuce vs Jedis, Valkey
- Builds on: [io-multiplexing] (Redis IS that Selector loop with a hash table behind it — framing, readiness, shared fate all reappear), [caching] (eviction policies and TTL, but the lab's key correction is that Redis is NOT by definition a cache — that is a config choice), [distributed-rate-limiting] (its "atomic Lua" is exactly this; the RATELIMIT command shows why), [cap-theorem] (async replication IS the AP corner; min-replicas-to-write is the dial), [btree-and-lsm] + [event-sourcing] (AOF+RDB is WAL+checkpoint / log+snapshot, third context), [java-memory-model] (the atomicity disambiguation), [two-phase-commit] (the padlock's blocking problem, in §4) · Related: [http-caching], [transactional-outbox] (the answer to "don't put the payment only in Redis") · Next: [consistent-hashing] (Cluster's 16384 slots — the one black box this lab left), [kafka?], [idempotency-keys?], [load-shedding?]

## ssh — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 15-ssh/
- Takeaway: SSH and TLS run the same cryptography and answer the identity question in opposite ways — TLS asks an authority you trusted in advance, SSH asks **your own memory of last time** (`known_hosts`), and that one substitution explains every strength and every failure. Encryption is never the hard part: verified by running it, a man in the middle who simply performs the key exchange twice reads `cat /home/ravi/.aws/credentials` in plaintext while both ends see a flawless encrypted session — so the host key exists only to SIGN the exchange hash, never to encrypt (leak the host key a year later and design A's recorded traffic decrypts, real SSH's does not: measured, both run in exp 6). A signature only ever proves "the holder of key X signed this", never that X is the key you wanted — Mallory's own host-key signature verifies as `true` — so everything rests on `known_hosts`, whose protection starts at connection **two**. TOFU's failure is worth memorising in its inverted form: if an attacker was present on your FIRST connection you pin HER key, and the alarm then fires on the honest server (exp 4 story B). Two independent checks that get conflated: *is this proof genuine and for this connection* (signature over the session identifier — remove it and a hostile server replays your login elsewhere: measured `true` then `false` in exp 5) versus *is this key permitted at all* (`authorized_keys`); `BrokenAuth.java` does the first perfectly, skips the second, and admits a stranger who generated a key five seconds ago. Verified with the real OpenSSH binaries: when the host key check fails the client sends NO username, NO key and NO signature — machine identity is settled before anything about the user is disclosed.
- Key terms now known: host key (vs user key — the most-confused pair), fingerprint (= SHA-256 of the public-key blob, so comparing fingerprints ≡ comparing keys; verified by building a key in Java that `ssh-keygen -lf` fingerprints identically), known_hosts vs authorized_keys (opposite ends, opposite questions), trust on first use, exchange hash H, session identifier (= first H, fixed for the connection's life even across rekeys, because auth signatures are bound to it), ephemeral key, forward secrecy, transport/authentication/connection layers, channel, multiplexing, port forwarding (-L/-R/-D), ssh-agent (signs on request, never reveals), agent forwarding (-A) vs ProxyJump (-J), bastion, StrictModes, SSH certificate/host CA/user CA/principal, @cert-authority, @revoked, TrustedUserCAKeys, AuthorizedPrincipalsFile, RevokedKeys, `restrict` + forced commands, ControlMaster, rekeying, Binary Packet Protocol, ssh-keyscan (convenience, NOT verification)
- Diagnostics worth keeping: `Permission denied (publickey)` has two unrelated causes — your key file being world-readable (client refuses to USE it; the real cause is three lines above the last line) and your key not being in `authorized_keys`. Separate them with ONE piece of evidence: the fingerprint. If it appears in the server's `Failed publickey for … SHA256:…` log the key travelled and the server said no; if not, it never left your laptop (`ssh -v` → `Offering public key`). Also: the parenthesised list in the error names the methods the SERVER accepts. And `H = hash(K)` alone already stops a MITM (measured 0/200 over three hash variants) — the exchange hash's other inputs buy DOWNGRADE protection, not participant binding.
- Builds on: [tls] (same three problems, opposite trust model — its trust store is filled in advance by someone else with authorities, `known_hosts` is filled by you as you go with individual machines; its forward-secrecy lesson transfers unchanged, and its "encryption ≠ identity" demo has an exact SSH twin), [networking-for-developers] (SSH is one TCP connection whose handshake is the expensive part — hence ControlMaster), [communication-protocols] (multiplexed channels over one persistent connection) · Related: [software-architecture-patterns] (bastion = a boundary pattern) · Next: [public-key-cryptography?] (three labs have now USED signatures/key exchange/hashing without opening the box), [certificate-management-and-pki?], [zero-trust-access?], [oauth-and-jwt?]
- Lab specifics: `java SshLab.java` = 7 experiments on JDK primitives only (X25519 + Ed25519 + AES-GCM, no deps); `./ssh-real.sh` = 8 scenarios against a REAL unprivileged `sshd` on 127.0.0.1:2222, sandboxed to `./lab` and provably never touching `~/.ssh` (agent socket pinned there too, since macOS otherwise puts it in `~/.ssh/agent/`); `java BrokenAuth.java` = planted bug that passes its happy path and admits a stranger. Viz "The Host Key Decision" has all 8 wire×memory×human combinations, each with a distinct ending; smoke-tested via osascript JXA (no node on this machine). NOT reproduced: certificate-based auth (described in stage 15, never run), agent-forwarding hijack (explained, not demonstrated), keystroke-timing recovery (cited, only lengths measured). Exp 3 was corrected mid-build: the "honest" exchange hash was first computed from the client's shared secret, which made a MITM fail for the wrong reason.

## tls — learned 2026-08-28 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-08 — lab: 14-tls/
- Takeaway: TLS solves three separate problems — privacy, integrity, and identity — and **only the third is hard**. Two strangers CAN agree a secret over a public wire (ephemeral key exchange), but that only gives you a secret with *whoever answered*; nothing in it says who that was. Identity is solved by delegating trust in advance: you pre-trust a handful of certificate authorities, and they sign certificates vouching for servers you have never met, which you verify OFFLINE with a key you already hold. The lab's central demonstration (exp 3, run for real): an impostor with a certificate saying `CN=localhost` signed by its own CA gets a flawless TLSv1.3 / TLS_AES_256_GCM_SHA384 connection and is handed the password — the ONLY thing that changed between rejection and compromise was which trust store the client used. So the padlock never meant "you are talking to your bank"; it means "nobody else can read this", and which of those two you get is decided by the trust store alone. Four checks run, in order, and each has a different owner: chain reaches a trusted root (yours), name matches the SAN list (the URL or the cert request), dates valid (the server team's renewal, or YOUR clock), and the server signed the transcript proving it holds the private key. Verified by running it: the SAME certificate is rejected for `localhost` and accepted for `other.example` — hostname verification isolated. Crucially in Java, hostname verification is OPT-IN on a raw SSLSocket (`setEndpointIdentificationAlgorithm("HTTPS")`) — delete that one line and a cert for `other.example` is accepted with "identity proved", every other check still passing.
- Key terms now known: symmetric vs asymmetric, key exchange, ephemeral, forward secrecy (TLS 1.3 mandatory — a stolen key can impersonate in future but NOT decrypt recorded past; the v1 RSA-key-transport design could, which is why it was removed), certificate/X.509/SAN, certificate authority, chain of trust, intermediate, trust store (the single most security-critical config), handshake, ClientHello/ServerHello, CertificateVerify (proves possession, since certs are public and copyable), cipher suite, hostname verification, SNI (unencrypted — measured, hostname found at byte offset 153 of the first 456 bytes), session resumption, 0-RTT (replayable → idempotent only, same rule as retries), mTLS, setNeedClientAuth vs setWantClientAuth ("want" enforces NOTHING — measured, anonymous client got the secret), revocation/CRL/OCSP (unreliable; short lifetimes are the mechanism that works), pinning (pin the KEY + a backup pin)
- Measured: TLS 1.3 = 1 RTT, TLS 1.2 = 2 RTT (1.3 guesses the key share in ClientHello); in 1.2 the certificate travels UNENCRYPTED, in 1.3 it does not. Session resumption showed NO measurable CPU win on loopback (5,858 vs 5,345 us for never-resumes) because what it saves is round trips — and an unwarmed version of that same benchmark reported a fake 8.6x speedup that was pure JVM warmup, the same trap as the networking lab.
- Builds on: [networking-for-developers] (that lab measured the TLS phase as one opaque 46 ms box and taught SSLHandshakeException = "connection succeeded, so not a connectivity problem"; this one says WHICH problem, and its connection-reuse lesson matters more here because the reused handshake contains real crypto), [communication-protocols] (HMAC → certificate signature is the same idea with an asymmetric key and a third party vouching; its idempotency rule reappears as the constraint on 0-RTT) · Next: [certificate-management-and-pki?] (the operational half — issuance, rotation, the expiry outage), [oauth-and-jwt?] (TLS proves MACHINE identity; OAuth carries USER identity on top), [service-mesh?], [public-key-cryptography?]
- Lab specifics: `./certs.sh` builds a real private CA + 5 identities with keytool; `java TlsLab.java` runs 6 REAL handshakes (nothing simulated); `java BrokenTrust.java` is the canonical copy-pasted empty-checkServerTrusted bug — PASSES against the real server, FAILS by handing a password to an impostor. NOT reproduced: revocation (failure 7) and 0-RTT replay (failure 8). Gotcha found while building: an expired cert ALSO fails path building and ALSO mentions PKIX, so a diagnostic testing for PKIX first misreports expiry as an untrusted issuer and sends you to the wrong team.

## networking-for-developers — learned 2026-08-28 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-08 — lab: 01-networking-for-developers/
- Takeaway: One line of code (`client.send(request)`) hides six independent systems — your process, the resolver, NAT, a firewall, a load balancer, the server — and each fails in a way that reaches your code as a generic exception or as nothing at all. The skill worth having is reading the symptom BACKWARDS to the layer: **a fast failure means something answered you** (measured: `ConnectException` in 0 ms = host up, wrong port/process down, network path proven healthy), **a slow failure means nothing did** (measured: `SocketTimeoutException` at 3,001 ms against a 3,000 ms limit = firewall DROP or a route to nowhere — and elapsed≈your-timeout is the fingerprint of silence). The worst failure raises no exception at all: a server that accepts and never replies leaves a healthy open socket, and a connect timeout does NOT cover it — measured, still blocked at 4,096 ms with a connect timeout set, versus 801 ms once a read timeout was added; in a bounded thread pool this kills a service with a green error dashboard. Connection setup is a per-CONNECTION cost, not per-request (measured 20 requests: 682 ms/20 conns vs 38 ms/1 conn = 17.9×, and 19.8× at 150 ms RTT), which is why pools exist — and pools create their own failure, since middleboxes silently evict idle connections and `isConnected()` still returns true until you write. TCP guarantees ordered, uncorrupted, retransmitted bytes between two KERNELS; it does not guarantee message boundaries (measured: 3 writes → 2 reads, and with flush() five identical runs gave three different groupings), nor that the application read or processed them — which is exactly why idempotency keys are mandatory rather than nice-to-have.
- Key terms now known: name resolution/DNS, A vs AAAA record, TTL, resolver chain, IP address, port, three-way handshake, RST/refused vs dropped (THE diagnostic), TLS handshake, connect timeout vs read timeout vs whole-request timeout (three different bounds; read timeout resets per read so a slow drip evades it), timeout budget/shrinking inward, keep-alive, connection pool, pool idle-eviction, middlebox, idle timeout, MTU, MTU black hole, ephemeral port/TIME_WAIT, retry storm, backoff+jitter, delivered≠processed
- Real fault diagnosed in-lab (worth remembering as a method): `api.github.com` took 5,027 ms in DNS on this machine while `www.google.com` took 12 ms. A suspiciously ROUND number is a timeout, not a measurement. Traced to the home router at 192.168.1.1 answering AAAA queries only when the answer is yes and staying silent on NODATA; falsified properly with 6 hostnames (3 with IPv6, 3 without) → 6/6 split along "has an AAAA record", not along vendor. Effect: every IPv4-only host costs a flat +5 s on every cold lookup, invisible to application profiling.
- Builds on: [communication-protocols] (this is the floor under every protocol it chose between; its idempotency-key rule gets its justification here — TCP cannot tell you if a request was processed), [io-multiplexing] (sockets/framing known; exp 6 shows the socket-level REASON framing is unavoidable), [software-architecture-patterns] (its timeout/retry/breaker/bulkhead family is abstract until you watch a retry storm cost 9 s against a DROP rule) · Related: [http-caching], [cap-theorem] · Next: [tls?] (this lab treats the handshake as one opaque RTT — that's the box to open; already flagged in communication-protocols), [load-balancing?], [tcp-congestion-control?], [dns-deep-dive?]
- Lab specifics: `java NetLab.java` runs 6 experiments (exp 1 uses the internet, rest are a deliberately-broken in-JVM server); `java BrokenClient.java` is a planted bug that PASSES the unreachable-host check and FAILS by hanging on a silent server. NOT reproduced: ephemeral-port exhaustion. Viz timings are modelled, not captured; exp 4's RTT is simulated with sleep. Methodology note from the solutions: the naive exp-4 benchmark showed a 3.2× "win" at n=1, which is impossible — it was JVM warmup biasing whichever arm ran second.

## compound-engineering-plugin — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 64-compound-engineering-plugin/
- Takeaway: Finishing a task saves the code but loses the reasoning — the dead ends, the trap, the why — so the same discovery gets paid for every time a session ends. Compound engineering makes capture a *step* (`/ce-compound` writes ONE solved problem as a labelled markdown file under `docs/solutions/`) and makes consultation a *search* (`/ce-plan` dispatches a subagent that greps YAML frontmatter across the whole corpus, shortlists ~25, and reads only ~5 bodies). That ordering is the entire design: measured, read-everything costs 68,187 tokens on 96 docs while grep-first costs 4,723 and stays flat as the corpus grows — so a repo that learns more isn't punished for it. But writing is the cheap half: the same corpus scored 12/12 recall when the new task used house vocabulary and **0/12** when it used symptom words (CONCEPTS.md, read before keyword extraction, recovers 9/12 — a mitigation, not a cure), a "lessons this week" batch file makes 4 of 5 learnings structurally unreachable because module/component/problem_type are each single-valued, and after a two-module rename 38% of docs cited dead paths while 52% of everything retrieved was silently rotten. Guarantees are weak and worth sorting honestly: mechanically enforced (frontmatter parse-safety, cited-path checks, fail-closed docs_root, one-learning-per-run, subagents write only to scratch), structurally encouraged (flat retrieval cost, findability), and not guaranteed at all (that the right learning is found, that it's still true, that two runs behave alike). Cost is certain and now; benefit is diffuse and later — nobody can point at the incident that didn't happen — which is the real reason the practice gets dropped.
- Key terms now known: plugin vs skill vs agent/subagent, the loop (brainstorm→plan→work→simplify→review→compound), return arrow, learning/solution doc, frontmatter, bug track vs knowledge track, artifact, docs_root, grep-first retrieval, shortlist cap, context budget, CONCEPTS.md, guidance layer (outranks a retrieved learning — it's loaded at the moment of acting), pattern doc, residual, independence (separately dispatched contexts, not separate personas), confidence anchor, model tier, host prompt budget (silent truncation makes section ORDER load-bearing), /lfg, ce-compound-refresh (Keep/Update/Consolidate/Replace/Delete)
- Builds on: [caching] (a learning is a derived copy that goes stale — but deleting the corpus loses reasoning, not just speed, so the "only a perf optimisation" intuition does NOT carry), [software-architecture-patterns] (ADRs are the pre-AI ancestor; this automates the why-record), [cqrs] (the corpus is a lossy projection of past work with its own consistency window) · Related: [transactional-outbox] (capture-at-the-end has the same "the world can crash between the two acts" shape) · Next: [retrieval-augmented-generation?], [prompt-and-context-engineering?], [architecture-decision-records?]
- Lab specifics: `java CompoundLab.java` runs 4 measured experiments (cost / recall / batching / rot); `java BrokenRetrieval.java` is a planted bug that PASSES correctness and FAILS the cost invariant (2.1x growth vs 1.2x). Read from plugin source v3.23.4 at ~/.claude/plugins/marketplaces/compound-engineering-plugin. NOT run end-to-end on a real repo — runtime claims are read from its instructions, not observed.

## distributed-rate-limiting — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 60-distributed-rate-limiting/
- Scope note: this lab is the **HLD/architecture** layer (requirements → capacity → placement → coordination → failure → production). The six counting algorithms live in `src/` as a runnable low-level appendix from an earlier session (12 tests pass, `java src/LimiterTest.java`).
- Takeaway: A rate limiter that is correct on one machine stops being correct the instant there are two, because the limit is a property of the whole system while the state enforcing it lives inside one process — 20 uncoordinated gateways turn "100/min" into 2,000/min, and the multiplier silently changes every time you autoscale. So the design is a **coordination** choice, not an algorithm choice, and the only real dial is how stale each enforcement point's view of the shared counter may be: exact costs one atomic round trip per request (simulated at 60/s promise, 4 nodes, 180 rps: 180 store ops/s and +6 ms, but exact, and immune to node count and clock skew because only the store's clock is read), while free means nodes count locally and swap totals every T (0 ms added, 8 ops/s, but 2.1× over-admission at T=500 ms, shrinking linearly as T shrinks). The insight that produces the real architecture: per-request coordination costs a trip **per request** while leases/sync cost a trip **per key per node per interval**, so they are cheap in opposite regimes — hence atomic-central for the cold long tail (99.9% of keys, tiny traffic each, and exactness is where small limits matter) and local-plus-sync for the few hundred hot keys, with fail-open / fail-closed / degrade-to-local chosen **per limit**, never once for the whole system.
- Second-order things worth remembering: memory is never the constraint (450k live counters ≈ 54 MB — it is a very small, very hot database, not a big one); sharding spreads *keys* so it does nothing for a hot key, which needs sub-key splitting instead; a **slow** store is far more dangerous than a dead one (F2 — it exhausts request threads while every dashboard stays green); routing the limit key to one owning node makes the distributed problem vanish entirely but only works for one dimension; and a dishonest `Retry-After` makes your own header the synchroniser of a retry storm.
- Key terms now known: rate limit vs quota vs throttling vs load shedding, enforcement point, counter store, key/limit dimension, over-admission vs under-admission, staleness budget, data/coordination/control plane, fail-open/fail-closed/degrade-to-local, hot key, sub-key splitting, policer vs shaper, fixed window, sliding log, sliding counter, token bucket, leaky bucket, GCRA, burst, cost weighting, read-modify-write, lost update, atomic Lua / CL.THROTTLE, sharding, lease/batch, stranded quota, negative cache, periodic sync/gossip, key-owner routing, circuit breaker, bulkhead, shadow mode, 429/Retry-After/RateLimit-*, retry storm, home-region pinning, regional share reallocation, Envoy RLS
- Builds on: [cap-theorem] (fail-open vs fail-closed IS the CAP choice, applied to your own infrastructure), [caching] (every local design is a cache of the counter store; the staleness budget is that lab's consistency window renamed), [software-architecture-patterns] (this is its one rate-limiting bullet expanded — and it needed four siblings, timeout/breaker/bulkhead/shedding, to survive), [communication-protocols] (429 + Retry-After are inputs to somebody else's retry policy) · Related: [http-caching] (a cached copy that keeps serving when its source is unreachable — the control plane rule) · Next: [consistent-hashing], [load-shedding?], [redis], [load-balancing?], [idempotency-keys?]

## io-multiplexing — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 03-io-multiplexing/
- Takeaway: A server's main job is waiting on many mostly-silent connections, so the only question that matters is what waiting costs. One thread per connection is correct but costs memory and scheduler capacity (measured: 2,000 connections = 2,000 threads), while one thread polling everyone burns a full core asking 4.2M pointless questions in 3 seconds. I/O multiplexing hands the whole connection set to the kernel and sleeps: the kernel already knows which connection each packet belongs to, so it wakes you only when something is ready and tells you which — 0 ms CPU and 0 wakeups for 2,000 idle connections. The trade is density for shared fate (one blocking handler freezes every connection on that loop: measured 905 ms of collateral latency) plus a harder programming model, because "readable" means bytes not messages (one 512 KB message = 76 readiness events), so framing and per-connection heap state become mandatory. Crucially for Java: virtual threads did not replace this — the JDK implements them ON it (`sun.nio.ch.Poller`, verified present), so you write blocking thread-per-connection code again and get event-loop efficiency, while framing, backpressure, idle timeouts and fd limits all remain yours.
- Key terms now known: C10K, file descriptor, blocking vs non-blocking (a socket mode), synchronous vs asynchronous (a DIFFERENT axis — who performs the I/O), readiness, interest set vs ready set, event loop, select/poll/epoll/kqueue, O(total) vs O(ready), Selector/SelectionKey/attachment, level-triggered vs edge-triggered, end-of-stream (-1), busy loop, framing, backpressure, thundering herd, SO_REUSEPORT, event loop lag, completion-based I/O (IOCP/io_uring), JDK Poller, ephemeral port exhaustion
- Builds on: [java-virtual-threads] (this is the machinery under mount/unmount — the lab closes the loop on it), [communication-protocols] (persistent connections are why idle-connection cost dominates) · Related: [structured-concurrency] · Next: [netty?], [io-uring?], [backpressure?], [tcp-flow-control?], [reactor-pattern?]

## caching — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 11-caching/
- Takeaway: A cache is a bounded copy of answers whose truth lives elsewhere — and the test that it IS a cache is that deleting it loses only speed. It works purely because access is skewed (measured: 10% of the keyspace served 67.3% of traffic; flatten the popularity and the same cache drops to 17.8%, making it near-worthless), and it demands two deliberate decisions: what to throw out when full, and how stale you'll allow it to be. Eviction is a bet about how the future resembles the past, and no policy wins everywhere (measured: LFU 75.2% on stable popularity and scan-immune, but 48.9% vs LRU's 74.9% once the hot set moves — cache squatting).
