The correctness half is harder than the speed half: cache+DB is a dual write, so even perfectly-executed invalidation loses a race (verified: cache serves ₹100 while truth is ₹120), one expired hot key turns 200 concurrent readers into 200 DB calls unless single-flight collapses them to 1, and a cache guarantees nothing by itself — bounded staleness comes from TTL, and read-your-own-writes/monotonic reads must be engineered.
- Key terms now known: cache vs source of truth, hit/miss/hit ratio, working set, skew, capacity invariant, eviction policy (FIFO/LRU/LFU/Random), scan pollution, cache squatting, W-TinyLFU, cache-aside/lazy loading, cold cache, invalidate-on-write (prefer delete over update), write-through, write-behind, TTL, jitter, cache stampede, single-flight, probabilistic early refresh, stale-fill race, lease (memcached/Facebook), negative caching, cache penetration, near cache / L1-L2, cold start, eventual consistency, read-your-own-writes, monotonic reads, memoization-vs-caching
- Builds on: [software-architecture-patterns] (its F6 + the data-pattern entry, opened up), [transactional-outbox] (the stale-fill race IS the dual write), [cap-theorem] (a cache is a replica, so it answers possibly-stale), [cqrs] (consistency window), [database-landscape] (hot key, derived copies), [btree-and-lsm] (bloom filters, reused for cache penetration), [java-virtual-threads] (the stampede demo) · Related: [http-caching] (the same physics between client and server — there you can only *suggest* an expiry; here you hold the delete button) · Next: [consistent-hashing], [redis], [load-balancing?]

## http-caching — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 12-http-caching/
- Takeaway: Every HTTP response carries its own reuse contract in headers, and a cache is a dumb, obedient contract-follower — so caching is a correctness contract before it is an optimization. Two mechanisms cover everything: **freshness** (`max-age`/`s-maxage` — reuse without asking, saving the entire round trip) and **validation** (`ETag` → `If-None-Match` → `304`, saving only the bytes; measured 98.7% on a 6.5 KB body but just 62.8% on a 239-byte one, since the 304's headers are a fixed floor), plus two rules that prevent the catastrophes: audience (`private`/`no-store`, or a shared cache serves Alice's account page to Bob) and identity (`Vary`, or a compressed copy is handed to a client that can't decompress it). The defining constraint is that **there is no revocation** — you cannot recall a copy from a browser you don't own — which is why production makes invalidation unnecessary (fingerprinted `immutable` URLs + a `no-cache` HTML shell) rather than trying to win it, and why `max-age` should be read as a promise about the future.
- Key terms now known: cache, origin, hit/miss, hit ratio, fresh/stale, freshness lifetime/TTL, Age header, private vs shared cache, public/private/no-store, no-cache (≠ no-store), s-maxage, validator, ETag/Last-Modified, conditional request, 304 Not Modified, cache key, Vary, heuristic caching, purge, fingerprinted URL/cache busting, immutable, safe methods, stale-while-revalidate, stale-if-error, must-revalidate, stampede/dogpile, single-flight, TTL jitter, negative caching, X-Cache/CF-Cache-Status, shield/mid-tier, surrogate key, origin offload
- Builds on: [communication-protocols] (HTTP wire format, RTT dominance, idempotent GET — this lab is that lab's promised "deepest unused superpower of REST"), [cqrs] (identical consistency-window trade, but here the *writer* declares the window and unknown readers obey), [cap-theorem] (`stale-if-error` = choosing AP per response) · Related: [transactional-outbox] (purge-by-surrogate-key rides the same event feed), [software-architecture-patterns] (caching as a data pattern) · Next: [cdn-architecture?], [cache-poisoning?], [application-caching?], [io-multiplexing?]

## btree-and-lsm — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 05-btree-and-lsm/
- Takeaway: Writes arrive in random order, reads want sorted order, and disks move page-sized chunks — so every storage engine must decide *when* to pay for sortedness. A B-tree pays at write time, in place: small pages with slack, one page rewritten per insert, a full page splits and promotes a separator (the tree grows only from the root, which is why all leaves stay at one depth) — flat reads of exactly `height` pages, at brutal write amplification (measured 119.2× vs the LSM's 2.5×) and key-order sensitivity (8 vs 914 cold pages for sequential vs UUID keys). An LSM pays later, in bulk: writes go to WAL + memtable, flush as immutable SSTables, deletes write tombstones, reads race newest-first with bloom filters skipping absent keys (98.3% of disk I/O eliminated), and compaction merges files dropping shadowed values — trading read amplification, perpetual compaction I/O, and tombstone/space debt for cheap sequential writes. Neither is faster; the scorecard (write/read/space amplification) says only which one bleeds.
- Key terms now known: page/node, internal node vs leaf, root, fanout, split/promote/split cascade/root split, B+ tree, separator key, leaf chain, fill factor, memtable, SSTable/sorted run, shadow, flush, newest-wins, bloom filter/false-positive rate, sparse index, size-tiered vs leveled vs time-windowed compaction, write/read/space amplification, write stall, bloat, torn write, gc_grace, clustered index (InnoDB) vs heap+index (Postgres), checkpoint, WAL-before-ack, compaction-is-logically-invisible (invariant)
- Builds on: [database-landscape] (tricks 2–3, one level up) · Related: [event-sourcing] (append-only kinship), [transactional-outbox] (idempotent replay makes WAL recovery safe) · Next: [database-indexing?], [mvcc-and-vacuum?], [kafka?], [consistent-hashing]

## database-landscape — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 04-database-landscape/
- Takeaway: Every database is an opinionated bet on one access pattern — about seven physical tricks (hash, B-tree, LSM, columnar, inverted index, adjacency, append-only log) each make one query shape nearly free and others expensive, so choosing a store means matching your dominant, measured query shape to the store's bet (exp3: same data/SQL, ~550× columnar win on the scan, ~50× row-store win on the point read). Run several stores but appoint one source of truth per fact; everything else is a lagging, rebuildable derived copy kept in sync via outbox/CDC/Kafka, with idempotent consumers and monitored consistency windows. Default to Postgres (+ extensions: JSONB, Timescale, PostGIS, pgvector) and admit new stores only on measured incidents — each added store is a marriage (ops, sync pipeline, on-call), not a download.
- Key terms now known: OLTP vs OLAP, access pattern/query shape, source of truth vs derived data, denormalization, polyglot persistence, schema-on-write/read, hot key/hot partition, B-tree vs LSM (compaction, tombstone), columnar layout, inverted index/analyzer, index-free adjacency, partition/clustering key, masterless, storage/compute separation, downsampling/retention, queue vs log, object storage/Parquet/lakehouse/table format, ANN/HNSW/embedding, cache stampede, consistency window, drift/reconciliation, wide-column vs columnar (opposites)
- Builds on: [cap-theorem] (posture per category), [transactional-outbox]+[cqrs] (derived stores & sync fabric), [event-sourcing] (log as truth), [paxos]+[two-phase-commit] (distributed SQL internals) · Next: [btree-and-lsm?], [kafka?], [consistent-hashing], [change-data-capture?], [database-indexing?]

## communication-protocols — learned 2026-08-28 — rewritten for delivery 2026-09-08 — lab: 02-communication-protocols/
- Takeaway: Every named protocol (HTTP/REST, GraphQL, gRPC, WebSocket, SSE, webhooks, MQTT/AMQP/Kafka, UDP/QUIC) is a reusable answer to one conversation shape, choosable via five axes: who initiates, one answer vs stream, one listener vs many, per-request vs persistent connection, sync vs async. Each was born from a predecessor's specific pain (REST's round trips → GraphQL; JSON's cost inside the walls → gRPC; polling's waste → WebSocket/SSE; TCP head-of-line → QUIC/HTTP-3) — and each has explicit when-NOT rules. Cross-cutting laws: timeout+retry+idempotency is one atom; any persistent connection = distributed state you now own (heartbeats, backoff+jitter, resync).
- Key terms now known: framing, transport, TCP/UDP, socket, request/response, round trip (RTT), push vs pull, serialization, head-of-line blocking, HTTP/2/3, QUIC, REST, statelessness, over/under-fetching, GraphQL, N+1, RPC, gRPC, protobuf, unary/streaming, polling, long polling, WebSocket, Upgrade/101, SSE, Last-Event-ID, webhook, HMAC signature, broker, at-least-once, idempotency key, MQTT, QoS, backpressure, thundering herd, consumer lag, DLQ, connection draining
- Builds on: [transactional-outbox] (queues = mailbox shape; at-least-once + idempotent consumers), [event-sourcing] (Kafka as replayable log; snapshot-vs-replay reappears in reconnect design), [cap-theorem] (partition behavior per protocol family) · Related: [cqrs], [java-virtual-threads] (cheap blocking = many connections) · Next: [io-multiplexing?], [kafka?], [http-caching?], [api-versioning?], [tls?]

## software-architecture-patterns — learned 2026-08-28 — lab: 06-software-architecture-patterns/
- Takeaway: Every architecture pattern is a named way of drawing boundaries so the effect of a change stays predictable and local — and every boundary sends a bill (the First Law: everything is a tradeoff). Patterns are chosen by ranked quality attributes (-ilities) under team and Conway constraints, never by fashion: the default is the simplest thing that fits (modular monolith, boring database), with each added pattern paid for by a named current requirement and its why recorded in an ADR. The seven families: codebase structure (layered/hexagonal/slices/modular monolith), distribution styles (microservices/EDA/serverless), communication (gateway/BFF/mesh/strangler/ACL), data (db-per-service + the saga/outbox/cqrs/ES labs + caching/replication/sharding), resilience (timeout/retry/breaker/bulkhead/shedding — slow is more dangerous than down), deployment (blue-green/canary/flags), and the anti-patterns (distributed monolith above all).
- Key terms now known: coupling/cohesion, temporal coupling, quality attributes/-ilities, blast radius, information hiding, SOLID, dependency inversion, Conway's law, layered, hexagonal/ports-and-adapters, clean/onion, vertical slices, modular monolith, microkernel, pipes-and-filters, microservices, SOA/ESB, event-driven architecture, broker, event notification vs event-carried state transfer, serverless, space-based, cell-based, API gateway, BFF, service mesh/sidecar, anti-corruption layer, strangler fig, DLQ, schema registry, database-per-service, CDC, cache-aside/write-through/write-behind, cache stampede, sharding, read replicas, materialized view, timeout, retry+backoff+jitter, retry storm, metastable failure, circuit breaker, bulkhead, rate limiting, load shedding, backpressure, graceful degradation, hedged requests, chaos engineering, blue-green, canary, feature flags, expand/contract, 12-factor, distributed monolith, golden hammer, ADR, C4 model, fitness function, evolutionary architecture, distributed tracing
- Builds on: [transactional-outbox], [saga], [cqrs], [event-sourcing], [two-phase-commit], [paxos], [cap-theorem] (this lab is the map those labs are territories of) · Next: [ddd-strategic-design?], [kafka?], [caching?], [distributed-tracing?], [load-balancing?]

## two-phase-commit — learned 2026-08-27 — lab: 45-two-phase-commit/
- Takeaway: 2PC makes several independent systems commit or abort one transaction as a unit via a coordinator and a prepare/commit round pair. Its defining weakness is blocking: after voting yes, participants hold locks and cannot decide alone if the coordinator dies — the commit point lives in one place. Paxos-style quorum consensus is the answer to exactly that weakness; outbox and saga are the answers that avoid distributed transactions entirely.
- Key terms: coordinator, participant, prepare/vote, commit point, blocking problem, XA, presumed abort
- Builds on: [transactional-outbox], [saga] · Related: [paxos] · Next: [three-phase-commit?], [percolator?]

## cap-theorem — learned 2026-08-27 — lab: 24-cap-theorem/
- Takeaway: When data lives on multiple machines and the network partitions, you must choose between refusing to answer (consistency) and answering possibly-stale (availability) — you cannot have both. Every distributed store has made this choice somewhere. Paxos implements the CP corner: the majority side keeps working, the minority refuses.
- Key terms: partition, consistency (CAP sense), availability, CP vs AP, split-brain
- Builds on: — · Related: [paxos], [linearizability?] · Next: [pacelc] (learned 2026-08-30), [split-brain] (learned 2026-08-30 — the term this entry named and never opened)

## event-sourcing — learned 2026-08-27 — lab: 50-event-sourcing/
- Takeaway: Store the passbook, compute the box: every change is an immutable event, and state (current or at any past moment) is a fold over the stream. Writes are load → decide → append-if-still-at-expected-version, and a conflict means re-decide against fresh truth, never re-send. Mistakes are corrected by compensating events; snapshots are pure cache; upcasting keeps old events readable forever.
- Key terms: event store, stream, aggregate, replay/fold, apply vs decide, expected version / optimistic concurrency, compensating event, snapshot, upcasting, crypto-shredding
- Builds on: [transactional-outbox], [cqrs] · Related: [saga] · Next: [kafka?], [ddd-aggregates?], [bitemporal-modeling?]

## transactional-outbox — learned before 2026-08-27 — lab: 48-transactional-outbox/
- Takeaway: Saving data and telling other systems about it are two physical acts, and the world can crash between them (the dual-write problem). The outbox writes the state change and the outgoing event in ONE local transaction, and a relay drains the outbox to the broker with at-least-once delivery. Consumers must therefore be idempotent; the pattern trades a little latency and plumbing for never losing or inventing an event.
- Key terms: dual-write problem, outbox table, relay/poller, CDC/Debezium, at-least-once, idempotent consumer
- Builds on: — · Related: [saga], [cqrs], [event-sourcing], [two-phase-commit] (the road not taken)

## saga — learned before 2026-08-27 — lab: 47-saga/
- Takeaway: A saga runs a multi-service business transaction as a sequence of local transactions, each with a compensating action; on failure it runs the compensations backwards instead of relying on a distributed commit. It trades atomicity and isolation for availability — intermediate states are visible, and compensations are business-level undo, not rollback. Orchestration (a coordinator) vs choreography (events) is the main design fork.
- Key terms: local transaction, compensation, orchestration vs choreography, pivot step, semantic lock
- Builds on: [transactional-outbox] · Related: [two-phase-commit], [cqrs], [event-sourcing]

## java-virtual-threads — learned 2026-08-27 — lab: 18-java-virtual-threads/
- Takeaway: A virtual thread is a cheap, heap-backed thread the JVM multiplexes onto a few carrier OS threads; blocking parks the stack on the heap and frees the carrier, making waiting nearly free at 100k+ concurrency. It does nothing for CPU-bound work, and pooling virtual threads or pinning (native frames; synchronized before JDK 24 / JEP 491) brings the old ceiling back. Semantics are unchanged — same Thread, same memory model, same interruption.
- Key terms: carrier thread, mount/unmount, park/unpark, continuation, pinning, JEP 444/491, thread-per-task, scheduler parallelism/compensation
- Builds on: [java-memory-model] · Next: [io-multiplexing?], [goroutine-scheduler?]

## structured-concurrency — learned 2026-08-27 — lab: 19-structured-concurrency/
- Takeaway: Confine every concurrent subtask's lifetime to a code block, making concurrency a tree where errors flow up and cancellation flows down. A scope forks children onto virtual threads, join() waits per a joiner policy (all-succeed fail-fast, first-success-wins, gather-all), and leaving the block guarantees nothing is left running. Cancellation is cooperative interruption; side effects don't roll back; API is JEP 505 preview (JDK 25).
- Key terms: scope, owner, fork, subtask, join, joiner, fail-fast, close guarantee, structure violation, cooperative cancellation
- Builds on: [java-virtual-threads] · Related: kotlin coroutines, trio nurseries · Next: [kotlin-coroutines?], [tail-latency-and-hedging?]

## scoped-values — learned 2026-08-27 — lab: 20-scoped-values/
- Takeaway: A ScopedValue is an invisible final parameter passed to every call beneath its where(...).run(...) block — immutable while bound, gone when the block exits on any path. This kills ThreadLocal's diseases: write-from-anywhere, lifetime-until-remove, copy-on-inherit. Context flows downward only; structured forks inherit by reference; anything escaping the block must capture the value explicitly and fails loudly if it forgets. Final in JDK 25 (JEP 506).
- Key terms: binding, carrier, dynamic scope, rebinding/shadowing, orElse/isBound, inheritance-into-forks, async escape
- Builds on: [java-virtual-threads], [structured-concurrency] · Next: [distributed-tracing?], [reactive-context-propagation?]

## java-memory-model — learned before 2026-08-27 — lab: 17-java-memory-model/ (no README; runnable lab + visualization)
- Takeaway: The JMM defines which reorderings and visibilities are legal between threads; happens-before is the contract, and data races make behavior undefined-ish. (Lab predates this map; entry minimal.)
- Key terms: happens-before, volatile, store buffer, reordering
